# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER
## OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS <note>The Jesuits</note>

# NEW PHONURGIA
<note>original: "Phonurgia Nova." Kircher coined this term from the Greek roots for "sound" and "work/production," effectively meaning "The New Science of Sound-Making."</note>

->OR<-

# A Mechano-Physical Marriage
## OF ART & NATURE
### WITH SOUND-WISDOM AS BRIDESMAID <note>original: "Paranympha Phonosophia." Phonosophia is Kircher's personification of the wisdom or science of sound.</note>

->Composed;<-
->in which<-

*THE ENTIRE NATURE OF SOUNDS, THEIR PROPERTIES, AND POWERS,*
*and the Causes of their prodigious effects, are explained through a new and manifold*
*exhibition of experiments; the method and rationale of Acoustic Instruments*
*and Machines, to be adapted to the prototype of Nature—both for propagating*
*sounds to the most remote distances, and for conversing, whether secretly or*
*openly, within the hidden recesses of homes by means of devices of more*
*concealed ingenuity—is handed down; and finally, the singular*
*Use and practice of such Instruments in the tumults of*
*War is described through a new <term>Phonology<gloss>In this 17th-century context, the systematic study and classification of sounds</gloss></term>.*

<note>original: "Paranympha Phonosophia." Phonosophia is Kircher's personification of the wisdom or science of sound.</note>

->Composed;<-
->in which<-

THE ENTIRE DOCTRINE OF SOUNDS, AND THE WISDOM OF SOUND, is explained through physical-prodigious forces, Causes, and a manifold exhibition of new experiments; a compendium of Acoustic Instruments and Machines to be adapted to the prototype of Nature—both for propagating sounds, and for the voice carried to remote and hidden places of homes through the secret vents of windings <note>Refers to Kircher's "speaking tubes" or acoustic conduits built into walls</note>; and finally, of the new Musical Phonarithms <note>A system for automatic musical composition using mathematical tables</note>, by which anyone, even those unskilled in Music, can with no effort be led most successfully to the composition of any songs in any musical arrangement.
->arranged through a new <term>Phonology<gloss>In this 17th-century context, the systematic study and classification of sounds</gloss></term>.<-

<summary>The title page of Athanasius Kircher's groundbreaking 1673 treatise on acoustics, framing the study of sound as a "marriage" between natural laws and mechanical art. It promises to reveal the secrets of sound propagation and a method for even non-musicians to compose music.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page contains the formal ecclesiastical and Jesuit permissions required for publication. It follows the ornate title page, serving as the legal and theological "green light" for Kircher’s work to be printed in Rome. The text establishes the institutional backing of the Society of Jesus and the Vatican.</meta>

# JOHN PAUL OLIVA
## SUPERIOR GENERAL OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS <note>The Society of Jesus is the religious order commonly known as the Jesuits, of which Kircher was a prominent member.</note>

Since this work, titled *New Phonurgia, or, on the wondrous miracles of sound and the voice to be propagated through machines of every kind*, written by Father Athanasius Kircher, a Priest of our Society, has been reviewed by several theologians of the same Society, and they have approved that it may be published, we grant the authority for it to be sent to the press, if it so seems fit to those to whom it pertains. Given at *Rome, December 1, 1672*.

->JOHN PAUL OLIVA<-

---

->Let it be printed<-
<note>original: "Imprimatur." This is the official license granted by the Roman Catholic Church, certifying that the work contains nothing contrary to faith or morals.</note>

->FRIAR HYACINTHUS LIBELLUS<-
->Master of the Sacred Apostolic Palace<-
<note>The Master of the Sacred Apostolic Palace was the Pope’s primary theologian and the chief censor for books published in Rome.</note>

<summary>This page presents the formal licenses for publication (Imprimatur) granted by the Jesuit Superior General and the Vatican's chief censor, authorizing the printing of Kircher's treatise on acoustics.</summary>
<keywords>John Paul Oliva, Hyacinthus Libellus, Imprimatur, censorship, Jesuit administration, Rome, 1672, publication rights</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page provides the legal and ecclesiastical authorization for the publication of Kircher's work. It contains the formal permission from the Jesuit Superior General and the official license (Imprimatur) from the Vatican's chief censor. This was a standard requirement for seventeenth-century books printed in Rome to ensure theological and political compliance.</meta>

# JOHN PAUL
## OLIVA
### SUPERIOR GENERAL
### OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS <note>The Society of Jesus, or Jesuits, was the influential Catholic religious order to which Athanasius Kircher belonged. John Paul Oliva served as its 11th Superior General.</note>

Since several theologians of our same Society have reviewed the work titled *New Phonurgia* <note>original: "Phonurgia Nova." The term "Phonurgia" was coined by Kircher from Greek roots meaning "the production of sound."</note>, or, on the prodigious wonders of the properties of sound, created both by nature and by art, written by Father Athanasius Kircher, a priest of our Society, and have approved that it may be published, we grant the authority for it to be sent to the press, if it so seems fit to those to whom it pertains. Given at Rome, December 1, 1672.

->JOHN PAUL OLIVA.<-

---

->Let it be printed.<-
<note>original: "Imprimatur." This Latin term is the formal declaration by a Roman Catholic censor that a book is free of doctrinal or moral error and is authorized for printing.</note>

->FRIAR HYACINTHUS LIBELLUS<-
->Master of the Sacred Apostolic Palace<-
<note>The Master of the Sacred Apostolic Palace was the Pope's personal theologian and the primary authority for book licensing in Rome.</note>

# LEOPOLD I
## HOLY ROMAN EMPEROR

->LEOPOLD, EXALTED EMPEROR, THE JUST, PIOUS, AND BLESSED<-
<note>original: "LEOPOLDVS IMP. AVG. IVSTVS PIVS FELIX." These Latin titles—"Augustus" (Exalted/Venerable), "Iustus" (Just), "Pius" (Pious), and "Felix" (Blessed/Fortunate)—were traditional honorifics for the Holy Roman Emperor, modeled after those of ancient Roman Caesars.</note>

---

Franz Herman, artist <note>original: "del." abbreviated from the Latin "delineavit," meaning "he drew this."</note>
G. Andreas Wolfgang, engraver <note>original: "sculp." abbreviated from the Latin "sculpsit," meaning "he engraved/carved this." Georg Andreas Wolfgang was a notable 17th-century German engraver.</note>

<note>The accompanying engraving depicts the Emperor in ornate armor, signifying his role as a military leader, while wearing a laurel wreath of victory. The scene is rich with symbolism: to the left, tools of science and mathematics (globes and books) represent his patronage of learning; to the right, cannons represent his power in war. At the top, the Imperial Crown is heralded by trumpets of fame.</note>

<summary>This frontispiece features a formal portrait and laudatory titles of Emperor Leopold I, Athanasius Kircher's patron, signaling the work's high status and the Emperor's dual role as a supporter of both science and military strength.</summary>

# LEOPOLD I.
## TO THE EMPEROR
## OF THE ROMANS,
### FOREVER
### AUGUST,
### JUST, PIOUS, AND BLESSED,

->PRAYS FOR EVERLASTING HAPPINESS<-

## ATHANASIUS KIRCHER
### OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS <note>The Society of Jesus is the Catholic religious order commonly known as the Jesuits, of which Kircher was a prominent member.</note>.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'V' depicting a seated figure, possibly King David, playing a harp, enclosed within a square frame with scrolling acanthus leaves and floral ornamentation.</image-desc>
WITH THE MIGHTY blast of the Trumpet I will lift up my voice; with the polyphony <note>original: polyphonismo; Kircher uses this to describe many-voiced or harmonious music, a central theme of his work.</note> of the military fanfares <note>original: classicorum; here referring to the ceremonial trumpet signals used to announce a commander or the start of battle.</note> I will shake the very vault of Heaven; with the martial din of horns and pipes <note>original: tibiarum, referring to ancient wind instruments similar to flutes or oboes.</note> I will strike the minds and ears of my listeners; with the most powerful...

<summary>This page serves as the formal dedication of Athanasius Kircher's work to Holy Roman Emperor Leopold I, using grand musical and martial metaphors to announce the author's intent.</summary>
<keywords>Leopold I, Athanasius Kircher, Jesuits, dedication, musical instruments, Trumpet, polyphony, Imperial titles</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Leopold I begun on the previous page. The text continues from the phrase "with the most powerful..." and concludes the sentence describing the Emperor's heroic virtues.</meta>

<header>E P I S T L E</header>
<page-num>4</page-num>
<meta>catchword: state</meta>

...Trumpet of heroic virtues, I say, I will sing a **PAEAN** <note>original: Pæana; a song of triumph or thanksgiving, originally dedicated to Apollo in Greek antiquity.</note>, by which the most Religious **LEOPOLD I**, Emperor of the Romans, forever August, shines forth to the World as a rare example. For if I examine the manner of Your Actions more deeply, I see, O most invincible **CAESAR** <note>Though the title of an ancient Roman leader, "Caesar" was used here as a standard title for the Holy Roman Emperor, emphasizing his role as the successor to the Roman Empire.</note>, within Your Great-Minded heart, in an entirely harmonious order, all the defenses of interlinked virtues which are found elsewhere only in scattered and incomplete forms: Love of Religion, Faith, and Steadfastness toward the Empire entrusted to Your Care; Zeal in routing the enemies of the Christian Commonwealth <note>original: Christianæ Reipublicæ; this refers to the collective body of Christian nations, often used in this era to describe Europe under the spiritual leadership of the Church and the temporal protection of the Emperor.</note>; and a spirit unshaken through prosperity and adversity, despite so many and such great attacks and plots. But indeed, in this precarious 

->state<-

<summary>Athanasius Kircher continues his florid dedication to Emperor Leopold I, praising the Emperor’s "harmonious" blend of virtues, including his religious devotion and his defense of the Christian world against its enemies.</summary>
<keywords>Leopold I, Caesar, heroic virtues, Christian Commonwealth, Paean, Imperial dedication, religious zeal</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Leopold I. It follows the previous page's concluding thought on the "precarious state" of the empire, moving from the Emperor's military and religious zeal to his internal governance and personal mercy.</meta>

# D E D I C A T O R Y.

...state, there were no lack of provocations for Your severity, nor seeds of Civil unrest, and bitter irritations of minds encountered everywhere; yet, by the rule <note>original: amussi; literally a mason's rule or level, used here as a metaphor for the precise and unwavering standard of justice.</note> of Your justice—that is, by the provident moderation of Your great Spirit—those things which were wounded, You healed with Your **Clemency** rather than cutting them away. You, who by even overlooking faults and not anxiously inquiring into everything, preferred to seem to have found good men rather than to have made them; to be loved rather than to be feared. These are the powerful trumpet-calls of virtue with which You fill the World, and with which, through the admirable sublimity of Your Mind, You resonate an **Echo** <note>original: Echonem; Kircher uses a popular Baroque literary device here called an "echo poem," where each subsequent word is formed by dropping the first letter of the previous word, creating a linguistic "fading" effect.</note> unto Your Subjects:

->BY THE SHOUT<- <note>original: CLAMORE</note>
->BY LOVE<- <note>original: AMORE</note>
->BY THE MOUTH<- <note>original: ORE</note>
->BY THE DEED<- <note>original: RE</note>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher praises Emperor Leopold I’s use of clemency and moderation in the face of civil unrest, concluding with a clever "echo" poem that mirrors how the Emperor's virtues resound through his deeds.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dedication to Emperor Leopold I, expanding on the "Echo" poem introduced at the end of the previous page. Kircher transitions from praising the Emperor's moral and political virtues—modeled as a harmonious musical composition—to his intellectual interests in science and philosophy.</meta>

. A I .
# . EPISTLE .

**BY YOUR SHOUT**, you stir us by the example of Your life; by the magnetism of Your **LOVE** and kindness, You draw us; by the uprightness of Your **CHARACTER**, You instruct us; **BY YOUR MOUTH**, for as many words as You pour out, You establish just as many Laws; **BY THE DEED** itself; through Justice in Your investigations, Steadfastness in times of doubt, Prudence in Your counsels, and through Fortitude and Clemency, You so temper the consonant-dissonant <note>original: consono-dissonos; Kircher, who was a renowned music theorist, uses a musical metaphor here. He suggests the Emperor acts as a composer or conductor, resolving the "discordant" or clashing interests of his subjects into a "concordant" or harmonious state.</note> movements of Your Subjects, that You seem to draw the minds of all into an absolute harmony. Indeed, I would prefer to pass over the excellence of these virtues in silence rather than touch upon them too sparingly.

But let me turn my pen to the study of true wisdom and the sciences, by which You are laudably occupied whenever You obtain some free time from the **Burden of the World** <note>original: Mole Mundi; a classical and Baroque trope referring to the heavy weight of imperial responsibility, often likening the ruler to the Titan Atlas who carried the celestial sphere on his shoulders.</note> which You sustain. It is to You, most Wise **CAESAR**,

<vocab>Letter, Caesar, Justice, Steadfastness, Prudence, Fortitude, Clemency, Virtue</vocab>

# . DEDICATION .

not only for the sake of grasping the knowledge of arts and sciences, but also a <term>mind</term> <note>original: Ingenium; refers to innate talent, character, or intellect.</note> wonderfully capable of penetrating into the more hidden mysteries of Nature. For you pave the path to the scholar’s laurel by no other means than through your own example, since even from a tender age, you have erected in your breast a <term>training ground</term> <note>original: palestram; literally a wrestling school or gymnasium, used here metaphorically for a place of rigorous intellectual exercise.</note> for all the <term>Muses</term> <note>original: Musarum; the nine Greek goddesses who presided over the arts and sciences.</note>. Thus, we now see the heights of the liberal disciplines adorned not only with the scholar's Laurel, but also with the Imperial Diadem; and the literary world recognizes that the conflict between **MARS** <note>The Roman god of war, representing the Emperor's military duties.</note> and **PALLAS** <note>Pallas Athena, the Greek goddess of wisdom and strategic warfare, representing his intellectual pursuits.</note> is finally settled.

And it is clearly evident from this that after the publication of several of my works under the happy auspices of Your <term>August Majesty</term> <note>original: Augustae Majestatis Tuae; a formal title for the Emperor derived from the first Roman Emperor, Augustus.</note>, when concerning <term>Phonurgia</term> <note>original: Phonurgia; a term coined by Kircher from the Greek words for "sound" and "work." It refers to his study of acoustics, echoes, and the science of sound.</note>

<meta>catchword: mea, (my)</meta>

<summary>Kircher praises Emperor Leopold I's exceptional intellect, noting how he harmonizes his military and political duties with a deep devotion to the arts and sciences. The author highlights that his own scientific works, specifically those on acoustics (Phonurgia), have flourished under the Emperor's patronage.</summary>

# DEDICATORY EPISTLE

my <term>little work</term> <note>original: Opufculi; a humble diminutive used by authors to describe their own books.</note>, and had heard of its secrets, you urgently requested that it be made public property as soon as possible. This work, now complete in all its parts, I reverently lay before the August feet of Your Majesty with all the affection of obedience and respect that is due, to You as a most munificent <term>patron</term> <note>original: Mecœnati; a reference to Gaius Maecenas, the famous Roman patron of the arts who supported Virgil and Horace.</note>. If this should find favor with You, most Sacred **EMPEROR**; behold, overflowing with joy, I shall sing these lines from the Poet more freely:

> Now we are worthy of the ears of the masters and the royal halls, 
> And the <term>lyre</term> <note>original: Chelys; a poetic term for a stringed instrument, literally "tortoise shell."</note> sounds, played with Augustus as judge. 
> <note>These lines are adapted from the Roman poet Martial (Epigrams, Book 4), comparing the Emperor to Augustus Caesar.</note>

And with these words, laying down my pen, I ardently pray to God, **the Best and Greatest** <note>original: Opt[imo] Max[imo]; a title for the Divine borrowed from the Roman "Jupiter Optimus Maximus."</note>, that with your enemies subdued, you may triumph in the happiness of the coming age. Farewell, Pillar of the World, Hope and Salvation of the Christian Commonwealth.

->From the Roman College, February 12th, 1673.<-

# INTERPRETATION
## OF THE PRECEDING FRONTISPIECE

*IN WHICH THE ARGUMENT OF THE EN-*
*tire work is explained as if by a certain emblem.*

Behold for you, what the wonderful Power of sound and voice
Accomplishes, and produces Marvelous things for the World!
The golden stars are filled with clear-sounding Voices,
They boast of always having Angelic Choirs.
The Saints do not cease to thicken their <term>songs of command</term> <note>original: Celeusmata; a Greek-derived term for the rhythmic calls given by a ship's pilot to rowers, here used for heavenly praise.</note> for the Most High,
The blessed crowd sings a threefold Applause.
The restless Winds breathe out contests among themselves,
They perform a Song with the hair-raising murmur of the Air.
From one side <term>Zephyrus</term> <note>The West Wind.</note> whistles, <term>Boreas</term> <note>The North Wind.</note> roars, as do <term>Eurus</term> <note>The East Wind.</note> and <term>Auster</term> <note>The South Wind.</note>.
Struggling, they make sounds travel to and fro.
Birds recite melodies with hymn-sounding beaks,
Which the light breeze rotates as they are reflected by the sphere.
By stretching heart-swaying strings, the famous <term>Phonascus</term> <note>original: Phonascus; a Greek term for a voice trainer or master of music.</note> of <term>Pindus</term> <note>A mountain range in Greece, sacred to Apollo and the Muses.</note>, Apollo,
Circles <term>Helicon</term> <note>Another mountain sacred to the Muses; home of the springs of poetic inspiration.</note> throughout the World, and teaches his Muses
To sound forth Wonders,
While he makes his resonant Work eternal with a charming voice.
The <term>Dryads</term> <note>Tree nymphs or nature spirits.</note> compete in Song, and two-horned <term>Satyrs</term>
Redouble their pipes through the joyful fields.
They rouse the wild beasts with the sound which the twisted horn
Brings forth, so that he may soothe the whole grove with his mouth,

Nourishing <term>Diana</term> <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and the moon, often representing the natural world or the "world soul" in Kircher's philosophy.</note> teaches. Drums, <term>Sistra</term> <note>original: Sistra; ancient metal rattles used in religious ceremonies, particularly in Egypt.</note>, and Trumpets
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Stir the spirit for battle whenever they resound.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Sounds his shell-trumpet and pours a pleasing melody to the heights.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which a wandering voice creates, bent and reflected here and there.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In which a Magical sound fascinates the mind with its art.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Carrying the <term>Emperor’s</term> <note>Refers to the Holy Roman Emperor Leopold I, Kircher's patron.</note> renowned Arms through the heavens.
These are the monuments of Kircher’s labors, which he brings forth,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which the present work demonstrates with marvelous teaching.

<meta>This page continues the celebratory prefatory verses for Athanasius Kircher’s "Phonurgia Nova" (1673). While the previous page focused on the mythological and imperial praise by Kestler, this page contains the beginning of a "Syncharmicum" (congratulatory poem) that emphasizes Kircher’s exploration of the physical world—from the deep underground to the stormy heavens—to uncover the secrets of sound.</meta>

### ON
# THE PHONURGIA OF KIRCHER
## A CONGRATULATORY POEM. <note>original: "Syncharmicum"; a term derived from Greek used in Neo-Latin to describe a poem of shared joy or celebration.</note>

Until now Fame has built only small Triumphs,
And she thinks she has barely touched the praises of Kircher.
Greater applause was meant to be scattered throughout the wide World,
And a louder sound echoing from a full voice.
Behold, a new trumpet strikes the high-reaching air,
And one which can surpass even Fame in its voice.
Seek instead new Worlds from here with no boundary,
And new lands beneath a new star.
This World is narrow; that other world is surely more resonant,
When the stirred-up voice strikes both sides.
So that there might be one voice of nature, and one more worthy <term>Echo</term> <note>Echo is both a physical phenomenon and a mythological figure; Kircher’s "Phonurgia" is the first modern treatise dedicated entirely to acoustics and the science of echoes.</note>,
Let it long to speak of Kircher with a single mouth.
Certainly, undaunted, he explores the subterranean
Kingdoms, having dared to enter the dark dwellings.
He follows the paths of the winds and the black recesses,
Where the earth often groans when its pact is broken.
And while he is lifted up again to gaze at the heavenly air,
At the thunder and the lightning hurled by a swift hand,
"What wonder is it," he says, "to resound with such a crash,
Which ruin seemed to bring back the ancient <term>Chaos</term> <note>In ancient philosophy, Chaos is the formless, disordered state of the universe before divine order was imposed; Kircher views the loud "crash" of nature as a momentary return to this disorder.</note>."
"A blind force, fit to be restrained by no laws,
Neither reason, but rather madness shakes these weapons."

<sig>b 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: Magna (Great)</meta>

<summary>This page contains a congratulatory poem praising Athanasius Kircher's scientific bravery in exploring the sounds of the natural world, from subterranean caves to the thundering heavens. It frames his work on acoustics as something that surpasses even the reach of Fame itself.</summary>

<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, Phonurgia Nova, acoustics, Echo, subterranean exploration, atmospheric phenomena, Neo-Latin poetry, scientific praise</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory Neo-Latin poem dedicated to Athanasius Kircher’s "Phonurgia Nova." The previous page ended with Kircher observing the "ancient Chaos" of nature's sounds—thunder and storms—and this page transitions into how Kircher, through his art, gives a "voice" and "meaning" to these raw physical forces. The catchword "Dum" (While) from the previous page's footer matches the start of the thirteenth line here, ensuring continuity.</meta>

Truly, there were great signs of wrath and signs sounding of deadly war,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But they were silent, and devoid of any meaningful marks. <note>The poet suggests that natural sounds like thunder are "mute" because they lack articulate meaning until Kircher’s science decodes or replicates them.</note>
This is my task: to weave another form,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Through which you might thunder and seem to speak at the same time.
May you give back fire-breathing voices and thundering words;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;May you bring forth winds from the mouth, and rivers through the voice.
I do not seek your bellows, <term>Vulcan</term> <note>The Roman god of fire and the forge.</note>, nor the arms of <term>Steropes</term> <note>One of the Cyclopes who forged lightning bolts for Jupiter; representing brute, noisy labor.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor do I seek your whirlwind noises, <term>Eurus</term> <note>The Greek name for the East Wind.</note>.
This is the work of the Muses, for the Muses can do all things;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Indeed, they teach how such wonders are to be bound by rhythmic measures.
From here, it seemed right to visit the seats of the Muses and the caves of <term>Apollo</term> <note>The god of music, poetry, and light.</note>,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And to stir up unusual melodies with the voice.
While he <note>Kircher</note> drinks in all the melody here with his mouth and unlocks the fountain,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He is now inspired by the closer divine power of the God.
Finally, <term>Musurgia</term> <note>original: "Musurgia"; referencing Kircher’s 1650 work *Musurgia Universalis*, it refers to the "art of making music" or the universal science of harmony.</note> inspires his divine breast,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And a spirit within nourishes a sound that is not mortal.
Here, one might be delighted by the modulations of sweet song,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Or by sounds made with compressed air, or by the hand. <note>A reference to various instruments, including organs (compressed air) and stringed instruments (the hand).</note>
He reveals here what <term>Orpheus</term> <note>The mythical musician who could charm trees and animals.</note> can do in the woods, and what <term>Arion</term> <note>A Greek poet saved by a dolphin charmed by his singing.</note> 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Can do among the waters, and what you, <term>Philomela</term> <note>A mythological figure transformed into a nightingale; here representing the beauty of birdsong.</note>, are capable of.
Nor is it enough to flex human voices in the throat in this way,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But he teaches how to animate words with a massive sound.
And here he recounts the trumpet <note>Referring to the "tuba stentorophonica" or speaking trumpet, one of Kircher's most famous acoustic inventions.</note> by which he can carry words through the breezes,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And let speech travel along distant paths.
No longer does a slow delay terrify absent friends,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor is a letter sent, written with a doubtful hand. <note>The poet suggests that Kircher’s inventions for long-distance sound transmission might replace the need for slow, uncertain written correspondence.</note>

<summary>This page celebrates Kircher’s ability to turn the raw, chaotic noises of nature into articulate music and speech through his "Musurgia." It highlights his inventions, particularly the speaking trumpet, which allows the human voice to travel over vast distances.</summary>

->In a final song—a day that shall never die.<-

->Behold, a pipe worthy of such ears awaits.<-

<meta>This page continues the celebratory poems dedicated to Athanasius Kircher’s work on acoustics, likely his "Phonurgia Nova" (1673). If Triton did not blow his cerulean conch-shell through the waters,

->TO<-
## FATHER ATHANASIUS KIRCHER.

By what means the Trumpet carries voices enclosed from here through the void,
->Even far away from us, you yourself explain the method.<-
Indeed, you pass on wonders, for what Nature has denied
->Until now, you—diligent as you are—teach how it may be done through art.<- <note>original: "artem". In the 17th century, 'art' often referred to technical skill, technology, or scientific ingenuity that improves upon the natural world.</note>
You amplify Nature; the barriers she greedily imposes
->On things, you make more spacious.<-
Should she be envious or should she love you? For you adorn her kingdom;
->From every side, you are said to be greater than her in Genius.<-
Let her be envious or love; for she will not only learn to love,
->Or to hate, this Noble Work of your mind.<-
Having been taught by you before you could instruct us in speech,
->Fame has learned better how to give life to trumpets.<-
For the trumpet which you depict sounds nearby; yet it spreads
->Your name, granted to these pages, everywhere.<-
Unless you perchance teach these same things, you yourself will teach less
->Than that which the trumpet, having been made learned by your methods, shall do.<-

->Father Joseph Pinus.<-

<meta>This page continues the series of laudatory poems and dedications to Athanasius Kircher. While the previous page focused on the acoustic theories of his "Phonurgia Nova," this page serves as a formal, high-style dedication page, celebrating Kircher’s status as a universal scholar and "citizen of the world" born in Germany but adopted by Rome.</meta>

# TO THE COMPENDIUM OF VIRTUES, THE UNIQUE GEM
### of the Muses, who carries away the World in admiration of himself, scarcely imitable,
### Oracle of the Antipodes; a polyglot <note>original: "πολυ-γλώσσω" (poly-glōssō) — a man of many languages.</note> Hero, the Flower of Fulda, Citizen of Italy,
### Roman Pallas, <note>Pallas is a title for Athena, the goddess of wisdom; the poet suggests Kircher is the embodiment of wisdom in Rome.</note> Very Reverend and Eminent
### Father in CHRIST, Father ATHANASIUS
# KIRCHER of the Society of JESUS.

---

Just honor pursues you, Kircher; to have fled
Does not profit you; it is swift—you flee, it flies.
When Virtue refuses rewards, she brings rewards to you.
The more you refuse these, the more she will give.
Fulda <note>Kircher's birthplace in Germany.</note> claims you as her own, but Latium <note>The region of Italy containing Rome.</note> rejoices in the honor;
If she, by nursing you, gave you your very Being—
Rome claims you as her own. But if Fulda wishes
To contend with the Romans, by a judge’s decree
The suit must be settled; let the decision of the case be thus:
Let the mind be Rome's, let the worth of genius be Fulda's.
Let Rome celebrate your Name in eternal circles,
And let Fulda engrave your <term>Symbols</term> <note>Likely referring to Kircher's work on hieroglyphics and symbolic communication, or his printer's emblems.</note> in her printing-presses.
Let the goal of your renown not only be that you know the Lands,
The Stars, the Sea, or whatever vast <term>Mathesis</term> <note>The Greek-derived term for the mathematical sciences, which in the 17th century included astronomy, geography, and music.</note> holds,
Or that you use the languages of the <term>Antipodes</term>, <note>A term for people or places on the exact opposite side of the globe.</note> and have summoned
The buried symbols of <term>Memphis</term> <note>An ancient capital of Egypt; Kircher was famous for his pioneering (though often speculative) attempts to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> for the Romans with a new art:
We desire something greater—let there be a Trumpet through which Fame speaks,
And let the heavens be seen to tremble at the sound:
Kircher, this is your Art; a pleasure granted to Eagles
That they may gaze upon the sun with a fixed eye. <note>In historical bestiaries and folklore, the eagle was the only creature capable of looking directly into the sun; the poet compares Kircher’s intellect to the eagle's sight.</note>

<summary>A formal dedication and celebratory poem honoring Athanasius Kircher as a polyglot and universal scholar whose fame spans from his German birthplace to his adopted home in Rome. The poem highlights his mastery of mathematics, Egyptian hieroglyphs, and the "art" of acoustics represented by the trumpet of fame.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, Fulda, Rome, Egyptology, Mathesis, polyglot, Jesuits, acoustics, scholarship</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the celebratory front matter of Kircher’s work. Having established Kircher’s reputation in the previous section, the text now transitions to a specific laudatory poem written by Giovanni Battista Podestà, a secretary of Oriental languages. The poem creates a symbolic link between Emperor Leopold I and Kircher, using clever wordplay on their names to frame them as pillars of the world and of wisdom.</meta>

*You have an eagle-like brow, and you vibrate with rays of light through hidden things,*
$\quad$ *Is it any wonder? Are you not a rare bird of the world?*
*Just as the wandering bowstring must be pressed so the arrow may fly higher,*
$\quad$ *You press upon pure hearts so that you may ascend by your genius.*
*Nothing pushes upward more quickly than a pressed heart,*
$\quad$ *And because you are so strong in your great dexterity,*
*You carry this instrument from the heavenly regions*
$\quad$ *In place of speech among the gods of both Earth and Sky.*
*Truly, you are the Delight of the World, thrice-greatest Caesar,* <note>original: "Cæsar" — referring here to the Holy Roman Emperor, Leopold I.</note>
$\quad$ *May Kircher’s gift be suitable for you,*
*Now, even now, you will be able to report your deeds to the Ferdinands,* <note>Refers to Leopold's predecessors, Emperors Ferdinand II and III.</note>
$\quad$ *And tell them with how strong a heart you accomplish so many things.*
*Until now, the fame of heroes echoed throughout the world,*
$\quad$ *With YOU AS CAESAR, it is fitting that Fame should know how to fly.*
*For the name LEOPOLD comes to you from heaven, and behold,*
$\quad$ *If the "D" is removed, Great LEO, you are the POLE,* <note>The poet plays with the Latin name LEOPOLDUS: "Leo" (Lion) and "Polus" (the Pole or Axis of the heavens). By adding the letter "D" (standing for *Deus* or divinity), he connects the Emperor to both earthly strength and celestial stability.</note>
*Join the "D" after "LEO," and the meaning is "The Lion of the Pole."*
$\quad$ *You are the Pole of Heroes; Fame shall be the North Star.*
*Thus Kircher's name reveals: KIRCH ER, that is, HE IS A CHURCH or TEMPLE* <note>The poet uses the German word *Kirche* (Church) and *Er* (He) to create a Latinized pun: "Kirch-er" means "He is a Temple."</note>
$\quad$ *(So he is called.)*
*You, Kircher, shall be the Noble Temple of Fame.*

---

Thus your most devoted client sang to you, when by the most merciful command of the Greatest
CAESAR, and under your direction in ROME, a specimen of Oriental languages was given by GIOVANNI BATTISTA Podestà,
Secretary of Oriental languages to his Sacred Imperial and Royal MAJESTY. *From my study in Rome, at the Maronite College of Mount Lebanon, on the Feast of St.* THOMAS *Aquinas, March 7, 1673.*

<summary>This page contains a sophisticated laudatory poem by the linguist Giovanni Battista Podestà, celebrating Emperor Leopold I and Athanasius Kircher through complex Latin and German wordplay on their names. It positions the Emperor as the "Pole" of the world and Kircher as a "Temple" of fame and wisdom.</summary>

# CONCERNING THE PRACTICAL USE AND CONSTRUCTION OF THESE 
## Tubes and Trumpets, it is said thus on folio 303:
### Practical Application VIII.

To arrange a tube within a building—whether it be conical or spiral-shaped <note>original: "cochleatum" — shaped like a snail shell or a screw, a design Kircher favored for amplifying sound</note>—in such a way that it transmits any sounds, whether articulated <note>meaning human speech</note> or inarticulate <note>meaning musical notes or noises</note>, into a particular room, however far removed from the public it may be (that is, across very great distances). It delivers these sounds so surely and distinctly that it is as if they were occurring right at the ears of those present, with no one suspecting where the sound might be coming from. This was proven by experience with both types of tubes at the Roman College <note>The Jesuit headquarters of learning in Rome</note> in the year 1649.

*IN THE ANNUAL REPORTS OF THE **SOCIETY OF JESUS** FOR THE YEARS 1594 & 1595,*
*written by Father **SEBASTIANO BERETARIO** in Rome in 1602 and published in Naples in 1604, page 751. Dealing with the so-called Mountain Peoples in America and the Province of Peru, the Author writes thus:*

There is another custom of these nations by which they seem to surpass all other peoples. For by blowing into tubes or flutes according to a specific method, they communicate their intentions so effectively that they are heard from a great distance; they are understood just as if they were speaking with distinct voices. Nevertheless, these signals are not understood even by those who know their spoken language, unless they have lived among them for a long time. And when our brethren <note>original: "Nostri" — referring to the Jesuit missionaries</note> among a certain village of that nation were suffering from a lack of provisions, very early one morning a great number of women appeared with bowls full of flour and other foods to relieve their poverty <note>The text implies the women were summoned or coordinated via these acoustic instruments</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the practical engineering of acoustic "speaking tubes" used to transmit voices through buildings and cites missionary reports from Peru as evidence of advanced long-distance communication using wind instruments.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, speaking tubes, Roman College, Jesuits, Peru, Sebastiano Beretario, communication technology, 1649</keywords>

... [certain people]. **Miratus Samaniacus** (a Priest of the Society of Jesus), marvelling at the strangeness of the matter, learned that they had been ordered to do this by the command of the Cacique <note>original: "Caciquii" — a title for a local chieftain or indigenous leader in the Americas</note>; and that the trumpet <note>original: "buccina"</note> which had blared just before nightfall on the previous day had signaled this very thing. *So much for that source.*

Many other examples could be brought forward from other authors, but these few shall suffice for testimony.

## SECTION I.
### *CONCERNING THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.*

**CHAPTER I. In which the Nature of reflected sound is described.**

*PRELUDE* 1. *Sound is the ape of Light.* <note>An analogy suggesting that sound behaves exactly like light, following the same laws of reflection and geometry.</note> page 6
2. *Concerning the sound-reflecting object.* <note>original: "phonocamptico" — from the Greek for "bending back sound."</note> 10
3. *Concerning the sound-reflecting Medium or the Line of Action.* 12
§. 1. *Concerning the Line of sonic Action.* Ibid.
§. 2. *Concerning the speed of sound as it passes through a Medium, or the quantity of sonic space.* 14

II. **Demonstrations of reflected sound.**

Proposition 1. *Every Angle of Sound-Incidence is equal to the Angle of Sound-Reflection; or, what is the same thing, the Angle of Incidence is equal to its angle of reflection.* <note>original: "Angulus Phonoptotus... angulo Phonocamptico" — Kircher uses these technical terms to apply the laws of optics to acoustics.</note> 18
2. *Whenever a voice falls upon any Right-Sounding object—that is, a perpendicular one—it will be reverberated back upon itself.* <term>Orthophone</term> <note>From the Greek "orthos" (straight/right) and "phone" (sound).</note> 19
3. *Whenever a voice falls upon any Slant-Sounding object—that is, obliquely—it will be reflected not back upon itself, but toward the opposite side.* <term>Loxophone</term> <note>From the Greek "loxos" (oblique/slanting).</note> 20

<margin>CHAPTER III.</margin>
## Rules of Sound-Reflection or Echo-Measurement.
<note>original: "Canones Phonocamptici sive Echometrici"</note>

PROPOSITION.
<margin>4.</margin> *Whenever two walls are joined at right angles, a very clear Echo is perceived by the Source so long as it is distant enough that the axis of the sonic Cone bisects the angle exactly.* 23

<margin>5.</margin> *Whenever a sound-reflecting object is a concave circle, one positioned in the sonic center—which is also the center of the circle—will perceive a most harmonious Echo.* 24

<margin>6.</margin> *The closer the vocal or sounding source is to the sound-reflecting object, the further away the reflected voice will be perceived; and the further the sounding source is from the sound-reflecting object, the closer the reflected voice will be perceived.* 25 <note>This describes the inverse relationship between the focal point of the sound source and its reflection in concave surfaces.</note>

<margin>7.</margin> *If several sound-reflecting objects are arranged in order so that each is progressively further away than the last, and the direct sound* <term>Orthophone</term> <note>original: "orthophonòs" — refers to the straight-line path of the voice before it hits a surface.</note> *reaches them more slowly, a many-voiced Echo will be born; that is, the sound will be returned from the walls multiple times in succession.* 26

->§. 1. Concerning Sonic Rays.<-
<note>original: "Phonismis" — a term coined to describe a 'beam' or 'ray' of sound, functioning like a ray of light.</note>

<margin>8.</margin> *A Cylindrical Sonic Ray is reflected back to the opposite side by a cylindrical projection.* 29
<margin>9.</margin> *A Direct Sonic Ray reflects back upon itself.* Ibid.
<margin>10.</margin> *In a conical sonic ray, only the axis is reflected by the equality of the Angles; the reflected ray, however, will be a truncated Cone. Just as in the most noble science of optical rays* <note>original: "Acti-nobilissimo optico"</note> *the axis of the Luminous or Visual pyramid has the power both to illuminate and to represent things distinctly: the matter is exactly the same in a Conical sonic ray.* 30
<margin>11.</margin> *A conical sonic ray hitting a wall at a right angle produces an inverted truncated sonic cone through its reflection.* 31

->§. 2. Concerning Multiple Reflections. 32<-
<note>original: "Poliphonismis"</note>

<margin>12.</margin> *When the Sonic Center is placed within the Axis of a concave, equilateral triangular prism* <note>original: "prismatis Trigoni Isopleuri"</note>*, the emitted voice, increased by a triple sonic ray, returns to its center.* Ibid.
<margin>13.</margin> *When the sonic Center is placed within the Axis of a concave quadrilateral prism, the emitted voice, increased by a fourfold sonic ray, will return to that center.*

Corporum Regularium" — the five Platonic solids: tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron.</note>, the reflected voice will return to itself multiplied by as many reflections as the shape has sides. Ibid.*

<margin>19.</margin> *A concave sphere is the most suitable shape of all for propagating sound.* 40

->INDEX OF SUBJECTS.<-

1. Artifice <term>Technasma</term> <note>original: "Technasma" — derived from the Greek for a clever device or artistic invention. This refers to the creation of an "acoustic illusion."</note>. *To present most pleasant music, both with voices and equipped with every kind of instrument, at a distance of two or three miles, so that no one can conceive where it comes from.* 142
2. *To fashion a conical tube in such a way that it presents a new, foreign, and to everyone unknown harmony into remote spaces.* 143
3. *To prepare another automated harmonic machine which, by the service <note>original: "ministerio"</note> of no wheels, bellows, or phonotactic cylinder <term>phonotactic cylinder</term> <note>original: "Cylindri Phonotactici" — a pinned barrel or drum, similar to those in music boxes, used to trigger specific notes.</note>, but by the wind or air alone, excites a certain perpetual harmonious sound as long as the wind lasts.* 145
Corollary 1. *To construct the aforementioned machine to be perpetually resonating.* 147
Corollary 2. *So that wondrous music may be perceived high in the air.* Ibid.

# BY ATHANASIUS KIRCHER OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS.

# PHONURGIA [THE SCIENCE OF SOUND]

## BOOK I.

### ANACAMPTIC SOUND-WISDOM <note>original: "Phonosophia Anacamptica" — From the Greek *anakamptikos*, meaning "bending back" or "reflective." This is Kircher's term for the science of reflected sound.</note> which is also called the Echonic Art.

### PREFACE.

<margin>What is an Echo?</margin>
Echo is a joke of playful Nature; she is called the "image of the voice" by the Poets, following that line of Virgil:
> *The rocks resound, and the struck image of the voice leaps back.* <note>Virgil, *Georgics*, 4.50. Kircher uses this classical reference to show how sound was personified in antiquity.</note>
->image.<-
By the Philosophers, she is called the reflected, repercussed, or reciprocal voice. By the Hebrews she is called <term>Bat Kol</term> <note>original: "Bat col" — Hebrew for "Daughter of a Voice." In Jewish tradition, this often refers to a heavenly or divine voice that echoes from the heavens.</note>, the daughter of the voice. Her nature is so deeply hidden that, even to this day, there is hardly anyone who has successfully explained her latent essence. It is indeed well known, and almost a common fact, that an echo is a reflected voice. However, exactly how it is formed—from what materials, by what propagation, at what speed, and at what distance—is so unknown that it matches the mystery of any other phenomenon. Nor does it seem that the vastness of the difficulties encountered can be exhausted by any reasoning, except by one who, equipped with the greatest experience and singular industry, has finally caught this fugitive Nymph <note>In Greek mythology, Echo was a mountain nymph who lost her speech, only able to repeat the words of others.</note>, eluding her marvelous arts. Since no one until now has achieved this, I—driven by the desire to investigate her—have searched the hidden recesses of the woods, the secret <margin>Description of the nature of the Echo.</margin> hiding places of groves and mountains, and the rough plains of valleys, ruins, fields, and marshes. I have left nothing untried, so that to the hidden...

<summary>This page introduces Book I of Kircher's treatise on acoustics, defining the echo through poetic, philosophical, and theological lenses (notably the Hebrew "Bat Kol"). Kircher positions himself as a pioneer who will use experimental "industry" to explain the mechanics of sound reflection where others have failed.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, echo, Athanasius Kircher, Bat Kol, Virgil, Phonosophia, reflection of sound, Jesuit science</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface of Book I. It completes the sentence from page 1 regarding the difficulty of explaining the nature of the echo, transitioning from a poetic/philosophical struggle to a mathematical solution. Kircher then outlines the contents of the treatise and acknowledges his predecessor, Giuseppe Biancani.</meta>

HEADER: OF PHONURGIA <note>The title "Phonurgia" is Kircher’s coinage, meaning "The Work of Sound" or "Sound-making."</note>

...reach its nature; but while I pursue her, she flees; while I flee, she pursues; while I speak softly, she softly mocks; while I cry out loudly, as if adopting familiar sounds as her own, she doubles the voices, refusing to yield; soon, as if indignant, she turns away and avoids a reply; sometimes at a single word, with a completely unusual talkativeness, she pours back ten others. Thus, having been mocked time and again by her variety and inconstancy—trying now with songs, now with musical instruments of every kind—I attempted to stop this most fleeting Little Goddess <note>original: "Deastram" — a playful diminutive for a minor goddess, personifying the Echo.</note> and to soothe her in every way with words composed now to severity, now to flattery; but she, accustomed to the woods and solitudes and thus unknowing of how to be tamed, easily eluded all my efforts, until at last I joined to myself as a companion

<margin>The nature of the Echo is to be investigated by the aid of Geometry.</margin>
*Reflective* Geometry <note>original: "Anacampticam" — from the Greek for "bending back."</note>, I attacked her again more fiercely, and by its wisdom it finally happened that she stood still, and my desires were fully satisfied.

Therefore, what I have observed about her during my various travels in different places, I have decided to communicate to the curious Reader in this treatise; and, so that the Reader may more clearly recognize the immense riches of sounds, it seemed fit to exhibit in this treatise a certain specimen of truly admirable things. And First, we shall bring forward new principles and Rules of the *Phonocamptic* <note>The art of reflecting sound.</note> art. Second, we shall reveal *Phonocamptic Architecture*, or the *Echometric* art <note>The science of measuring echoes.</note>, from the innermost secrets of nature. Third, we shall produce *Echotectonics* <note>The construction of spaces for sound.</note>, or the construction of *Acoustic* Instruments, which we also call *Phonocamptic Catoptrics* <note>original: "Catoptricam Phonocampticam" — Catoptrics is the study of mirrors; Kircher uses this as an analogy for how sound reflects off surfaces like light off a mirror.</note>; in which we shall demonstrate in many ways, and perhaps by inventions not to be despised, that nothing is contained in that prodigious *Catoptric Magic* [Optical Magic] that *Phonocamptics* cannot also exhibit.

<margin>Biancani was the first to write about Echometry.</margin>
And although our countryman Blancanus <note>Giuseppe Biancani (1566–1624), a Jesuit mathematician and astronomer who wrote about the physics of echoes.</note> was the first, as far as I know, who wrote about *Echometry* (for which he clearly earned distinguished praise among posterity), we, following in his footsteps—yet not at all content with what was handed down by him—lifting our mind to higher things and opening a leap-spring of various inventions that is always new, have tried to raise this *Echosophia* [Echo-wisdom] to that level which a science—both hidden and known to few—seemed to demand as if by its own right.

<margin>What the Author has provided.</margin>
I do not doubt that Kings and Princes, enticed by these things, will hereafter restore with all zeal the magical constructions of the ancients for the exhibition of any sounds whatsoever. But leaving behind the ambiguities of words, let us proceed to the matter itself; and so that we may proceed more solidly in our *Echo-wisdom*, first

BOOK I. 3

...it seemed appropriate, in the manner accustomed to Geometers, to set forth Definitions, Axioms, and Postulates first, so that by precisely maintaining the standard of the art, we may proceed methodically <note>original: "εὐμεθόδως" — with good method or system.</note> in all things.

# DEFINITIONS.

1. **Magic**, or the *Phonocamptic Art* <note>From the Greek "phone" (sound) and "kampsis" (bending/reflection). Kircher defines this as the science of sound reflection.</note>, is the more hidden science of sounds, by which, through the power of reflected and multiplied voice, we produce prodigious and—for those ignorant of the causes—miraculous effects.
2. **Phonocampsis** is nothing other than the reflection of the voice, which the Greeks and thereafter others call *Echo*.
3. The **Phonic Center** is the name given to the point from which the sound lines originate, as in the following figure, point A.

<image-desc>A scientific diagram illustrating the principles of acoustics and sound reflection. On the right, a point marked 'A' represents the "Centrum Phonicum" (source of sound). Lines representing sound rays travel from 'A' to a rectangular vertical surface on the left labeled 'GKHI' (the "Objectum Phonocampticum"). The rays reflect off this surface at various points (such as L, C, B, O, E, P) and converge or pass through a series of points labeled B, O, V, C, and E, which are the "Centra Phonocamptica". Auxiliary points D, Q, X, R, S, F, and T are used to define the geometry of the reflection paths.</image-desc>

4. The **Phonocamptic Center** is the furthest point on the Phonocamptic line where the reflected voice presents itself to the ears, such as centers B, O, V, C, and E.
5. The **Phonocamptic Object** is the name for any obstacle that reflects the voice, such as the wall GKHI.
6. The **Phonocamptic Medium** is what we call the medium through which the sound is propagated <note>Usually referring to the air, though Kircher investigates other substances.</note>.

<sig>A 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: 7. Linea</meta>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher establishes the formal geometric foundation for his study of acoustics, defining key terms like "Phonic Center" and "Phonocampsis" to treat the behavior of sound like the reflection of light.</summary>
<keywords>Phonocampsis, Phonic Center, sound reflection, acoustics, natural magic, geometry of sound</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Definitions" section of Book I of Kircher’s *Phonurgia Nova*. Having established the centers of sound and reflection on the previous page, Kircher now defines the geometric lines and angles that govern how sound moves and bounces off surfaces.</meta>

->PHONURGIÆ<-

7. The **Phonic Line**, also called the *sonorous* or *vocal line*, is the path through which the voice travels and returns, such as line A–B in the figure. It is categorized in several ways, as follows:

8. An **Orthophonic Line** <note>From the Greek *orthos* (straight) and *phone* (sound).</note>, or a *straight sound line*, is that by which the voice flows back to the same place from which it originated; these are all lines reflected from a wall at a right angle to the sounding body. In the figure, this is represented by line A–B, and it corresponds to a "normal" or perpendicular ray of light. A **Loxophonic Line** <note>From the Greek *loxos* (slanting/oblique).</note> is a sound line that strikes the opposite wall at an angle and reflects at that same angle, such as lines O–S and C–D in the figure. This is fourfold: it is either reflected upward from the bottom (from C to D), called **Anophona** <note>Upward-sounding.</note>; or it is reflected downward from the top (from E to F, or from A to E), called **Catophona** <note>Downward-sounding.</note>; or it is reflected to the right, such as V–X; or to the left, such as O–S.

9. A **Phonic Triangle** is the name for any triangle formed by the *orthophonic* and *loxophonic* lines together with the distance of the center of reflection from the perpendicular point, such as A–B–C or A–B–E.

10. The **Line of Action** is the total length of the sound line within which the sound remains perceptible.

11. The **Phonoptotic Angle**, or the *angle of incidence*, is the angle that the sound line makes with the **Phonocamptic object** <note>The reflecting surface.</note>, such as angle A–B–C, or angles A–O–B, A–V–B, and A–E–B.

12. The **Phonocamptic Angle** <note>The angle of reflection.</note> is the angle that the reflected line forms with the reflecting object, such as angle L–C–D, S–O–R, P–E–F, or X–V–Q.

13. The **Angle of Sound Confusion** is the angle caused by two conical or cylindrical *phonisms* <note>Sound waves or emissions.</note> intersecting one another.

14. **Polyphonism** is the multiplication of the voice when it is reflected in various ways within a concave body; just as a **Phonism** is nothing other than the act of sound spreading itself out in a spherical, conical, or cylindrical shape.

| | | | | | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 15. } | | { Parabolic | | { Parabolic | |
| 16. } | A Phonocamptic Object | { Hyperbolic | is the surface of a concave body that is | { Hyperbolic | } |
| 17. } | | { Elliptic | | { Elliptic | |

18. **Acoustic Instruments** are tools which, when applied to the ears, have the power to multiply a loud voice.

19. A **Phonoclastic body** <note>From the Greek *klasis* (breaking/refraction).</note> is a medium within which sound is refracted; **Phonoclasis** is the actual refraction of the voice, as occurs in water.

<meta>Catchword: Axio- [Axioms]</meta>

<summary>This page defines the geometric terminology of acoustics, classifying sound waves as straight, slanting, or refracted, and establishing the angles of incidence and reflection.</summary>
<keywords>Phonic line, Orthophonic, Loxophonic, Phonic Triangle, Angle of Incidence, Angle of Reflection, Polyphonism, Acoustic Instruments, Phonoclasis, Refraction</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific definitions of phonic lines and angles into the fundamental laws of acoustics as Kircher understands them. It moves from "Definitions" into "Axioms and Hypotheses," which serve as the theoretical foundation for his experiments with megaphones and echoing chambers.</meta>

BOOK I.

## Axioms & Hypotheses.

1. Every **Phonoptotic** <note>original: "Phonoptotus"; the angle of incidence or the incoming sound.</note> Angle is equal to the **Phonocamptic** <note>original: "Phonocamptico"; the angle of reflection or the outgoing sound.</note> angle; or, what is the same, every Angle of incidence is equal to the Angle of reflection.
2. Every **Phonic** <note>sound-producing</note> center radiates outward in a circle along straight lines.
3. In the movement of sound, Nature only achieves its goal through the shortest possible lines.
4. Only that which the sonorous line is able to reach can be heard.
5. Sound is greatly intensified when multiple reflections radiate toward a single point.
6. Every reflection of sound occurs either back toward its source or toward the opposite side.
7. The further away the **Anacamptic** <note>original: "Anacampticum"; a reflecting object or surface that produces an echo.</note> object is, the more slowly the voice reaches the ears; the closer it is, the faster it arrives.
8. Sound is measured by the active range of the sonorous line.
9. The **Phonic** or **Conic Tube** <note>A speaking trumpet or megaphone.</note> is an Instrument or Tool by which someone can conduct a conversation over great distances. These take many forms, as will be seen later.
10. Acoustic instruments are tools by which someone may communicate with another either secretly or openly.

---

## Postulates & Given Data.

1. Given any specific point on a **Phonocamptic** <note>reflecting</note> object where reflection occurs, to determine the path of the reflected line.
2. From any point on an object, a reflection can be established toward any other point.
3. Given a Tube of a length proportioned to a specific distance, one can use it to converse with others at that distance.
4. Given the intensity of a voice, to know to what distance it can extend itself through a tube.

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut ornament featuring symmetrical scrolling foliage and stylized floral patterns.</image-desc>

<summary>Kircher establishes ten fundamental axioms of acoustics, most notably that sound reflects at the same angle it hits a surface, similar to the behavior of light. He also introduces the concept of the 'Phonic Tube' (megaphone) as a primary tool for acoustic science.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical postulates of the previous section into the physical nature of sound itself. Kircher moves from geometry to natural philosophy, establishing his fundamental thesis: that sound and light behave according to the same physical laws.</meta>

BOOK I OF PHONURGIA. SECTION I.

# SECTION I.
## CHAPTER I.
*In which the nature and properties of reflected sound are described.*

### PRELUDE I.
->SOUND IS THE APE OF LIGHT.<-
<note>original: "Sonus lucis simia est." This is one of Kircher’s most famous maxims. He argues that acoustics (Phonurgia) is the "ape" or mirror-image of optics, meaning sound follows the same rules of reflection as light.</note>

<margin>What is sound?</margin> **SOUND** is nothing other than a sensible quality that can be perceived by the sense of hearing. It is not, as some believe, the actual motion of bodies striking one another; rather, sound follows the movement of colliding bodies. It does not do so immediately, but through the "breaking" of the intervening air. Consequently, bodies that contain more air (and lighter air) are granted the greatest power for making sound, because air is "broken" more easily in light and airy bodies than in dense and thick ones. <margin>The collision of two solid bodies is not always necessary for sound.</margin> For this reason, the collision of two solid bodies is not always required to produce sound; the impulse of air and water is perfectly suited for producing it, as flutes, the roaring of the sea, and thunder clearly demonstrate. Therefore, when air is "broken" by the collision of any bodies whatsoever, sound is created. From the point of collision, it spreads out in a radiating sphere, not unlike the "species" <note>In 17th-century science, "species" refers to the outward-radiating form or sensory image of an object that travels through a medium to the eye or ear.</note> of colors. For just as colors emit their "species" in every direction through radiation as representatives of the object, so too does sound emit its own species.

Furthermore, the medium of sound, by which its species are carried to the hearing, is not the "subject" <note>The physical source</note> of the sounds, but rather air and water. Regarding air, there is no doubt; but regarding water, experience also makes us certain. <margin>Water is a medium for sound.</margin> **PLINY** <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman naturalist.</note> reports that fish are known to gather at a certain sound. <margin>Fish hear.</margin> The same **PLINY** relates that fish have been known to appear when called by name; indeed, they are terrified by thunder, which would not happen unless they could hear beneath the water. Divers also assert that they perceive louder sounds beneath the waves, and they do so more easily the closer they are to the surface of the water. This is a manifest sign that sound penetrates through the air into porous bodies—such as water, wood, and walls—to reach the power of hearing. Water, therefore, is a medium for sound, even though sound is carried much more fluidly and easily through the air than through water. For just as the ray of light relates to its medium...
<meta>catchword: medium</meta>

<summary>Kircher defines sound as a sensible quality carried through the "breaking" of air or water, rather than the movement of solid objects themselves. He establishes his core principle that sound behaves like light, radiating in spheres and reflecting off surfaces.</summary>
<keywords>definition of sound, acoustics vs optics, Pliny the Elder, aquatic hearing, medium of sound, species, physics of air</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter I of the first section, further developing Athanasius Kircher’s core argument that sound follows the laws of optics. Having established that sound is a "species" (a sensory image) traveling through air, he now compares its behavior in different environments—such as water, tapestried rooms, and crowded halls—to how light passes through various media.</meta>

<header>ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.</header>

<margin>...in air and in water.</margin>
Just as a denser medium, in which light is refracted, also weakens that light, so too is a "sound ray" refracted in a denser medium. Therefore, the "audible species" <note>In 17th-century science, "species" are the invisible forms or images of things that travel from an object to our senses.</note> are weakened and blunted by the thickness of water, just as visible species are by a denser medium. For this reason, we hear less in rainy weather and misty air than when the air is clear. <margin>Why sound is not so clear in a hall decorated with tapestries.</margin> In halls covered with wall-hangings, the voice is perceived with greater difficulty—being blunted and absorbed—than when the walls are stripped of tapestries. From this we also derive the reason why music "languishes" in a place full of people; because the sound is absorbed within clothing and the hollow spaces of human bodies, it loses its power. Similarly, a hall spread with wool or straw blunts the sound so much that it is barely perceived. More shall be said on these matters in the following pages.

<margin>Sound imitates light.</margin>
With these things established, I must now show that sound is the "Ape of Light"—that is, it emulates light in almost all respects. In doing so, it must first be noted that sound creates a sphere of its own power, within which alone it can be perceived, and outside of which it cannot. This sphere can only be formed by straight lines diffused in every direction from the sounding object through the medium, as if from a center. Therefore, the radiation of sound closely mimics the flow of light, and there is no difference in their *aktinobolia* <note>original: "ακτινοβολίας"—a Greek term meaning the emission of rays or radiation.</note> except that light is propagated by instantaneous motion, while sound travels by successive motion through the air; furthermore, light rays do not travel through circular tubes, whereas the radiant diffusion of sound does.

Certainly, Virgil himself seems to hint that light and sound are, in some way, the same thing when he says:

> Then the fire is heard more clearly
<note>original: "Tum clarior ignis auditur." From Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book 2, line 705. Kircher uses this poetic line to suggest that even the ancients saw a physical link between the "brightness" of light and the "clarity" of sound.</note>

<margin>Book 6.</margin>
And likewise:
> And dogs were seen to bark through the shadows.
<note>original: "Visáque Canes Latrare per Umbram." From Virgil's *Aeneid*, Book 6, line 257.</note>

<margin>A parallel comparison of light to sound.</margin>
Indeed, nothing can present itself to the eyes that cannot also present itself to the ears through a certain analogical relationship. Therefore, just as it is the property of light to represent the different colors of bodies according to the different directions of rays—whether falling directly or reflected—onto surfaces and then to the eyes; so it is the property of sounds to represent the different qualities of bodies by means of the moved air striking and hitting their surfaces. Thus, we might not incorrectly say that colors are nothing other than the different immersion and reflection of rays in a medium, just as sounds are nothing other than different motions of the air. For if anyone were to perceive the most subtle motions of the air while an instrument is sounding,

<summary>Kircher argues that sound behaves like light, being refracted by dense media like water or absorbed by soft materials like tapestries and clothing. He uses Virgil’s poetry and the concept of "species" to claim that acoustics and optics are governed by nearly identical physical laws of radiation.</summary>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues Kircher's argument that sound is physically analogous to light. On the previous page, he established that sound travels in straight lines and forms a "sphere of power." Here, he explores the invisibility of sound and light in their abstract states and dismisses the idea that sound can be "stored" in a container without constant motion.</meta>

# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION I.

...if one were to perceive sound, he would certainly see nothing else but a picture sketched with a remarkable variety of colors, through which the diverse qualities of sounding bodies would present themselves to the eyes, as has been said. Furthermore, just as light considered in the abstract is invisible, so too is sound; for in this world we can see nothing except colored surfaces representing, as much as they are able, the sun and other luminous bodies. Moreover, that light is invisible is shown more than enough by concave mirrors, which leave no trace of rays except at the point where the rays converge, and only then if an opaque reflecting body is introduced. 

<margin>No property is perceptible by itself.</margin>
Indeed, to speak more strictly, I say that no accident <note>In the Aristotelian philosophy used by Kircher, an "accident" is a property—like color, sound, or heat—that cannot exist on its own but must belong to a physical substance.</note> is perceptible by itself, except through the bodies by which it is sustained, or through "quantity," which gives them extension, without which they would vanish into a mathematical point and nothingness. Just as light is invisible without air, so too is sound; while sound makes the air perceptible to us, it also reveals many qualities of bodies that we would not know how to recognize without the help of sounds. And if anyone should look a little more deeply into the nature of light, they will find it to be nothing other than a certain movement of the air, which carries with it the image of its "prime mover"—that is, the luminous body—to present it to the eyes under the name and appearance of color or light. Likewise, sound is nothing other than a movement of that same air, which carries with it the different qualities of its cause, namely the bodies moving it; from this, it presents an image to us under the name and appearance of sound. For the sensible form, for example, of some ringing bell, presents itself to the eyes of a deaf person in much the same way as it reaches the ears of a blind person. Furthermore, just as light cannot be preserved without the body from which it flows through actual influence, so too sound cannot exist without the motion of the air.

<margin>Light reaches the eyes in the same way sound reaches the ears.</margin>
Therefore, those are to be laughed at who think that sound, when enclosed in a pipe, can be preserved for a long time, as will be discussed more at length in our *Echotectonic Magic* <note>original: *Magia nostra Echotectonica*. Kircher often cross-references his own works on the "magic" of echoes and acoustics.</note>; indeed, experience teaches us better, and we are affected with greater pleasure while we read some history or a vivid description of a thing, or hear it sketched by an eminent Orator with various figures of words and sentences—as if with certain colors—than if we were to see those same things with our own eyes.

Furthermore, just as the species <note>original: *species*—referring to the invisible sensory forms or images that early scientists believed were emitted by objects and traveled to the observer.</note> of perceived things affect the eye and the optic nerve in a certain hidden way to produce a similar image with the help of the spirits <note>Kircher refers to "animal spirits," which 17th-century science described as a subtle fluid that traveled through the nerves to transmit sensations to the brain.</note>, so too the image of a sounding body is carried by the air...

<meta>catchword: carried</meta>

<summary>Kircher argues that sound and light are invisible in themselves and only become perceptible through the medium of air and the qualities of the bodies that produce them. He refutes the idea that sound can be trapped or preserved in a tube, insisting that it requires continuous motion.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, optics, species, animal spirits, Echotectonic Magic, Aristotelian accidents, medium of air, sensory perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kircher’s comparison between the physics of light and sound. The previous page ended by discussing how light and sound are invisible "accidents" until they interact with a medium or a body; this page picks up by explaining how sound reaches the ear and how its behavior changes in different substances like water.</meta>

# ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.

...carried along, it affects the air implanted in the acoustic—or ear—nerve to represent the image of the sounding body. From these things, unless I am mistaken, it is clearly shown that there is a vast similarity between the "bodies" of optics and acoustics in how they act and produce both sight and hearing in humans.

<margin>Refraction and reflection of sound.</margin>
Finally, just as sight is refracted <note>Refraction is the bending of a wave (like light or sound) when it passes from one medium, like air, into another, like water.</note> in a denser medium, so too is sound; as will be shown later in detail. For what else is sound when it is received into a very porous and soft body, but a kind of "shadow" of sound—an obstacle that prevents it from stretching out any further?

### COROLLARY

<margin>How much deeper a sound is underwater than the same sound outside of water.</margin>
From this follows a method of determining how much deeper a sound produced inside water is compared to the sound of the same body produced outside water, and consequently how much rarer air is than water. For it was discovered through experience not long ago by a distinguished mathematician that the sound of a certain bell, which was at "two degrees" outside water, for example, was at "five degrees" inside water. There is no other cause for this than the rarity and density of the different media. For water resists the <term>species</term> <note>original: *speciei*; in 17th-century science, the "species" was the sensible form or 'image' of an object that traveled through the air to reach our senses.</note> of a sounding body more when it is inside the water than outside; from this resistance arises a slowness in the movement of the medium, and a deeper sound follows this slowness. Just as one medium relates to another, so the vibrations made in one medium relate to the vibrations made in the other; and as vibrations relate to vibrations, so sound relates to sound. If, therefore, as in the example provided, the ratio was 5 to 2, the motion of air and the motion of water will exist in that same proportion; thus the rarity of air compared to the density of water will be as 125 to 8 <note>Kircher calculates this by cubing the ratio: 5³ = 125 and 2³ = 8, a common method in period physics to relate linear ratios to volume or density.</note>.

<margin>Sound-production is similar to light-production.</margin>
You see, therefore, that <term>Phonisms</term> <note>original: *Phonismos*; Kircher’s technical term for the physical behavior and propagation of sound.</note> are entirely parallel in their operations to <term>Photisms</term> <note>original: *photismis*; Kircher’s term for the propagation of light.</note>, except for their motion: the diffusion of light is instantaneous, while sound is successive <note>That is, sound takes a measurable amount of time to travel, whereas Kircher followed the traditional view that light travels across distances instantly.</note>. Furthermore, sound is not only able to propagate along straight lines, but also along curves, as is evident when it passes through channels and tubes, or similar "sound-ducts." In these, sound is not only carried conveniently, but is also greatly increased and strengthened; indeed, it travels further than it does in the open air, as will be proven later through various experiments.
