# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION I.

For GB and BD joined together equal the straight line of action VR <note>Kircher is using geometric proofs here, treating paths of sound like lines in a diagram</note>; therefore, etc. Furthermore, if the source of the voice is placed at C, I say that the reflected voice will be perceived at E. For CB and BE taken together equal the line of action VR. Therefore, the closer the source of the voice is to the <term>sound-reflecting object</term> <note>original: *objecto phonocamptico*; from the Greek words for 'sound' and 'bent' or 'curved'</note>, the further from the object the reflected voice happens, and vice versa; which was to be demonstrated.

## PROPOSITION VII.

->***IF SEVERAL SOUND-REFLECTING OBJECTS***<-
->***are so arranged in order that one is always further away than the other, and***<-
->***the direct sound can reach them more slowly, a many-voiced echo will be born,***<-
->***that is, the projected voice will be returned several times in succession.***<-

Let the sound-reflecting objects be A, B, and C, arranged in order so that one is always further away than the other. Let the voice source be at D, and perpendicular to each object. If someone calls out from there, I say that the voice will be repeated three times. Since, by hypothesis, the objects E, F, and G are perpendicular to the source of the voice...

<image-desc>A woodcut diagram illustrating an acoustic principle of multiple reflections. Point 'D' represents a sound source directed toward three vertical rectangular reflecting surfaces at staggered distances, labeled E, F, and G. These surfaces are mounted on supporting structures labeled A, B, and C at the top. Straight lines indicate the sound's path to and from the origin point D.</image-desc>

...and the <term>direct sound</term> <note>original: *Ortophonos*; literally 'right-sounding' or the primary sound wave</note> lines DE, DF, and DG are perpendicular, they will necessarily reflect back on themselves to D from points E, F, and G. Since the line DE is shorter, the reflection from there to D will happen faster. But since DF is longer than DE, it consumes more time in returning to D, and consequently, the reflected voice F will be perceived after the reflected voice E. And since DG is even longer than DF, it will reach the ears at D even later. Therefore, from the different unequal times caused by the different lengths of the lines, a <term>triple-voiced echo</term> <note>original: *Echo triphona*</note> DE, DF, and DG will be born. Therefore, if sound-reflecting objects are so arranged in order, etc., which was to be demonstrated.

Here it should be noted that a twofold duration of the voice can be considered in this place: first, the sound of the voice itself, which lasts as long as it is uttered, and is vigorous and strong in its first production; second— <note>the text breaks off here; the catchword 'dò' indicates the next page begins with 'secundò' (secondly)</note>

<summary>This page explains the geometric principles of sound reflection, demonstrating how multiple surfaces at varying distances create a sequence of echoes, known as a polyphonic echo.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, polyphonic echo, sound-reflecting objects, orthophonos, geometry of sound, Athanasius Kircher, Phonurgia Nova</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physics of acoustics, moving from the geometric paths of sound to the specific behavior of echoes and how distance affects the clarity of spoken syllables.</meta>

# ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.

...concerning the propagation of the voice itself along a line of action. This propagation is of such a condition that while the primary voice lasts, the propagation also lasts; however, once it has moved further along and the primary voice has fallen silent, the propagated sound still persists until, struck and reflected by some obstacle, an Echo is generated—specifically along what we call a direct-reflected line. Hence, experience teaches us that we hear the reflected voice a significant time after the primary voice. Therefore, the entire duration of the voice, from its first production until its end, is measured by the space through which the voice travels and returns. If the distance is smaller, the reflected voice will return sooner; if greater, it will return later. Consequently, at a smaller distance, the echo can repeat fewer syllables; at a greater distance, it can repeat more. And although all the sounds of the initial syllables are reflected, they are not all heard, because the stronger primary voice so blunts <note>or masks</note> the weaker reflected sounds that they cannot be heard while the primary voice is still sounding. Once the primary voice ceases, the reflected sounds are finally heard, sooner or later, depending on the extent of the distance.

## COROLLARY.

From this it is clear why, in certain instances, only the final syllables of a word are heard and never the first; this is because the reflected voice returns while the primary voice is still being uttered. Once the speaker falls silent, the remaining syllables are finally perceived. Let there be a wall A B and a distance

<columns>2</columns>
smaller than C D. From point C, if someone calls out the word **PARATIS** <note>Latin for "you are prepared" or "you are ready"</note> with a loud voice, it is certain that the first syllable **PA** first strikes the wall, then the second syllable **RA**, and thirdly **TIS**. These strike in a forward order, but they are reflected in a completely retrograde <note>reversed</note> order, so that **PA** presents itself to the ears first, followed in order by the two subsequent syllables, **RA**,
<column-break/>
<image-desc>A horizontal diagram illustrating the reflection of sound syllables against a wall. On the left is a vertical line marked A at the top and B at the bottom, representing a reflecting wall. A horizontal line extends from the wall at point D to the right at point C. Markers f and e are placed at intervals between D and C. Above the line, moving toward the wall from right to left, are the syllables 'Pa', 'ra', and 'tis', representing the outgoing sound. Below the line, moving away from the wall from left to right, are the reflected syllables 'tis', 'ra', and 'Pa', showing the order of the returning echo.</image-desc>

and **TIS**. If, therefore, the distance is only suitable for uttering a single syllable <note>a "monosyllabic" distance</note>, then in pronouncing the word **PARATIS**, only the syllable **TIS** will be perceived. For the first syllable **PA**, upon reaching the point of reflection at D, will be reflected back to C; **RA** will reach point e, and **TIS** will reach point f. Thus, the syllables confused at that point will return nothing intelligible. Meanwhile, as the reflected sound of **PA** travels from e to reach C, **RA** comes from D to f and then from f to e; in the meantime, while the direct sound of the last syllable...

<meta>catchword: TIS, per-</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains the mechanics of an echo, specifically how the distance between a speaker and a wall determines how many syllables are reflected clearly. He demonstrates that a speaker's own voice can mask the beginning of an echo, resulting in only the final syllables being audible at shorter distances.</summary>

-TIS, it will arrive at D. Therefore, a confusion occurs there once again, so that the *Echo* cannot be heard until the two syllables PA and RA cease; finally, the last syllable TIS will be perceived alone in C without confusion. Having thus seen the nature of sound according to simple lines, it remains only for us to speak about composite ones as well.

->§ I.<-
### Concerning Phonisms.

A Phonism <term>Phonismus: Kircher's term for the physical "body" or volume of sound as it spreads through the air</term>, as is clear from the definition, is a cylindrical or conical diffusion of the voice; that is, a radiation of the voice in the shape of a cone or a cylinder. It must be noted, however, that sound does not occur through a simple projection of lines, or *actinobolia* <note>original: actinoboliâ; a Greek-derived term for the emission of rays</note>—otherwise nothing at all could be heard, and in this, sound differs from light—but in its projection and reflection, it acts by way of a sonorous cylinder or cone. 

<image-desc>A scientific diagram illustrating the propagation and reflection of sound. A source at A, depicted as a drum, emits a cylindrical or conical volume of sound toward a masonry wall (labeled C D). The central ray A-O reflects to point B. The diagram shows the entire volume of sound hitting the wall between points E and F and reflecting as a solid cylinder toward B. A cross-section of this sound cylinder is shown separately and labeled X.</image-desc>

Sound, therefore, does not have a purely mathematical <note>meaning infinitely thin or abstract</note> width, but a thoroughly sensible and corporeal <note>physical</note> one, so that in reflection, it is not a single line, but a continuous multitude of lines that produces the sound. Let us take, for example: the source of sound A, the endpoint of reflection B, and the reflecting object or wall C D. Let the motion of the sound begin at A. I say that it is not just a single line A-O, which, having made a reflection from O into B, will produce the sound there; rather, the entire cylindrical cone A-F-E will be reflected into B through the cylinder E-F-B. But let us demonstrate the matter a little more precisely.

<meta>catchword: PROPO-</meta>

<summary>Kircher introduces the concept of the 'Phonism,' arguing that sound travels as a physical volume—a cone or cylinder—rather than a single geometric line. This explains why sound is audible across a space and how it reflects off surfaces as a solid 'body' of noise.</summary>

<keywords>Phonism, acoustics, sound reflection, geometry of sound, Athanasius Kircher, echo mechanics, physics of sound</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical transition from understanding sound as simple lines to understanding it as a physical volume (phonism). It builds on the previous page's introduction of sound as a "body" that behaves like a cylinder or cone when striking a surface.</meta>

<header>ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.</header>

# PROPOSITION VIII.

## CYLINDRICAL PHONISM IS REFLECTED TOWARD
### the opposite side in a cylindrical projection.

Suppose there is a drum at point A, the sound of which strikes the surface DC. I assert that the resulting phonism <term>phonism: Kircher’s term for the physical volume or "body" of sound as it travels</term> will be cylindrical; that is, the reflection will not be a simple line, but will take a cylindrical shape. If we imagine sonorous lines drawn from every point of the drum at A toward the surface EF, these will undoubtedly form a phonism in the shape of a cylinder. I maintain that this same sound will be reflected toward B in that same cylindrical shape. Since all the sonorous lines within the phonic cylinder AFE are parallel to one another, each one—according to Proposition 2 of this book and Proposition 6 of our *Anacamptic Art* <note>original: Artis nostrae anacampticae; the mathematical study of reflected waves, such as light or sound</note>—will reflect in parallel <note>original: παραλλήλως</note> toward B, which forms the base of the sound-reflecting <note>original: phonocamptici</note> cylinder. Thus, the proposition is clear. These conclusions are entirely proven by what has come before.

Therefore, it must be noted that we call the triangle FHE the <term>triangle of sound confusion</term>. As long as the ear is positioned within this triangle, the reflected voice will be imperceptible, and the reflected sound will be blurred or "confused" with the direct sound. However, outside of that triangle, in the entire space BHF, the sound is perceived more clearly the closer the ear is to the point B <note>original: τὸ B</note>.

Note secondly that this triangle will be sharper at point H the more the line AO approaches the straight line XO.

# PROPOSITION IX.

## ORTHOPHONIC PHONISM REFLECTS BACK
### into itself.

If the phonism <term>orthophonus: "straight-sounding," or a sound wave hitting a surface at a 90-degree angle</term> is directed straight at the reflecting object, a "sonorous column" or a straight cylinder of sound is created. Let X be the sounding source and CD a wall that is parallel and perpendicular <note>original: normalis</note> to the source. I assert that the sonorous lines of the cylinder will reflect back onto themselves. Since the sound source is, by hypothesis, perpendicular to the wall CD, all resulting sonorous lines will be parallel. Therefore, according to Proposition 2, they will all reflect back into themselves. The proposition is thus proven.

<meta>Catchword: PROPO-</meta>

<summary>This page explains how sound functions as a three-dimensional cylinder rather than a single line, describing how these volumes of sound reflect off surfaces. It introduces the "triangle of confusion" where direct and reflected sounds overlap, making the echo unintelligible.</summary>
<keywords>phonism, acoustics, reflection, sound volume, cylinder of sound, triangle of confusion, orthophonic sound, Athanasius Kircher, physics of sound</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exploration of how sound travels in geometric shapes. Having previously established how sound behaves in a cylindrical path, Kircher now moves to conical sound-paths, applying the laws of optics (specifically the behavior of light rays) to the behavior of sound waves.</meta>

<header>THE ART OF SOUND, BOOK 1, SECTION 1.</header>

# PROPOSITION X.

**IN CONICAL PHONISM, ONLY THE AXIS REFLECTS WITH**
*equal angles; moreover, the reflected phonism will be a truncated cone. Just as in the patterns of light <note>original: photismis</note> and the optics of ray behavior <note>original: actinobilismo optico</note>, the axis of the pyramid of light or vision has the primary power both to illuminate and to represent things clearly; the same holds true for conical phonism <term>phonism: Kircher’s term for the physical volume or "body" of sound</term>.*

<margin>2</margin>
Suppose there is a direct conical phonism A B C, or direct sound <term>phonoptotus: sound falling directly from the source</term>; let the sound-reflecting object <note>original: objectum phonocampticum</note> be C H B. I assert that only the axis A H reflects at equal angles toward D, the center point of the cone's base. Since these two lines constitute the axes of the cones, the

endpoints of these axes must necessarily be the centers of the bases for both the direct and reflected cones. Therefore, A H B forms an angle of incidence equal to the angle of reflection D H C; thus, this ray alone will be reflected to D. All other rays will be reflected toward various other points at their own equal angles: where A B reflects to F; A K to G; A L to D; A M to N; and finally, A C to I. For if they did not reflect to those specific points, let us suppose they all reflected to D; in that case, the angle of reflection for each individual ray would be unequal to its angle of incidence. This is absurd and contrary to our hypothesis. Therefore, only the axis reflects to D at equal angles, and nothing else; consequently, the reflected phonism will not be a perfect cone, but will instead take on the character of something like a truncated and inverted cone.

<meta>catchword: PROPOSITION-</meta>

<summary>Kircher applies the optical laws of reflection to sound, arguing that when sound travels in a cone shape, only the central axis reflects at a perfect, equal angle to the center of the new sound field. He concludes that the resulting reflected sound-body is not a perfect cone but a distorted, truncated one.</summary>

<keywords>conical phonism, angle of incidence, angle of reflection, acoustics, sound geometry, Athanasius Kircher, optics of sound</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the geometric analysis of sound propagation. Having established in Proposition X that only the central axis of a sound cone reflects directly back on itself, Kircher now expands this to describe the entire shape formed by a reflected sound cone when it hits a flat surface perpendicularly.</meta>

<header>ON THE NATURE AND PROPERTY OF SOUND.</header>

## PROPOSITION XI.

### A CONICAL PHONISM PERPENDICULAR TO A WALL
*will produce, through its reflection, an inverted truncated sound cone.*

Let there be a conical phonism <term>phonism: a term coined by Kircher to describe the physical "body" or volume of sound</term> **A** perpendicular to the wall **B D C**. I assert that the reflected sound will form an inverted truncated cone. Because none of the lines falling from point **A** onto the wall—except for the axis or central line—reflects back upon itself (as proven in Proposition 2 of this section), all other lines will reflect toward various points away from point **A**. However, since those points on the periphery of the circle—that is, the base of the sound cone—are reflected at equal angles, the boundary of these reflected lines **V X** will necessarily form a circle. The center of this circle, **A**, will be the "phonic center" or the source of the sound, which is also the apex of the original direct sound cone. The common circular surface where the cones meet will be the base of the sound cone **A B C**. Thus, the inverted truncated cone relative to the direct one will be **V X B C**, which the wall **B C** (perpendicular to the axis) intersects at point **D**. Therefore, a conical phonism, etc. Which was to be shown.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the reflection of a sound cone. A source point A projects a cone towards a flat wall BDC. The central axis is labeled D. The reflection forms a wider, inverted truncated cone extending back past the source point to a circular base labeled VX.</image-desc>

## COROLLARY.

It follows from this proposition that if the ears of different people were placed along the circle **V X**, they would all hear the same sound, or the same *Echo* <note>original: Echum</note>. Furthermore, the base of this truncated cone **V X** will be wider the closer it is to the sound-reflecting object <term>phonocamptic object: any surface, like a wall or cliff, that reflects sound</term> **B C**, and it will be narrower the further the sound-point **A** is from the wall, as was stated in *prelude 3, section 3.*

<header>PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION I.</header>
<meta>catchword: trum</meta>

### §. II.

->*On Polyphonisms, or the Prodigious Multiplication of Sounds.*<-

<margin>What polyphonism and polyphotism are.</margin>
Just as light acquires its greatest increase when confined within concave bodies, so too does sound. What acoustic mirrors achieve in the convergence of "photisms" <note>original: *photismorum*—Kircher’s term for the physical volume or "body" of light</note>, the "otic" or acoustic organs achieve in the convergence of "phonisms" <term>phonism: a term coined by Kircher to describe the physical "body" or cone of sound as it travels through space</term>. Thus, polyphonism is nothing other than the gathering of many phonisms, or vocal cones, into a single point. Similarly, polyphotism is nothing other than the meeting of many photisms in one point. Since this polyphonism belongs properly to concave bodies—whether they be many-sided polygons or circular—I have decided to explain this subtle and illustrious topic at greater length, especially since no one else, as far as I am aware, has yet touched upon it.

I suppose first that a voice in an open field is subject to no reflection, but radiates spherically in every direction; however, as soon as it encounters some obstacle perpendicular to it, it then establishes its first phonism. If the voice is positioned between two obstacles or walls, it establishes two phonisms in that location, and so on for others. With this assumed, nothing remains but to see how many and what kind of phonisms the voice produces within various bodies. *Let it be thus:*

---

## PROPOSITION XII.

->***WITH THE PHONIC CENTER PLACED ON THE AXIS OF A HOLLOW***<-
->***Equilateral Triangular Prism, the emitted voice will return***<-
->***to its center increased by a triple phonism.***<-

Let there be a concave three-sided prism **A B C**. Let the phonic center <note>The "phonic center" is the point of origin where the sound is produced, such as a person's mouth.</note> be placed at **D** on the axis of the prism. I assert that the emitted voice, increased by a triple phonism, returns to its own center. Because the radii—or rather the axes of the phonisms—**D E**, **D F**, and **D G** are perpendicular to the sides of the prism (according to Proposition 2), they will necessarily return upon themselves, tripled in strength. Furthermore, since all nearby radii are received in the manner of perpendicular lines, they also, according to Hypothesis 3, around the same center...

Thus, with the phonic center <term>phonic center: the point of origin or the focal point where sound is concentrated</term> established in the axis of a concave three-sided prism, the emitted voice will return to its center increased by a triple phonism <note>original: *phonifmo*—Kircher’s term for a distinct "body" or unit of sound</note>, *which was to be demonstrated.*

## PROPOSITION XIII.

->**WITH THE PHONIC CENTER ESTABLISHED IN THE AXIS OF A CONCAVE**<-
->*quadrilateral prism, the emitted voice will return to the same center*<-
->*increased by a fourfold phonism.*<-

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a quadrilateral (trapezoidal) prism (labeled A, B, C, D) with a central point E. Like the previous image, this prism sits on a brick wall structure. Points A, B, C, and D are marked on the centers of each interior face.</image-desc>

Let ABCD be a concave quadrilateral <note>four-sided</note> prism, with the phonic center at E. I say that the voice emitted will return to E increased by a fourfold phonism. For since the rays—or the axes of the phonisms—EB, EC, ED, and EA are perpendicular to the sides (according to Proposition 2 of this book), the voice will be reflected back upon itself. Furthermore, since these rays are equal in length, they will sound together at E at the same time. Since the neighboring rays are received in the manner of perpendiculars, and according to Proposition 11 they will be reflected around E at the width of the ears: the voice will therefore return to the center E, increased by the four phonisms which the four sides of the prism produce, *which was to be shown.*

<header>PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION I.</header>
<meta>catchword: PROPO-</meta>

It must now be explained how the remaining lines finally return to the center E through various reflections. We have already spoken of the perpendicular lines <note>lines hitting the wall at a 90-degree angle</note>; let us now speak of the oblique ones: Let EC and BE, as shown in the following figure, be oblique sound lines <term>loxophones: from the Greek 'loxos' (slanting) and 'phone' (sound); Kircher uses this to describe the path of sound traveling at an angle</term>. I say they will return to E. For EC, being reflected at C under the equality of angles <note>original: *sub æqualitate angulorum*—the law of reflection where the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection</note> ECI and VCD, will be reflected to D; from here, under the same equality of angles VDC and SDO, it will be reflected to O; and finally from O, under the equality of angles SOD and XOE, back to the center E. In the same way, EB reflected from B to G, and from there to F, will finally return to E. EF also, from F, will be reflected back by the opposite path to G, then to B, and finally to E; thus EO, from O to D and from D to C, will finally return to E along a path opposite to the first. 

<image-desc>A horizontal sequence of four geometric diagrams illustrating the reflection of lines within square frames. The diagrams progress in complexity from left to right, showing paths (loxophones) bouncing off the boundaries of the squares. Points are labeled with letters: A, B, C, D, E, F, G, I, N, O, P, S, V, X.</image-desc>

Again, as is clear in the third *figure*, the line EF, reflected from F to G, then to V, then to I, then to N, then to O, and from O to P, will finally reflect back to E, always under the equality of angles, so that after seven reflections it finally returns to E. If, indeed, the flow of the voice were set on the opposite path from E to P, it would return to E after the same number of reflections. The same must be said of the remaining intermediate lines between O and F.

## COROLLARY I <note>original: *consectarium*—a logical conclusion or deduction following a proof</note>

From these things it is clear why there is such an intensification of the voice and such a powerful resonance <note>original: *bombus*—a deep, humming, or booming sound</note> in square wells. For lines reflected in various ways and returning to the same starting point usually intensify sound wonderfully; the marvelous diligence of nature in increasing the voice within concave bodies is also evident.

## PROPOSITION XIV.

->*WHEN THE PHONIC CENTER IS ESTABLISHED IN THE AXIS OF AN EQUI-*<-
->*lateral pentagonal prism, a voice emitted, increased by five phonic sounds,*<-
->*will return to the place from which it flowed out.*<-

Let there be an equilateral pentagonal prism A B C D E, and let a voice emitted from point L on the axis, or the phonic center, be propagated in every direction: I say that it will return to L increased by the five phonic echoes <term>phonismis: Kircher uses this term to describe the distinct repetitions or reflections of a sound</term> L B C, L C D, L D E, L E A, and L A B. First, indeed, by *Proposition 2*, the axes of the phonic cones <note>The "cones" represent the three-dimensional spread of sound waves from a point</note> L G, L I, L K, L F, and L H, since they are perpendicular <note>original: *normales*</note> to the sides, will necessarily return to L. But the remaining neighboring rays, driven by various reflections, will also finally be reflected back to L. For the voice falling from L into point O will be reflected from there to P, from P to V, from V to S, from S to I, and from here will finally return to L. You see, therefore, that there is no phonic line intercepted between points H A or H B that does not return to L after various reflections. The same must be said of the other phonic lines falling upon any side whatsoever. Therefore, with the ear placed in the axis of an equilateral pentagonal prism, etc.—which was to be demonstrated.

Let there be a concave equilateral <term>isopleurum: from the Greek for "equal sides"; here used as a synonym for equilateral</term> hexagonal prism A B C D E F, and the center on axis O, in which a voice emitted, increased by 6 phonic sounds, will return to the place from which it flowed, namely to O. For since all the lines or axes of the cones are perpendicular to the midpoints of the sides A, B, C, D, E, and F, they will be reflected back into themselves by *Proposition 2*, namely to O. But the remaining neighboring lines will wander back and forth through various reflections...

**Note**, that if someone were to place surfaces perpendicular <note>original: *normales*</note> to the rays at points G, H, I, K, L, and M, I say that the Echo would describe a perfect hexagon with its reflected lines. For a voice falling from A into G, from G into H, from H into I, from I into K, from K into L, from L into M, and from there back into A, will finally necessarily be reflected according to the equality of angles <note>This refers to the law of reflection: the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection</note>. ->COROLLARY I.<-

Finally, from what has been said, it is most clearly evident that the more sides concave prisms have, the more the sound will be augmented; for as the <term>phonisms: Kircher's term for the individual instances of reflected sound or the "units" of echo</term> are increased, the sound is increased. However, the phonisms increase according to the increment of the sides, etc. Furthermore, the more sides there are, the closer the angles of incidence and reflection will be to a right angle, and consequently, the phonisms will be more united around the center according to Hypothesis 3. 

But let it suffice to have explained the matter with one example <note>original: *paradigmate*</note>. Let there be a dodecagonal <note>twelve-sided</note> prism, as the following figure shows, whose center is O. Let A C B O be one of the twelve phonisms; I say that the sound emitted from O, besides being increased by twelve phonisms, will also be increased by the lines approaching more closely to the perpendicular <note>original: *normalem*</note>, such as are all those middle lines between A and B. Truly, the extreme lines O A and Q B of each phonism [are directed] so much from the center in the first...

## COROLLARY II.

It is also clear that if, for example, a sound were located at A, and at each of the angles perpendicular <note>original: *normales*</note> surfaces were erected, it would happen that the sound, through reflection, would describe a dodecagon <note>A twelve-sided polygon</note>. If, however, the alternating surfaces A, H, K, M, O, and F were removed, a voice falling from E into B would be reflected into I, and from there into L, and from there into N, and from there into G, and would finally be reverberated back to E; and by its circular reflection, it would form a hexagon. Truly, from F, having reflected through B, K, N, and F, it would describe a square, as the figure clearly shows.

<image-desc>A circular geometric diagram demonstrating the paths of sound reflection within a dodecagon. The circle is marked with vertices labeled A, B, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, M, N, and O. Internal straight lines connect these vertices to form a regular hexagon (E-B-I-L-N-G), a square (F-B-K-N), and an equilateral triangle (F-H-M). All reflection paths intersect at a common central point, illustrating the convergence of sound waves.</image-desc>

Again, reflecting from F into H, and from there into M, and again into F, it would describe an equilateral triangle through triple reflection. You see, therefore, how a voice within a single dodecagon describes all the lines of astrological aspects <note>In historical science, "aspects" were the geometric angles (like 120° for a triangle or 90° for a square) formed between planets, which were believed to influence the world much like the harmony of music</note> through its sonic reflection. Many other hidden wonders of reflections lie hidden beneath figures of this kind, which will reveal themselves only to those who have more deeply examined what we have presented here.

## CONSECTARY II <note>A natural consequence or conclusion</note>

From this it follows that since the multiplication of polygons increases unto infinity, the multiplication of sound-reflections <term>phonisinorum</term> <note>Likely referring to the points where sound strikes and creates a new impulse or echo</note> also increases unto infinity according to every variation. However, the union of sounds occurs most powerfully at the axis of a concave body; for in this place, all the sonic lines reflected from the vertical mid-lines of the polygon sides are united. A deep booming <term>bombus</term> <note>A low-frequency resonance</note> or a ringing <term>tinnitus</term> will especially arise in the concave angles of concave solids; hence, the reason for such great resonance in wells is explained with no trouble at all.

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION I.

## PROPOSITION XVI.

### WITH THE PHONIC CENTER <term>Phonic center: The point of origin from which a sound is emitted.</term> ESTABLISHED IN THE AXIS of a hollow cylinder, a voice emitted from all points of the periphery will reflect back upon itself.

<image-desc>A circular geometric diagram representing the cross-section of a hollow cylinder (labeled X and Y). A central point N acts as the "acoustic center," with radial lines connecting to points on the circumference labeled A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, and M, illustrating how sound reflects back to its source from all points on the perimeter.</image-desc>

Let there be a hollow cylinder X Y, with the voice established at the center N; I say that it will be reflected back into N from all points of the periphery—A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, M—and from any other infinite points. For since all lines drawn from the center to the circumference are perpendicular to it, and perpendicular lines—according to *proposition 2*—reflect back upon themselves, all lines from the periphery will therefore be reflected back into N, where they will all be united; thus the proposition is clear. Furthermore, since the voice does not fall only upon the points of a single circle, but upon as many points as can be considered along the longitudinal lines of the cylinder; and since these longitudinal lines are all parallel to the axis, all points of these lines will be reverberated <term>Reverberation: The process of sound bouncing off a surface; here, Kircher uses it to describe the concentration of sound toward a central line.</term> somewhere onto the axis. From this, the wonderful and truly miraculous multiplication of sound, such as we experience in deeper wells, is revealed without any difficulty;

<image-desc>A vertical rectangular diagram depicting the interior of a well or cylinder (labeled A, B at the top and C, D at the bottom). A central axis line O-P is marked with various points (M, V, N, R, S). Diagonal lines show the path of sound waves originating from point N, reflecting off the walls at points E, G, F and H, I, K, and repeatedly intersecting the central axis.</image-desc>

Let the well be A B C D, and the axis O P. Let the phonic center be N, from which the emitted voice falls upon E; from E, it will therefore be reflected to G, and from G to F, always intersecting the axis at points M and V. Similarly, the voice falling on H will be reverberated to I, and from I to K, cutting the axis at R and S; and similar reflections occur at all points of the vertical lines of the well, such as A C and B D in the preceding diagram.

# PROPOSITION XVII.

->**IN PYRAMIDS OF ANY NUMBER OF SIDES**<-
->*as well as in hollow cones, if the ear is placed on the axis, a voice sent out will never return to itself, except at points L and M, and not at any other points.*<-

The sound of the voice does not return to itself within hollow conical bodies outside of points L and M; consequently, such bodies are unsuitable for multiplying sound. For since an oblique sound <term>Oblique sound: From the Greek λοξόφωνος (*loxophonos*), referring to sound that strikes a surface at an angle rather than perpendicularly.</term> strikes the individual sides, it reflects upwards. Let there be a Pyramid A B C with the phonic center <term>Phonic center: The point of origin from which a sound is emitted.</term> at K. From this point, the voice falling upon G reflects to E, and from H to F, from N to O, and from here again to L. From this it is evident that the reflection always strives upward and does not return to itself except at the two points L and M, as has been stated.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of an inverted pyramid or cone labeled with points. The vertex at the bottom is A, and the base at the top is defined by points B and C. A central vertical axis contains points I (at the top), K (the acoustic source), and M and L below it. Various zigzagging lines illustrate the path of sound reflecting off the interior walls at points labeled G, E, H, F, N, O, and L, showing the sound moving upward toward the opening.</image-desc>

# PROPOSITION XVIII.

->**WITH THE PHONIC CENTER ESTABLISHED AT THE CENTER**<-
->*of the five hollow Regular bodies, the reflected voice, increased by as many phonisms as the body has sides, will return to itself.*<-

Let there be 5 regular bodies <term>Regular bodies: Now commonly known as the Platonic Solids.</term>: the tetrahedron, cube, octahedron, dodecahedron, and icosahedron <note>original: "vicosaedrum"</note>. They are called such because they have equal sides and can be inscribed within a single sphere <note>original: "solo circulo," though in this geometric context Kircher refers to the circumsphere of the polyhedra.</note>. Each of their flat faces is equidistant from the center. From this, and from the very definition of each, it is clear how sound can be propagated within them. For with the phonic center established at the center of a tetrahedron, the voice—increased by 4 phonisms <term>Phonism: A single unit of reflected sound or a distinct echo.</term>—will return to itself; in an octahedron, 8; in a dodecahedron, 12; and finally, in an icosahedron, 20 pho-

<meta>catchword: pho- (continued on next page as "phonisms")</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains that pyramids and cones are poor for sound reinforcement because sound reflects upward and out rather than returning to the source. Conversely, he posits that sound centered in Platonic solids reflects perfectly back to the center from every face.</summary>

...increased by 20 phonisms <note>Phonism: A term used by Kircher to describe a distinct unit or instance of reflected sound, similar to an echo or a sound-wave reflection.</note>, the sound will return to itself. The demonstration of all these points depends on the preceding arguments and is so simple that it requires no further explanation.

# PROPOSITION XIX.

## *A HOLLOW SPHERE is the most suitable of all shapes for propagating sound.*

For since all lines drawn from every point of a circular surface to the center are equal to one another, and all are perpendicular to the spherical surface, the sound-forms <note>original: *species* - in 17th-century science, this referred to the "images" or waves of sound traveling through the air.</note> will necessarily be reflected back upon themselves and unite at the center. Therefore, the most powerful sound will be produced at that point. A hollow sphere, *therefore*, is the most spacious of all bodies for propagating sound and is likewise the most suitable for multiplying it. Which was to be demonstrated.

### PROBLEM I.

*IF PHONOCAMPTIC OBJECTS <term>Phonocamptic: sound-reflecting; from the Greek 'phone' (voice) and 'kamptos' (bent/reflected).</term> are arranged from a fixed location in such a way that they are not only parallel to the vocal source, but also perpendicular to it, the voice will be reflected from each object back upon itself.*

Let the sound-reflecting objects be **B, C, D, O, E,** and **I**. Let the fixed location of the voice be **A**, situated above the well. I maintain that the sound lines striking the aforementioned objects will return to point **A**. For since, according to our hypothesis, the voice striking the designated points of these solid bodies is reflected orthogonally <note>original: ὀρθογώνιος (orthogonios); meaning at a right angle or 90 degrees.</note>—

Likewise, one standing in valley F will perceive the voice from A reflected at point O, and conversely, from the bottom of the well at D, the reflected voice will return to A with a multiplied sound.

## PROBLEM III.

->*GIVEN ANY POINTS OR STATIONS*<-
->*arranged in any manner, provided they have a suitable distance for producing*<-
->*an echo, to arrange the reflecting objects such that*<-
->*the reflected voice is known only to those who occupy*<-

LET the given stations be A, B, C, D, E, F, G. Let the first voice be at point A. It is required to arrange the reflecting objects near the given points such that the reflected voice is perceived by no one else except those occupying the said station points. Let the said points be connected by straight lines; once this is done, divide each of the angles into two equal parts according to the preceding rule. I say that the perpendicular lines erected at the endpoints of the lines that bisect the angles will mark the required position of the objects. Thus, the reflected voice will be heard only by those occupying the given stations or the direct-reflection lines.

<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from Problem IV, explaining how sound reflects internally and externally between points to reach the ear. ->*The points therefore being given, etc., which was to be done, etc.*<-

We say that it is reflected externally when a certain sound-reflecting <term>phonocamptic: from the Greek 'phōnē' (voice) and 'kamptos' (bent), referring to objects that reflect sound.</term> object has its surface of reflection along with its perpendicular line <note>original: cathetus; the line perpendicular to the reflecting surface.</note> outside the range of the given points—or rather, when the perpendicular lines of reflection extend outward. They reflect internally, however, when the perpendicular lines of reflection incline inward. In the proposed example, the sound-reflecting objects reflecting externally are C and H, and their perpendicular lines CV and HV wander outside; the remaining objects having perpendicular lines inclining inward, such as BV, DV, EV, and FV, are internally reflecting.

# PROBLEM V.
## *TO CONSTRUCT A POLYPHONIC ECHO, OR one which, after the first utterance, repeats the same voice and other phrases several times.*

At this point, those things we delivered in the *third prelude* concerning the interval of the *Echo* must be recalled to memory. For since a certain and mathematical distance cannot be given in this slippery business, he will do best of all who explores the said distance by sense, or mechanically <note>original: mechanice; through practical experiment rather than pure theory.</note>. He shall do it thus: let him choose for himself a solid, polished wall, most suitable for the reflection of the voice, then approach and recede from it perpendicularly until he has perfectly returned one syllable, and let him carefully explore the space with some known measure. Then, at an opportune and quiet time, let him go back in a straight path until he has perfectly returned a two-syllable word, and let him measure the space carefully. Afterward, let him go back until the *Echo* has returned three syllables, and so always receding more and more from the wall until he has perfectly returned ten syllables, or any verse, perfectly observing the distance in all polysyllables.

And by experience he will find that the space by which the *Echo* is increased by one syllable is not equal, but always less and less; in the same way, so that a polyphonic...

# NEW PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION II.

...be a [poly]phonic *Echo* and it repeats a syllable several times, the spaces in which it clearly repeats up to seven syllables do not grow equally, but become smaller and smaller.

The reason for this is nothing other than the "weakening" of the voice, which, the further the distance it travels, always becomes more and more faint, until it is completely exhausted; therefore, since the voice is always more and more weakened,

| *Example* | | |

it is certain that the spaces cannot be equal at all, but the further ones must always be more compressed (just as happens in the visual perspective of a row of columns). However, by what proportion they decrease is difficult to know, since the speed of sound’s movement can hardly admit an exact and certain mathematical ratio in relation to the space; therefore, one will act more reliably if they explore the spaces for multi-syllable echoes and <term>polyphonic: multiple-sounding or repeating.</term> *Echoes* through the senses. Furthermore, this mechanical matter can be investigated with the help of several people. Let there be a wall A B, from which the <term>Echometra: an "echo-measurer," referring here to the person performing the acoustic experiment.</term> retreats until that phrase: *I sing of arms and the man* <note>original: "Arma virúmque cano"; the famous opening line of Virgil’s *Aeneid*.</note>, is perfectly returned, which will happen at point C. There, the first echo-measurer, standing still, will give a signal of the last reflected syllable (*cano*) to another partner, who retreats along the line C L 

<image-desc>A geometric line diagram. A horizontal line has points B, C, and L. At point B, a vertical line extends upwards to point A, representing a wall or reflecting surface.</image-desc>

until, having been given the signal of the first completed reflection at C, the *Echo* begins a second time. And here, having given the signal of the second completed reflection, he will give it to a third person retreating on the same line, until, the second reflection having finished, the third begins; and so on to infinity. For these spaces, explored by diligent measurement, will provide the locations where walls may be erected to repeat entire phrases several times.

But since these observations are difficult, the *Echometra* will do better if they choose walls separated by an equal gap and a space proportioned to the reflecting voice; for these, for the most part, repeat voices perfectly several times, just as experience has taught me in the walls of various towered cities. First, in the walls of the *City of Avignon*, in which the voice is repeated distinctly eight times, even if the <term>phonocamptic: sound-reflecting objects.</term> obstacles are separated by an equal space. Similarly in the walls of the *City of Rome*, where, due to the multitude of towers, the voice reflects now twice, now four times, now five, six, or seven times; but let us now approach more closely the problem proposed at the beginning.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how to calculate the physical space required for echoes. Having previously discussed how distance relates to the number of syllables, Kircher now moves into the practical "architecture" of echoes—specifically, how to arrange structures to create multiple repetitions of a single sound.</meta>

# ECHO ARCHITECTURE

Let there be a wall extended with seven towers, as is clear in the present *figure*; let them be separated from one another by the same distance that the source of the voice, X, stands from the first tower, A. Let the individual surfaces of the towers be parallel and <term>perpendicular: standing at a 90-degree angle (also called "normal" in geometry).</term> to the voice at X. I say that the voice at X, striking each of the walls A, B, C, D, E, F, and G, will return upon itself and form a <term>seven-fold echo: an echo that repeats a sound seven times, also known as a heptaphonic echo.</term>

<note>The illustration shows a man labeled 'X' projecting sound toward a series of seven staggered towers. Because each tower is placed at a specific, increasing distance, the sound reflects off each one at a different time, creating seven distinct repetitions of the original sound.</note>

For since, by our assumption, the voice reflects back on itself from all walls that are perpendicular to the sounds, and because the voice returns more and more slowly from the increasingly distant towers, the last tower will necessarily return the voice later than the second-to-last, and the second-to-last later than the third-to-last, and so on for the others. Therefore, an ear positioned at X will perceive a *seven-fold echo*. Thus, we have constructed a *seven-fold echo*, which was the task at hand.

<note>This second illustration demonstrates a "polysyllabic" or "diminishing" echo using a famous Latin wordplay. As the sound "Clamore" reflects off the successive towers, the listener hears the word shortened syllable by syllable.</note>

*   **Tower A:** "By shouting" <note>original: *Clamore*</note>
*   **Tower B:** "By love" <note>original: *Amore*</note>
*   **Tower C:** "By custom" <note>original: *more*</note>
*   **Tower D:** "From the mouth" <note>original: *ore*</note>
*   **Tower E:** "By the matter" <note>original: *re*</note>
*   **Tower F:** "Out of" <note>original: *e*</note>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher explains the geometric arrangement of walls or towers required to produce a "heptaphonic" (seven-fold) echo. He illustrates how sound waves reflecting off surfaces at staggered distances return to the speaker at different intervals, creating a repeating effect.</summary>

BOOK I, SECTION II OF THE PHONURGY

## PROBLEM VI.

### *GIVEN ANY POINTS arranged in a circle, how to construct a circular and multi-voice echo.*

Let there be <term>phonocamptic points: from the Greek "phono" (sound) and "kamptikos" (bending), these are points specifically designed to reflect or "bend" sound back to its source</term> in a circle A B C D E F G H I K L M, arranged in any way you like. From the <term>centrophonic point: the central location where the sound originates (the "sound-center")</term> at A, let lines A B, A C, A D, etc., be drawn. I say that from point G, at the end of the circle's radius, 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of a circle illustrating sound reflection. A vertical line (diameter) connects point G at the top of the circle to point A at the bottom. Point A serves as the focal point ("centrophonico"). Ten additional points are marked on the upper half of the circle's circumference, labeled B, C, D, E, F on the left and H, I, K, L, M on the right. Radial lines are drawn from point A to each of these points. Horizontal chords connect corresponding pairs of points across the vertical axis (e.g., B to M, C to L).</image-desc>

the lines A B, A C, A D, A E, A F, A H, A I, A K, A L, A M, when drawn, will mark the positions of the reflecting objects. The sound-waves drawn from A will be <term>normal: a mathematical term meaning perpendicular, or meeting at a 90-degree angle</term> to these objects. Therefore, a voice sent from A toward a reflecting object placed upon line B will reflect the voice back to A. Since the lines C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, and M are also perpendicular to the sound-lines drawn from A, the reflecting objects built upon them will likewise reflect the voice back to A. Furthermore, because the lines extended from A become progressively longer and longer <note>Kircher is noting that as you move along the arc of the circle away from the center-line, the distance the sound must travel increases</note>, it follows that the voice will be reflected back to A later and later. This will give rise to a *polyphonic echo*, repeating the voices in succession as many times as there were points arranged in the circle. If these points are placed at equal distances from A on both semicircles, they will reverberate into the *Echo* at the same time, making the sound twice as strong.

<meta>catchword: CON-</meta>

<summary>A geometric proof demonstrating how to arrange reflecting surfaces along the arc of a circle so that sound originating from a single point returns as a series of distinct, timed repetitions.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, phonocamptic, centrophonic, polyphonic echo, circular reflection, geometry of sound, reverberation</keywords>

# ACOUSTIC ARCHITECTURE.

<center>G</center>

<meta>catchword: pau-</meta>

# COROLLARY.

From these points, it is clear with what ease one might construct a *polyphonic echo* <note>original: "Echum polyphonam"; a multi-voiced echo that repeats several times</note> in a circle. Here you also see a certain admirable property of *this figure of sonorous motion* which expresses a sort of fading; for the <term>anacamptic spaces: from the Greek "anakamptikos," referring to the paths along which sound is reflected back</term> G H, H I, I K, and K L are not equal, as has been said. Instead, they constantly decrease, always falling shorter as they approach the quantity of the line of action represented by A. Thus, just as the spaces G H relate to H I, and I K to K L, so do the <term>phonocamptic objects: physical surfaces designed to reflect sound</term> relate in terms of distance, so that they repeat the voices. This corresponds most excellently to all the experiments we have conducted.

## PROBLEM VII.

### TO CONSTRUCT THE CHOIR OF ANY CHURCH
### with such artifice that only three singers perform as much as a hundred.

Let the <term>dome: original "tholus," the rounded interior of a cupola</term> of the choir be so arranged that it has a spherical surface inside. Let the Organ and the singers be placed around the center of this dome, and you will achieve your intent. Because all the vocal lines <note>the paths of the sound waves</note> will be <term>normal: meeting the surface at a perpendicular or 90-degree angle</term> to the dome, they will return to the place from which they flowed back, and will intensify the sound most vehemently. 

Furthermore, if a distance is chosen from the choir that is sufficient for repeating a *three-syllable echo*, and at that spot a spherical surface is made in the wall—described as if from a center at that location in the church where people most often indulge in devotion—the voices will not only be most vehemently intensified in this place, but they will also constitute a different choir from the first. If the singers arrange their songs in <term>clauses: "clausulas," which here refers to short musical phrases or cadences</term> of three notes, so that after the given phrases they always pause for as much time as the said phrases are worth, the people present in that location will be entirely persuaded that there are two choirs, and that the phrase was performed by a great crowd of singers.

<image-desc>A musical staff of five lines featuring four measures of notation. The notes are written in a style typical of the 17th or 18th century, including various durations and rests, intended to illustrate the rhythmic pauses described in the text for creating an echo effect.</image-desc>

Thus, with the first choir singing the first phrase, while in the meantime...

# PHONURGIA <note>original: "Phonurgia"; Kircher’s term for the "science of sound-making"</note> BOOK I. SECTION II.

...while [one] pauses, the other dome will repeat what was sung. This arrangement could be put into practice with remarkable success in the vast temple of SAINT PETER <note>St. Peter's Basilica in the Vatican</note>, if there were a musician who possessed knowledge of such hidden matters. Also, the church of SAINT JAMES *of the Incurables* <note>The church of San Giacomo degli Incurabili in Rome, known for its elliptical floor plan</note> here in ROME is so well-suited to this purpose that it seems as if the architect had this in mind.

## COROLLARY.

From these and the previous examples, it is sufficiently clear how artistic *Echoes* may be established. Certainly, not only in churches but also in open fields, *Echoes* can be arranged according to the preceding principles so that suitable musical phrases are repeated not just once, but twice, three, or four times, or as often as you wish. Thus, everyone would believe that the music is performed by various distributed choirs, when in fact it is sung by only one. To represent two choirs, after the first phrase is performed, a pause of two <term>beats: original "temporum," referring to the unit of measure or time in music</term> must be used; for three choirs, three beats; and finally, for four choirs, four beats of pause must be set, as follows.

->Music by way of Echo.<-

<image-desc>A single line of musical notation on a five-line staff using a C-clef. The notation is divided into five sections labeled above as "Pro 1. Choro.", "2. Chor.", "3. Chor.", "4. Chor.", and "5. Chor.", and lettered below as A, B, C, D, and E. The musical phrases are separated by rests of increasing duration to illustrate the timing of an echo effect across different choirs.</image-desc>

You see in this <term>diagram: original "paradigmate"</term> that the first phrase, A, reflects once; the second phrase, B, is repeated twice because of the longer <term>sonorous lines: the geometric paths that sound waves travel</term>; phrase C is repeated a third time because the sonorous lines are carried even further; thus phrase D is repeated four times due to the distance of the sonorous lines, and so on for the others.

It is therefore clear from what has been said how an *Echo* can be constructed to represent different choirs. If, however, the distance were so great that the human voice would fade away over the longer spans, I would more wisely suggest using trumpets, drums, and other instruments of powerful sound. Since their reflected sounds travel further than voices, they will present themselves more pleasantly to the ears from a distance than they do from up close.

ARCHITECTURA ECHONICA. <note>Acoustic Architecture</note>

# AN ENQUIRY.

## WHETHER A HETEROPHONIC <note>original: "Heterophona"; meaning "different-sounding"</note> ECHO CAN BE ESTABLISHED,
### *which returns different words from the original ones?*

This matter seems to involve a certain contradiction <note>original: "ἀντιλογίαν" (antilogian)</note> at first glance. For you may ask, "How can a voice differ from itself?" I do not see it; since an *Echo* is nothing other than a certain continued and reflected flow of the first voice, and it cannot by any means utter anything through reflection except what was first produced, it seems entirely contradictory that an *Echo* could represent something else. I respond that an *Echo* can be considered in many ways: either as direct <note>original: "ὀρθόφωνον" (orthophona)</note> or oblique <note>original: "λοξόφωνον" (loxophona)</note>; and again, as either a single-sounding <note>original: "μονόφωνον" (monophona)</note> or a multi-sounding <note>original: "πολύφονον" (polyphona)</note> reflection of syllables. I say, then, that it is true a direct *Echo* can represent nothing other than what was spoken, just as a single-syllable one cannot. However, I maintain that there are some oblique *Echoes* which we say can be suited to represent something other than what was spoken. Nevertheless, some think that even a direct *Echo* can be so arranged that the direct voice carries one meaning, and the reflected voice carries another. Thus, while some speak Greek words, they cause the *Echo* to respond in Latin, as is evident in the following words.

| Original Phrase (spoken in Greek) | Echo Response (heard as Latin) |
|---|---|
| The gods do all things with labors <note>original: "Οἱ Θεοὶ πάντα ποιοῦσι πόνοις" (Oi Theoi panta poiousi ponois)</note> | **to the good.** <note>original: "bonis"; a Latin pun on the Greek "ponois"</note> |
| [They] inhabit all things <note>original: "πάντα πολοῦσι" (panta polousi)</note> | **I played.** <note>original: "lusi"; a Latin pun on the Greek "polousi"</note> |
| The gods are here <note>original: "Οἱ Θεοὶ ἐνθάδε" (Oi Theoi enthade)</note> | **in age.** <note>original: "ætate"; a Latin pun on the Greek "enthade"</note> |

In this or a similar way, words that end similarly could be selected from any language, so that they have different meanings in the different languages. Yet, such things are as forced and of meager wit as they are difficult to put into practice—requiring long study with little reward. Perhaps these things could be better achieved in one and the same language, of which we shall provide some examples. *See Figure 2 on the preceding page 47.*

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION II.

<margin>
WITH A SHOUT
WITH LOVE
BY CUSTOM
FROM THE MOUTH
BY THE THING
<note>original: "CLAMORE, AMORE, MORE, ORE, RE." This is a classic Latin word-play where each word is formed by dropping the first letter of the previous one.</note>
</margin>
Suppose, for example: a four-syllable **Echo** is to be established, with objects B, C, D, E, and F arranged in such a way that each reflects one syllable later than the last. Let the following word be the three-syllable <note>In the text, "CLAMORE" is treated as the base for the echo, though it is technically three syllables in Latin (cla-mo-re).</note> voice **WITH A SHOUT**. Let the source of the sounds, or the speaker, be at point A. Since the objects are assumed to be arranged so that each reflects a syllable later, it is certain that each object will reflect different words with distinct meanings. Thus, object B will return **WITH A SHOUT**; object C, **WITH LOVE**; object D, **BY CUSTOM**; object E, **FROM THE MOUTH**; and finally, object F will return **BY THE THING**. Therefore, a **Polyphonic** <note>original: "Polyphona"; meaning "many-sounding"</note> **Echo** will always repeat different and distinct words. For instance, if someone should cry out: *"To you indeed I shall give thanks, but with what shout?"* the **Echo** will respond: *I shall give thanks* **WITH A SHOUT, WITH LOVE, BY CUSTOM, FROM THE MOUTH, BY THE THING.**

<margin>
YOU WILL STAND FIRM
YOU WILL STAND
YOU GO AWAY
TWICE
HE GOES
<note>original: "CONSTABIS, STABIS, ABIS, BIS, IS." Another phonetic word-play where the echo changes meaning as it fades.</note>
</margin>
In the same way, the word **YOU WILL STAND FIRM** will reflect different meanings across various objects. As is evident here, countless examples of this kind can be found, reflecting one after another in succession from the original voice, so that the **Echo** no longer returns the first word, but always other and different words. In these two ways, therefore—namely, through a **Direct Echo** <note>original: "Echo ortophona"</note> and across various objects—it is possible to return words different from the original, and not otherwise.

The question then arises: can an **Oblique Echo** <note>original: "loxophona Echo"</note> be constructed so that it can respond to inquiries in any other language?

---

## PROBLEM VIII.
### TO CONSTRUCT A HETEROPHONIC <note>original: "Heterophonon"; meaning "different-sounding"</note> ECHO
->*whose reflected voice always responds with different words in any language.*<-

This **Echo** requires the labor of two people, and should be set up as follows: first, a space must be determined that is suitable for perfectly pronouncing four or five syllables—that is, a space that perfectly expresses a word of 4 or 5 syllables. Once this is done, let the reflecting object be arranged with such ingenuity that the voice of someone standing at a first station, hitting the object, is reflected to the ears of someone standing at a second station; but in such a way that the first person cannot see the second, but only hear him. For this reason, the object should be placed at the meeting point of two walls or a pro-

# ACOUSTIC ARCHITECTURE.
<meta>catchword: COROL-</meta>

...of some promontory to be arranged, as is clear in the present diagram, in which **E F** are the walls joined at **D**, and **C** is the rock or reflecting obstacle, whether it be artificial or natural—it makes no difference. Furthermore, let the reciprocal phonic center be at points **A** and **B**, separated from the object **C D** by such a space that it perfectly reflects four or five syllables, as I have already stated.

<image-desc>A perspective diagram of an acoustic experiment. On the left, a stone masonry wall labeled E and F meets at a corner labeled D. On the right, a rocky cliff face is labeled C. Two diagonal lines trace the path of sound: one line from point A at the bottom to the rock face C is labeled "What is your name?", and a reflecting line from C to point B is labeled "Constantine." The diagram demonstrates a loxophonic echo where a sound from A is heard at B, and B's response is reflected back to A.</image-desc>

In this way, as soon as the first person begins to shout a voice from point **A**, that sound, falling obliquely <note>original: "loxophonè"; from Greek meaning "slanting voice," referring to sound hitting a surface at an angle rather than head-on</note> upon **C**, will not be reflected back to **A**, but rather to **B**. As soon as the person standing at **B** hears this, they respond with other words spoken in any language, which, having struck **C**, will immediately force themselves into the ears of the person standing at **A**. Therefore, the first person will perceive something different than what was originally uttered. For instance, if the first person asks by shouting: **WHAT IS YOUR NAME?** and the other person positioned at **B** responds **CONSTANTINE**, the person standing at **A** will immediately perceive a response to the words of the interrogation—not through the reflection of his own words, but through the reflected words of another. 

Certainly, this artifice, especially if it is set up with diligence, is so secret that when I made a trial of this here in Rome at our estate <note>Kircher is likely referring to the Jesuit vineyard or "Vigna" on the Quirinal Hill in Rome, where he conducted many experiments.</note>, there was almost no one who grasped the reasoning behind it; everyone was marveling and as if struck with wonder, while they heard the *Echo* respond to all questions with different words. To hide the trick, however, there should be two people who are endowed with voices as equal as possible, for in this way the illusion will remain better hidden. Thus we have constructed a **Different-sounding** <note>original: "Ε'τερόφωνον" (Heterophonon)</note> *Echo*, etc.

<summary>This page describes an acoustic experiment where two people are positioned at specific focal points to create a "Heterophonic" or "Different-sounding" echo. By reflecting sound at an angle off a central rock or wall, Kircher demonstrates how a person can seem to receive an intelligent, spoken answer from a natural echo.</summary>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION II.

## COROLLARY I.

From what has been said, it is clear that two people at points **A** and **B** can signal anything to each other by this method; indeed, two choirs can harmonize in alternating voices in a wonderful way, and other things can be performed according to the judgment and ingenuity of a skilled craftsman.

## COROLLARY II.

It is also clear that, just as there are optical illusions of sight, so there are also Acoustic illusions. For a person positioned at **A** will clearly think the voice is coming from **D**, and thus that it is the voice they themselves emitted. The artifice will be all the more secret the more similar the voices are, and the less one person knows about the other. However, there will be more abundant material for discussing these matters in the following sections.

If you truly desire the length of the oblique line <term>loxoptotæ: a slanted or diagonal path</term> **A D**, let it be that as the sine of the angle of incidence **A D B** is to the "total sine" <note>In early trigonometry, the "total sine" refers to the sine of 90 degrees, used as a radius for calculations</note>, so the known measure of 25 paces is to another; the result will be **A D**, the sought-after oblique line. Since the reflected line **E D** is equal to it, its length is known by extension.

If you wish to know at which point on the line **A X** the voice from **D** is reflected, proceed as follows: Double the distance **B D** from point **A** along the line **A X**, and you will have point **E**, the sought-after location where the voice from **A** is perceived after reflecting from **D**. For since the perpendicular <term>cathetus: a line falling perpendicularly on another</term> **C D** bisects the triangle of reflection **E D A**, the angles **E D C** and **C D A** will be equal; therefore, the sines subtending them—namely **A C** and **C E**—will also be equal. The endpoints of these, **A** and **E**, determine the space between **A** (the phonic center) and the point **E** where the reflection terminates: *Therefore, given the data, etc.*

## COROLLARY.

*From this, three things are clear: First, that oblique lines of sound reflected directly between two parallels are always equal. Second, that the straight sound ray <note>original: "orthophonum radium"</note> **A B** or **C D** directed toward the reflecting object <note>original: "objectum phonocampticum"</note> **F D B** is always equal to the phonic perpendicular **C D** positioned between the parallels **E A** and **F B**. Third, that whatever the distance **B D** may be, doubling it will always measure the distance **A E**, at whose endpoint **E** the voice reflected from **D** terminates.*

## PROBLEM II.
*GIVEN THE DISTANCE OF THE PHONIC CENTER FROM THE point of reflection, to find the length of the direct sound path.*

Let the distance **A C** or **C E** be known, and let the center of reflection **E** or **A** be known; the <term>orthophona: a straight or direct sound path</term> **A B** or **C D** will be found if it is done such that the sine of angle **G A I** of the <term>cathetus phonicus: the vertical line or perpendicular used in acoustic geometry</term> is to the sine of the angle of incidence **A D B** (known on arc **C G**), as the known measure **D B** is to another value. Once the calculation is performed, the line **A B** or **C D** is produced. If, however, you wish to know the <term>loxophona: an oblique or diagonal sound path</term> lines **A D** and **D E**, let it be that as the sine of the phonic perpendicular angle is to the "total sine" <note>In 17th-century mathematics, the "total sine" refers to the sine of 90 degrees, used as the radius for trigonometric calculations</note>, so the distance **D B** is to another value. After the operation is completed, the sought-after oblique lines **A D** and **D E** will be produced. The reasoning for this is clear from Proposition 15 of the *Resolution of Triangles* by **CLAVIUS** <note>Christopher Clavius (1538–1612), a renowned Jesuit mathematician and astronomer whom Kircher frequently cites as an authority</note>, whom you should consult; specifically, problems 1, 2, and 3 of his Geometry.

## PROBLEM III.
*GIVEN THE OBLIQUE LINE, TO FIND THE DIRECT PATH and the distance of the phonic center from the perpendicular point or center of reflection.*

Let the oblique line **A D** be known. First, you shall investigate the direct path **C D** in this manner: let it be that as the total sine is to the sine of the angle **A D B** <term>phonoptoti: the angle at which sound strikes a surface</term>, so the known measure **A D** is to another value; and once the calculation is done, the sought-after **A B** or **C D** will be produced. Again, to find the quantity of line **D B**, let it be that as the total sine is to the sine of the phonic perpendicular angle **G A I**, so the known measure **A D** is to another; and with the operation performed, the sought **B D** will be produced.

## PROBLEM IV.
*GIVEN THE DIRECT PATH, TO FIND THE OBLIQUE lines and the distance of the phonic center from the center of reflection through tangents and secants.*

Let **A B** be the known direct path line; and should you desire to know the oblique line **A D**, let it be that as the total sine **A I** is to **A G** (the <term>secant: a trigonometric function representing the ratio of the hypotenuse to the adjacent side</term> of the phonic perpendicular angle of 14 degrees), so the known measure **A B** of 25 paces is to another; and the sought-after quantity of the oblique line **A D** will be produced. # ECHONIC ARCHITECTURE

...to find B D. Let it be done so: as the total sine **A I** <note>In 17th-century mathematics, the "total sine" refers to the sine of 90 degrees, used as the radius for trigonometric calculations</note> is to **I G**, the tangent of the 14-degree angle, so is the known measure of 25 paces to another value, and the sought-after quantity **B D** will be produced. If, however, **B D** were known, then **A B** and **A D** will become known in this way: let it be done so that as the tangent **G I** is to the total sine, so the known measure **B D** is to another value, and the <term>orthophona: the direct or straight sound path</term> line **A B** will be obtained. Again, for the <term>loxophona: the oblique or diagonal sound path</term> line **A D**, let it be that as the tangent **G I** of the 14-degree angle is to the secant **A G**, so the known value **B D** is to another, and the sought-after quantity **A D** will be produced.

->III. *Given the quantity of the oblique path, you will seek the direct path* **C D** *and* **B D** *in this manner.*<-

Let it be that as the secant **A G** is to **A I**, so the known measure **A D** is to another, and the sought-after direct path **C D** or **A B** will be obtained. Again, as the secant **A G** is to the secant **G I**, so **A D** is to another, and the sought quantity **B D** will be produced. Having finished this, all lines relating to the business of acoustics will have been investigated.

## PROBLEM V.

### GIVEN TWO SIDES IN A PHONIC TRIANGLE, TO FIND THE THIRD by the extraction of the square root.

First, in the phonic triangle **A D B**, let the side **A B** (the direct path) be given as 25 paces, and let the line **A C** (the distance of the phonic center from the <term>cathetus phonicus: the vertical line or perpendicular used in acoustic geometry</term>) be 8 paces; you will find the third side **A D** in this way. Let the given sides be multiplied by themselves [squared], and from the sum of the two sides joined together, let the square root be extracted; and you will have the sought-after line **A D** by the 47th proposition <note>The OCR reads "4. lib. 1," but the text describes the Pythagorean theorem, which is Proposition 47 of Book 1 of Euclid's Elements.</note> of Book 1 of Euclid's *Elements*.

Secondly, let the sides **B D** and **A D** be known; you will find the third side **A B** if you subtract the square of line **D B** from the square of the whole **A D**. For the root of the remaining square will give the sought-after line **A B**. Thirdly, let two sides **A B** and **A D** be given; you will find the third, **B D**, in this way. Subtract the square of **A B** from the square of **A D**, and the root of the remaining square will give the sought-after line **B D**.

...in the whole <term>phonocamptic: the science of reflected sound</term> system, the angle of incidence is always equal to the angle of reflection; with any side known along with an angle in the phonic triangle, the remaining parts will be investigated by the exact same method we prescribed. Therefore, whether the reflection occurs in regular or irregular bodies, the same ratio of dimension must always be observed, which I wished to indicate briefly in this place, lest anything seem to be lacking from our phonocamptic art.

# SECTION III.
## THE CONSTRUCTION OF ACOUSTIC TUBES AND TRUMPETS.

->*******************<-

### *PREFACE.*

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'A'. The interior of the letter contains a landscape scene featuring a classical building with a dome or rounded roof, set among trees and rolling hills under a sky with light clouds.</image-desc>

We call those "acoustic instruments" by which we represent remote sounds to the ears—sounds otherwise hidden from the sense of hearing. Just as with optical instruments <note>original: "opticis instrumentis"</note> we bring remote objects that are entirely inaccessible to the sense of sight before the eyes as if they were nearby and close (about which we have treated extensively in the last book of the *Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: "Arte magna Lucis & Umbræ"</note>), so with acoustic instruments, sounds are narrowed and channeled <note>original: "coarctatos"</note> within organs manufactured with wondrous industry and skill according to nature’s own model.

This section contains, *First*, the construction of Halls, Private Rooms, and Porticos disposed with such ingenuity that within them, at a certain and determined point, Princes <note>referring to rulers or patrons</note> may be able to hear every utterance of words, no matter how whispered. *Second*, it covers the construction of acoustic instruments for the use of the hard of hearing <note>original: "surdastrorum"</note>, together with *Acoustic Cryptology* <note>The study of secret communication using sound</note>, or the hidden communication of mutual concepts through sounds, and other new and most secret inventions. But before we approach the matter, we have thought it necessary to set forth the properties and experiments of the Tubes and Trumpets themselves, which we shall use in the architecture of the work.

THE MANUFACTURE OF TUBES AND ACOUSTIC TRUMPETS.

# CHAPTER I.
## ON TRUMPETS AND THEIR PROPERTIES.

Sacred Scripture testifies in many places that the trumpet <note>original: "Tubam"</note> is one of the most ancient instruments; for we read in *Numbers 10* that Moses made two out of silver by the command of God, and the *Book of Joshua* also mentions its use. The *First Book of Kings* <note>In the Vulgate, 1 Kings often refers to what modern Bibles call 1 Samuel</note> shows that the trumpet was also used before the Ark of the Covenant. But we have already dealt with these matters in our *Treatise on the Musical Instruments of the Ancients*. In modern times, trumpets are divided into various classes; some provide all the variations of sound through the skill of the trumpeter alone, using both the stroke of the tongue and forceful blowing, as the following **figure** shows.

<margin>The ordinary trumpet.</margin>
<image-desc>An engraving of a natural trumpet (Tuba ordinaria) showing its long tube coiled in a single loop ending in a wide flared bell.</image-desc>

Others, which are called "sliding" <note>original: "ductiles"</note>, are constructed so that one tube can be moved tightly within another. In this kind of trumpet, the diversity of sounds arises not so much from the breath and tongue as from the lengthening and shortening—or, what is the same thing, the pulling out or pushing in of the lower coil. But let us first examine the properties of the first type, and then the second.

Among other hidden qualities, the trumpet possesses this one, confirmed by the experience of all trumpeters: that the ascent of sounds cannot happen by steps <note>original: "tonatim", meaning a diatonic scale of whole tones</note>. That is, it is impossible for the first note, for example, to be followed by the notes RE and MI <note>D and E in the hexachord system</note>. Instead, the second note will always and unfailingly be the Octave, the third will be a Fifth, the fourth a Fourth, and so on, as the following table shows.

<image-desc>Two musical staves illustrating the harmonic series of a trumpet. The first staff shows notes with numerical labels above: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 12, 15, 16. The second staff shows a more continuous sequence of notes numbered 1 through 13. Below the second staff, labels indicate the intervals between the notes: Octave, Fifth, Fourth, Major Third, Minor Third, Quarter Tone, Major Tone, Minor Tone, Major Tone, Minor Tone.</image-desc>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter I regarding the natural properties of the trumpet.
