# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III.

<margin>The Remarkable Property of the Trumpet.</margin>
In the preceding table you see numbers; the further they recede from unity [the number 1], the more imperfect the consonances they produce. The trumpet follows this natural progression of numbers. For the first tone—for example, *C sol fa ut*—starting out, it gives what is essentially a unison. But for the second tone, which is signified by the number 2, it does not sound a whole tone, but rather that consonance which the two numbers in a 1 to 2 ratio express: namely, the **Octave**. For the third tone, it does not sound a major third <note>original: "ditonum"</note>, but that consonance which the number 3 holds in relation to 2: namely, the **Fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note>, and 3 to 1 (the first note) a **Twelfth**. In the same way, the fourth tone produces a **Fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> in relation to the 3rd, and a **Double Octave** <note>original: "disdiapason"</note> in relation to the 1st. Thus, the fifth tone will give a **Major Third** in relation to the 4th, and the sixth will give a **Minor Third** in relation to the 5th. Here, having rejected the number 7 as useless to harmony <note>Kircher is referring to the "septimal" or harmonic seventh, which sounds out of tune to the ears of 17th-century musicians</note>, it seeks another part with a leap. Then, it gradually reaches through whole tones and semitones up to the 29th degree, or **four octaves** <note>original: "tetradiapason"</note>, so that the trumpet possesses nearly all the degrees found on the **Keyboard of a Harpsichord** <note>original: "Clavicymbali Abacus"</note> across four octaves. Indeed, the generation of the voices to be produced by the trumpet is so clearly presented in the current diagram that, beyond visual inspection, hardly anything else is required; the only difficulty remaining is assigning the cause of such great leaps.

I say, therefore, first: it follows from the nature of the form and construction of the trumpet that the air must necessarily move twice as fast for the second sound as it does for the first. Because no other number comes between 1 and 2, the instrument—out of an appetite implanted in it by nature to achieve the proper end intended through these numbers—will necessarily sound the Octave. For the third sound, a Fifth necessarily follows, since no other harmonic number can be placed between 2 and 3. Consequently, the excitation of the air for the third tone will relate to the excitation of the air in the second tone as 2 relates to 3; that is, in a "subsesquialter" ratio <note>A mathematical ratio of 2:3, which defines the interval of a perfect fifth</note>. By a similar reasoning, air excited by the breath for the fourth tone will give a Fourth, since by a certain natural consequence, the excitation of the air produced in both tones relates as 3 to 4, which is the ratio that constitutes the Fourth. You see, therefore, how much nature abhors dissonances, such that a trumpet would rather break apart than admit them.

Hence, fleeing the number 7 as if it were an enemy of harmony, it repeats the Octave, its friend, with a certain leap. You see also that the higher the trumpet ascends, it always moves further away from the more perfect consonances

# OF TUBES AND THE CONSTRUCTION OF ACOUSTIC TRUMPETS
<meta>catchword: CHAPTER</meta>

...it recedes from the larger intervals and approaches more perfect ones, until at last it proceeds through mere whole tones and semitones. Thus, the trumpet alone follows the order of nature in numbers and sounds. The reason for this is none other than what I have stated: namely, both the intensity of the trumpeter’s breath and the agitation of the air are proportional to the numbers following one another in their natural order.

From these points it is also clear that the harmony of all things consists in these six numbers <note>Known as the *Senario*, these are the numbers 1 through 6, which Kircher and his contemporaries believed formed the foundation of all musical consonance</note>, as will be discussed more fully in its proper place. However, the reason the trumpet proceeds by whole tones in its highest registers is that the excessive intensity of the breath cannot be increased except by the smallest intervals. For if the intensity were always increased by Octaves, Fifths, and Fourths, the trumpet would necessarily exceed the natural limits of sounds. Since this is against nature, the trumpet gradually advances and propagates its sounds from the largest intervals to smaller and smaller ones, until it rests at the limit prefixed by nature—which is the 29th degree, or according to some, the 32nd. See **MERSENNE** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath often called the "father of acoustics"</note> for a more extensive treatment of these matters *in his book on Universal Harmony.*

---

## INFERENCE.

From this it follows that the trumpet performs the same action in the generation of sounds through addition that a string performs through its division. For just as a string divided in the middle causes one of the two parts to sound an octave <note>original: "diapason"</note> relative to the whole, so the trumpet, by adding a second tone to the first while making the air twice as agitated, will similarly elicit an Octave. Again, just as a fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note> is born through a second bisection of a string, so through the addition of three vibrations in the trumpet, there emerges an octave with a fifth, or a fifth similarly. One should reason no differently regarding the remaining intervals. I have chosen to explain all of these briefly here so that the harmonic consensus of all things might be thoroughly understood. 

Ductile trumpets <note>These are "drawn" or "sliding" trumpets, specifically referring to the trombone family</note> share the same properties as military trumpets, with this exception: by the drawing out and pushing in—or the back-and-forth motion—of the <term>slide</term> <note>original Greek: "hypofalpingis", referring to the telescopic slide mechanism</note>, all the tones can be expressed in order. We mentioned that this is not possible in the former [the fixed military trumpet]. The lengthening and shortening of the slide achieves the same result as the closing or opening of the finger-holes in pipes. Since all these things are clear, we shall by no means dwell on them any longer.

BOOK I OF PHONURGIA. SECTION III.

# CHAPTER II.
## ACOUSTIC EXPERIMENTS.
### EXPERIMENT I.
### CHANNELS OR TUBES WONDERFULLY
#### *propagate sound.*

<margin>
Roman Aqueducts propagate sound species over a great distance.
</margin>
Just as light enclosed in polished containers takes on a maximum increase in intensity, so also does sound when propagated through channels. For I believe that if a cylinder with the capacity of one mile were provided for the entire "sounding sphere" <note>The spherical area over which a sound normally spreads in open air</note>; the sound, which in the open air has a line of action of no more than 24 paces, would propagate its line of action beyond a thousand paces in that cylindrical channel. Experience itself supports my opinion. For the Overseers of the Roman Aqueducts say that within the aqueducts, over a very long distance, the voice of speakers is perceived even at five hundred feet as if they were present, especially if the channel has been remarkably polished. Experience also teaches that a channel of two hundred feet renders even whispered voices audible at either end. Nor should this seem strange to anyone; since the voice, enclosed in channels where it cannot escape, strives to recover through longitudinal propagation what it had lost in the open medium through the diffusion, dispersion, and dissipation of its <term>species</term> <note>In 17th-century physics, "species" were the invisible forms or "images" of sound or light that traveled from an object to the senses</note>. For in these narrow spaces, being repeatedly reflected and multiplied, it acquires a massive increase as well as the most distant boundaries for its propagation. But at this point, I cannot omit a remarkable question, not yet decided by anyone as far as I know, which is as follows:

***

# THE CONSTRUCTION OF TUBES AND ACOUSTIC TRUMPETS.

...from the other end closed most tightly, it [the sound] remains within the said channel so that some days later, when one or the other end is opened, the voices suddenly present themselves to the listener’s ears as if released from a kind of captivity. This notion was so plausible that it gained not only many patrons, but even its most fierce defenders. **WECKER** <note>Johann Jacob Wecker (1528–1586), a Swiss physician known for his "Book of Secrets"</note>, **ALEXIUS** <note>Girolamo Ruscelli, who wrote under the pseudonym Alessio Piemontese, author of another famous collection of "secrets"</note>, and others have attempted to defend this matter—as if it were the most certain and true thing, beyond all doubt—even adding many fabricated stories declaring the usefulness of such a remarkable experiment, defending it with all their might <note>original Latin: "pro aris et focis," literally "for their altars and hearths," a classical idiom for total defense</note>.

Thus it happens that while experience—the mistress of all things—is left unconsulted, we rashly and precipitously subscribe to the fantastical mental stirrings of anyone. In this way, intolerable errors are spread by those substituted in the academic chairs. If they had first taken an experiment of this matter, or had properly understood the nature of sound, they would never have fallen into such a shameful opinion. I say, therefore, that water will sooner be drawn with a sieve than a voice can be enclosed in channels; and I conclude the <term>impossibility</term> <note>original Greek: ἀδυναμίαν (adynamian)</note> of the matter by this argument.

For either the voice is carried through the channel by <term>real species</term> <note>In 17th-century physics, "real species" refers to the physical agitation of the air</note>, or by <term>intentional species</term> <note>These were the "forms" or "images" of sound that the mind perceives as distinct from the physical medium</note>: neither can be said to be true. Not the first, for the real species of sound are nothing other than the continuous and successive agitation of the air carrying the voice; when this air is enclosed and finds its exit obstructed from both ends, and since it cannot be reflected because the air left behind refuses to yield, it follows necessarily that in a single moment all agitation of the air will cease, and with it, the sonorous motion. And thus, he who would attempt to enclose sound within a channel would be doing the same as one who believed he could draw water with a sieve.

Suppose, for example: a channel A-B of 400 feet, which is of such length as suffices for the forming of a three-syllable Echo. While the person occupying station A whispers a voice, for instance: *Sing!* <note>original Latin: "Cantate"</note>, let the other at station B, at the signal of the uttered voice <margin>Explanation of the false experiment.</margin>, close the end B as quickly as possible. Now, the sealing must occur while the species of sound are still lingering within the channel (hence they require a very long channel). They say, therefore, that the voice concluded in this way can remain for a long time, and when the channel is finally opened, it presents itself to the ears like a prisoner—not unlike how a direct or reflected voice can remain in an open medium without any dependence on the person who produced it <margin>Refutation.</margin>. O stupid mechanism! These impostors do not see the manifest contradiction of their own opinion, for it would clearly follow from this that the voice both *is* and *is not*. For the voice exists and remains as

### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III.

the motion of the air lasts for a long time, but when it ceases, the sound itself also ceases. For every sound depends essentially upon the motion of the air as its vehicle—as was stated in *Book 1 of our Work on Music* <note>original: "Musurgia nostra," referring to Kircher's own encyclopedic work, *Musurgia Universalis* (1650)</note>. Therefore, if both ends A and B of the tube are closed, the sounding air will either continue to move or it will come to rest. I demonstrate that it certainly cannot move as follows: Suppose a voice is propagated in the section C D, with both ends now closed; I say that all motion will cease at once, since neither the air in front, C A, nor the air behind, D B, can yield in any way, nor can any reflection occur by any means,

<image-desc>A horizontal diagram of an acoustic tube. The left end is marked A, the right end B. Two internal points are marked C and D. In the space between C and D, the word "Cantate" (Latin for "Sing!") is written, representing the sound trapped within that segment.</image-desc>

since in every reflection the air initially set in motion is pushed backward by the reflection itself. Here, however, the air B D cannot be pushed back because of the compression of the forward air A C, which is unable to yield. In the very moment, therefore, that both ends are closed, all the air comes to rest, and along with it, the propagation of the voice. 

But they will say that the <term>sonorous species</term> <note>In 17th-century physics, "species" were the forms or qualities of an object (like its sound or color) that traveled through the air to reach our senses.</note> remain there motionless. Even if I were to grant—though I do not concede it—that the species remain there, I still say that once the opening B is unblocked, the stagnant species could not possibly exit, since there would be nothing to propel them. For sound, as has been said, essentially depends on motion; when the motion ceases, the sound itself is destroyed. Nor is it the same logic as a voice persisting in an open medium without depending on its source <note>Kircher is referring to how a sound travels away from a speaker even after they stop talking.</note>, for in such a medium the air does not rest at all, but the sounding air remains in continuous motion, as the nature of an **Echo** clearly demonstrates. Therefore, real sonorous species cannot be permanently enclosed within a channel, *which was the first thing to be proven*.

<margin>Intentional species of sound cannot be enclosed.</margin>
Nor can <term>intentional species</term> <note>While "real species" were thought to be physical disturbances, "intentional species" were the more subtle, almost spiritual versions of sound that interact with the mind.</note> be enclosed; for since they possess an entity close to the spiritual and incorporeal, no obstacles can be so great as to block them. Daily experience teaches us that sound can pass even through the thickest walls. It is therefore ridiculous, not to say foolish, to wish to restrain sound with barriers, since sound enclosed in a channel passes through a barrier as easily as it passes through a window or a wall. You see, then, from this discourse of ours, how contrary to reason and how discordant are the arguments of these impostors. Let this fable, which has held the academic chairs until now—this fiction about enclosing sound in channels—be abolished. Investigators of the truth should not accept anything unless it has first been weighed on the scales of reason...

# ON TUBES AND THE MANUFACTURE OF ACOUSTIC TRUMPETS
### I
<meta>catchword: COROLLARY</meta>

...learn to trust those things which have been most carefully weighed. But having set these things forth, let us now return to our proposal. *Let it be thus:*

## EXPERIMENT II.

### THE VOICE IS WONDERFULLY *propagated* THROUGH LONG BEAMS.

<margin>
What might be the reason that a scraping sound produced at one end of a long beam is perceived so easily at the other end?
</margin>
This is a wonderful, though nearly common, experiment regarding the propagation of sound in long beams, and it is performed in this way: let there be a beam A–B, two hundred feet long. I say that at either end, A or B, no noise can be made so small that it is not perceived at the <margin>A</margin> other end as if it were present right there. The cause of this phenomenon is sought; for it seems a paradox to many that a voice, which would be imperceptible across a gap of 200 feet of open air, is suddenly perceived when a beam is placed between the points. But anyone who has properly understood what we said concerning the nature of sound in the first book of our *Musurgia* <note>original: "Musurgia nostra," referring to his work *Musurgia Universalis* (1650)</note> will find no difficulty here. 

To explain the effect of this experiment more exactly, two things must be noted: *first*, the efficient cause of sound, which we have said is nothing other than a certain "tremor" <note>Kircher uses "tremor" to mean what we now call vibration.</note> of bodies striking together; *second*, the material object of sound, which is the air. Since a wooden beam is exceedingly porous, it is also highly suited for the propagation of sound and is most receptive to vibration. Thus, a ear placed at end A will easily perceive a scraping sound excited by even the slight friction of a finger at the other end B. This is because the entire beam is considered to vibrate when it is excited to a scraping sound by the motion of a single sounding body. Consequently, it communicates the <term>sonorous species</term> <note>Forms or qualities of a sound that travel to the senses.</note> partly to the internal air <note>The air trapped inside the pores of the wood.</note> and partly to the vibrating external surrounding air. 

It is necessary, therefore, for the ear to perceive the continued vibration of the air, which is the vehicle of the voice, at the end A. The experiments themselves testify to the truth of this matter: for if there should be a continuous break in the beam anywhere, or if you bind it tightly with a band, or if you apply a board to the other end, you will now perceive nothing at all. This is because the sonorous vibration, now being discontinuous, is <margin>B</margin> unable to reach the air—just as a bell tied with a rope is deprived of its proper sound because the vibration is stopped.

<summary>Kircher explains why sound travels better through a solid wooden beam than through open air, identifying vibration (tremor) and the porous nature of wood as the keys to this acoustic phenomenon.</summary>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III.

## COROLLARY.

And from this it is clear why the experiment succeeds less well if the entire beam rests upon the ground or is enclosed in a wall; for it is prevented from vibrating by the earth and the surrounding wall. Therefore, the experiment will succeed best with a beam lightly placed on two supports, and even better if it could be suspended in the open air. Whether a similar success is obtained in bronze or metallic beams is not yet certain; I do not doubt, however, that the same thing happens in every sonorous body, even glass. For the more vibrating <note>original: "tremula," referring to the capacity for vibration</note>, polished, clean, and porous the bodies are, the better effect they will achieve. Since the whole logic of this experiment depends, as we said, on the vibration of the long body, strings themselves testify that sound is propagated better along the length of a body than across its width. Hence thicker walls, because they cannot easily vibrate, lack the aforementioned effect.

## EXPERIMENT III.

### THE VOICE IS PROPAGATED BETTER THROUGH CIRCULAR TUBES AND IS MORE VEHEMENTLY INTENSIFIED THAN THROUGH STRAIGHT ONES.

Daily experience teaches that sound is intensified more vehemently in a twisted tube than in a straight one. This is shown primarily by trumpets, and notably by the horn of **Alexander the Great**, with which he used to assemble his entire army; it was twisted into a circle, as was stated in the *History of Prodigious Sounds* <note>Kircher likely refers to a section within this work or his earlier research on historical acoustic wonders</note>. The cause of this matter is sought. *It should be noted, therefore*, that just as the multiplication of light and heat occurs through the multiple reflection of light—as we taught in *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>referring to Kircher’s 1646 work *Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae*</note>—so the vehemence of sound occurs through the multiple reflection of sound within a circular hollow. For in a straight tube, the propagation of sound is merely compressed and gathered into one; but in a circular one, it is not only gathered, but is also greatly increased and intensified from a certain infinite reflection of "sound lines" <note>Kircher conceptualizes sound as traveling in "rays" or lines similar to light</note>.

Suppose there is a tube A V. curved into an arc; I say that the sound is intensified more in it than in a straight tube. For since, besides the collection of sound, a multiple reflection of the same occurs, a vehement intensification also arises therefrom. Thus you see the sound ray reflected from A into B, from B into C, and hence into D, from D into E, and so into other and further points by a multiple-

<summary>Kircher explains that sound travels best through objects free to vibrate, like suspended beams, and argues that curved tubes amplify sound better than straight ones by reflecting sound "rays" multiple times.</summary>

The previous page ended by stating that sound can be multiplied and intensified through specific geometric arrangements. This page details the specific mechanism of reflection (rebound) that occurs within a tapering, twisted acoustic trumpet.</meta>

# THE CONSTRUCTION OF TUBES AND ACOUSTIC TRUMPETS. 67

...and can be multiplied and intensified; thus the ray X is reflected into Y, and from there into Z, and Z into α <note>alpha</note>, and from there into β <note>beta</note>, following another series of reflections. Since truly infinite sound lines <note>original: "lineae sonorae," analogous to rays of light</note> enter the mouth of the tube AX, each will observe its own laws of reflection. Thus, because there is an infinite accumulation of sound lines, from their reflection a "polyphonism" <note>original: "polyphonismus"; here Kircher means the multiplication or reinforcement of sound through overlapping reflections</note> will necessarily be born—all the more intense as the sound is more compressed toward point V. Conversely, one sounding the tube AXV through the small opening V will be heard at a much greater distance, as the sound becomes more vehement from the multitude of reflections. This happens very little in a straight tube, for in such a tube, as has been said, the propagation of sounds occurs only through collection and narrowing, without such great reflection.

<image-desc>A diagram illustrating sound reflection within a curved acoustic tube (Tuba Acustica). The tube originates at a wide opening marked A-X and tapers as it follows a curved, spiral-like trajectory toward a narrow opening marked V. Straight lines represent sound rays reflecting off the inner walls at points labeled with capital letters (B, Y, Z, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, L, M, N, O, P, Q, R, S) and Greek letters (α, β, γ, δ, ε, ζ, η, θ, ι, κ, λ, μ, ν, ξ), demonstrating how the density of reflections increases as the tube narrows.</image-desc>

# COROLLARY

<margin>A conical channel intensifies the voice more than a cylindrical one.</margin>
From this it is clear that a Conical Tube intensifies the voice more than a cylindrical tube, for the former is more suited for reflecting the sound species <note>original: "species sonoris"; in 17th-century science, "species" were the forms or images of a sensation traveling through a medium</note> than the latter. Hence, if you twist both a conical and a cylindrical tube into a circle, the conical one twisted into a circle will be more powerful than the cylindrical one twisted into a circle; for as the circular conical tube goes from wide to narrow, the narrowed and compressed sound species will be vehemently intensified, just as wind constricted in narrow places gains greater force. In the cylindrical tube...

<meta>catchword: truly (original: verò)</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains that sound is amplified in a curved, conical tube because the sound rays undergo infinite reflections that "compress" the sound, a phenomenon he calls polyphonism. He concludes that tapering (conical) tubes are far more effective than straight or cylindrical ones for long-distance communication.</summary>

# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III. 68
I
<meta>catchword: Sit</meta>

...whereas in a circular [tube] there is an equal crowding of sound <note>The OCR reads "foni," a common error for "soni" (sound)</note> everywhere. Hence the horn of *Alexander the Great* was constructed in such a way that it expanded from a narrow opening into a wide one. But concerning this, see what follows.

### EXPERIMENT IV.
#### *SOUND PROPAGATED ALONG CIRCULAR SURFACES acquires immense power.*

Experience has many times taught me in various buildings that sound propagated along circular surfaces is most intense. There is in *Heidelberg*, *Germany*, a certain very large round tower, built with such ingenuity that two people standing diametrically opposite one another can speak whatever they wish, even in the lowest whispers. I hear there is a similar Hall in the *Palace of the Duke of Mantua*; I have also experienced the same thing in the arches of great bridges. In the dome of *St. Peter’s* here in *Rome*, I have also conducted an experiment on this matter with remarkable success. In the interior of the dome, there is a cornice of such great width that a carriage could easily travel along it without danger. In the opposite diameter locations of this encircling cornice—a distance of nearly a hundred geometric feet—two people facing each other speak to one another in even the lowest voices, notwithstanding the noise of crowds and musicians making a din. In the other points along the wall, however, nothing is perceived. There is no doubt that the same thing happens in all circular structures. To reveal the cause of this, it must be known that sound has this particular quality over light: it is propagated according to the movement of the air. Therefore, if the movement of the air is in a straight line, the sound will also be propagated in a straight line; if it is in a circle, the sound will also be propagated along the circle; and the same must be said for other surfaces. <margin>Sound carried through circular surfaces is most perceptible.</margin> Furthermore, since sound diffused spherically within a circular surface seeks an escape <note>original: "meditetur fugam"; literally "meditates flight"</note> but finds no way out due to the obstructing walls, it is constrained along the circular surface. Eventually, it meets the vocal air from the other side of the wall at the diametrically opposite point, reaching there at the same moment in time, where it is vehemently intensified. From this kind of conjunction and the meeting of species <note>In Kircher's time, "species" referred to the sensible form of an object, like sound or light, as it traveled through a medium</note>, that great intensity of the carried voice is born. Thus it happens that this <term>Phonismus</term> <note>A term Kircher uses to describe a specific acoustic phenomenon or sound effect</note> occurs nowhere else but in those opposite locations. Truly, we have decided that this matter must be demonstrated more deeply.

<summary>Kircher describes the "whispering gallery" effect, where circular architecture like the dome of St. Peter's allows low whispers to be heard across vast distances. He explains this as sound being constrained by circular walls and focusing at a single opposite point.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, whispering gallery, circular surfaces, St. Peter's Basilica, Heidelberg, sound propagation, reflection, Alexander the Great, Phonismus</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of Experiment IV regarding sound reflection in circular spaces. The previous page established that sound travels in equal time along equal arcs; here, Kircher explains why sound is muffled at non-opposite points and why spherical domes are the most efficient acoustic amplifiers.</meta>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III.

will be perceived. For since the circumference of *A C* is an unequal part compared to the path *A D B C*—the former being the shortest and the latter the longest—it follows that the voice carried from *A* to *C* would fly away too quickly, while the voice carried from *A* through *D* and *B* to *C* would arrive too slowly. From this it happens that the union of voices and sound-paths <term>phonismorum</term> <note>original: "phonismorum" — Kircher's term for the specific paths or "rays" of sound waves</note> cannot be achieved at that spot, and consequently, nothing is audible there. Therefore, since all the arcs measured in this way are unequal and are traversed by the voice in unequal times, no intensification of the voice can occur anywhere except at the opposite points *A, C,* and *B*, where the motion of equal duration will perfectly correspond to the equality of the lines.

## COROLLARY I.

From this it follows, first, that a spherical vault <term>Fornicem sphæricam</term>—such as the dome <term>tholus</term> <note>original: "tholus" — a circular building or the cupola of a dome</note> of a choir or a cupola—is most suitable for this purpose. Let there be a spherical hemisphere *A B C D*, or a cornice <term>coronis</term>. Let there be two diametrically opposite locations *A* and *B*. I maintain that voices uttered even softly at *A* will be perceived at *B*. For since all lines from *A* to *C* are semicircles, according to the preceding principles, the voice carried through innumerable semicircles will eventually be gathered at *C* <note>The text alternates between B and C as the focal point; here it treats C as the "pole" where all sound-paths converge</note>, where it is intensely amplified as if all the semicircles were united at a pole.

## COROLLARY II.

From this it follows, second, that this intensification of sound-paths at the opposite points of a semicircular dome, or a very flat and polished hemispherical cupola, will be twice as great, since all the sound lines in the entire hemisphere meet at *C* at the same time.

## COROLLARY III.

Thirdly, it is evident from this that in the opposite poles of a sphere with a diameter of 90 geometric feet <term>pedum Geometricorum</term> <note>A "geometric foot" was a standard of measurement used by Kircher, roughly equivalent to 12.1 English inches</note>, this increase of sound-paths will be truly prodigious, since the semicircular lines of the entire spherical surface converge at the opposite pole in exactly equal time.

# EXPERIMENT V.

## THE VOICE IN SHELLS, OR A CONICAL TUBE TWISTED ACCORDING TO
## *a helical or spiral line; just as it is the most fitting of all bodies,*
## *so too does it obtain the greatest power.*

This experiment is indeed most worthy of observation: that sound within a conically-shaped tube twisted into a spiral—of the kind found in shells—is increased in a marvelous way beyond all other bodies mentioned so far. I would never have believed it had experience not more than once made me certain of it. In this genre, I have seen nothing more miraculous than the **Cave of Dionysius the Tyrant** <note>original: "Antro Dionysii Tyranni" — a famous limestone cave in Syracuse, Sicily, shaped like a human ear and known for its remarkable acoustics</note>, constructed in the shape of an inner ear, which still survives in **Syracuse**, **Sicily**; see the description of it in my *History of Prodigious Sounds*. Certainly, we have shown in *Book 1 of Musurgia, in the chapter on the Anatomy of the Ears of all kinds of animals*, that the most wise Nature chose this spiral tube above all other shapes for the architecture of the ear. We showed that the more certain animals possess a more perfect sense of hearing, the more Nature has granted them a more perfect and enduring spiral, as is most clearly seen in the ears of pigs, hares, mice, dogs, and donkeys. In the construction of these ears, Nature established perfectly helical tubes; for what reason, or by what design of Nature? We have deemed it worthwhile to declare it.

Why, then, does a spiral tube intensify sound more than all others? I assign this cause: because whatever has been said regarding conical and cylindrical tubes—whether straight or twisted—applies most perfectly to this spiral cone. For it not only collects the species of sound <note>The "species" refers to the sensory information or "image" of the sound</note> perfectly and reflects them many times over, but it reflects them in every direction—backward and forward—using mostly orthophonic <note>original: "orthophonâ" — meaning "right-sounding" or reflections that preserve the clarity of the voice</note> reflection. Furthermore, since the passage of the spiral is separated only by a thin diaphragm, by striking the sides, it does not only increase the sound by reflection, but by penetrating into the other passage, it wonderfully intensifies the <term>phonisms</term> <note>original: "phonismos" — Kircher's term for the specific paths or "rays" of sound waves</note>. But so that these matters may be better understood:

Let there be a conical tube twisted into a spiral helix **A M Q C.** I say that the voice is intensified in it more than in all other hollow bodies. Let the voice be at **A**, from which the voice is propagated within the hollow of the tube. Since the inner chambers of the tube are arranged with an irregular curvature, it happens that a certain "star-like" reflection is perpetually born; the further this penetrates into the interior, it always becomes more sharply and

<summary>Kircher argues that spiral-shaped (helical) tubes are the most effective acoustic amplifiers, citing the Cave of Dionysius and the anatomy of animal ears as evidence. He explains that the spiral shape creates multiple "star-like" reflections that intensify sound more than straight or simple curved tubes.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, spiral tubes, helix, Ear of Dionysius, animal anatomy, sound reflection, Musurgia Universalis, Syracuse, phonisms</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Experiment V regarding the acoustic properties of spiral or helical tubes, using a geometric diagram to explain how sound waves intensify as they travel toward the center of a "cochlea" or shell-like shape.</meta>

<header>PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION III.</header>

...will reflect more sharply, until finally, in a right-sounding reflection <note>original: "reflexione orthophona" — Kircher uses "orthophonic" to describe reflections that perfectly reinforce the sound rather than scattering it</note> it produces the most intense sound. Thus you see the sound line starting from point **A** striking **E**, and from there reflecting to **F**, and hence to **G**, **H**, **I**, **K**, and **L**. This line, since it is

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of an acoustic spiral or "cochlea" illustrating sound reflection. A point source 'A', depicted with a stylized human face representing the voice, emits sound rays into the wide mouth of the spiral (marked by points V and X). The rays follow various paths (labeled with letters E, F, G, H, I, K, L and M, N, O, P, Q) as they reflect off the internal curved surfaces, eventually converging at the center 'C'. Letters R, S, and T mark points of reflection within the inner whorl where the sound becomes most concentrated.</image-desc>

right-sounding, will greatly intensify the sound as it reflects back upon itself. In the same way, the vocal line starting at **A** and striking **M** will reflect from there into **N**, **O**, **P**, **Q**, and the remaining points in a star-like pattern, until at **R** and **S** it produces a most intense sound through right-sounding reflection once again. Since, however, infinite lines can be drawn from point **A**, each one striking different points on the interior surface, they will reflect more and more sharply until they come to rest in some right-sounding path.

But since the "point of sound" <note>original: "phonicum punctum" — the physical source of the sound, like a mouth</note> at **A** is not a mathematical point <note>an abstract point with no dimensions</note> but a physical one endowed with its own perceptible width, the lines of sound proceeding from **V** and **X** will follow similar laws of reflection. From this it happens that infinite lines, reflecting more and more sharply, leave behind a "right-sounding resonance" <note>original: "orthophonismum"</note> everywhere. From these reflections is born that powerful intensity of sound which we marvel at in tubes of this kind, and which occurs in no other tube. Furthermore, since the tube always decreases from wide to narrow, the construction of the tube will increase the intensity of the sound in a marvelous way beyond the right-sounding resonances within those chambers <note>original: "gurgustia" — literally "huts" or "small rooms," here referring to the spiral segments</note>; the sound will finally be most intense around the orifice **C**. Sound propagated by the same law by which it moved within will be spread outside, increased by infinite resonances <note>original: "phonismis"</note>. You see clearly from these things, therefore, why such a great intensity of voice occurs in tubes of this kind.

But regarding these matters, more material for discussion will be given in the following distinction concerning acoustic instruments.

# SECTION IV.

## ON CONSTRUCTING STRUCTURES FOR THE USE
## and recreation of Princes, through which they may secretly communicate their plans to one another.

### CHAPTER I.

### CONCERNING THE *ECHEA* OR SOUND-SYSTEMS <term>Phonismis: the science or manifestation of sound</term> OF THE CORINTHIAN
### *Theater described by Vitruvius.*

### PRELUDE I.

->*How the "echea" vessels in the Corinthian Theater sounded and by what means they were activated.*<-

<image-desc>The text of this page is overlaid on a faint background illustration showing the architectural details of an ancient theater with tiered arches, likely representing the Corinthian theater discussed in the text.</image-desc>

<margin>An experiment discovered by me</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative initial 'M' featuring a figure, likely a scholar, seated at a desk within an ornate rectangular frame.</image-desc>
There is a great controversy here regarding how these vessels were activated, as many cannot grasp how these harmonic vessels could have produced sound. Some believe the vessels had small hammers attached by iron wires or cords, which sounded when struck against the bells like a pull-cord, not unlike what is done in modern bell-tower organs; **CESARE CESARIANO** <note>An Italian architect and painter (1475–1543), famous for his 1521 commentary on Vitruvius.</note> argues this *in his Commentaries on Vitruvius*. Others judge that the vessels sounded solely by the force and impact of the air stirred by the voices of the actors <note>original: "comœdorum" — specifically the performers in Greek/Roman drama</note>. However, this theory cannot hold, since no voice is so powerful that it would suffice to activate a bell. This is evident in our own bells: if they could sound by the movement of air alone, I would certainly say they would produce a perpetual sound, since they are constantly exposed to the air, and usually in high places where the wind

<meta>catchword: wind</meta>

# BOOK I OF PHONURGIA, SECTION IV.

->A true description of the<-
->*form of the Vitruvian theater*<-

<image-desc>A detailed architectural engraving depicting the interior of a classical Roman theater, identified as the "Vitruvian theater." <margin>
An experiment on the sound of the Echea.
</margin>
...the wind dominates much, yet no experience has taught that even a very powerful wind ever caused a sound of this kind. Indeed, to gain some practical experience of this matter, I arranged for glass bells to be cast, and I carefully positioned them in concave places according to the *Vitruvian* method. I applied voices at every level of intensity, yet no sound was perceived except for a ringing <note>original: "tinnitus"</note>, such as is usually heard in wells and other hollow places. Most people claim, however, that those concave cells and vessels were used only to provide a great resonance for the theater. I do not deny this; but I do not see what benefit was gained by such ingenious industry in proportioning the vessels according to harmonic consonances <term>consonantias: intervals that sound pleasant together</term> without them being directly struck. I am quite unable to discern for what purpose such a careful and subtle distribution of vessels—ordered according to the triple genus of *Music*, the *diatonic*, *chromatic*, and *enharmonic* <note>These are the three types of scales used in ancient Greek music theory</note>—was arranged, since without actual "symphony" <term>symphonia: here meaning the sounding-together of multiple notes</term> all these things would have been useless and of no value. Therefore, it must either be said that they were established only to increase the voices of the actors, or that they had some other use; but it is not likely that the *Echea* were constructed with such great harmonic artifice solely for the sake of greater vocal resonance. Nor can it be said—as was proven a little while ago—that by the voice alone, or by the striking of the air against them, they were stirred into a harmonic symphony.

<meta>catchword: Whence (Unde)</meta>

<meta>This page continues Kircher's analysis of the "echea" (acoustic vessels) in ancient theaters. # ON STRUCTURES FOR THE USE OF PRINCES.

<margin>How the *Echea* vessels were stirred into harmony.</margin>

Hence, BONAVENTURA CAVALIERI <note>original: "BONAVENTURA CAVALLERIUS"</note> in his *Short Treatise on the Burning Mirror* <note>original: "Opusculo de Speculo Ustorio"</note>, thinks that these *Vitruvian* cells, as well as the *Echea* <term>Echea: resonant bronze or ceramic vessels used in ancient theaters to amplify sound</term>, were of a shape that was partly parabolic and partly hyperbolic. In these, the voice, having entered and struck against the surfaces, was increased by a certain infinite multiplication of rays; and to prove this, he brings forward a construction of this theater much different from the one we read described by VITRUVIUS. For he imagines parabolic vaults stretching from the place of the voice all the way to the cells, in which the voice—diffused as if from the center of the parabolic vaults and multiplied infinitely—produced a marvelous effect, both in the intensity of the voice and in the stimulation of the *Echea*. But since, as I have said, he imagines a new theater in this way, about which nothing similar is read in VITRUVIUS, he does not seem to have satisfied architects, since by straying from the text of VITRUVIUS anyone could easily devise new *Echea* constructions. The question is asked, therefore: how did these theaters have such great resonance, and how were the vessels arranged to produce such a brilliant effect as historians report?

It should be noted, therefore, that a twofold harmonic sound can be considered in this type of *Echea*. The first occurred through a certain ringing <note>original: "tinnitus"</note>, or harmonic hum <note>original: "bombum harmonicum"</note>, while the external voices entering the cells and being struck by various agitations finally—with the sound multiplied and a certain force created—returned that ringing in the *Echea* vessels which the vibration of the vessels exhibited. The second could be achieved by little hammers <note>original: "malleolos"</note> operated by cords and struck against the *Echea*. The use of the first seems to have been so that the voices of the actors, received into those cavities and multiplied there, would acquire greater resonance, as has been said; and all commentators on VITRUVIUS agree with me on this. This was indeed nothing other than a certain symphonic ringing, caused by the vibration of the *Echea* distributed in various harmonic proportions. The second use could have been during preludes or interludes. For, in order to excite the audience and sharpen their expectation of things to follow before the actors entered the theater, the *Echea* were sounded; but they could not sound unless iron wires, equipped with their own hammers, were led through all the cells. Indeed, with this wire passed through all the cells with singular industry, someone specifically assigned to this task stirred all the vessels into the most perfect harmony. These vessels excited a powerful sound in the cells, perceptible far and wide, and it was all the more admired because the music-making machinery <note>original: "machinatio musurgiaca"</note> was

<sig>K 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: hidden [occul-]</meta>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher critiques Cavalieri's parabolic theories of theater acoustics and proposes that ancient Roman theaters used a combination of natural vocal resonance and mechanical hammers to activate acoustic vessels.</summary>
<keywords>Vitruvius, Bonaventura Cavalieri, Echea, theater acoustics, resonance, parabolic vaults, harmonic proportions, mechanical music</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues Kircher's argument that the "echea" (acoustic vessels) in ancient theaters were not merely passive resonators, but were likely activated by hidden mechanical means (strings or mallets) to produce a specific musical harmony, as the human voice alone would be insufficient to cause such a powerful effect.</meta>

->### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION IV.<-

more hidden; and VITRUVIUS himself suggests this was the chief use in these words: *He produced an increased clarity and a harmony suited to itself by its musical concord.* <note>original Latin: "Excitavit auctam claritatem & concentu convenientem sibi consonantiam."</note> The concord, therefore, was of vessels arranged according to musical harmonies; but as was said above, such a powerful concord cannot be produced by the striking of the voice alone. Thus, for the reason mentioned, they sounded by being stirred into harmony through hidden strings <note>Kircher suggests a "mechanical" theory of acoustics here, where strings linked the vessels.</note>, which CÆSAR CÆSARIANUS <note>An Italian architect and the first to translate Vitruvius into a modern language (1521).</note>, cited above, also suspected. And unless it happened by this means, I do not see what other way there could have been by which they were prompted into harmony. For according to the testimony of PLINY, they exhibited thunders and other rumblings by this same artifice. 

However, the fact that VITRUVIUS says nothing openly about these strings and mallets is because he himself never saw such a theater, but only received the account from the Greeks. For this reason, he frequently cites ARISTOXENUS <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle, famous for his writings on music theory.</note>, who is likely the author of this kind of theater, and Vitruvius follows his musical rules while remaining doubtful himself. Nor was there any other theater of this kind in Greece or ROME besides the *Corinthian* one; from which, according to the same VITRUVIUS, we read that PERICLES <note>Kircher's text mentions Pericles here, though historical accounts usually credit Mummius with the removal of the vessels.</note> took away the *Echea* <term>Echea: bronze vessels used for acoustic amplification</term> and hung them in the Temple of Fortune at Rome. And certainly, VITRUVIUS himself seems to have had some objection regarding the construction of this theater from the more skilled architects of his time, which he attempts to propose and solve at once in these words:

<margin>Vitruvius, Book 5, Chapter 5.</margin>
"Perhaps someone will say that many theaters are built in ROME every year, and in them no account of these things is taken. But he is mistaken in this: that all public wooden theaters have many tiers of boards, which must necessarily resonate. Indeed, one may observe this even among Lute-players <note>original: "Cytharædis"</note>, who, when they wish to sing in a higher tone, turn toward the doors of the stage, and thus receive help for the harmony of the voice from them. However, when theaters are built from solid materials—that is, from masonry, stone, and marble—which cannot resonate, then these vessels must be explained by this reasoning. If it is asked in which theater in ROME these things were made? We cannot show one, but we can in the regions of Italy and in several Cities of the Greeks. We also have the authority of L. MUMMIUS <note>The Roman general who destroyed Corinth in 146 BCE and brought its treasures to Rome.</note>, who, after the theater of the *Corinthians* was destroyed, brought its bronze vessels to ROME and dedicated them from the spoils at the Temple of the Moon. Many clever architects, on account of poverty, have achieved very useful effects by choosing clay jars <note>original: "fictilibus doliis"</note> that resonate in this way and arranging them according to this reasoning."

<meta>catchword: Et (And)</meta>

<summary>Kircher concludes his investigation of the ancient "echea" by citing Vitruvius to explain that while wooden theaters resonate naturally, stone theaters required bronze or clay vessels to amplify the actors' voices. He posits that these vessels were likely struck by hidden mechanical strings rather than just vibrating via the voice.</summary>

<keywords>Vitruvius, architectural acoustics, echea, Aristoxenus, L. Mummius, Corinth, bronze vessels, clay resonators, theater design</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues Kircher's critique of Vitruvius's account of theater acoustics. Kircher argues that the "echea" (acoustic vessels) described by the Roman architect could not have worked as simply as described, suggesting Vitruvius lacked deep musical knowledge and merely copied Greek sources without practical testing.</meta>

# ON BUILDINGS FOR THE USE OF SOVEREIGNS.

And indeed, the invention of this theater so pleased <term>Vitruvius</term> <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the 1st-century BC Roman architect whose "De Architectura" is the primary source for ancient building techniques.</note> that for its sake he set forth the foundations of the whole theory of <term>Music</term>, although even he, doubtful in many respects, did not foresee the difficulties that arise. For if he had possessed a perfect knowledge of the science of <term>Music</term>, he would never have used the <term>Enharmonic</term> genus <note>A system of tuning in ancient Greek music that used very small intervals, including quarter-tones.</note> when describing the <term>Echea</term> <note>Acoustic resonance jars, usually made of bronze, placed in theaters to amplify the voice.</note> of a smaller theater; since this is useless for producing harmony because of the sounds of various mixtures entering into the bronze vessels, as we have proved at length in the seventh book of our *Musurgia* <note>Kircher's own massive encyclopedia of music, "Musurgia Universalis" (1650).</note>. Nor could it have been possible for those bronze vessels to be cast so exactly that they would distinctly exhibit a <term>diesis</term> and a <term>diesis</term> <note>Small microtonal intervals; Kircher argues such precision was impossible for 17th-century (and ancient) metallurgy.</note> in the tiniest intervals. Therefore, he ought more wisely to have applied the <term>Diatonic</term> genus <note>The standard musical scale consisting of whole tones and semitones, familiar to modern ears.</note> as the most suitable for this task; for since he employs the <term>Diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth.</note> and <term>Diapente</term> <note>A perfect fifth.</note> in the Enharmonic genus, what else is he describing but the diatonic?

From this it is clear how many errors lie hidden even in the monuments of the greatest authors, which, if they were closely examined, even in the suns themselves unsightly spots would be detected. Hence I conclude that it is likely <term>Vitruvius</term> presented the construction of such a theater to posterity simply as described from some manuscript of <term>Aristoxenus</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle, whose work on music theory Vitruvius frequently cited.</note>; since many things occur in the said description of the three musical genera which cannot agree with musical rules.

But I hear some people grumbling that the Greeks had many wonderful things, the reasoning of which has escaped posterity, and that the *Vitruvian Theater* was one such thing, in which the <term>Echea</term> were adapted with such diligence and a certain incomprehensible art that they were excited by the voice alone. Truly, since such a thing is contrary to all experience, no wise man should argue about it, unless perhaps they persuade us that some hidden intelligence stirred them at the sound of the voice, which would be ridiculous, not to say foolish, to assert. For it is characteristic of admirers of such Greek matters to fashion dreams for themselves, and to devise whatever they please to establish their opinions, even those dissonant with reason and experience. Yet I would not deny, as was said above, that some symphonic ringing was perceived from the recitation of the actors, just as experience above has taught us. But it was by no means sufficient to achieve the end intended by the ancients. Some, noticing in the surviving remains of the <term>Roman Hippodrome</term> <note>Specifically the Circus Maximus or other large arenas in Rome.</note> certain earthenware vessels inserted into the bowels of the walls, in

<meta>catchword: it</meta>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher critiques Vitruvius's descriptions of ancient theater acoustics, arguing that the musical scales Vitruvius proposed (the Enharmonic genus) were practically impossible to achieve with bronze resonance jars. He suggests that Vitruvius likely copied these "wondrous" accounts from Greek sources without understanding the physics or music theory involved.</summary>

<keywords>Vitruvius, Aristoxenus, Echea, resonance jars, Enharmonic genus, Diatonic genus, theater acoustics, Musurgia Universalis, Roman Hippodrome, acoustics skepticism</keywords>

Kircher argued that the musical precision Vitruvius claimed—specifically the use of the microtonal enharmonic genus—was practically impossible to achieve in bronze casting and placement. This page transitions from ancient theater theory to a real-world marvel of the 17th century: the Villa Simonetta.</meta>

->PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION IV.<-

...they arrived at this opinion: that vessels of this kind were arranged there for no other reason than to increase the resonance of the theater. But they are greatly mistaken, since the aforementioned <term>Hippodrome</term> <note>original: "Hyppodromus"; a stadium designed for horse and chariot racing.</note> had neither the form of a theater, nor—especially given the noise of running horses and clamoring men—did it require resonance. Therefore, I say that those earthenware vessels were inserted to lighten the weight of the walls, just as we still see being done today to lighten the weight in <term>tufa</term> <note>original: "tophaceis"; a porous volcanic rock commonly used in Italian architecture for vaulting because of its light weight.</note> vaults. And these are the things that occurred to us to say concerning the theater of <term>Vitruvius</term>, but let us now turn our pen to other matters.

# CHAPTER II.

## *CONCERNING THE WONDERFUL ECHO OF VILLA*
## *Simonetta in Milan.*

Located about one mile from **Milan**, outside the gate called the **Porta degli Ortolani** <note>The Gate of the Gardeners.</note>, is that most celebrated villa, commonly known as **Simonetta**, named after the Counts of that name who possess it. In this villa, **Ferrante Gonzaga** <note>Ferdinand I Gonzaga (1507–1557), a powerful general and Governor of Milan under Emperor Charles V.</note>, the Governor of Milan, once erected a building noted not so much for its architectural symmetry as for its particularly marvelous **Echo**. On the highest floor of this building, a certain window stands open (as will be explained shortly), in which they say a spoken voice is multiplied twenty-four times—and depending on the intensity of the voice and sound, even more—indeed, multiplied almost infinitely. When I heard this from several trustworthy Fathers <note>Refers to Kircher's fellow Jesuit priests.</note>, a great desire came over me to investigate the cause of such a manifold reflection. Therefore, I entrusted the task of mapping the entire building and the layout of the place as accurately as possible to Father **Mattheo Storr**, a priest of our **Society** <note>The Society of Jesus (Jesuits).</note>, a man most worthy of trust, and distinguished no less for his varied erudition than for his genuine sincerity. He measured the structure of the whole building in minute detail and recently sent it to me, along with a <term>scenography</term> <note>original: "schoenographia"; an architectural perspective drawing or plan.</note> of the same and other circumstances concerning the **Echo**. So that it may be clear what the cause of such a portentous echo might be, let us first place before the reader's eyes the dimensions of the building, explained part by part.

<meta>catchword: Of the whole (Totius)</meta>

<summary>Kircher dismisses the idea that ancient wall vessels were always for acoustics, arguing they were often structural, before introducing his investigation into the famous twenty-four-fold echo at the Villa Simonetta in Milan.</summary>

### CONCERNING BUILDINGS FOR THE USE OF PRINCES.

<margin>
Description of the Villa Simonetta, near Milan.

The height of the entire structure, as is evident from the present figure, has two stories separated by a walkway. The lower part boasts peristyles <note>An open colonnade or gallery surrounding a courtyard.</note> built beneath the walkway. **K** is a courtyard paved with stones. The upper story contains three parts especially worthy of consideration: the first is the interior and principal part of the Palace, marked **F L X M**; next are two lateral and parallel buildings, **X M V N** and **G F H L**. The dimensions of these are as follows: the width **F X** (or **L M**) is 62 Milanese paces <note>original: "passuum Mediolanensium"; a local unit of length used in Milan, roughly equivalent to a long step.</note> and four inches. So that we may proceed more precisely in this matter, we have seen fit to include in the figure a fourth part of a pace, or a Milanese cubit <note>original: "ulnæ Mediolanensis"; an "ulna" or ell was a standard measure for cloth or construction.</note>, indicated by the letters **C** and **Z**.

The height **F L** (or **X M**) is 16 paces and 4 inches; the length **M N** (or **H L**) is 33 paces and 3 inches. The width of the walkway **H L** is 8 paces and 6 inches. The window from which the **Echo** is triggered is a single one in the middle of the highest part of the wall **X V M N**, marked with the letters **R** and **S**. Let these suffice as far as dimensions are concerned.

Therefore, that window **R S** is the one and only place for hearing the **Echo**, for it does not resonate elsewhere. Truly, I have received the testimony of many that a voice sent out from there is returned twenty-four times—even thirty times or more—depending on the greater or lesser exertion of the voice. Indeed, I believe that there is no certain and determined number of reflections for the voice, but that it is multiplied infinitely...

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Villa Simonetta's acoustics. While the previous page provided the architectural layout and dimensions, this page moves into the physics of sound reflection, explaining why this specific geometry produces such a famous multiple echo.</meta>

# THE NEW SCIENCE OF SOUND <note>original: "Phonurgia"</note> BOOK I. SECTION IV.

...can be sought, and the same thing happens as in the vibrations of a string, whose strikes appear clearly enough at the beginning, yet in the end they disappear due to the extreme speed, and the sound gradually dies out. This same phenomenon appears in the reflection of mirrors. The question is asked, therefore: what is the cause of this *Echo*? I respond that the cause is the positioning of two parallel buildings situated at a proportionate distance from one another. Furthermore, since the aforementioned walls possess the greatest uniformity and a flatness free from all roughness, the voice is reflected with the greatest ease. I shall demonstrate the matter in this way:

Let **AC** and **BD** in the *figure* placed here be parallel and very flat walls, having a distance proportioned to return a two-syllable voice. I say that a voice emitted from window **E** must necessarily be returned very many times; for the sound-path <note>original: "phonismus"; a term used by Kircher to describe the trajectory or "ray" of a sound wave.</note>, according to *Proposition 1 of Echometry* <note>original: "Echometria"; the science of measuring echoes, a field Kircher pioneered.</note>, having propagated from 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the reflection of sound between two parallel vertical walls, AC on the left and BD on the right. Point E is marked on the right wall, and point I on the left wall. Horizontal lines show sound waves traveling directly between E and I. Other lines represent sound rays originating from E, striking wall AC at points A and C, and reflecting outwards at angles towards points K and N respectively.</image-desc>

**E** to **I**, returns by the same path to **E**; from **E** again to **I**, and from **I** once more to **E**; and thus by infinite alternating repercussions, until the voice, weakened by too much striking, finally fails. This is not unlike a ball which, being violently struck back and forth between two narrow walls, finally fails after five or six rebounds as its momentum ceases. And it is certain that there is no other cause for this phenomenon than what we have stated, because since the angle of incidence is equal to the angle of reflection (according to *Proposition 1 of Echometry*), no *Echo* can occur in any way except through that "normal" <note>In geometry, a "normal" line is one that is perfectly perpendicular to a surface.</note> cylindrical sound-path established between two parallel walls. For if other parts of the wall—for example **A** and **C**—were to reflect the voice, the angles **EAI** and **ICE** would necessarily have to be right angles to return the sound to the source; but according to Book 1, Proposition 15 <note>Likely referring to a principle of geometry or Kircher's earlier acoustic theorems.</note>, these angles are acute and would propagate the voice into other areas, for example through **K** and **N**. Therefore, the multiple reflection of the voice occurs from point **I** and no other place, even though the hollows of the foundations, as well as the Courtyard **K** and the intermediate wall, serve to increase the reflected voice and to [produce] a clearer...

<meta>The catchword "sonum" indicates the next page begins with the word "sound."</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains the physics behind the Villa Simonetta's echo, attributing it to sound waves bouncing perpendicularly between two perfectly smooth, parallel walls, much like a ball bouncing between two barriers.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, echo, Villa Simonetta, reflection, sound-path, phonismus, Echometry, geometry of sound</keywords>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the Villa Simonetta’s acoustics by using the analogy of a well or cistern to explain how sound reflects between two parallel surfaces. It transitions from the specific case study of the villa to general architectural principles (corollaries) for creating similar acoustic effects.</meta>

-># ON BUILDINGS FOR THE USE OF PRINCES<-

...produce a great deal of sound; just as we said concerning the *Vitruvian Theater* <note>Vitruvius was an ancient Roman architect whose writings on theater acoustics—specifically the use of resonant bronze jars—influenced Renaissance scientists like Kircher.</note>.

And this is the reason why many wells and cisterns also produce such a great reflection of the voice. Let **A B C D** be a well, and **D E B** the water. Let the opening of the lid of the well or cistern be **I**, and the ceiling of the cistern be **C A**. I say that the voice introduced through **I** is returned many times for the reason that the sound-path <note>original: "phonismus"; Kircher’s technical term for the trajectory or "ray" of a sound wave.</note>, falling upon the very flat surface of the water **E B**, is reflected from it back to the ceiling of the cistern, and then again to the surface of the water, and this happens so many times until at last, having been weakened, it fades away. The matter needs no further demonstration, as it is self-evident. Thus the cause of such a manifold *Echo* in the *Villa Simonetta* <note>A famous villa near Milan, renowned in the 17th century for an echo that could repeat a single word up to 40 or 50 times.</note> is clear.

<image-desc>A cross-sectional diagram of a well or cistern labeled with points A, B, C, D, E, I, N, and K. Point I is an opening at the top center from which sound rays originate. These rays bounce off the bottom surface (labeled E B, representing water) and reflect up to the ceiling (labeled C A), repeating this zigzag pattern to illustrate the mechanism of multiple echo reflections. Points N and K are located on the left and right interior walls respectively.</image-desc>

## COROLLARY I.

From these things it is established that if anyone were to construct a similar building—that is, two walls separated by a distance of about 63 *paces* <note>original: "passuum"; a Roman pace is approximately 5 feet or 1.5 meters. Kircher is specifying the physical distance required for the human ear to perceive a distinct reflected sound.</note>—he would have a similar *Echo*, since nothing else is required to return a voice distinctly except a proportionate distance, which 63 *paces* will provide. If someone were indeed to make a cistern or well as many paces deep, he would also find a similar *Echo*.

## COROLLARY II.

It is therefore clear that it is not the foundations, nor the courtyard, nor the central palace equipped with many windows, nor the walkways that cause this prodigious *Echo*, but, as I have said, the parallel placement of very flat walls unhindered by any roughness. Nor must the window necessarily be situated at **R S**; for if a window were made in another part of the wall, it is certain that you would have the same *Echo*, and perhaps a window at the central point **I** of the wall would achieve a much greater effect, but let us leave these things to be considered by Architects. And *these* are the things which we wished to describe briefly concerning the *Echo of the Villa Simonetta*.

# PHONURGY, BOOK I. SECTION VI.

## CHAPTER III.
### CONCERNING THE SURVIVING EAR OF DIONYSIUS AT SYRACUSE.

To this day, a most famous structure remains on display in SYRACUSE, *Sicily*, which some call the *Prison* of DIONYSIUS the *Tyrant* <note>Dionysius I (c. 432–367 BCE), a Greek tyrant of Syracuse known for his cruelty and paranoia.</note>; others call it the *Ear*, and others by various names. It was engineered with such ingenuity that someone standing across from the entrance can hear whatever is spoken returned as a perfect and multiplied sound. Having heard much about this place, partly from authors and partly from the reports of others, I undertook an investigation of it in the *Year* 1638 while passing through SYRACUSE, and I finally uncovered its causes. MIRABELLA <note>Vincenzo Mirabella (1570–1624), a Sicilian archaeologist and author of a famous plan of Syracuse.</note> describes this in detail in his *Ichnography of Syracuse* <note>original: "Ichnographia Syracusana"; a technical term for a ground-plan or map.</note>. The structure is situated as follows: the site is outside the walls of the *City of Syracuse*, commonly called the *Prison* of DIONYSIUS. Anyone who carefully considers the craftsmanship and the skill with which it was designed by the *Tyrant* will see that there was no other purpose for this building than that the captives held inside could not even breathe without being overheard by the prison guard. It was constructed by the *Tyrant* in the shape of an ear, modeled after nature, with singular genius and industry.

<margin>The Syracusan Echo.</margin>
It is carved out of the living rock, and by a spiral duct <note>original: "cochleato ductu"; literally "snail-like," referring to the anatomical shape of the inner ear.</note> marked **F F.**, it tapers into a narrow channel that enters the chamber **A.** of the prison guard, which is situated above the cavern. Thus it happened that every sound, even the smallest noise or whisper, entering the spiral work, was channeled into the guard’s chamber. There, anything uttered in a low voice was perceived as if the guard were present. Today, with the wall blocked up, the whispered voices degenerate into a most beautiful and miraculous *Echo*, which is why it is commonly called *The Grotto of Speech* <note>original: "La Grotta della favella"; Kircher provides the Italian name for the site.</note>. Indeed, it does not return voices clearly like other *Echoes*, but it amplifies a low voice into a shout; it presents the sound of clearing one’s throat as a clap of thunder, and the slapping of a cloak with the hand might seem like the explosion of a cannon. Moreover, it does not just intensify the voice, but repeats it several times. For this reason, a musical canon <note>A piece of music where voices sing the same melody starting at different times.</note> sung here by two people soon turns into a harmony of four voices, as the reflected voice of the first beautifully joins the voice of the second—a thing altogether most worthy of being heard. This wonderful architecture has truly taught me much regarding the mechanical production of hidden sounds and a more recondite <note>Hidden or specialized knowledge.</note> doctrine beyond all...

<meta>The text cuts off at "captum," which is the end of the thought "beyond all comprehension" or similar.</meta>

<summary>Kircher describes his 1638 visit to the Ear of Dionysius in Syracuse, explaining how its ear-shaped architecture was designed for acoustic surveillance and its modern transformation into a powerful echo chamber.</summary>

Our Fathers <note>Referring to members of the Society of Jesus (Jesuits) stationed in Syracuse.</note> sent this Ground Plan <term>Ichnographia: a technical term for a floor plan or horizontal section of a building.</term> to ROME, having taken it from the cliff with the greatest care. **A.** The Floor or Ground Plan of the cave. **B.** signifies the inner chamber, which rises to 4 ells and 4 palms; with its opening narrowing toward the ceiling along with the wall to 1 *ell* and 7 palms. **C.** the entrance into the cave or the gate. **D.** the end of the cave, whose height reaches 15 *ells*. **F.** a canal 3 palms wide, carved with the greatest diligence into the ceiling of the cave itself, or the upper rock; this canal is 15 *ells* in height from the floor in Figure 2, and completes its circuit **F F F** in exactly the manner you see drawn here. The Reader should note that it is altogether difficult to provide an exact drawing of this *polyphonic cave* <note>original: "Antri polyphoni"; a cave designed to produce multiple echoes or complex acoustic effects.</note>; since the passage is exceedingly winding, I have wondered not a little with what industry it could be fashioned into this spiral form; in a word, the whole work tastes of *Archimedean* <note>Archimedes (c. 287–212 BC), the legendary mathematician and inventor of Syracuse, to whom Kircher attributes the cave's sophisticated acoustic design.</note> genius. # ON STRUCTURES FOR THE USE OF PRINCES

How much I have progressed through the constant contemplation of these things I have seen, the Reader will perceive more abundantly from what follows.

Here I shall add another miraculous building still standing in **PAVIA** <note>The Latin text uses "Papia," the medieval name for the city of Pavia in Lombardy, Italy.</note> which was constructed with such skill that anyone standing in the doorway perceives an *Echo* that is miraculously prodigious; for to one who asks a question, it seems to respond thirty times over. This is done with such distinct and exact pronunciation of every single syllable that many people have quite simply persuaded themselves that it is not a work of nature, but an illusion created by a demon confined within. Among these people was even **GIROLAMO CARDANO** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a celebrated Italian polymath, physician, and mathematician.</note>, which is indeed surprising in a man so ingenious and by no means unacquainted with the secrets of nature. But let us hear the words of the said **CARDANO** in his *Books on Subtlety, Book 18, On Wonders*, printed in **NUREMBERG**. At the end of the book, he speaks thus:

<margin>The Echo of Pavia.</margin> <note>original: "Echo Ticinenfis"; Ticinum is the ancient Roman name for the city of Pavia.</note>

> *The third kind of phenomenon* <note>original: "Vigiliæ genus"; Cardano uses this to categorize different classes of sensory or natural wonders.</note> *is pure and sincere, in which one may happen to see wonders in many ways: just as when an Echo resonates so greatly multiplied, it happens that sometimes it returns voices seven times. At night this is marvelous in a wonderful way, and unless the thing were common knowledge, it could terrify anyone, so clearly and slowly does it sometimes return the voices. The cause is the air, which is struck back by flat and hollow surfaces; for only caves or walls resonate, and they resonate from old structures, and more so from a distance than from nearby. It is necessary for the voice to be struck in the Echo and reflected by a flat intermediate space. It is very often multiple; we ourselves have heard a triple one.*
>
> *Not only did the ancients know examples of a multiple voice, or one returned seven times: but in **PAVIA**, anciently called **TICINUM**, there was once a great Basilica—the Italians call it a **HALL** <note>original: "SALAM"</note>—which, located near the city’s citadel, returns voices from the doorway so many times that you cannot count them; sometimes a thirteenth voice is heard. Many witnesses were present, and among others, that distinguished Professor of the medical art, **MELCHIOR MALHEUSER**, our German disciple. You would have believed someone was answering you, or mocking you. The voices die away gradually and cease; from which the word **AHIME**—which in Latin sounds like **alas** <note>original: "heu"</note>—is returned in a wonderful way, like the voice of a dying and failing person. This building is square, with several wide windows; it is illuminated in the upper part from the sides. However, the front and the wall placed opposite are solid, except that the doorway is in the front. The length is about one hundred feet, the width nearly 25. It resonates, and the voice is multiplied, if you stand near the other wall opposite the door. And it seems then to be returned from the high part of the door. And so from both sides and especially outside...*

<summary>This page describes a famous acoustic wonder in a building in Pavia, Italy, which could repeat sounds up to thirty times. The author cites the 16th-century polymath Girolamo Cardano to explain how the hall's architecture creates such a persistent and haunting echo.</summary>

<keywords>Pavia, Girolamo Cardano, acoustics, architecture, Echo, Basilica, Ticinum, Melchior Malheuser, sound reflection</keywords>
