### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION IV.

...outside the door itself, the voice resonates for those standing there, but not at all in the middle. For this reason, no one can doubt that it is either a demon or some trickery. For it can hardly be believed to be an *Echo*, so clearly, so promptly, so often, and in such a fixed order are all voices heard. The height, I believe, is half as much again as the width; for this is a lofty Basilica. I have chosen to describe it thus so that men may know where it is appropriate to experience such things, or if they wish to build another such example. I heard from those standing before the door that once there was an archway of a portico placed directly opposite, a large part of which still remains. And the usual voices were returned much better to those standing outside the door under the arch, and much more richly, and with a greater "interest" <note>original: "fænore"; here referring to the "profit" or multiplication of the sound.</note>, so that they sometimes resonated thirty times; it is certain that as the voice is produced more quickly, and with a louder and more excited tone, so it is returned more clearly and more frequently.

<margin>The explanation for the Echo of Pavia.</margin>
However, since not long ago a certain Priest of *our* Society <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits.</note>, by no means unskilled in Music, gave me complete information regarding both the quality of this *Echo* and the layout of the building, I thought the description he sent should be opportunely inserted here, so that the reasoning behind such a famous and celebrated Echo might become more deeply known to the curious Reader. The matter stands thus: The building is in the city of *Ticinum* <note>The ancient Roman name for Pavia.</note>, commonly called <term>Pavia</term>, in the highest part of the city near the Castle or Ducal Palace, commonly called the <term>Salone</term> or <term>Arsenale</term>. It formerly served for public games; now it is a storehouse for military equipment. Its shape is quadrangular; its height is 35 *Roman Braccia* <note>A "braccio" (literally "arm") was a traditional Italian unit of length; a Roman braccio was approximately 67 centimeters.</note>, up to the ceiling; its length is 124 *Roman Braccia*, and its width is 24. It has two doors, one in the front facing *North* <note>original: "Boream"</note>, six *Roman Braccia* in height and 4 in width (though formerly it was 5). In the part opposite the front, namely the southern side, 3 windows are seen, the middle of which is larger than the others; below these, a door is opened of equal size to the Northern door, through which access is made to the Portico indicated by <term>Cardano</term>; it now serves as a workshop. We have briefly set out the description of the entire Building, insofar as it pertains to our purpose; therefore, nothing remains but to explain the reasoning for such a multiple *Echo*. With the Northern and Southern doors open—the latter being about 30 *paces* distant from the city walls—the *Echo* responds eleven times; with the Southern door closed, eight times; and with both closed, it responds only six times to someone positioned in the middle. Of this, indeed, there can be no other cause than the walls...

<summary>Kircher concludes a quote from Girolamo Cardano about a "demonic" thirty-fold echo in Pavia and begins a technical architectural analysis of the building provided by a Jesuit correspondent. He notes how opening or closing the doors changes the number of times the echo repeats.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of the Echo of Pavia (Ticinensis Urbis), comparing its current state with historical accounts and moving into a new chapter discussing similar acoustic phenomena in Mantua and Caprarola.</meta>

# ON STRUCTURES FOR THE USE OF PRINCES.

...outside the gates, there are obstacles which, when the gates are open, reflect the <term>species</term> <note>Kircher uses the term "species" to refer to the "audible form" or "image" of the sound that travels through the air.</note> eleven times in proportion to the distance. However, when the gates are closed, the distance is shorter, and thus there are necessarily fewer reflections. It has been observed that this *Echo* has its greatest power in reflecting two-syllable words, just as was said regarding the *Echo of Milan*. A natural voice is best suited for producing an *Echo* in this location; an artificial sound, such as from a trumpet or drum, confuses the echo because of the sheer violence of the noise. I have shown the reason for this at length in the preceding sections. Nevertheless, it is recorded that if the musical intervals *ut, mi, sol* <note>original: "ut, mi, sol"; these are the three notes of a major triad (C, E, and G).</note> are sounded through a trumpet or the flute commonly called a *Cornetto*, the *Echo* forms a harmony in the manner of an eleven-voiced fugue—a truly wonderful thing to hear. Furthermore, this *Echo* possesses different properties depending on the various positions of those shouting. For it appears one way to those standing in the middle while others shout from the side or the front, and another way to those standing at the gates while others shout from the middle. The reason for this is nothing other than the placement of the opposing walls, where the reflected <term>species</term> of sounds, through either intentional or accidental distance, are struck back multiple times and present various illusions to the ears. Only a person who has either not read or, if they have read, not understood our previous reasoning concerning <term>polyphonisms</term> <note>Technical term for echoes that repeat a sound many times.</note> would be unable to know the cause of all these things. As for why it does not perform those same miracles of the reflected voice today as it did in the time of <term>Cardanus</term> <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a famous Italian polymath.</note> and <term>Blancanus</term> <note>Giuseppe Biancani (1566–1624), a Jesuit astronomer and mathematician.</note>, the reason is that the place is now filled with various military equipment, which hinders the manifold reflection of the voice. A clear proof of this is that the *Echo* is not perceived by those standing on the western side, where such obstacles are seen to be placed; the sound images are scattered and absorbed. These are the things I thought should be said regarding this famous *Echo of the City of Pavia*.

### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION IV.

...a plaster molding in the shape of a semicircle, impressed to the width of nearly a *palm* <note>A "palm" (palmus) was a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of a human hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches.</note>. If someone whispers into one end, the <term>audible species</term> <note>Kircher uses "species" to describe the physical "images" or "forms" of sound that travel through the medium of air.</note> enter the channel and are propagated through it. Since they cannot escape the channel, they necessarily present themselves to the ears of someone standing at the opposite end. This is a simple trick of craftsmanship and can easily be introduced by architects into any vaulted building. *But let us demonstrate the matter in a few words.*

Let a channel be made through the vault of any room in the manner we have shown for describing circular channels in gypsum or lime. Let the channel be described in this way: **A B C**, not enclosed, but consisting only of its own concavity. I say that two people standing at opposite points **A** and **C** will understand each other mutually, even if they speak into the channel in the lowest possible whispers, regardless of the noise of others chattering in the room. The reason is clear: because the sound whispered into the channel is carried entirely to the opposite place without any dispersion of the <term>species</term>. The sound travels united from **A** through **B** to **C**, reaching the ears of the person standing at **C**. Certainly, no one should doubt that this effect will necessarily follow.

<image-desc>A technical diagram of an acoustic archway. A semi-circular structure containing four arched windows is depicted. Along the exterior curve of the main arch is a thin channel labeled with point A at the bottom left, B at the top center, and C at the bottom right. Two human figures are shown at the bases: one at A appears to be whispering into the channel, and one at C appears to be listening to the sound transmitted through it.</image-desc>

<margin>
Echo in the valley of Mount Vesuvius.
</margin>
Not long ago, <term>James Bonvicinus</term> <note>Jacobus Bonvicinus was a distinguished Jesuit mathematician and linguist who corresponded with Kircher.</note>, a renowned mathematician of *our Society* and highly skilled in languages, wrote to me about another marvelous Echo that recently revealed itself in the *Valley of Mount Vesuvius*. This Echo was born from the layout of the site after the last eruption <note>original: "incendium ultimum," likely referring to the major eruption of 1631.</note>, established by the chance industry of the local people. While they were digging various channels necessary for diverting rainwater to prevent the destruction of their vineyards, it happened that as soon as someone standing at the opening of a channel at the top of the mountain spoke, the voice—propagated through the channels and increased by various reflections—presented itself so perfectly to those walking near the channels at the foot of the mountain that you would swear people were speaking right next to you. In this place, through the mere "chatter" of nature, you may frequently hear the conversations of shepherds and human voices most distinctly, even though no one is present or visible in the surrounding areas. This phenomenon has been regarded by many as a miracle. *But let these accounts of marvelous Echoes suffice.*

ON BUILDINGS FOR THE USE OF PRINCES.

# CHAPTER V.
## ON THE WONDERFUL EFFECTS OF PHONURGY *in the Palaces of Princes.*

# PRACTICAL APPLICATION I.
## HOW TO ARRANGE THE HALLS OF PRINCES SO THAT Nothing can be said so quietly that it is not heard, either in the same or in another Room.

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'N' featuring floral patterns and a landscape background with a small building.</image-desc>

<margin>
Conduits have a marvelous power of gathering voices.
</margin>
Some have claimed that this can be done through a hidden system of conduits, as <term>Porta</term> <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615), an Italian scholar and polymath known for his work *Magia Naturalis* (Natural Magic), which included early studies on acoustics.</note> suggested. The idea is that while conduits are led from various rooms into a single location, the Prince, by applying his ears to the opening where all the conduits meet, might perceive the voices from various rooms. If this is understood to mean that any words spoken quietly in the bedrooms would fly through the conduits and present themselves to the Prince's ears, it is false. This is because voices produced in a room are by no means gathered into the opening of a conduit; instead, being variously dispersed and absorbed in the open air before they can reach the conduit, they expire, already weakened. However, if the mouth is applied directly to the conduit and voices are murmured into it, there is no doubt that the Prince will distinctly perceive those whispered voices from each of the rooms. For not only *I* alone, in a lead tube of great length, but also others with me in an earthen one, have heard a voice at a distance of over 500 feet. The tube, however, should not be too wide, nor too narrow; rather, the proper width or diameter for tubes intended to transmit voices should be three inches. For drums and trumpets, however, somewhat larger conduits can be used.

<margin>
A conduit of 500 feet conveys voices.
</margin>

<margin>
Why tubes embedded in a wall convey sound better than those exposed to the open air. The opposite occurs in an unattached beam.
</margin>
When these tubes are embedded in masonry, they convey the voice better than when they are exposed to the open air. There is no other reason for this fact than that those built into the walls do not admit as much noticeable vibration <note>original: "tremorem"</note> as they would if they were free. Vibration greatly hinders the advancement of voices, as it easily confuses them with its own shaking. The exact opposite is found in beams and other solid, oblong bodies; these lose all the power of a whispered voice when embedded in a wall, yet they acquire much greater power to transmit sound the more they are exposed to an open medium, as was demonstrated above. For since the vibration of the whole object stirs up a noise from one end to the other...

<meta>catchword: mo</meta>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher discusses the practical construction of speaking tubes for secret surveillance in palaces, noting that sound travels best when tubes are embedded in walls to prevent vibration. He distinguishes between sound traveling through hollow pipes and sound traveling through solid materials like wooden beams.</summary>

...remains stable, but if a beam is buried in the earth or set into a wall, it loses its freedom to vibrate, and it necessarily loses the ability to propagate a voice along with it.

<margin>
The Author's experiment regarding subterranean channels.
</margin>
And although this matter of conduits may seem very simple, it cannot be denied that many truly paradoxical effects can be achieved through the help of such hidden channels, provided that a clever *Echo-architect* <note>original: *Echo-tectúsque*; Kircher coins this term to describe a specialist in acoustic architecture.</note> uses the necessary skill and ingenuity. A certain chemist’s underground workshop taught me the truth of this; it had no openings or windows, using only a single channel for venting smoke, and was fitted with a very small door. When the door was closed, any voices spoken inside were carried to the far end of the channel and heard perfectly, to the great wonder of the listeners. Having been made wiser by this experiment, I am fully persuaded that something similar could undoubtedly be put into effect in the palaces of Princes. Thus, let the *Echo-architect* begin the work as follows.

Let three chambers <note>original: *receptacula*</note> be made, each 8 *palms* <term>palm</term> <note>A traditional unit of measurement based on the width of a hand; in Kircher's Rome, a "palmo" was approximately 22.3 centimeters or 8.8 inches.</note> long, as shown in the current figure, and just as wide and high. In the illustration, **R X** represents the width of 8 *palms*, and **H V** indicates the height of the same number of palms. Let there be a very small and low door, **A**, which can be closed very tightly. A window of very thick crystal should also be set into the side, walled in so that when voices resonate inside, the sound cannot escape through the window or the door, but instead travels through the conduits **D E**, **Z E**, and **S E**, which [lead] through hidden...

<margin>How an acoustic channel should be routed.</margin>
You shall connect the hidden passages of the house led into room F with the dome <note>original: *tholo*</note> D of the chamber (which, according to the proposed diagram, must be very smooth and shaped like a twisted cone) in such a way that it appears to be one continuous surface. At the other end, the channel should have an opening flared out at E, so that the exiting sound can spread freely, and you will have a finished structure. For if anyone, with the door and window closed, enters chamber A D and utters any voices, their sound—since it cannot escape elsewhere—will be variously agitated and compressed by the conical surface D, eventually entering the tube D E, and emerging there at E, it will present itself to the ears of those situated in room F. *See the illustration of this placed below in section vii, chapter xi.*

## COROLLARY.

From this it is clear that music performed in the receptacle A D will be perfectly represented in room F via the hidden passage D E. If anyone wishes to exhibit various auditory tricks <note>original: *ludibria*; literally "playthings" or "delights," referring here to acoustic illusions.</note>, they will easily accomplish the task with several such receptacles led into F through separate tubes. These separate acoustic receptacles are Z, S, and D, all led through separate channels into the Prince's bedchamber marked with the letter F; see the present figure, where you will see our intention better explained.

# PRACTICE II.

### *TO ARRANGE A STRUCTURE SO THAT SOUND CAN BE*
### *HEARD ONLY IN TWO OPPOSITE PLACES AND NOWHERE ELSE.*

In various palaces of Princes, auditory tricks of this kind are seen, concerning which we have written in the *History of Echoes* <note>original: *Historia Echonica*</note>. Therefore, how any Prince might construct similar structures must now be told.

First, the structure must be round like a dome <note>original: *cupulæ*</note>, such as the dome shown in the following figure and reduced to a very even surface by means of a circular mold <note>original: *cycloplastam*</note>; the base of the structure should be perfectly round, as should the wall situated between the base and the beginning of the dome's arching. I remember having once observed a tower of this kind, constructed with acoustic-architectural <note>original: *Echotectonicâ*</note> art, at HEIDELBERG in the palace of the ELECTOR *Palatine*.

This Hall possesses a wonderful quality: if someone enters and stamps on the ground a bit more firmly, they might imagine a crowd is following them; standing in the center, one produces a most resonant *Echo*. 

<image-desc>A diagram showing the cross-section of a vaulted dome or cupola. At the top is a lantern structure topped with a cross. In the opposite locations F and G, within the perimeter C E F G, nothing can be whispered so softly that it is not heard by another person positioned at F or G. This effect is common to all circular structures, just as we demonstrated regarding the Dome <note>original: *Cupula*</note> of <term>Saint Peter's</term> <note>The great Basilica in Rome, famous for its acoustic properties where sound "creeps" along the curved walls.</note> in the preceding pages. 

<margin>
Echo in the Dome of St. Peter's in Rome.
</margin>

I hear that a similar thing is experienced in <term>Mantua</term> in a certain room within the Duke's suburban palace, but I shall discuss that elsewhere. There is, therefore, no other hidden artifice in such structures except that they be built according to the model of the aforementioned tower. Furthermore, in these kinds of buildings, no cornice <note>original: *coronide*; an architectural molding that could break the smooth flow of sound waves.</note> should obstruct the surface; rather, the entire interior surface must continue without interruption, for by this means the sound will be better propagated.

---

## TECHNICAL METHOD III.

### TO ARRANGE HALLS *shaped into parabolic surfaces so that an ear placed at a certain point may perceive anything uttered even in a whisper.*

Although the nature and properties of the parabola have been discussed at length above, I have decided to address certain difficulties here so that you do not commit any errors in practice. It must be understood that a parabolic surface can be viewed in two ways: obtuse <note>wide and shallow</note> and acute <note>narrow and deep</note>. The more obtuse the concave surface is, the closer its focus or <term>acoustic point</term> <note>original: *punctum acusticum*; the specific spot where sound waves converge.</note> will be to the peak <note>original: *vertici*</note> of the parabola; conversely, the more acute the surface is, the further the acoustic point will be from the peak. This point, or the meeting of the sound rays, is always located on the axis of the parabola.

<note>Vocabulary: Echo, Dome of St. Peter's, Rome, Mantua, Parabola, acoustic point, Phonurgia (The Science of Sound)</note>

<summary>Kircher describes the "whispering gallery" effect found in the circular architecture of Heidelberg, Rome, and Mantua, explaining that smooth, uninterrupted curves allow sound to travel perfectly between distant points. He then introduces the geometry of parabolic rooms, specifically how the shape of the curve determines the location of the 'acoustic point' or focus.</summary>
<keywords>Heidelberg Palace, whispering gallery, St. Peter's Basilica, Mantua, parabolic acoustics, acoustic point, architectural acoustics</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of acoustic architecture, moving from the general observations of circular rooms (like the Dome of St. Peter's) on the previous page to the specific geometric properties of parabolic surfaces. It focuses on how sound waves interact with a parabola to focus sound at a single point.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>

-> # ON STRUCTURES FOR THE USE OF PRINCES # <-

Secondly, it should be known that for all sound-lines to converge at the **acoustic point**, it is necessary that the sound-lines falling upon a concave parabolic surface be parallel to the axis. Hence, if the parabola is sufficiently large, even the smallest <term>species</term> <note>In 17th-century physics, "species" refers to the sensible form or representation of a sound or image as it travels through a medium.</note> of the voice will all converge into a single point. But let us demonstrate the matter a little more clearly. Let the parabola be A B C and the focus D. I say that the species of the voice will not radiate to point D unless they are all carried parallel to the axis B D E.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram illustrating the reflection of sound waves within a parabolic surface. The parabola is labeled A B C with vertex B. A central axis BDE runs vertically. Below the parabola is a horizontal baseline containing points F, G, H, E, I, K, L. Vertical lines extend from these baseline points to meet the parabolic curve at points marked N, O, V, R, B, T, M, Z. From these points on the curve, lines of reflection are drawn back towards the axis, converging at specific points such as D (the focus), X, and P. The diagram is designed to show how rays parallel to the axis focus at D, while rays from other sources (like point E) reflect to different points on the axis.</image-desc>

For if the voice radiates from E into O and I (for we have shown above that the voice is propagated through <term>conic soundings</term> <note>original: *phonismos conicos*; Kircher's term for the way sound spreads in a cone shape from a source.</note>), it is certain, according to the rules of **reflection** handed down above, that lines falling from E into O and I will reflect not into D, but into X; for in this, the angle of incidence will equal the angle of reflection. Again, if a voice positioned outside the axis radiates into the concave surface, it is indeed true that only a single ray will be reflected into D; however, the other radiations will establish different centers of reflection outside the axis. As is clear in the sound-line falling from G into V, and reflected from there into D, the lines radiating from G into R and Z will converge <note>original: *corradiabunt*</note> neither in D nor in X, but in P.

<margin>
Conic sounding in a parabola produces different centers on the axis.
</margin>

You see, therefore, that a voice radiating conically within a parabola achieves nothing singular unless it is positioned on the axis; for then, only the lines of a single conic sounding will converge into one point; there is a sounding A B C, the following *figure*, parabola B D C. of sounds...
it becomes

<summary>This page explains the geometry of parabolic acoustics, demonstrating that sound only perfectly converges at a single focus point if the source is positioned correctly along the parabola's axis. It uses geometric proofs to show why sounds from other locations will reflect to different points, resulting in a less concentrated acoustic effect.</summary>

### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION IV. ###

<margin>
[The sound] acquires, or reflections.
</margin>

[...continued from previous page, the source] is placed on the axis of the parabola A D. I say that the lines of the <term>conical sounding</term> <note>original: *phonismi conici*; Kircher uses the term "phonismus" to describe the geometric shape or manifestation of sound waves as they travel through the air.</note> A B C, falling upon the surface of the concave parabola, will reflect toward point D. Since all the lines on the concave surface of the parabola form a circle which is the same as the base of the cone, it is certain that all lines, according to the equality of angles, will reflect into a certain and determined point on the axis.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of a conical or parabolic section labeled with points A, B, C, and D. Point A is the vertex, from which multiple lines radiate toward the curved boundary BC. Point D is located on the central axis between A and the base.</image-desc>

However, since this cone is not just of one <term>species</term> <note>In 17th-century physics, "species" refers to the sensible form or representation of a sound or image as it travels through a medium.</note>, but always includes infinite smaller and smaller cones within itself, they will not all focus into a single point; rather, each will form different acoustic centers on the axis. This happens in the same way as in a <term>spherical concave burning mirror</term> <note>original: *speculo ustorio sphaerico concavo*; an optical device used to focus sunlight to a point intense enough to start fires.</note>, in which the rays of light falling upon it do not converge at a single point, but different radiations form different centers of burning along the axis.

<margin>
When a cylindrical sounding falls into a parabola, all lines flow together into a single point.
</margin>

If, however, the sounding is not conical but cylindrical, then I assert that all the sound-lines parallel to the axis will flow together into a single point; for the demonstration of this matter, see my work *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: *Arte magna Lucis & Umbræ*; Kircher’s influential 1646 treatise on optics and projection.</note>.

These things having been properly established: if you should have the intention to construct a similar device in a certain Palace, you may conveniently install it in a long gallery in the following manner. At each end, by means of a parabolic mold, a parabolic surface should be described, so that if the axis were extended, it would pass through the center of each parabola. 

Let there be a Parabola at each end of the gallery: the first A B C, and the second G E F. Let the axis common to both be E B. Let the <term>foci</term> <note>The specific points where the curved shape concentrates the sound.</note> of each parabola be H and I. Let the source of the sounds be at O, at the midpoint of the axis E B. If the sound radiates as a conical sounding, it will indeed have an effect, but not as intense an effect as the parabola promises, for the reasons mentioned a little earlier. But if the sounding should be cylindrical, propagated from P O R into both ends, an ear placed at either focus H or I will find what it desires; for all sound-lines, no matter how softly the voice is imbued, will nevertheless all reflect at H or I, where they will increase the sound in a wondrous manner. It will be understood, therefore

<summary>Kircher explains that while conical sound waves create multiple focus points, cylindrical waves (parallel to the axis) focus perfectly in a parabola. He proposes using this geometry to create "secret" speaking galleries in palaces where even a whisper can be heard at great distances.</summary>

# ON BUILDINGS FOR THE USE OF PRINCES. #

...therefore in H or I. Whatever was whispered in the middle of P O R [would be heard]. This <term>mechanism</term> <note>original: *machinamentum*; referring here to the architectural arrangement of walls and curves designed to conduct sound.</note> will be all the more hidden the more obtuse the <term>parabolas</term> <note>original: *parabolae*; a symmetrical open curve. In acoustics, this shape is used to reflect sound waves from a wide area into a single focal point.</note> are; for from this it will happen that the <term>foci</term> <note>original: *foci*; the points at which rays or sound waves converge after being reflected.</note> are positioned far outside the concavity of the parabolas. Indeed, the listeners will marvel that they perceive such great voices under that determined point in the middle of the air, even though no arch or other surface appears within the <term>walkway</term> <note>original: *ambulacrum*; a covered corridor or gallery, often found in palaces or gardens.</note> that could be the cause of such a prodigious sound. 

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing the construction of an acoustic or optical chamber using two intersecting parabolic arcs that form an elongated oval. A horizontal axis is labeled with points E (left) and B (right), and contains internal points H and I. A vertical axis is labeled with internal points P, X, O, Y, Z, R. The outer boundary is marked with letters G, A (top) and F, G (bottom). Multiple rays originate from points H and I, reflecting off the curved interior surfaces.</image-desc>

Nay, the sound will reveal itself to those standing at six different points: P, X, O, Y, Z, and R. But if one wishes to speak to only one of those six—for example, to someone standing at P—they could achieve this through a tube directed at point G of the parabola; for the voice falling on G will necessarily present itself to the ears of the person positioned at P, since a ray necessarily reflects according to the same nature by which it falls.

### Another Method. ###

Mersenne, in his *Universal Harmony* <note>original: *Harmonia universali*; referring to the 1636 work by Marin Mersenne, a French polymath and "father of acoustics" who was a contemporary of Kircher.</note>, provides another method, which I will subjoin here so that it can be judged what should be thought of it. Since he saw that a <term>cylindrical sounding</term> <note>original: *phonisnum cylindraceum*; Kircher’s term for sound waves that travel in a focused, parallel beam rather than dispersing like a cone.</note> was difficult to achieve, and a conical one was insufficient, he hoped to accomplish something better with a doubled parabola. He turned a smaller concave parabola toward a larger one, such that they shared a common focus. In this way, the <term>sounding</term> <note>original: *phonismus*; the physical manifestation or "shape" of the sound wave.</note> falling into the larger concave surface is reflected through the focus into the smaller concave surface; from there, it is finally propagated in <term>parallelism</term> <note>original: *parallelismum*; sound waves moving in parallel lines so they do not lose intensity over a distance.</note> outside the surface of the larger parabola (which must be cut out according to the size of the smaller parabola) to the ears of someone standing behind it.

It also discusses Marin Mersenne's theory of using nested parabolas to create parallel beams of sound for long-distance communication.</summary>

# THE PHONURGIA, BOOK I, SECTION IV. #

Let there be a larger <term>parabola</term> <note>A symmetrical open curve. In acoustics, its shape is used to reflect sound waves from a wide area into a single focal point.</note> A B C, and a smaller one D E F, joined to one another in such a way that the <term>focal point</term> <note>original: *focus*; the point at which rays or sound waves converge after being reflected.</note> G is common to both. However, let the 

-> [Diagram showing two nested parabolas, A-B-C and D-E-F, sharing a common focal point G. Sound rays from points X and Y reflect off the inner surface of the large parabola and then off the outer surface of the small parabola to emerge as parallel lines labeled H-I and L-M.] <-

larger parabola be cut away from V to P. With this completed, let the sounds be at X and Y, and the sound lines be X T, X V, Y P, and Y Q. These, falling upon the concave surface of the larger parabola, will be reflected from there through G into L and R. But those hitting X T and X V at N and O will from there be reflected again into L M and H I, until finally they present themselves as <term>cylindrical acoustics</term> <note>original: *phonismo cylindraceo*; this refers to sound waves being reflected into parallel lines, like a column or cylinder, rather than scattering, allowing the sound to travel further without losing clarity.</note> to the ears positioned at H I L M.

Certainly, these things possess a beautiful theoretical appearance at first glance, but in <term>practice</term> <note>original: *praxi*; the actual application of the theory.</note> they achieve no success at all. For besides the very few lines which the larger parabola is capable of reflecting, the smaller one will also transmit very few, and those will be significantly weakened by that parallelism, so much so that I would dare to say the method we described previously is a hundred times better than this one. Indeed, even *Mersenne* <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath often called the "father of acoustics."</note> himself, foreseeing the difficulty of the matter as if it were a lost cause, fled to another method, though it was not much better than this. For it is clearly evident that there can be no success for this mechanism, both for the reasons stated and because of the focal point; since it is common to both parabolas, it would require more than *Archimedean* <note>Referring to Archimedes of Syracuse, the ancient Greek mathematician renowned for extreme precision in engineering and geometry.</note> industriousness to position both of them with such precision.

Let there be a larger parabola A B C; let a smaller one, D, be placed near it, as appears in the *figure*, but let it have a convex parabolic surface and a common focal point for both at E. With this mechanism thus arranged, the sound lines from G and F falling upon the concave surface of the larger one—reflecting from points O, N, X, and H, not into the focal point E, but onto the convex surface of the smaller parabola D—will finally reverberate the received sound as cylindrical acoustics into the ears of the listener at I and R. But who does not see that this too, besides theoretical specu-

<summary>Kircher critiques a complex acoustic design involving nested parabolas, arguing that while the geometry is theoretically perfect, the precision required makes it impossible to build effectively.</summary>

# ON STRUCTURES FOR THE USE OF PRINCES. #

...that the theory <note>original: *speculationem*</note> contains nothing of value within it. For the smaller parabola, besides the fact that it matches the focal point of the larger one only with difficulty, also reflects so few and such weak reflections back onto the convex surface of the smaller one, that I believe a person positioned at I R could hardly acquire any singular increase in their voice. 

-> [A geometric diagram illustrating the reflection of sound or light rays within a parabolic system. A primary, wide parabola is labeled with points A, O, N, M, B, L, X, H, and C along its curve. Inside this, a smaller, flatter convex parabola is positioned with its vertex at D and an intersection or focal point E above it. Parallel vertical lines descend from points along the primary parabola to a horizontal baseline labeled F, I, R, and G. Reflection lines originate from the points M, N, O, B, L, and X on the upper parabola, converging towards point E and intersecting the lower convex parabolic surface D.] <-

Even if this notable increase in voice were granted, though not conceded, I still think this mechanism would be so difficult in <term>practice</term> <note>original: *praxi*</note> that it could hardly have any success. For there are many things beautiful according to theory which can almost never be brought into actual work; and lest the same be said of our inventions and acoustic structures in this place, we have taken singular care with zeal not to put forward anything that was not first proven by experiment. Therefore, whoever intends to build an acoustic structure using parabolas should proceed by no method other than what we have described, and they will achieve an infallible effect. For it is not necessary for the parabolic surfaces in this business of <term>Echo-tectonics</term> <note>original: *Echo-tectonico*; Kircher's term for the architecture or construction of acoustic spaces.</note> to be as mathematically exact as is required for <term>burning mirrors</term> <note>original: *speculis causticis*; concave mirrors designed to focus sunlight into a point of intense heat to start fires.</note>. A *shape* worked with whatever diligence possible by the help of a <term>parabolic mold</term> <note>original: *paraboloplastæ*; a specialized tool or template used to ensure the wall follows a parabolic curve.</note> will suffice. For since sound lines have their own width—and a quite notable one at that—the acoustic center of the parabola is not a <term>mathematical point</term> but a <term>physical center</term> <note>Kircher is observing that because sound waves have physical "width" (wavelength), they do not focus into a single microscopic point like light, but into a broader area. This makes the construction of sound-reflecting walls more forgiving than the construction of optical mirrors.</note> endowed with its own breadth; only someone who hasn't understood the preceding sections could be ignorant of this.

<summary>Kircher argues that overly complex nested parabolic designs are failures in practice, insisting that acoustic architecture must be grounded in experimental reality. He explains that because sound has physical width, acoustic chambers do not require the impossible mathematical precision of optical mirrors.</summary>

PHONURGIA, BOOK 1, SECTION IV.

# PRACTICAL EXERCISE IV.
## ESTABLISHING A HYPERBOLIC STRUCTURE
### *for gathering sounds.*

Whatever we have said regarding the Parabola must also be applied to hyperbolic surfaces, so that nothing further remains to be said about forming surfaces of this kind. If someone possesses a hyperbola-mold <note>original: *hyperboloplasten* — a tool or template used to shape a hyperbolic curve</note> suited to the capacity of the space, they shall describe two hyperbolic surfaces in some walkway, along with their acoustic centers <note>the foci of the hyperbola</note>. An ear placed at these centers will perceive voices, however softly uttered, just as in parabolic structures. Since all these matters are very easy, let us hasten to others.

# PRACTICAL EXERCISE V.
## CONSTRUCTING ELLIPTICAL STRUCTURES
### *which wonderfully intensify sounds.*

Among the remaining conic sections, the Ellipse is the most suitable of all for our work in <term>Acoustical Architecture</term> <note>original: *Echo-tectonico* — a term Kircher coined for the design of buildings with specific echo properties</note>; therefore, we have decided to treat elliptical structures more extensively here. What an Ellipse is, and how it is described, has been stated above. To the task at hand, then. The elliptical vaults <note>original: *tholi* — referring to domes or arched ceilings</note> of these structures have this unique property: in an open space, they always produce two points or centers. First, let there be an ellipse-mold <note>original: *elliptico-plastes*</note> of such length as the desired length of the oval vault requires.

Let there be, therefore, a vault A H B made of plaster; at the poles A and B, you should apply the ellipse-mold <note>original: *ellipsioplasten*</note>; and let there be acoustic centers C and D, which should be marked most carefully. Rotate the ellipse-mold within the plaster in a semicircular motion until the mold has left a perfect elliptical surface inside the vault, and the structure will be complete. For if someone stands at D and speaks words ever so softly, I say that one positioned at C will perceive everything perfectly and distinctly. For since the voice from D, however it strikes the elliptical vault, is always reflected <note>original: *reverberetur*</note> to C, and there infinite species <note>Kircher uses the term "species" to describe the sensible form or "rays" of sound traveling through the air</note> of sound reach at one and the same moment, these intensified species will present themselves perfectly to the ears.

## PRACTICAL EXERCISE VI.

### *Establishing an acoustic ellipse in a palace so that two Princes, positioned in separate rooms, can converse about any matter as if they were together.*

<margin>A technique for secretly communicating plans.</margin>
This invention is of the greatest utility for the mutual communication of plans. For it is of great importance to confer on a secret plan—removed from all human knowledge, lest it become known prematurely through the traps of spies—so much so that those secret stairs and entrances arranged for the rooms of Princes seem to have been ordained for no other purpose than that they might meet to communicate plans regarding matters of great importance as secretly as they are securely.

How, therefore, we might architect something similar through the art of <term>Echo-tectonics</term> <note>original: *Echo-tectonicâ* — a term coined by the author to describe the architectural science of sound and echoes.</note> must now be seen. So that two Princes, situated in different rooms, may safely and securely [communicate] their plans to one another...

...so that they might be able to communicate the plan; the structure is to be established by this art. Sound originating at one focus reflects off the walls and converges perfectly at the other.</note> of the Ellipse terminate at the small windows of each room, arranged for this purpose; and you shall have what was sought, but a visual demonstration will reveal the matter better.

<margin><warning>faded text</warning> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear> <unclear>-inammo</unclear> <unclear>...</unclear></margin>
Let there be two rooms, **D** and **F**. Let the space thrown between both, and enclosed by walls, be **G E**. Within this space, by means of an <term>ellipsioplasta</term> <note>original: "ellipsioplastæ." A specialized tool or template devised by Kircher for molding perfectly elliptical surfaces in plaster.</note>, you should describe a concave **elliptical dome** within the plaster, polished most exactly, marked with the letter **A**. Let the acoustic centers (or centers of the elliptical hollow) **B** and **C** perfectly align with the small windows **B** and **C**, which have been purposefully arranged in each room; and you shall have a perfect secret structure. For the words of a Prince positioned at window **B**, even if uttered in the lowest whisper, will be perceived by the other Prince positioned at window **C** as clearly and distinctly as if they had been spoken right next to him. Indeed, it can hardly be expressed how much the voice acquires an increase in volume within the hollow of this elliptical shape; for whether the voice is carried upward, downward, or to the sides, all the <term>sonorous lines</term> <note>A term used by Kircher to describe sound waves or paths, modeled on the behavior of light rays in optics.</note> striking the hollow are reflected back to **C** in that very place.

# PRACTICAL PROJECT VII.

### *BY ANOTHER MUCH MORE INGENIOUS METHOD*
### *to construct the same structure, and extend it*
### *over a greater distance.*

Truly, if anyone should wish to architect a similar work of art for a very remote distance, he shall begin the matter thus: within the two walls determining the space, let 4, 5, 6, or seven elliptical "eggs" <note>The author uses the Latin 'ova' (eggs) to describe the three-dimensional ovoid or ellipsoidal shape of the acoustic chambers.</note> be formed in exactly the same way as you see done in the preceding *figure*. The ellipses, moreover, must be so constructed that they are each truncated <note>Cut off or shortened.</note> according to lines applied in order and passing through the <term>foci</term> <note>The focal points of an ellipse. In acoustics, sound produced at one focus reflects off the curved walls and gathers perfectly at the other focus.</note>, as is clearly evident in the following *figure*.

<image-desc>A technical diagram illustrating the geometric construction of an acoustic gallery. It shows a series of four overlapping elliptical shapes arranged horizontally between two parallel lines (walls) marked H-I and F-G. At the leftmost focal point (A), a small profile of a person is shown speaking into the apparatus. At the rightmost end (E), another profile of a person is shown listening. Focal points and intersection points are labeled with letters (A, B, C, D, E) and lines of truncation are marked with V and X.</image-desc>

In this figure, within the walls **F G** and **H I**, let 4 elliptical "eggs" be arranged so that the acoustic centers or foci are common to each pair of ellipses. Thus, the acoustic center **B** of ellipse **A B** is common to ellipse **B C**; and the acoustic center **C** of ellipse **B C** is common to ellipse **C D**; and the acoustic center **D** of ellipse **C D** is common to ellipse **D E**. Therefore, the ellipses must be truncated along the lines **V X** passing through the centers, <margin>The arrangement of elliptical vessels.</margin> so that the individual ellipses may appear like perforated jars, and you will have a remarkable secret prepared. For if someone, positioned at **A**, whispers even the lowest voice, it...

...[the sound] at **E**, another person situated even five hundred feet away will perceive it as clearly as if it were whispered into his nearby ears. For all the sound-lines <term>lineæ sonoræ</term> <note>The author treats sound waves like rays of light, following geometric paths.</note> reflected in the elliptical vault **A B** will converge at **B** as if at a center, and there, multiplied and intensified without confusion, each progressing further, they will form another reflection in the second elliptical vault at **C**. Once again united and multiplied in this center, they will enter the elliptical vault **C D**, and being reflected there, they will join together at **D**. From here, finally reflected again in the fourth vault, they at last arrive, intensified and multiplied, at the ears of the person stationed at **E**, who will perceive the sound-images <term>sonoras species</term> <note>In 17th-century science, 'species' were the forms or qualities of an object—in this case, sound—that traveled through a medium to the senses.</note> as clearly as if he were present at point **A**. The demonstration of this matter is so clear that nothing besides a consideration of the *figure* <note>Referring to the technical diagram provided on the previous pages.</note> seems to be required to understand it.

### COROLLARY.

From what has been said, it is clear how many architectural miracles of "echo-building" <term>Echo-tectonica</term> <note>A term coined by the author for the art of designing buildings specifically for acoustic effects.</note> can be performed from this single proposed Practice <term>Pragmatia</term> <note>A technical or practical instruction.</note>. Certainly, with the help of this machine, you will be able to display musical harmonies of every kind, without anyone even suspecting where they might be coming from. But let these things suffice for the ingenious Reader to discover countless other inventions.

# SECTION V.

## ON THE WONDERFUL
->Construction of Acoustic Instruments, by which both articulate and inarticulate voices can be propagated over immense distances.<-

### CHAPTER I.

->*ON THE USE OF CONIC SECTIONS*<-
->*in the Science of Sound.*<-

From the sections of certain bodies <note>Kircher is referring to the geometric "sections" created by cutting through a cone at different angles.</note>, surfaces emerge whose powers and properties are so wondrous and hidden that nature alone seems to have reserved them for performing miracles; and while I shall pass over countless others for now, who is not aware of the paradoxes of the conic section? There are three types of these especially filled with truly admirable powers: the Ellipse, the Parabola, and the Hyperbola. Their most marvelous property is this: if concave bodies are shaped by introducing these figures into physical material, certain points are produced in the "imaginary medium" <term>Medio imaginario</term> <note>Today we call these "foci" or focal points.</note> toward which all lines falling within the concavity are reflected. These lines, united at the point of reflection, produce wonderful effects, both in illuminating and in setting things on fire; from this principle, acoustic mirrors also find their origin.

However, since I have treated these matters formally in my work *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: *Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae*, Kircher's 1646 treatise on optics and catoptrics.</note>, I refer the reader there. Here, I shall only repeat those things that seem most conducive to our present purpose. Just as the united rays of light within the aforementioned bodies produce wonderful effects of combustion, so too can sound-rays <term>sonori radii</term> within the same concavities achieve the same; I assume no one who has correctly understood the preceding pages can doubt this. Therefore, we shall explain how acoustic conic sections are to be designed in physical constructions to provide these wonderful hearing effects, after we have first briefly taught how to describe said sections in material form. Let it be, then:

<summary>This page introduces the mathematical foundation of acoustic instruments, focusing on how conic sections (ellipses, parabolas, and hyperbolas) reflect sound rays to a single focal point, much like light in a burning mirror.</summary>
<keywords>conic sections, acoustics, Ellipse, Parabola, Hyperbola, sound rays, reflection, Phonurgia, focal points</keywords>

# PROBLEM I.
## TO DRAW AN ELLIPSE WITH THE SINGLE PULL OF A STRING.

Since we have already provided various methods for drawing ellipses and other conic sections in Books 3 and 10 of *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: *Artis magnæ Lucis & Umbræ*, Kircher’s monumental 1646 work on optics.</note>, we did not wish to repeat them all here. Instead, we shall teach only that method which seemed most suitable for mechanical "Echo-architects" <term>Echo-tectonicis</term> <note>Kircher's term for those who design buildings specifically for their acoustic properties.</note>; in this way, you may draw an ellipse with the single pull of a string.

Every ellipse consists of two diameters: a major and a minor. If these were equal, it would not be an ellipse but a circle. For the more the minor diameter decreases, the "sharper" <note>meaning more eccentric or elongated.</note> the ellipse will be; and the more it increases, the more "obtuse" the ellipse will be, and more similar to a circle, until the diameters become equal in size and the ellipse transforms into a perfect circle. Note, moreover, that in every non-circular ellipse, two points are found which geometricians commonly call "foci" <note>The Latin *foci* means "hearths" or "fireplaces."</note> based on the comparisons made, because it is at these points that ignition occurs in burning mirrors <term>speculis causticis</term>. These two points are located on the major semi-diameter; they will always be further from each other the sharper the ellipse is (that is, the more it differs from a circle), but closer together the more the ellipse approaches a circular shape, until they meet in a single point, which is the center of the circle. These things being observed:

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing the construction of an ellipse within a circle. A circle with center E has horizontal diameter A-B and vertical line C-D. An ellipse is drawn inside, sharing the vertical line C-D as its minor axis. Two focal points, I and L, are marked on the horizontal line A-B, each depicted with a small vertical pin. A triangle is formed by the foci I and L and a point R on the upper curve of the ellipse, illustrating the string-loop method of drawing.</image-desc>

Let A B be the major diameter and C D the minor diameter of the ellipse to be formed. Let the minor diameter C D be placed at the midpoint E of the major diameter at right angles, so that the ends of the minor diameter are equidistant from the center point E. This being established, you will find the foci—or "points of comparison"—as follows: with a compass <term>circinus</term>, transfer the length of the major semi-diameter (either A E or B E) starting from either end of the minor diameter (C or D) onto the major diameter A B. Where the compass intersects the line A B on either side, there the two foci will be: namely, at I and L. Having found these points of comparison, I and L, [place] in them...

<meta>The catchword "duos" (two) indicates the next page begins with a description of the "two pins" used to secure the string.</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains the basic geometry of the ellipse, defining its axes and focal points, and begins a step-by-step practical guide for drawing one using a compass and string.</summary>
<keywords>ellipse construction, geometry of sound, focal points, foci, Echo-tectonics, acoustic architecture, string method</keywords>

...you will fix two pegs, and around the pegs [place] a string twice the length of L A or I B. For a stylus moved within this string will describe the desired Ellipse; the string is represented for us by the two lines L R and R I, which is tied at two points I and L and is applied precisely to the stylus R at point A. Then, with the stylus R guided within it from point A, it will describe the sought-after Ellipse. From this it is clearly evident that the two parts of the string extended from R toward either focus—R L and R I—are always equal to the major diameter A B of the Ellipse. For the mathematical proof of all these things, see *The Art of Light and Shadow*, Book 4, Treatise on Conic Sections <note>original: *Arte Lucis & Umbra lib. 4. tract. de sect. conicis*, referring to Kircher's 1646 work on optics and geometry.</note>.

## PROBLEM II.
### TO CREATE AN ELLIPSIOPLASTES
### *in solid material.*

We call an *Ellipsioplastes* <term>Ellipsioplasten</term> <note>A term coined by Kircher from the Greek *ellipsis* and *plastos* (molded), meaning an "ellipse-molder."</note> that form or template by whose rotation in soft material—such as plaster, cement, or clay—we produce an Ellipse. For just as a potter or sculptor <term>plastes</term> uses various instruments to describe various figures, so too is a plastic form needed to describe Ellipses in plaster, by the rotation of which they may be produced. And this form or rule we call an *Ellipsioplastes*, as if it were a potter's mold for an Ellipse. In the same way, for describing Parabolas and Hyperbolas, we use forms which we call *Paraboloplastes* and *Hyperboloplastes*.

You shall make an *Ellipsioplastes* in this manner: on a flat, even, and polished floor, let an Ellipse be described according to the previous problem based on the size of the given diameters. Once this is done, according to the Ellipse—

## PROBLEM III.

->**TO DESCRIBE A PARABOLA**<-
->**BY THE DRAWING OF A SINGLE STRING.**<-

A Parabola is a section of a Cone parallel to its side, within which there is a point in which all lines falling parallel within its surface are reflected and united. We have demonstrated all these things at length in the cited books of *The Great Art of Light and Shadow* <note>original: *Artis magnae Lucis & Umbrae*, referring to Kircher's 1646 work on optics and geometry.</note>, and we refer the reader there, where they will also find various descriptions of parabolas; for that reason, in this place, we propose only one that is more easy and more suitable for our purpose. Let a very flat table X Y be prepared, to which a square <term>norma</term> <note>A carpenter’s or mason's square used for drawing right angles.</note> D E is applied at right angles, so that the square

applied to E G, scrapes normally against the side E G of the table, and as it is moved forward, let it always remain equidistant from the axis of the parabola. Therefore, to describe a parabola by means of this instrument, fix a very fine string to the center L.

...by the necessary movement of the square <note>original: *norma*, a tool used to ensure right angles.</note>, the parabola F B G is drawn. This point will be the focus of the parabola, just as in the *figure* O B is one-fourth of the *latus rectum* N R, and O is the sought-after point of reflection. However, you will find it more effective if you first determine the point of reflection, then the *latus rectum* of any length; one-fourth of this distance from O will give the vertex of the parabola, and O will be the center of reflection.

ON any flat and polished floor, let a parabola be drawn using the traditional rules and the stated method, in the size you desire or which best suits your plan. According to this drawing, you will cut out another parabola from wooden boards or an iron plate so that

<image-desc>A technical diagram of a "paraboloplasten," a tool used for drafting or shaping a parabola. It features a parabolic arc with points labeled N, A (at the vertex), and R. the cutout precisely matches the one drawn on the floor. You will then have a parabola-molding tool <term>paraboloplasten</term> <note>From the Greek *parabola* and *plassein* (to mold); a mechanical template used for carving or casting precise parabolic shapes.</note> ready in the shape shown in the present diagram, in which the vertex A is equipped with a sharpened iron tip. Indeed, with the handle I M P at C D, the sharpened iron tip, which continues the axis, is fixed into a wall during the drafting of the parabola so that the surface is not distorted by a shifting axis. Then, the handle is placed under another pivot point <term>hypomochlio</term> <note>original: *hypomochlio*; from the Greek for the support or fulcrum of a lever.</note> and rotated within lime, plaster <note>original: *gypsum*</note>, or other material piled around it; it will then mold the desired parabola into that material.

### *ing of a single thread, and to construct a hyperbola-molder from the described shape.*

Let A be the center of reflection <note>the focus</note>, B the opposite center, and C the vertex of the hyperbola. With these three points given, you will describe a hyperbola by the pull of a thread in this manner. In both centers or points established by comparison 

<image-desc>A schematic diagram illustrating the mechanical construction of a hyperbola. It features a curved line representing a hyperbola with its vertex labeled G (though the text refers to C). in the opposite hyperbolas, let needles be fixed, to each of which a thread is tied. # SECTION VI.

# THE ARCHITECTURE
## OF ACOUSTIC INSTRUMENTS, *BY WHICH it is demonstrated through experience that anyone can manifest their mind to another, however distant, through sounds—both articulate and inarticulate—at the greatest distance of places, with a reciprocal response.*

## CHAPTER I.
### ON THE ORIGIN OF THE ACOUSTIC ART.

<margin>The *Universal Musurgia* <note>Kircher’s 1650 work on music and acoustics</note> [is the origin] of this instrument.</margin>
About twenty-five years <note>original: *quinque circiter annorum lustra*; a "lustrum" is a period of five years</note> have now passed since I composed the *Great Art of Consonance and Dissonance*, inscribed with the title *Musurgia*, in which, among other things in Book 9, I treated most abundantly the **Echonic Art** <term>Echonica Arte</term> <note>The study of echoes and sound reflection</note>, or the reflection of sounds and their multiplication. Observing the great similarity and analogy between light and sound in the diffusion of visible and audible **species** <note>In 17th-century science, "species" were the forms or "images" emitted by objects that allow them to be seen or heard</note>, I decided that the same things which Opticians are accustomed to perform through helioscope tubes constructed by the **catoptric art** <term>catoptrica arte</term> <note>The science of reflected light and mirrors</note> should also be provided through sounds. Therefore, following the **Ariadnean thread** <note>A guiding path, referring to the myth of the Labyrinth</note> of **Catoptrics** (which consists mostly in the skillful elaboration of glasses, both concave and convex and lenticular, and their proportioned fitting within a tube), I explored whether I could achieve anything in the transmission of acoustic species and the powerful multiplication of sounds. As I investigated, various modes and reasons for arranging **sound-magnification** <note>original: *ἠχαύξησις* (ēchaúxēsis)</note> occurred to me. But when I saw that it was a dangerous work of chance, <margin>The Author's effort in arranging the instruments.</margin> I devoted myself uniquely to this: that those modes of performing things which I had conceived **according to theory** <note>original: *κατὰ τὴν θεωρίαν* (katà tēn theōrian)</note>, I should first prove by experiment. To this end, I left no stone unturned so that through instruments and tubes—partly simple and cylindrical, conical, or elliptical, and partly spiral-shaped towards the end—I might reach the goal as if through proportioned means. First of all, therefore, I made an experiment in concave cylindrical tubes; though it had a not unsuccessful outcome, the voice how-

...however, it did not extend to the remote distance I had hoped for; hence I made a trial with a conical tube, or one shaped like a spinning top, and the resulting effect gave me hope of finally obtaining what I sought through a continued proportion of length. There was

<margin>When the acoustic trumpet was first invented, and on what occasion.</margin>
in my Museum a storage room separated by a wall and a door. At the end of this storeroom was an oval-shaped window which looked out upon the private garden of the *Roman College* <note>The Jesuit university in Rome where Kircher taught and kept his famous collection of curiosities</note>, which stretched far and wide for about 300 palms <note>A "palm" was a measurement of roughly 9 inches; 300 palms is about 225 feet</note> in length. Inside this storeroom, or workshop, I fitted a conical tube according to the length of the space. It was 22 palms <note>Roughly 16.5 feet</note> long and fashioned from iron plates. Its orifice intended for speaking did not exceed 1/4 of a palm, while the small tube, shaped like a funnel, was about one palm in diameter. From there, by a continuous and proportionate increase in width, it gradually broadened into a flared opening three palms in diameter, which extended outside the oval window toward the garden. We have seen the construction of the tube; now let us also explain its effect.

<margin>The wonderful effects of this acoustic tube.</margin>
When our doorkeepers <term>Janitores</term> wished to notify me of something—whether the arrival of guests or any other matter—they would stand within the main gate and speak to me while I stayed in the remote recess of my chamber. To avoid the inconvenience of them wandering through the many twists and turns of the house to reach my Museum, they spoke as if they were standing right there, uttering whatever they wished clearly and distinctly. I would then immediately respond to them through the opening of the tube with the same tone of voice, as the business required. Indeed, no one could say anything in a slightly raised voice within the garden district that I would not hear inside my room. This seemed a new and unheard-of thing to those visiting the Museum; while they heard the speakers, they could not see who was speaking. Therefore, lest I keep them in suspense or astonished by the suspicion of some **forbidden art** <note>A reference to "black magic" or necromancy, which Kircher was always careful to distance himself from by explaining the mechanical physics of his inventions</note>, I showed them the hidden structure of the device. It can hardly be said how many of the City’s nobles were attracted to see and hear this machine. I have decided this must be pointed out to the Reader here, lest he persuade himself that this is a new invention of these times brought forth from *England* <note>Kircher is likely referring to Sir Samuel Morland, who claimed the invention of the "Tuba Stentoro-Phonica" in 1670</note>. Rather, it was exhibited in the Roman College about 24 years ago in the manner I just described, and committed to print <note>In Kircher’s 1650 work, *Musurgia Universalis*</note>, as many still living can testify—both our Fathers and foreigners who deigned to visit my Museum when it was filled with rarer things. Later, the same tube...

<margin>The stentorian trumpet <note>A speaking trumpet or megaphone</note> is not an invention of this time, since it was [demonstrated] many years ago in Rome by the Author of the *Musurgia* and this [book].</margin>

<margin>
...of this book, it was described and printed.
</margin>
The tube was confirmed with successful results to the astonishment of all, due to the propagation of the voice into very remote spaces. It happened further that I was compelled to move my private Museum into a more suitable and more open location in the *Roman College* <note>The Jesuit headquarters in Rome where Kircher established one of the world's first public museums</note> which they call the *Gallery*. The aforementioned tube was moved inside this gallery and is still seen and heard today by all foreigners, entitled under the name of the *Delphic Oracle*. There is, however, this difference: whereas the tube previously broadcasted words spoken with a raised voice openly into remote spaces, it now performs secret "oracles" and staged consultations with a low and hidden voice. It is done with such artifice that no one standing by is able to perceive anything of the secret, mutual whispering of those speaking back and forth. <margin>The Delphic Oracle in the Kircherian Museum.</margin> To this very day, the machine is exhibited to visitors not without some suspicion of a hidden demon by those who do not understand the machine; for the statue even opens and closes its mouth in the manner of a speaker and moves its eyes. I constructed this kind of artifice specifically to show the impostures, fallacies, and frauds of ancient Priests in their consultation of Oracles. For while they gave responses through hidden tubes (which you may see described in my work *Oedipus* <note>Kircher's *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, a massive study of ancient mysteries and hieroglyphs</note>), they simultaneously forced the people to make lavish offerings if they wished to be heard; and thus, through this fraud, a great increase in profit fell to them. However, I do not deny that demons also secretly mixed themselves into their works. *But let us now return to our Conical Tube.*

A great desire always possessed me to know to what <margin>An experiment of the trumpet to take place on Mount Eustachio.</margin> distance the power and efficacy of this tube might extend. It happened this year, as I was about to depart from ROME for a crag on a high mountain sacred to the *Conversion of St. Eustace* <note>Mount Mentorella, east of Rome, where Kircher famously restored a ruined sanctuary</note>. There stands a Church formerly built by CONSTANTINE THE GREAT and consecrated by ST. SYLVESTER in honor of the MOTHER OF GOD; it was nearly destroyed by the passage of time, but has now been restored by the generosity of the EMPEROR and other Princes of *Germany*, and improved for habitation (regarding this, Reader, see the small work titled: *The Eustachio-Marian History*, which treats the origin of this most celebrated place). To this place, then, I went—both out of love for solitude and a desire for my own devotion—to achieve some fruit for souls, according to my solid yearly custom. Since I found the site of this place to be more convenient than any other, and wonderfully suited for testing the tube, I brought with me a tube 15 palms <note>Roughly 11 feet long</note> long, fashioned with singular care, which, at an opportune and quiet time...

<margin>2,200 people flocked together at the sound of the trumpet to attend the sacred assembly <note>original: "sacram synaxim," a term for the Eucharist or Mass</note> in the Church of the Mother of God, situated on a high cliff.</margin>
We tested this both by day and by night, and truly with such success that we could not have hoped for a more favorable outcome. In the surrounding cluster of fortified towns <note>original: "castellorum"</note> that can be seen from this very high cliff, we first made an agreement with the inhabitants regarding the hour of speaking. At distances of 2, 3, 4, and 5 Italian miles <note>An Italian mile was approximately 1.5 kilometers</note> away, we began to converse with a powerful voice. They testified that they had understood every single word distinctly by extending a linen cloth during the day and by lighting a flame at night. In this manner, for the solemnity of the Feast of PENTECOST and for the sacred assembly, we invited the people by composing an appropriate set of words; we sang litanies through the tube, and we requested supplies for necessary provisions—the outcome of the things sent to us proved that they had understood everything. It seemed a miracle to everyone that by such a prodigious sound, like a voice falling from heaven, the astonished people were gathered for the exercise of devotion. By the benefit of the tube, as many as two thousand two hundred people were assembled that very night at the place to which they had been invited. Indeed, as the news spread, we were amazed that many men of distinguished rank hurried there from even more remote places, moved not so much by devotion as by the desire to see and the excitement of this <term>polyphonic instrument</term> <note>Kircher uses "polyphonic" here to mean "many-voiced" or "magnifying the voice" rather than the modern musical sense of multiple melodies</note>. There, I also discovered a new method by which, through the grace of God, we experienced that someone could respond using only a strong intensity of the voice without the help of another tube. We have decided to show the operations we performed with the benefit of the tube here through the present diagram <note>Kircher refers to a printed illustration typically found on the facing page of the original edition</note>.

I add to these things what the Reverend Father PHILIPP MILLER, Confessor to His SACRED IMPERIAL MAJESTY (whom I had instructed by letter regarding the construction and proper adaptation of the tube), writes: *"We saw to it that a tube was made by General WERTMILLER, a Swiss, according to the method Your Reverence had indicated to me, and it was quite long. By means of this, the EMPEROR spoke from the castle of Eberstorff and commanded—speaking, however, in an ordinary voice—Lords SCHAFFTENBERG and ST. JULIAN, who were at Neugebeu, whatever he wished; and they understood everything and carried out the orders right there."*

And so, being shrewdly and skillfully intent on these matters, I see now that what I once promised in Book 9 of my *Musurgia* <note>Referring to his 1650 work *Musurgia Universalis*, a massive encyclopedia of music theory and acoustics</note> can be verified: namely, the account of the Horn of ALEXANDER <note>Alexander the Great</note>, with which he used to assemble his army from a distance of one hundred stadia <note>Roughly 12 miles or 18.5 kilometers</note>, and other new inventions involving spiral tubes that propagate voices over an incredibly great distance. Through these, I also promised to show how, through various distinctions in the sounds themselves, commanders of war...

## CHAPTER II.
### ON THE POWER AND EFFICACY OF SIMPLE
### tubes, and their causes.

### PROPOSITION.

->*In cylindrical, polygonal, or polyhedral acoustic tubes, no*<-
->*reflection of the voice can occur; rather, only a unification of audible rays*<-
->*is propagated over a very long distance.*<-

Let there be a cylindrical tube, or one constructed in the shape of any polygonal prism; let that form be marked with the letters **A B**. Let this conical one be marked **C D**. I say that the sound in these is not produced by the multiplication of a true and real reflection, but is propagated to the most remote places solely by the union and constriction of the acoustic rays. For since all

# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VI.

<margin>The excavated passage of Mount Posillipo.</margin>
Regarding closed channels arranged over a very long distance, as I stated above concerning the Roman Aqueducts, the overseers of the stone-quarry <note>original: *Latomis*. This likely refers to the limestone quarries of Syracuse or Rome used as prisons or conduits.</note> aqueducts have reported to me that people are able to understand one another within them at a distance of five hundred paces, even without using a particularly loud voice. The Neapolitans also relate that in that passage excavated beneath Mount **Posillipo** <note>The *Crypta Neapolitana*, an ancient Roman road tunnel connecting Naples to Pozzuoli.</note>, which extends in a straight line for almost a full mile, words spoken with a raised voice—provided the mouth is applied to the sides of the walls—can be heard at the very end of the same vault. Who does not see that the same result can be achieved with our tube, even using a moderate sound? Since the voice is diffused against the sides of the vault, and the **audible species** <note>original: *species audibiles*. In Kircher's time, "species" referred to the physical form or "sensible image" of a sound or sight traveling through a medium.</note> cannot escape, it is necessary that they produce the intended effect as they are united within the concave vault.

## COROLLARY.

<margin>The marvelous canal of the Egyptians.</margin>
From these things it follows that if any Prince, overflowing with wealth, were to construct a canal three spans or more in diameter for a distance of several miles (which we showed in our **Oedipus** <note>referring to Kircher’s own massive work on Egyptology, *Oedipus Aegyptiacus* (1652-54)</note> that the **Egyptian Kings** achieved from the Pyramids all the way to the **Oracle of Ammon in Libya** via a subterranean passage), I say that he would undoubtedly obtain the same effect I just described. That is, those positioned within it would understand each other in reciprocal conversation at whatever distance the canal might be extended, whether by a loud voice alone or by using our tube, as is evident from what has been said. However, I discovered through my own experiment that this does not happen in the angular and intricate passages of **Subterranean Rome** <note>The Roman catacombs.</note>. The reason is obvious: because the voices, striking so many turnings in the passages, immediately vanished, or else the **tufa** earth <note>original: *terra tophacea*. A porous volcanic rock common in Italy.</note> itself smothered the voice once it was received into the bowels of its porous substance.

<image-desc>A detailed diagram of a long, squared wooden beam or log. At the left end, a human profile is shown with its mouth open, emitting sound waves into the wood. At the right end, an ear is positioned against the wood to listen, demonstrating the transmission of sound through solid material.</image-desc>

Experience establishes that voices are indeed propagated through the sides of a medium, as seen in the experiment of the very long beams mentioned above. If anyone makes even the slightest sound by tapping on the end of such a beam, no one can doubt that another person positioned at the opposite end with their ear applied will immediately perceive it. We ourselves have very often made trial of this in beams extending to a hundred feet and spans in length.
