<header>THE ARCHITECTURE OF ACOUSTIC INSTRUMENTS</header>

# CHAPTER III.

## ON THE PROPERTIES AND EFFICACY OF THE CONICAL TUBE,
## and the cause of Polyphonism.

It has been established by experience when conducting speech through the open air that cylindrical and prismatic tubes do not possess the same power as conical ones; the reason for this was shown in the previous proposition. I shall now briefly explain what the conical tube possesses uniquely over others regarding the multiplication of sounds. Many are of the opinion that this occurs through the physical reflection of **acoustic species** <note>original: *species acusticarum*. In the 17th century, "species" referred to the physical form or "sensible image" of a sound traveling through a medium.</note> occurring within the sides of the tube. However, since in a conical tube the reflection of the voice cannot happen in that way—seeing as it is extended from a narrow point into a long conical diffusion toward wide spaces under a **polyphonism** <note>original: *polyphonismo*. Kircher uses this term to mean the multiplication, reinforcement, or echoing of sound.</note> of straight lines—this cannot be the case. Let there be a conical tube **A B**, extending from the narrow point **A** to the wide breadth **B**.

-> Simple Conical Tube, 15 Palms <note>A "palm" (*palmus*) was a common unit of measurement; the Roman architectural palm was roughly 8.8 inches or 22.3 cm.</note> <-

<image-desc>A horizontal diagram of a conical speaking tube or trumpet. It is narrow at the left end, labeled A, and widens toward the right end, labeled B. A point in the middle of the tube is labeled C.</image-desc>

When the voice is raised at **A**, it will radiate just like the <term>actinobolismus</term> <note>original: *actinobolismus*. From the Greek words for "ray" and "to throw," referring to the emission or radiation of rays, usually of light but here applied to sound.</note> of light, propagated through the conical diffusion of rays at **B** and out through that "gate" into remote spaces. In this process, absolutely no reflection occurs against the sides of the tube, and consequently, no multiplication of sound can be assigned to it beyond that which the conical flow of acoustic rays causes. Therefore, there must be something else hidden in the conical tube that produces the intensity of the sound, which I have decided to explain in a few words.

<margin>
The cause of **Polyphonism** in the conical tube is explained.
</margin>
I say, therefore, that the very cause of this Polyphonism is the shape of the trumpet, which extends from a narrow opening and gradually increases proportionally around point **C** into wider and wider circles. From this it happens that as soon as the forceful voice of the speaker sounds in the narrow opening of the tube, with the movement of the enclosed air added to it, the voice causes the entire tube to tremble all at once. From the reciprocal reflections—which are reverberated from every part of the tube toward opposite points everywhere by the aforementioned vibration—that powerful sound is born...

<meta>catchword: fo-</meta>

<summary>Kircher argues that the superior sound amplification of conical tubes (trumpets) is not due to simple internal reflections, but rather the vibration of the entire instrument’s body—a phenomenon he calls Polyphonism.</summary>
<keywords>conical tube, trumpet, acoustics, Polyphonism, actinobolismus, vibration, reflection, sound propagation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical properties of the conical tube, transitioning from the mathematical causes of sound multiplication to the practical considerations of an instrument's size and length. It concludes by applying these acoustic principles to the massive infrastructure of ancient Rome.</meta>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VI.

the multiplication of sounds, as *Proposition 9* demonstrates; it is no different than a stretched string, which, when moved by even a light touch, causes all parts of the entire string to resonate. Experience teaches the same in the ringing of bells and other conical, concave objects; if these are constricted externally (such as by a string), they lose much of their vibration and likewise greatly hinder the multiplication of sound. Although nearly the same effect occurs in cylindrical and prismatic tubes, the opening of such a tube is too wide to constrict the voice, and therefore it does not cause that intensification of the voice which the conical shape provides.

# CHAPTER IV.
## ON THE SIZE OF THE ACOUSTIC TUBE.

<margin>a</margin>
No one should rightly doubt that the longer a conical tube is, the greater power it possesses for sounding, and the further it propagates sound (provided the proper proportion is not lacking). Daily experience teaches us that where there is greater **sound-growth** <term>ἠχοαύξησις</term> <note>original: *ēchoauxēsis*. From the Greek words for "echo" and "increase." Kircher uses this to describe the mechanical amplification of sound within the tube.</note> or a greater multiplication of sounds, the sound can extend into more remote spaces. There is, however, this difference: the longer the tube, the more forceful a voice it requires to propagate the sound. I once discovered this through an experiment here in ROME with a conical tube 21 palms <note>The Roman architectural palm was about 8.8 inches; this tube was roughly 15 feet long.</note> long, which I constructed myself. A slightly longer tube has this disadvantage: it is somewhat difficult to carry and handle, unless it is fixed and stabilized in a specific place intended for conversation. Optical craftsmen <term>catoptrici</term> <note>original: *catoptrici*—specialists in the study of mirrors and reflected light.</note> note this same disadvantage in optical tubes [telescopes] extended to an excessive length. Regarding the width of the outermost opening, it can be of any width depending on the size of the machine; the opening of our tube was curved back and measured about three cubits <note>A cubit was roughly 1.5 feet; three cubits would be approximately 4.5 feet wide.</note>. But all these matters should be left to the judgment and experience of the craftsmen, as will be clear in the following sections.

## COROLLARY.

*It is clear from these points:* That if the *Aqueducts of the Ancient Romans*—which were built through various windings of the earth and the turnings of mountains—had instead been constructed by Claudius Caesar from *Tivoli* <note>original: *Tybure*. Tivoli is a town near Rome, the source of the Anio Novus and Aqua Claudia aqueducts.</note> to ROME in a direct line, someone using our conical tube inside the aqueduct...

<summary>Kircher discusses how the length and diameter of a conical tube (speaking trumpet) affect sound amplification, noting that while longer tubes are more powerful, they require a stronger voice and are harder to transport. He begins a corollary suggesting that straight Roman aqueducts could have functioned as massive acoustic conduits.</summary>
<keywords>acoustic tube, sound amplification, echo-growth, catoptrics, Roman aqueducts, Claudius Caesar, Tivoli, measurement units</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of acoustic tubes, moving from the theoretical size of the instruments to a bold practical application: using existing infrastructure like aqueducts as giant speaking tubes to connect distant cities.</meta>

->ARCHITECTONICS OF ACOUSTIC INSTRUMENTS<-

<margin>
A conversation to be established between Rome and Tivoli by means of an aqueduct.
</margin>

<image-desc>
A vertical diagram on the right margin illustrates a distance between two points. Point A at the top is labeled "Rome." Point B at the bottom is labeled "Tivoli." A vertical line connects these points, with the sideways text: "16 Miles or 16,000 paces."
</image-desc>

...stationed at the conduit, one could have held a conversation with distinct voices from ROME with the people of *Tivoli*, and vice versa, from there to here. Lest this should perhaps <margin>A.</margin> seem incredible to anyone, I show it thus. First, let us assume the distance between *Tivoli* and ROME is reckoned by common human estimation to be 16 miles—that is, 16,000 *paces* <note>The Roman pace (*passus*) was a measure of distance approximately equal to five feet. 16,000 paces is roughly 15 modern miles.</note>. Thus, an aqueduct of just as many *paces*, closed on all sides and throughout, is required, as the *figure* placed here shows. 

Experience on the *Eustachian* Mount <note>Likely referring to Monte Guadagnolo near Rome, where Kircher restored the Sanctuary of Mentorella; the mountain was associated with the vision of St. Eustace.</note> also taught us that the speech from our tube was perceived at a distance of five miles, or five thousand paces—not through the tube of a closed aqueduct, but through the medium of the completely open air. From this, one can easily recognize at how many miles' distance someone could speak with an articulate voice by the benefit of our tube. For example: if you wish to know how much vocal intensity is needed so that speech achieves its effect over the distance from ROME to *Tivoli*, which we have said is 16 *miles*, let us show it <term>demonstratively</term> <note>original: *apodeiktikōs*. A Greek term meaning "by way of certain proof."</note>. 

It is therefore certain and well-tested by us that the relationship of an intense sound to a more intense sound is the same as the relationship of one distance to another distance between speakers. In this manner, therefore, you shall work by the rule of proportions: if a tube's sound intensity of 4 carries words to 5 *miles*, as was discovered by experiment, what will a sound intensity of 13 provide? When the operation is performed, 16 $1/4$ *miles* will result, within which distance an articulate sound of intensity 13 could be perceived. 

If, however, someone wishes to know at what intensity a mentioned sound can be perceived at 16 miles, he shall act thus: Say, at 5 miles the mentioned sound is perceived at intensity 4; at 16 *miles*, with how much intensity will it be perceived? When the operation is performed, $16 \frac{3}{10}$ will result. In this way, you will be able to find any distance and sound, and its intensity, provided that the medium is always free from all blasts of wind and of equal temperature. 

Since, therefore, it has already been demonstrated that voices can be transmitted through the open air to very remote distances, <margin>B.</margin> how much greater an effect will it achieve in an aqueduct closed on every side and over its whole length, extended to 16 *miles*? Since in such a place voices do not escape, nor are they disturbed by the calm and quiet flow of water (which is balanced), nor do they experience the violence of the air, it is certain that by the benefit of the tube, a conversation could be established between *Tivoli* and ROME with no difficulty. But more on these matters in what follows.

<summary>Kircher proposes a method for long-distance communication between Rome and Tivoli by using a closed aqueduct as a massive acoustic conduit. He uses mathematical proportions and previous open-air experiments to calculate the vocal intensity required to bridge the 16-mile gap.</summary>
<keywords>Rome, Tivoli, acoustics, aqueduct, sound propagation, mathematical proportion, speaking tube, communication</keywords>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VI.

# CHAPTER V.

## *HOW TWO OR MORE PEOPLE, at a remote distance in locations from which they cannot see one another, can nevertheless converse with each other.*

<margin>How two who do not see each other may converse by means of a reflected voice.</margin>
We have conducted an experiment of this matter on the *Eustachian* Mount <note>Likely referring to Monte Guadagnolo near Rome, where Kircher restored the Sanctuary of Mentorella; the mountain was historically associated with the vision of Saint Eustace.</note>; this is how we operated: Let Mount N be situated so that it slopes down into two valleys on either side; and let the distance between the two stations E and F be 1,200 paces. Let *Titus* be at location H in the valley, and *Sempronius* at location G, from which places they are unable to see one another. They desire to converse, however; because this cannot happen except by a reflected voice <note>original: "reflexam vocem"; Kircher is describing what we would call an echo or sound reflection.</note>, they shall proceed thus: Let there be a *phonocamptic* <note>original: "phonocampticum"; a term coined by Kircher from Greek meaning "sound-bending" or "echo-reflecting."</note> object O, such as a wall, situated about one mile away, located opposite the mountain at a distance of 1,000 paces. I say that *Sempronius*, being at location G, will signal whatever he wishes to the person at the corresponding location H through the tube I, which is directed at the wall O. For since the tube is aimed precisely at the wall O, it will happen that the voice, reflected from there at equal angles <note>Kircher is applying the laws of optics—specifically that the angle of incidence equals the angle of reflection—to the behavior of sound waves.</note>, will present itself to the ears of *Titus* standing at location H, even though, being separated from each other by a whole mile, they cannot see one another.

## COROLLARY.

From this it is evident: how a conversation can be established between two people, even if they cannot see each other, by means of a voice reflected off some wall which can nevertheless be seen by both. Innumerable things of this kind can be discovered. However, lest I detain myself too long in such easy practical matters <note>original: "pragmatia"; here referring to the practical application of the theory.</note>, I have decided that they should be left to the clever *Reader* for exercise.

# CHAPTER VI.

## CONCERNING THE SOUND OF THUNDER, WAR CANNONS, *and bells.*

<margin>
How far the sound of thunder extends.
</margin>
<margin>
How far the sound of a war cannon [extends].
</margin>

No sound in the nature of things has been heard more vehemently than the sound of thunder, whether it originates in the Aerial Region or in subterranean passages—that formidable precursor of earthquakes. I have learned through various experiments that the former [atmospheric thunder] reaches 24 *miles*; the latter [subterranean] I have even heard at a distance of 60 *miles*, when in the year 1638 I found myself present during the terrifying earthquakes of *Calabria*. For while I was staying in the fields near the town of the *Lopizii* <note>Referring to Oppido Mamertina in Calabria, which was devastated by the 1638 earthquake; the "Lopizii" were a local noble family.</note> in *Calabria*, I frequently perceived *Stromboli* <note>original: "Strongylum"; the volcanic island in the Tyrrhenian Sea.</note> and the *Mountain* [Etna] raging with fire 60 *miles* <note>original: "millia passuum"; literally 'thousands of paces,' the Roman mile.</note> away, emitting certain massive bellows of such intensity that they could easily have been heard at 100 *miles*. But concerning these matters, let the *Reader* consult *The Subterranean World* <note>original: "Mundum Subterraneum"; Kircher’s 1665 masterpiece on geology and the earth's interior.</note>. Closely following this sound of thunder is the sound arising from the explosion of cannons <note>original: "tormentorum"; specifically heavy siege engines or artillery.</note>. Just as these acquire their sonic vehemence from the same cause as the aforementioned thunder, so too do they propagate it into the most remote spaces; certainly the explosion of cannons made at ROME is perceived both at TUSCULUM <note>The modern city of Frascati.</note> and TIBUR <note>The modern city of Tivoli.</note>, especially if the wind is favorable. At NAPLES, the inhabitants testify that the sound of cannons is heard from the *Island of Capri*, 30 *miles* away. However, as to whether the explosion of said cannons at GRAVELINES in *Belgium* could be heard in *England*, as some testify, I deservedly suspend my belief until I obtain more certain witnesses. For it could have happened that they heard the explosions of ships made on the high seas. Following these are the larger bells; such as the very large one the CHURCH OF THE BLESSED VIRGIN in the *City of Erfurt* holds—the greatest of all those seen in *Europe*—concerning which see *volume 1 of our Musurgia* <note>original: "Musurgia nostra"; Kircher's 'Musurgia Universalis' (1650), his encyclopedic work on music theory and acoustics.</note>.

PHONURGY BOOK I. SECTION VI.

<margin>How far the sound of a larger bell extends.</margin> where we have described it exactly. The locals report that, depending on the favorable disposition of the wind, this bell can be heard up to 24 miles, or Italian miles (which comprise six *German* leagues <note>A "league" was a unit of distance; a German league was roughly 4.6 miles.</note>). Indeed, its sound might easily be propagated to 10 *leagues* if it were exposed to the open air rather than being enclosed within the vast barriers of walls. If the things related in the *Book of Secrets* regarding the Horn of ALEXANDER *the Great* are true—namely, that it assembled his army when it was scattered over 100 *stadia* <note>A stadium was an ancient Greek unit of length; 100 stadia is roughly 11.5 miles.</note>—I would certainly say that it produced a sound of no less intensity than that of the aforementioned bell. But enough has already been said of these matters in the preceding pages. One thing remains to be elucidated in this place: since light spreads itself with a nearly instantaneous motion, while sound moves successively <note>meaning: sound takes time to travel through a medium, whereas light appeared to Kircher's era to be instant.</note>, it may rightly be asked: By how much time does sound arrive at the ears of the listeners later than the light is seen? This matter can easily be observed in the explosion of cannons by means of pendulums, in the following manner.

# EXPERIMENT

->By how much time does sound arrive later to the listener after<-
->the light is seen?<-

You should have a pendulum prepared, of which one <term>swing</term> <note>original: "diadromus"; a single oscillation of a pendulum.</note> or vibration precisely equals one minute of time <note>In this context, Kircher likely refers to "seconds" (minuta secunda), as a pendulum swinging once per clock-minute would be nearly 12,000 feet long.</note>. Then, as soon as you see the fire of the ignited powder, make the pendulum run; and once the sound is perceived, stop it immediately. By carefully observing how many vibrations the pendulum completed between you and the place of the intervening explosion, you will have the quantity of space and the minutes of the hours. Then, if according to the rule of proportions you convert the vibrations into the paces by which the war cannon is distant from you, you will have the distance by which the sound reaches you later than the light. If you then convert the paces or miles into hours or minutes of hours, you will find by how many minutes of the hours the sound reached you later. For example: if by diligent observation you find that a space of 100 *paces* corresponds to one vibration of the pendulum, you will have, according to the "Golden Rule" <note>A historical term for the Rule of Three in mathematics, used to find a fourth value from three known ratios.</note>, the paces to which each vibration corresponds in natural order, in this manner: 10 vibrations will correspond to 1000 *paces*—that is, to one Italian mile. If you assign 15 minutes to one 1/4 part of an hour in completing this distance, you will also have the moments of time by which the sound arrives later than you. Let all these things be said based on hypothesis.

<meta>This page continues from the discussion on page 122 regarding the speed of sound and its measurement via pendulums and cannons. It transitions from the theoretical physics of sound propagation to the practical construction of acoustic devices.</meta>

->ARCHITECTURAL CONSTRUCTION OF ACOUSTIC INSTRUMENTS<-

For just as it has not yet been decided or defined whether sound proceeds uniformly in its motion, or in a "uniformly-deforming" manner—or, what is the same thing: whether it always proceeds through equal spaces, or unequal ones, with smaller and smaller increments of the intervening spaces—nothing certain can be pronounced until this very subtle and slippery matter is examined more precisely through experience, which the Florentine Academy <note>The *Accademia del Cimento* (Academy of Experiment), founded in Florence in 1657, was one of the first scientific societies to use an experimental method for physics.</note> is reported to have already discovered. For as we said previously, there are innumerable obstacles in the medium <term>medium</term> <note>original: "Intermedii"; the physical substance, such as air, through which a wave or sound travels.</note> that can hinder the speed of sound: such as wind, turbulent air, vapors, and rain, which change the medium completely. There are the seasons: Winter, Summer, Spring, and Autumn. There are the times of each day: Day, Night, Morning, and Evening; because these have different constitutions, they can certainly alter the impulse of sound in various ways, as experience has more than sufficiently taught us.

Finally, the instruments for producing sound follow. If you construct these correctly according to our instructions, you will certainly find in actual practice that they are not at all inferior to the sounds of engines of war <note>original: "tormentorum bellicorum"; referring to cannons or heavy artillery.</note>. Nothing remains, therefore, except that we take up the pen and describe their construction in order.

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.

# SECTION VII.

## ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS
## instruments, designed for producing sound 
## at a very great distance.

# CHAPTER I.

## *ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF THE CONICAL TUBE.*

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'C' at the start of the first paragraph, featuring foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>

<margin>A cylindrical tube is unsuitable for sound.</margin>
We have shown, for the reasons mentioned above, that a cylindrical tube does not serve for conducting conversation over long distances through the open air. Therefore, we have judged that a conical tube <note>Kircher is describing what we would now call a megaphone or a speaking trumpet.</note> should be selected, modeled after the shape of a trumpet <note>original: "sub forma tubæ"</note>, the construction of which can vary. We previously gave it a length of 21 palms <term>palm</term> <note>original: "palm."; a unit of length based on the hand, roughly 8 to 10 inches depending on the local standard.</note>, but later reduced it to only 15 palms, so that the machine would not become too massive and unmanageable. That 21-palm version was fixed in place and suitable for asking and answering questions, as we explained earlier in the *Preface*. Therefore, a trumpet 15 palms long will suffice for the desired effect, especially since the intensity of the speakers' shouting can easily make up for the lack of length. The small opening intended to be applied to the mouth should be 1/4 of a palm wide; however, it should be fitted to the lips in such a way that the mouth can be inserted comfortably and the voice cannot escape while someone is speaking. In its length, it should grow with a proportional increase in width for about 13 palms, up to the base of the larger flaring opening intended for the emission and diffusion of sound, as shown in the *figure on page 117*. However, I would like the reader to note that this trumpet can be made of various materials depending on the intended use. If the operation is to be conducted through the open air, it should consist of iron, brass, or wooden plates. Furthermore, the plates chosen from these materials must be polished to a high shine and brought into an exact round shape, with all roughness <note>original: "scabrities"</note> banished. For it can hardly be said how much such roughness hinders the propagation of the voice. Today, almost all trumpets of this type are usually divided into four or five parts, with the goal that one may be fitted into another for convenience...

<meta>catchword: ...ience</meta>

<summary>Kircher argues that conical tubes are superior to cylindrical ones for long-distance communication and provides specific dimensions and material requirements for constructing a "speaking trumpet." He emphasizes the need for a smooth internal surface to ensure the voice carries without interference.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, speaking trumpet, conical tube, megaphone construction, sound propagation, Athanasius Kircher, Phonurgia Nova</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for building a long-distance acoustic tube. It transitions from the physical assembly of the segments to solving the practical problems of two-way communication and privacy.</meta>

ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS INSTRUMENTS.

<margin>
Separation of the parts of the tube hinders the sound.
</margin>
...once [the segments are] inserted and extended to the proper length, the desired effect is achieved. Taught by long experience, I have observed that unless the parts are properly fitted together, the Sound-Maker <note>original: "Phonurgum"; Kircher uses this term to refer to his acoustic devices or the science of sound-work.</note> will be cheated of its purpose. It follows, as will be explained later, that if a reply must be given by the voice alone, the business will certainly not succeed, since the sound, striking against the joints of the joined parts, loses much of its force and efficacy. If anyone should wish to insert tubes of this kind permanently into a wall, or even into rooms, he will achieve the best success if they are either carved out of living rock and polished smooth, or constructed from iron plates and then coated on the inside with melted pitch. For on a conical concave surface, once it has been smoothed and polished as well as possible with pitch or a gummy liquid, the sounds can be propagated to the designated place without any obstacle.

# CHAPTER II.

## ON ADAPTING THE TUBES SO THAT *one may respond to questions by the voice alone, without another tube.*

Two defects occur in tubes of this kind. The first is that a response cannot be given to questions without the benefit of a second tube. The other is that nothing private—or what is most intended, a secret—can be communicated to another in specific words through the tube without everyone situated in the middle space, from the starting point <note>original: "à termino à quo"</note> to the destination <note>original: "ad quem"</note>, understanding it; thus, an instrument of this kind cannot be of use in military matters <note>original: "bellicis negotijs"</note> of great importance. Recognizing this kind of defect long ago, I never held the proposed tubes in high regard for that reason, but I decided they should be so adapted that a reply could be given even without a second tube, and no one except the person situated in the room would understand what was being said. Regarding these matters, a more extensive subject for discussion will be provided in the following pages; for now, I thought it necessary to explain only how a tube can be prepared so that someone can respond to questions with the voice alone, without the help of another tube.

There should be no doubt in anyone's mind that someone can receive a response through the same tube through which the speech is made, because...

<meta>catchword: expe- (likely "experientia" or "expectatio")</meta>

<summary>Kircher discusses the technical requirements for building effective acoustic tubes, emphasizing that joints must be perfectly smooth to prevent sound loss. He also identifies two major flaws in current designs: the need for a second tube for replies and a lack of privacy for military or sensitive communications.</summary>
<keywords>acoustic engineering, sound propagation, megaphone, military secrets, architectural acoustics, Phonurgus, speaking tubes</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from the previous page regarding the challenges of using acoustic tubes for private or long-distance communication. While the previous page ended mid-word discussing how listeners in the middle might "understand" the secrets being whispered, this page moves directly into the practical requirements for successful transmission and reception.</meta>

...standing, has long been known to me through experience. I say, therefore, that to achieve this, the following must be considered: *First*, the distance, or the *destination point* <note>original: "terminus ad quem"</note>; *Second*, the precise direction of the tube along the extension of the acoustic line, from the *starting point* <note>original: "a termino a quo"</note> to the *destination point*; *Third*, a suitable time, free and clear of all atmospheric disturbance; *Fourth*, a greater intensity of voice in the person responding.

The distance must be weighed first, according to what we said a little earlier, then the intensity of the voice of the person speaking through the tube. For example: let the distance of the place be 2 *miles*, and let the intensity of the voice from the ordinary and accustomed manner of speaking be set as 1. A rule is established in this way: if 2 *miles* require a voice intensity of 1 through the tube, how much intensity do 3 miles require? After performing the calculation, you will find the voice intensity to be 1 ½, and so on for the rest. Although I do not believe this rule must be followed precisely, any voice louder and more forceful than the one coming through the tube is sufficient for hearing the responses of the speaker without a second tube, using only the voice (which, as I said, must be stronger).

*The reason is* that the sound of the tube is carried to the object in the same proportion—by the spreading of the audible forms <note>original: "specierum audibilium"; in Kircher's acoustic theory, "species" or "forms" were physical images of sound that traveled through the air.</note> gathered within the tube—as a louder voice travels through the open air. As soon as this voice hits the mouth of the tube, it is multiplied there and finally reaches the ears of the Sound-Maker <note>original: "Phonurgi"</note> as complete verbal concepts, especially if he presses his ears closely to the opening. For whatever is received is received according to the capacity of the receiver <note>This is a reference to a common Scholastic philosophical axiom: "quicquid recipitur per modum recipientis recipitur."</note>, which we discovered through many experiments with voices at *Mount Eustachio* <note>Kircher is likely referring to his experiments at the Mentorella sanctuary on Monte Guadagnolo, which he called the "Eustachian Mountain" after the saint associated with the site.</note>. But the matter will be made clear by an example.

Let the speakers be *Sempronius* and *Titius*; let the Tube through which you wish to speak to your correspondent be *A B*. Let the location or *destination* <note>original: "terminus ad quem"</note> be *C*. Let *Titius*, the respondent, stand exactly at point *I*, and let the tube *A B* be directed precisely toward this point along the line of the acoustic axis *A I*. I say that *Titius* standing at *I*, by projecting his voice a little more loudly into *A*, will be heard by *Sempronius*, provided his ears are properly pressed to the opening *A*. For the forms <note>original: "species"</note> of the voice spread from *A* through the tube will return to the tube *B A* in the same way, and with the same speed, as they were sent out through tube *A B*. I said this provided that *Titius* uses a stronger voice, and at a suitable and calm time. For it has already been more than proven that nothing can be achieved with tubes when the air is turbulent or weighed down by harsh weather. It is therefore necessary for the respondent to use a slightly stronger voice, so that, having broken through the obstacles of the Medium <note>The "Medium" refers to the air or the physical space through which sound must travel.</note>, the sound forms may reach the tube *B A* fr- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword indicates the word is "liberius," meaning "more freely."</note>

<summary>Kircher outlines the mathematical and physical conditions required for two-way communication through a single acoustic tube, emphasizing the need for a louder response to overcome atmospheric resistance. He cites his own experiments at Mount Eustachio to support his theory of how sound "forms" travel through a medium.</summary>

### ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS INSTRUMENTS.

...more freely, so that once they have arrived, having been amplified within the tube, they present themselves as clearly as possible to the ears applied to opening A for *Sempronius* <note>Sempronius is a traditional placeholder name in Latin texts, similar to "John Doe" or "Person B."</note>; for a louder voice will easily compensate for the lack of a second tube in the transmission of the species <term>species: in 17th-century science, "species" referred to the physical forms or vibrations that carry sensory information, like sound or light, through a medium.</term>. Regarding the timing of this: I certainly consider the quiet, serene, and tranquil state of the nighttime to be most suitable and opportune for this business; and I should not fail to mention something that will be of no small help: namely, if the opening of the tube at B is made with a slightly wider flared rim <note>original: "umbone"</note>, as frequent experience has taught us. And in this way, someone will be able to give responses with the voice alone, without the help of a second tube.

<margin>By what art someone may talk secretly through a tube.</margin>
How acoustic tubes <term>tubæ acuſticæ: literally "acoustic trumpets," these were long, often coiled pipes used for listening or speaking over distances.</term> should be arranged so that secret plans can be communicated to one another, so that no one in the spaces between can understand what is being said, we shall teach in the following sections. For tubes of this kind, which receive a response by voice alone, achieve a better effect when they are fixed and stable, led through one room and inserted into the innermost corner of another room, as I myself have experienced for many years in my own room, to the shock and wonder of those listening.

He also introduces the concept of "secret" tubes installed within building walls for private conversation, a device he famously used to surprise guests.</summary>

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.

# CHAPTER III.

## ON THE MANIFOLD INCREASE OF SOUND IN *the said Trumpets, or on Trumpets of prodigious sound, and their various types and forms.*

It is certain, and discovered by me through experience, that the sound of a simple trumpet of this kind—for example, 15 palms long <note>A "palm" was a traditional unit of measurement based on the width of the hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches depending on the region.</note>—which is otherwise strong, can be increased immensely, so that what at first reached 2, 3, or 4 *miles* with its voice, might now be propagated to 15 or even 20 *miles*. How this can indeed be done: first I will discuss the different forms of trumpets, then the increase given to them through additional instruments.

### *ON THE SPHERICAL TRUMPET.*

The Spherical Trumpet is the same as a hollow globe. If someone, therefore, were to have a globe made with a diameter of 10 or 15 palms—as we shall say of the horn of ALEXANDER *the Great* <note>Kircher frequently cites the legendary "Horn of Alexander," which was said to be able to summon his entire army from miles away.</note> in the following pages—he would undoubtedly achieve an immense multiplication of sound, as was evident from the proposition; for sound in a hollow space would acquire greater force due to the immense reflection of the voice created within it, compared to a straight trumpet, where a simple diffusion of sound occurs.

<image-desc>A technical diagram of a "Tuba Sphoerica" (Spherical Trumpet). It features a large central sphere labeled 'A' filled with concentric circular lines representing sound waves or echoes. On the left side, a narrow tube labeled 'B' (the mouthpiece) enters the sphere. On the right, a flared, cone-shaped horn labeled 'C' serves as the output. The diagram illustrates how sound waves reflect within the spherical chamber before being directed out.</image-desc>

Let A be the Spherical globe, B the mouth intended for sounding the voice, and C the other orifice spread out into a wide flare <note>original: "umbonem," referring to a rounded projection or boss, like that on a shield.</note>; it will happen that any voice sounded within will strike as many points on the concave surface of the sphere, producing as many reflections, which will then be diffused through C with a great roar in circles that expand themselves. But the *Reader* will perceive these things better from the present *figure* than I could describe in many words.

<summary>Athanasius Kircher explains how sound can be amplified to travel vast distances (up to 20 miles) using specialized acoustic instruments, focusing on the "Spherical Trumpet" which uses a hollow globe to create multiple internal reflections.</summary>
<keywords>acoustic amplification, Spherical Trumpet, sound reflection, Alexander the Great, Phonurgia, early acoustics</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general concept of sound multiplication to the specific geometric application of the ellipse in acoustic design. It builds on the previous chapter's discussion of spherical trumpets by introducing a more focused and efficient shape for sound reflection.</meta>

->ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS INSTRUMENTS.<-

# CHAPTER IV.
## *ON THE ELLIPTICAL TUBE.*

What an Ellipse is, and what an Elliptical tube is, was explained in the preceding pages; likewise, we taught how such a figure is described in Section 1. This figure has two points on its major diameter <term>diameter: the longest axis crossing the center of the ellipse</term> determined by comparison, which are also called **foci** <note>original: "foci". In geometry, these are the two fixed points that define the shape of an ellipse. Kircher uses the analogy of "burning points" where light or sound concentrates.</note>, because just as all the sun's rays united in these two points possess a most great and marvelous power to kindle fire, so the reflection of phonic lines <note>original: "linearum phonicarum". These are lines representing the path of sound waves, similar to how we treat light rays in optics.</note> created and united in them acquires a greater power in multiplying sound than all others. But let us explain the proposal.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram of an elliptical sound tube. An ellipse is depicted with two focal points labeled A and B. Multiple lines representing sound rays originate from focus A, strike various points on the internal circumference (labeled T, V, S, O), and reflect to converge at focus B. The top of the tube is labeled C, R, E, and the bottom narrows to a base labeled X. The points F and D mark the lower curve of the ellipse.</image-desc>

Let the present Ellipse be **C E F D**, and the points of the foci determined by comparison be **A** and **B**. I say that voices produced from point **A** or **B** toward the elliptical circumference will be united there by innumerable reflections of the voice at point **B**. In this way, the voice reflected at **O** will be reflected from there to **B**; again from **A** to **V**, and from there to **B**; again from **S** and **T** to **B**; and in this way, from every single point of the elliptical circumference **C E F D**, reflections will occur which will only unite at **B** (or from **B** at **A**), where a violent and most powerful multiplication of sound will take place. Since, indeed, in all elliptical figures—whether sharp or blunt—the property of reflection is the same, it is possible for a trumpet ten or more palms long <note>A "palm" was a traditional unit of measurement, roughly 3 to 4 inches.</note> to be fashioned in an elliptical shape, out of any material, whose foci are **A** and **B**; and you will have a trumpet increased by a marvelous multiplication of sounds, as the present figure shows, especially if it is made by a skilled craftsman. For it is difficult to give metallic material such an exact elliptical shape, especially if one wishes it to be portable; the business will be more easily accomplished by means of an **Ellipsioplasta** <note>original: "Ellipsioplastam". This refers to a specialized tool or machine Kircher designed for drawing or molding perfect ellipses.</note> mold, which [we described] above—

->R<-
<meta>catchword: explain-</meta>

<summary>This page explains the acoustic properties of elliptical tubes, demonstrating how sound produced at one focal point reflects off the walls to concentrate intensely at the second focal point. Kircher argues that this geometry is superior for amplifying sound, though he acknowledges the technical difficulty of manufacturing such precise shapes in metal.</summary>

...we have explained, if he introduces the shape of a trumpet into plaster; the task, I admit, requires a craftsman endowed with genius and skill. If he performs it correctly, he himself will marvel at how much power and efficacy the device acquires. Even the slightest voice, almost imperceptible, when whispered into orifice **X**, will be perceived as loud at **R**. Furthermore, if a louder voice is sounded into the tube, it will stir up such a clamor that it will make itself heard even at several miles' distance.

## CHAPTER V.

->*ON THE SPIRAL TUBE.*<-

What a spiral tube <note>original: "tubus cochleatus". From the Latin *cochlea*, meaning snail or snail-shell; referring here to any tube with a helical or spiral winding.</note> is, and what a spiral is, has already been stated quite often; but for the sake of the *Reader*, let us repeat the definition. A spiral tube is nothing other than a tube twisted into a spiral; it is of two kinds: Cylindrical and Conical. The Cylindrical kind is that which always maintains a uniform spiral shape, set between sides parallel to the axis. Almost all spiral staircases <note>original: "schalæ cochleares". These are common architectural examples Kircher uses to demonstrate how sound travels along curved surfaces.</note> are of this type, in which frequent experience has taught us this: that no one at the very bottom of the spiral, with their mouth applied to the spiral surface, can speak in so low a voice that a corresponding person at the highest vault of the spiral, with their ears applied to the surface, does not perceive it. This holds provided that the surface of the spiral is so smoothly continued that it is not hindered by even the smallest obstacle from the propagation of the sound. Let this be said against those who, being inexperienced, doubt whether an articulate voice can be propagated through a spiral tube.

The Conical kind is that which has a spiral evolved from a narrow point, always and continually into a wider space by a proportional increase, constructed according to nature's archetype <note>original: "ectypon". A model or mold copied from nature.</note>, which is expressed in the shells of snails, or better yet, in sea trumpets <note>original: "buccinis marinis". These are large gastropod shells, like conchs, often used as natural trumpets.</note>. The cylindrical spiral tube presents no difficulty in its manufacture: for it is sufficient to twist a cylinder into a spiral passage, as is done in the *Archimedean Machine* <term>Archimedean Machine: the Archimedean screw, a device historically used to raise water by rotating a spiral within a cylinder</term>, which is called a water-screw, different forms of which are seen in my Museum. But a spiral tube is Conical when the tube is twisted into a spiral conically, as was said a little before—that is, from a narrow space into a continually wider space, as happens in sea trumpets; and the manufacture of this requires a skillful mastery and a clever mind, so that it may be formed under the proper proportions of amplitude and width...

R
<meta>catchword: propor-</meta>

<summary>Kircher defines the spiral (cochleate) tube, distinguishing between cylindrical and conical forms while using spiral staircases and sea shells as acoustic models. He asserts that sound travels efficiently along these curved surfaces, even over long distances or from very faint whispers.</summary>

...proportion, and constructed with an equal smoothness of the internal surface. The different shapes of each follow below.

->The Spiral Tube.<-

<image-desc>A technical illustration of a conical, spiral-grooved horn or tube labeled "Tubus Cochleatus". The narrow end (mouthpiece) is marked with the letter 'A' and the wide end (bell) is marked with 'B'.</image-desc>

This spiral tube <note>original: "Tubus cochleatus". As noted previously, Kircher uses the snail-shell as a primary model for acoustic amplification.</note> deservedly surpasses the power and efficacy of all other tubes. This is due to a certain infinite multiplication of the voice, which is reflected from every point of the spiral into every other point of the same; this effect is clearly seen in the very loud sound of the Sea Trumpet <note>original: "buccina Marina". This refers to the large, spiral-shaped shells of tritons or conchs used since antiquity as horns.</note>. If, therefore, the sound can be so great within the small coils of a shell, how much greater might it be in the tube just described? This is especially true since we have already demonstrated in the preceding sections that the spiral <note>original: "cochlidem"</note> expresses something of a parabolic *shape* within its helical surface, which increases the voice in a wonderful manner.

## CHAPTER VI.

### ON TUBES WHICH PRODUCE ONLY A POWERFUL
### sound, but are unable to give articulate speech.

There are some tubes that can indeed produce a very strong sound extending over many miles, yet without any propagation of articulate speech <note>By "articulate speech," Kircher means the ability to transmit recognizable words and consonants, rather than just a tonal blast.</note>. Of this kind are: military trumpets, horns, and signal flutes, which they call cornetts; drums, and all those instruments of the "Trumpeters" (as Frontinus <note>Sextus Julius Frontinus (c. 40–103 AD), a Roman civil engineer and military author.</note> calls them), the pipers, and the horn-blowers. From these, you cannot draw a sound except by a violent contortion of the mouth and lips through the *mouthpiece* <note>original: "Salpingium". A Latinized Greek term for the mouthpiece of a trumpet.</note>—for so we shall hereafter call the orifice of those instruments used by military "Sound-makers" <note>original: "Phonurgi". This is Kircher's specific term for those who manipulate or "work" sound.</note>, or in German: **The Mouthpiece** <note>original: "Das Mundstuck".</note>. In trumpets and horns, this piece is inseparable, while in others it is detachable and can be moved from one instrument to another. Having established these points, let us now see what power and efficacy instruments of this kind possess.

### §. I.

### ON THE HORN OF ALEXANDER THE GREAT.

Many lustra <note>original: "lustra". A lustrum is a five-year period; Kircher means several decades have passed.</note> have now gone by since I, while acting as an *Oedipus* <note>A metaphor for a solver of riddles, referencing his famous work on Egyptian hieroglyphs, *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*.</note> for the hieroglyphs in the *Vatican Library*, happened by chance upon a book titled: *The Secrets of* ARISTOTLE *to* ALEXANDER *the Great* <note>original: "Secreta ARISTOTELIS ad ALEXANDRUM Magnum". This refers to the *Secretum Secretorum*, a pseudo-Aristotelian treatise popular in the Middle Ages.</note>; where among other things concerning the prodigious Horn of ALEXANDER *the Great*, these words are read: "He made this horn produce so vehement a sound that he is said to have summoned his army by it even when dispersed over a hundred stadia <note>original: "stadia". An ancient unit of distance; a hundred stadia is roughly 11.5 miles or 18.5 kilometers.</note> (eight of which make one Italian mile)." It had, however, as the booklet shows, five cubits <note>original: "cubitos". A cubit is an ancient unit of length; Kircher's measurement here would make the horn about 7.5 to 9 feet in diameter.</note> in diameter; and it was suspended from a ring on a support, as I believe, though the book does not describe its figure. ...worthy of note, that whenever they enter a town and sound the horn, the cows immediately leave their stalls, stirred by the blast of the horn; and having become accustomed to its sound, they go out to the pastures without any further prompting from the servants.

<column-break/>

And when the horn signal is given toward evening, each cow returns of its own accord to the same home it left in the morning, following the same order. Since, therefore, the Horn of **ALEXANDER the Great** extended its sound even beyond a hundred <note>The Latin *stadium* was an ancient unit of distance; 100 stadia is roughly 11.5 miles or 18.5 kilometers.</note> stadia, while a common cow-horn creates a massive noise up to one or two miles; who could deny, arguing from the greater to the lesser, that the Horn of **ALEXANDER the Great**—being a much larger machine—must have produced a truly powerful sound? And not without reason: for since it measured five cubits, or 15 <term>palms</term> <note>A palm was a unit of measure based on the width of the hand, roughly 3 to 4 inches.</note> in circumference, it necessarily measured about 45 <term>palms</term> in length, according to the ratio of diameter to circumference. But how the <term>Phonurgus</term> <note>From the Greek, meaning a 'sound-maker' or the technician operating the instrument.</note> was able to hold it in his hands, or whether it was suspended, the Little Book <note>Likely referring to a specific historical source or manual Kircher is citing.</note> does not say. This method occurs to us: we say the instrument was suspended by a ring from a foldable tripod base (marked S T V), and was able to be rotated in every direction toward whichever region it was intended to be aimed, as the figure placed here demonstrates. Whatever the case, we hold for certain that if one were to stretch out the middle of such a horn to ten palms, as horn A B C D shows; I say that it would produce, if not a greater, then at least an equal or even clearer sound. For it is certain that in the former [large horn], the <term>acoustic species</term> <note>original: *species phonicas*. In 17th-century science, "species" were the physical forms or "images" of sound or light traveling through a medium.</note> would not emerge so completely and clearly due to the excessive confusion of the acoustic forms—which does not happen in this halved horn. Although, I do not deny that in the horn of **ALEXANDER**, the sound at the wide opening was twice as strong as the horn A B C D just described, due to the meeting of the acoustic lines united on both sides in space I—though not with that clarity I mentioned.

### COROLLARY II.

Both sacred and secular histories show us that the ancient *Hebrews* in wars used trumpets twisted into various spiral lines, as can be seen in the monuments of the ancient *Hebrews*. Furthermore, the *Romans* used similar trumpets both in battle and in Triumphs, as demonstrated by the old monuments of Antiquity, tombs, and Triumphal arches. There should be no doubt that these excited a massive noise; post-riders <note>Historical mail carriers who blew horns to signal their arrival at a station.</note> are also accustomed to use them, though in a smaller form. By these sounds, even those situated at a great distance might signal their proximity to the post-house. But these matters are too well known to require further description.

->§. II.<-

## ON THE DRUM-TUBE.

No one is unaware that the war-drum emits a powerful sound, perceptible over a great distance. If anyone wishes to increase this to the highest degree, he shall proceed thus: Let a cylindrical tube be made ten <term>palms</term> <note>A unit of measurement; ten palms is roughly 7.5 feet or 2.3 meters.</note> in length and three palms in diameter. As is usually done with common drums, you shall cover one end with the customary animal skin; then, at a distance of 4 or 5 *palms*, you shall install another skin, just as in common drums. Next, with either the cylinder or a cone continued for 5, 6, or 7 *palms*, the channel should end in a flared opening. You shall also tighten the entire surface with cords in the usual manner, and you will obtain a marvelous drum, perfect in every respect. As soon as the drummer, having placed it upon slender supports, strikes it with mallets, the sound, communicated to the entire channel, [will produce] an immense...

The amplification of this will be greatly increased by the vibration of the drum tightened by strings, as we have shown in the preceding sections.

#### §. III.
### ON THE SERPENTINE TUBE.

This instrument is very commonly used in *France*, which they commonly call the *serpent* <note>The serpent is a low-register wind instrument, descended from the cornett, with a characteristically snakelike shape.</note>, and it emits a sufficiently powerful sound that is exceedingly suitable and appropriate for the bass voice. It has a length of 3 palms; but if it were stretched out to 10 or 15 palms, I do not doubt that it would produce a sound equal to that of the spiral-shaped tube <note>original: "tubi cochleati" - referring to the spiral "Horn of Alexander" discussed previously.</note>.

PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.

# CHAPTER VIII.
## ON THE INCREASE OF PERCEPTIBLE SOUNDS
### *by attaching various instruments to the mouth of the Tube, whatever its shape might be.*

Just as one light added to another wonderfully increases the total light in proportion to the additional source; let me explain: suppose there is a lamp that fully illuminates a nearby wall. If that lamp is then enclosed within a tube, it will illuminate the said wall more fully through the union of its *actinobolismus* <note>original: "actinobolismi"; a term Kircher used to describe the radiant emission or "throwing out" of rays of light or sound</note>, or radiant projection. If, furthermore, you place a concave mirror behind the lamp in a proportionate position, you will see the wall—along with any figures inscribed upon it—illuminated as fully as possible, just as we have taught at length in the 10th book of *The Great Art of Light and Shadow, concerning the preparation of the Magic Lantern.* <note>Kircher refers here to his famous 1646 work *Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae*, where he described the precursor to the modern slide projector.</note>

What has been said regarding the increase of light, I wish to be said similarly regarding the increase of sound constrained within tubes, since the radiant *species* <note>In 17th-century physics, "species" were the invisible forms or "images" of sound and light that traveled through a medium to the senses.</note> of sound perfectly imitate the radiant projections of light in all respects, as was stated in the preceding sections. However, I speak here only of propagating inarticulate sound <note>Meaning raw noise, signals, or musical notes rather than speech.</note> across any distance, by which you might later be able to secretly reveal whatever you wish to a correspondent through the sound itself, in place of an inarticulate voice. There are, moreover, various ways and methods of multiplying sound immensely: and first, there are certain instruments of more powerful sound which, when applied to the tubes already explained in the preceding sections, achieve the desired amplification <note>original Greek: αὔξησιν (auxēsin)</note>; these we shall describe in order.

And first, let a concave sphere be made, provided with its own openings, which may be attached to any tube described previously, as appears in the figure placed below. <note>The author notes a figure "supposita" (placed below), though the physical illustration may appear on the following page or have been omitted in this printing.</note>

Let the concave sphere be constructed of glass, wood, or metal, labeled A B C D. Let the openings be A and C. The Tube, however, may be simple, elliptical, or coiled, labeled C G H. I say that a voice blown through opening A will be immensely increased, according to the proportion of the tube's stronger or weaker sound; for the sound, increased by multiple circulation within the sphere A B C D, will likewise communicate its power to the tube, from which a wonderful increase of sound must necessarily result—and this is true for articulate sound as well as 

<meta>catchword: as well as (quàm)</meta>

as it is fit and suitable for the pronunciation of an inarticulate voice. <note>In this context, "inarticulate voice" refers to raw sound or musical tones rather than distinct speech.</note>

<image-desc>A detailed drawing of an acoustic instrument, likely an amplification tube or megaphone. It features a large, bulbous resonator on the left with points labeled A, B, C, and D. A is at the left tip, B is at the top, D is at the bottom, and C is at the junction where the bulb meets the tube. A long, tapering tube labeled G extends to the right, ending in a wide, flared bell labeled H.</image-desc>

*Second.* If to the aforementioned tubes you insert either a *salpingium* <note>original: "salpingium"; a Greek-derived term for the flared bell or orifice of a trumpet.</note>—that is, an opening similar to that of a war trumpet—or the trumpet itself, *as figure A shows*, and the *Phonurgus* <term>Phonurgus: A term coined by Kircher meaning "sound-worker" or the operator of an acoustic device.</term> sounds the trumpet with a blast from his mouth and pursed lips, there is no doubt that he will rouse a fearsome sound, to the shock and wonder of all present. For if an ordinary trumpet is so easily heard at such a remote distance, even up to a thousand paces, to how great a distance do you think that sound will extend when it is joined to a tube that is already powerful for multiplying sound?

*Third.* There are vessels crafted in an elliptical shape, *as figure B teaches*, which, when applied to any tube, will increase the intensity of the sound in a marvelous way. The same result will be achieved by a sea-shell horn [C] attached to the tube, or a herdsman’s horn [D], when these instruments are brought to life by sound. But since I leave these things for the wise Reader to consider further, I have judged that they should not be so much recounted in detail as merely suggested.

## CHAPTER IX.

### ON SECRECY THROUGH INARTICULATE SOUND
->*to be signaled to another correspondent.*<-

The tubes described thus far are for use in the open, and the things whispered into them in a loud voice can be understood by anyone nearby. Because of this, many people have already argued with me regarding how, in those spaces where the articulated voice <note>Speech consisting of distinct words and syllables.</note> cannot reach, the hidden thoughts of the mind might be revealed to another person through the sound of these very instruments—so that while everyone may hear the sound, they do not know what the sound means. This matter is surely of great importance in the tumults of war. For what is more fitting, indeed more necessary, than that which...

A: A tube that spreads sound by means of an attached trumpet.
B: An elliptical tube that spreads sound by means of an elliptical trumpet attached above.
C: A spiral tube <note>original: *tubus cochleatus*; literally "snail-shell shaped," referring to the internal winding of the instrument to increase the sound's path.</note> that spreads sound by means of a horn attached above.
D: A simple tube propagating sound through a horn placed on top.

<margin>A strategy by which the Turkish army can be thrown into panic.</margin>
...which you cannot safely signal to another without writing, especially while enemy armies are mixed with friendly ones through spies. Therefore, these kinds of tubes with more powerful sound serve not only for gathering and uniting armies, but also for indicating the hidden plans of the *Commander-in-Chief* <term>Archistrategus: A Greek-derived term for a supreme military leader or Generalissimo.</term> to his officers and leaders through distinct sounds. They also serve to dismay and put to flight the Turkish armies; by sounding words previously composed in the Arabic or Turkish language, the enemies are thrown into consternation when they hear a sound as if sent from heaven, while remaining ignorant of where it originates. Many similar feats can be achieved through the use of these tubes, the execution of which I leave to those whom it concerns. Let us now proceed to what we promised to show at the beginning: acoustic secret-communication <term>Cryptologia phonica: The art of sending hidden or encrypted messages using sound instead of writing.</term>.

Countless methods and ways are handed down by various authors for communicating the secret plans of the mind to another, which we have described as extensively as possible in the third section of our *Universal Polygraphy* <note>Kircher refers here to his 1663 work *Polygraphia nova et universalis*, a foundational text on cryptography and universal languages.</note>. However, omitting those in this place, let us demonstrate only those which pertain to communicating secrets to those located at a remote distance through sounds.

# ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS INSTRUMENTS.

...only a very few words can be transmitted due to the multitude of operations, and the matter is also exposed to massive errors. For example: those surrounded by a siege will be able to ask for these things, as follows:

| **The request of those besieged in a City or Fortress.** | **Response.** |
|----------|----------|
| 1. *We are surrounded by a tight siege.* | 1. *Resist, and we will come to your aid.* |
| 2. <note>The following four lines are grouped together in the original as a single category of distress.</note><br>*Gunpowder* <note>original: *Pulvis pyrius*; literally "fire powder."</note> *is lacking.*<br>*Small provisions* <note>original: *annona*; refers to the grain supply or military rations.</note> *remain for us.*<br>*Soldiers are failing through slaughter,*<br>*or are falling ill.* | 2. *We will send powder through the river, as well as provisions and soldiers.* |
| 3. *The enemy prepares an assault tomorrow.* | 3. *We will prevent it, attacking the enemy from behind.* |
| 4. *Unless you hinder them, we must come to terms* <note>original: *ad pacta veniendum*; implying surrender negotiations.</note>. | 4. *Delay the agreement until we can help.* |
| 5. *We cannot hold out beyond eight days.* | 5. *Sustain yourselves as much as you can, and we will provide relief.* |
| 6. *The citizens are trembling and refuse to fight.* | 6. *The rebels will pay* <note>original: *luent*; meaning they will be punished or "atone."</note> *when we arrive.* |
| 7. *Everyone judges that the City must be surrendered by agreement.* | 7. *If you can do nothing else, agree on good terms.* |

I am only showing the method here by which one must proceed in this business. Any Captain can arrange more points of this kind by numbers, from which questions and answers can be given on both sides. For example: If they are surrounded by a siege, as the first point shows, then only one blast of a more powerful sound should be given through the acoustic tube <term>tubo phonico: The "phonic tube" or speaking trumpet Kircher has been developing throughout this section.</term>, and the friendly army will easily understand the meaning. If powder, provisions, and soldiers are lacking, they will sound the tube twice with a prolonged voice. If the enemy prepares an assault, they will give a sound through the tube a third time, and so on for the rest, up to the 7th, where 7 blasts of the tube with continuous voices will signal whether they should agree to terms. ## COROLLARY.

*From this it follows: How in any Island—such as Sicily, Sardinia, Malta, and similar places—whatever hostility might occur in the surrounding sea (whether you consider the raids of Turks or Pirates <note>In the 17th century, the Mediterranean was frequently patrolled by Ottoman fleets and Barbary pirates; coastal defense was a matter of extreme urgency.</note>), they could signal to one another not with mute signs, but with living voices through acoustic instruments of this kind from towers or watchtowers. For I have heard, and seen with my own eyes on the shore of the Sicilian sea, very frequent watchtowers <note>original: *speculas*</note> of this kind separated by no more than 3, or at most 5, miles. When I asked the reason for this, the sailors replied: These were built so close to each other for this purpose, so that if the guards in the towers noticed hostile ships, the guards of the remaining towers in order could warn of the enemy's arrival—using smoke by day, but fire by night. In this way, they signify what is happening at sea to the whole Island within a few hours. However, so that the matter may appear more clearly, I have decided it timely to display here the circumference of the entire Island of Sicily together with its towers and watchtowers.*

Let the "Three-Cornered Island" <note>original: *Insula Triquetra*; a reference to Sicily’s triangular shape.</note>, or Trinacria—I mean Sicily—be **A B C.** It is bounded by three promontories: the first, **A.**, is called *Lylibaeum* <note>Modern Marsala.</note> by geographers; the second, **B.**, is *Pelorum* <note>Cape Peloro, near Messina.</note>; the third, **C.**, is *Pachinum* <note>Cape Passero.</note>. The towers by which the Island is surrounded are marked **O.**, separated by 3, 4, or at most 5 *miles*. These things having been established: if the guards who preside at the Lighthouse <note>original: *Pharo*</note> of *Pelorum* observe friendly ships, they show a stationary fire-pot <note>original: *lebetem*; a cauldron or large bowl.</note> filled with pitch and resin and set ablaze, which is a sign of safety. But if they see hostile ships, they raise and lower the fire—or cover it with a cloth—as many times as they have spotted ships. In this way, they give signals from tower to tower, and from there to the rest following in order, until within a few hours they have revealed the event to all of *Sicily* through signs of this kind, which I have heard also happens in other places. Nevertheless, anyone can see that this could be done better through our tubes of stronger sound; since not

# COROLLARY II.

From this it follows: how, with the aid of tubes of this kind, mutual communication <note>original: *commercium*</note> could be established between the most remote regions. This would be done by first selecting intermediate stations for this purpose, whether they be higher mountains, towns, or the taller watchtowers <term>watchtowers<note>original: *speculæ*; elevated observation posts designed for signaling.</note></term> with which all the roads in **Italy** are filled. For example, a matter of great importance in **Rome** could be reported within two hours to **Florence** or **Loreto**, and from there to **Venice** by this practice. This could happen if the intermediate distances were correctly determined, and if the sound-workers <term>sound-workers<note>original: *Phonurgi*; the author's term for specialists trained in the science and operation of acoustic communication devices.</note></term> were to meet at a designated time according to a prior agreement; but these things are easier to conceive than to describe with further words.

## CONCERNING VARIOUS OTHER WONDERFUL DEVICES WHICH

### ARTIFICE I.

## TO PRESENT THE MOST PLEASANT MUSIC, PROVIDED BY
*both voices and every kind of instrument, at a distance of two or three miles, so that no one can conceive from where it originates.*

This music requires a separate place, built with deliberate effort in the Palace of some curious Prince <note>In the 17th century, a "curious" Prince was one who patronized the "sciences of curiosities"—early modern physics, mechanics, and natural wonders.</note>. Let the Prince's Palace be denoted by A B C D. Let a space be made within the Palace, separated from all others, marked E F G P; and in it, a vault of plaster shaped into the concave surface of a sphere, worked as smoothly as possible in a circular-molded form <term>circular-molded<note>original: *cycloplaſticâ*; likely referring to a perfectly spherical or parabolic molding technique intended to reflect sound waves toward a single point.</note></term>, as marked by F G S H. At the cut F I H, let the space situated below, F I G P, be assigned to Singers, Flutists <term>flutists<note>original: *aulædis*; specifically players of the *aulos*, a double-reed instrument, but used here generally for woodwind players.</note></term>, and other Instrumentalists <term>instrumentalists<note>original: *organedis*; musicians who play "organs" or instruments in general.</note></term>.

This receptacle should have a secret door K, and a glass window through which the place can be sufficiently illuminated. When all these things are prepared, let one of the aforementioned tubes be extended, having a length of 22 palms <note>Approximately 16 to 18 feet, depending on the specific regional "palm" measurement used by Kircher.</note>, such as the tube I L M N. This tube should be joined at L and I with the circular surface of the vault, with all angular roughness removed; it is then extended through the interior of the house to its final terminus, where a wide opening N O, directed toward a certain place frequented by people, will present the music to everyone’s amazement.

For when the musicians have entered within the spherical vault I F G H through the small door K, and the door has been tightly closed, as soon as they begin to sing—behold!—the harmonic sound will be wonderfully intensified in that enclosed space. Acquiring the greatest increase through various reflections made within the vault, and since it cannot release itself except through the tube L I M N O, the sound will pour out through that tube with a voice even louder than it first acquired. It will exit through the opening N O into the designated place, even at a distance of two or three miles, with no one in the intermediate space failing to perceive this prodigious music, as all are struck speechless with wonder,

Therefore, when you are about to present music to listeners, direct the machine by its opening **E A F** upon its swiveling base against the wind, which ought to be quite strong. This wind, entering the tube and its opening **E A F** with great force, will stir the strings stretched in the mechanism **I K G H** into a harmonious sound. This sound, then greatly amplified in the rest of the tube and diffused into distant spaces through the opening **L M**, will be perceived in the manner of wailing and lamenting, to the amazement and wonder of all. ## DEVICE III.

->TO CONSTRUCT ANOTHER AUTOMATIC HARMONIC<-
->*machine, which by no service of wheels, bellows, or*<-
->*phonotactic* <term>cylinder<note>original: *Cylindri phonotactici*; a pinned barrel used in early mechanical instruments like music boxes to "program" the melody</note></term> *but by the wind and air alone, excites*<-

This invention is as new as it is altogether easy and pleasant, and in my <term>Museum<note>The Museum Kircherianum in Rome, where Kircher displayed his many inventions and curiosities</note></term> it is perceived with the greatest admiration by listeners. The instrument remains silent as long as the window is closed; but as soon as it is opened, behold! A certain harmonious sound suddenly arising makes everyone as if thunderstruck, since they cannot know where the sound comes from, or what kind of instrument it is. For it resembles neither stringed instruments nor wind instruments, but conveys a certain intermediate and altogether foreign sound. The instrument is made in this way: Let an instrument be made from very resonant pine wood, from which stringed instruments are usually made, 5 <term>palmi<note>A 'palm' is a traditional unit of length; in Rome, it was approximately 223 millimeters or 8.8 inches</note></term> long, two wide, and one deep. You should equip this instrument with 15 equal strings made from animal intestines, or even more, as shown in the present figure.

The instrument is **A B C D**. The tuning pegs are **C A**. The bridges are **I K** and **S D**. The strings are wound around the pegs and drawn across the bridges **I K** and **S D**; they are fastened with pins at **B D**. The <term>roses<note>original: *Rosæ*; decorative carved sound-holes commonly found on lutes and harpsichords</note></term> are **F F F**. **S** is the handle by which it can be hung. There remains the tuning,

### PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.

It is almost a <note>original: *παράδοξον* (paradoxon)</note> paradox how strings tuned in unison or in octaves can constitute a diverse harmony. However, so that this musical <note>original: *φαινόμενον* (phainomenon)</note> phenomenon—which I do not know if anyone has yet observed or thoroughly explained—may have the causes of its sound assigned, we shall begin our experiment "from the egg" <note>original: *ab ovo*; a Latin idiom meaning from the very beginning</note>, as the saying goes, once we have first declared the conditions of the instrument and where it ought to be placed.

The location of the instrument should not be in the open air, but in an enclosed space; yet in such a way that the air has free entry and exit on both sides. The wind, moreover, can be constricted in various ways: first, through conical and spiral channels, by which we previously gathered sound from outside into the house; then, by means of valves. Let there be two valves made of wood, **E F**, and **B V C D**. These are joined at **F** and **V D** in such a way that they nonetheless offer an entry for the wind to the space contained between the parallel boards **F K** and **F E**.

The valves are to be placed outside, and the boards inside the chamber; to these, at the back, the instrument is attached at the slit **S N**. It is turned in such an oblique <note>slanted or angled</note> position relative to the slit 

<image-desc>A technical diagram illustrating the funneling of wind into an acoustic instrument. On the left, a personified cloud head (a "wind boy") blows air toward two large vertical panels or "valves" labeled E, F, C, D, and V. These panels are angled to form a funnel that directs the wind through a narrow vertical slit labeled S and N in a wall. Behind the wall, on the right, sits a rectangular instrument box containing vertical strings, labeled O and R. The box is positioned at an angle relative to the slit to maximize the contact of the focused air stream with the strings.</image-desc>

**S N**, so that the wind, gathered by the valves and constricted within the narrow space of the boards **B V** and **E F**, and having escaped through the slit, may strike all the strings of the instrument **S O N R**. For if the instruments were placed parallel to the boards, the result would not be so successful; but if, as we have said, it is angled so that all the strings are exposed to the wind, it will achieve the best success. For according to the wind's gentle-

Suddenly all the strings will produce a certain trembling sound; at times they will express the songs of birds, or a <term>water organ<note>original: *organum hydraulicum*; an organ where air is moved by water pressure</note></term>, and sometimes a concert of pipes and other foreign sounds. No one will even suspect what kind of instrument it is, or by what hand, what bellows, or what artifice it produces the harmony. This instrument will be all the more secret and worthy of admiration the more it is hidden and covered. Furthermore, if you close all the windows of the room, the sound, likewise shut out, will fall silent; but as soon as even one is opened, it will bathe those present with its usual sound and wonderful pleasure.

## COROLLARY I.
### *The construction of the aforementioned machine to resonate perpetually.*

If, however, someone should wish to continue such a harmonious sound forever, he shall prepare a machine in an open place of some tower, arranged in such a way that, in the manner of a <term>weather-vane<note>original: *aplustri*; originally a ship's stern ornament, here used for a wind-indicator</note></term> or wind indicator, it turns toward whichever wind is currently blowing. Thus, animated by the air it catches, it will always resonate of its own accord with whatever wind is blowing.

In order that this marvelous harmonious sound may be perceived with amazement high in the air, one should [use] a flying fish or Dragon (the construction of which I have described at length in the <note>original: *Magia Parastatica*</note> "Representative Magic" section of *The Great Art of Light and Shadow*...

As soon as you expose this to the open air, whether the rope is pulled tight or loosened, the strings will always be vibrated with great force, producing the intended musical effect.
