Nothing remains now except for us to demonstrate why strings stretched to the same pitch nevertheless produce a diverse harmony. I propose, therefore, the following experiment.

### EXPERIMENT.

If anyone exposes a string made from <term>animal intestines<note>original: *chordam ex animalium intestinis*; commonly known as catgut, the standard material for musical strings at the time</note></term> and stretched tight to the wind in the place previously prepared, he will observe that the string soon begins to sound. It does not produce the single tone expected from a stretched string, but something entirely different; now it maintains *a third, now a fifth, now a fifteenth, or a twenty-second; at other times a third, fourth, or sixth*. Since no one has yet been able to explain why this happens, we have considered it our duty to reveal the true cause of this matter here.

I assume first that the wind does not always strike the string with equal force. Instead, like certain rays, it strikes now this part and now that part of the string, sometimes with a slower and sometimes a faster impulse. I will show that this unequal force of the wind is the cause of such diverse sounds in the following way.

Let the string be **A F** and the wind be **G**. If the wind strikes the entire string **A F** with a single, undivided force, it is certain that the string will produce its own specific sound that corresponds to its tension.

If, however, a single "ray" of wind **C** touches it—that is, it divides the string into two parts—it will happen that the remaining parts **C A** and **C F** will sound an <term>octave<note>original: *diapason*; the musical interval where the frequency is doubled</note></term> relative to the whole, just as we have demonstrated in the division of the <term>monochord<note>original: *monochordum*; a single-stringed instrument used since antiquity to study the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note></term>.

For the ray of wind will act in place of a <term>plectrum<note>original: *plectri*; a pick or tool used to pluck or strike a string</note></term>. When it strikes point **C**, the vibration of the entire string in sections **C A** and **C F** will sound twice as fast...

<summary>Kircher describes how to turn a musical kite into a "flying angel" and begins a scientific investigation into why wind-blown strings produce various harmonic intervals (overtones) rather than a single pitch. [will] sound, from which the string will necessarily sound higher by the interval of one <term>octave<note>original: *octava*</note></term>. If indeed the ray of wind should press upon [the segment] A B, the remaining part of the string B F will necessarily sound a <term>fifth<note>original: *quintam*</note></term> for the reasons given in the assigned division of the <term>monochord<note>original: *monochordi*; a single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals</note></term>. If again the wind should press upon the whole space A D, D F will necessarily sound a <term>double octave<note>original: *disdiapason*</note></term>, that is, a fifteenth; and so on for the others. Thus, the ray of wind, according to various points of contact upon the string, will produce different sounds within it.

<margin>Why a string excited by the wind produces different sounds.</margin>

Hence, the larger the portion of the string the windy ray leaves untouched, the higher <note>original: *leviorem*; while "levis" usually means light or high-pitched in this context, Kircher's physics here is complex, as a longer string typically produces a deeper sound. He may be referring to the "lightness" of the overtones produced.</note> the sound it will produce; conversely, the smaller the portion, the deeper <note>original: *graviorem*</note> the sound. Therefore, what the wind does to one string, it will do to countless other strings tuned to the same pitch, and this is the genuine cause of such great diversity of sounds perceived in this instrument. A certain tremulous sound is then born, affecting the ears wonderfully, which certainly has no other origin than the <term>undulation of the wind<note>original: *undulatione venti*</note></term>, which does not always flow with a straight impulse, but glides against the string in the manner of waves, and consequently excites the string with the same motion with which it strikes it.

<image-desc>A diagram illustrating the physical principles of air (wind) acting upon a musical string (chord). The string is anchored at points A (top) and G (bottom), with intermediate division points labeled B, C, D, E, and F. ### COROLLARY.

From these things it is also clear that one and the same string can give forth infinite different sounds; for even if the wind presses the string more strongly in one place, by pressing it more weakly in other various places, it will give birth to more and more variations of sounds. For if, for example, it presses strongly at point C and weakly at point B, it is certain that the segment C A will not quite achieve an <term>octave<note>original: *diapason*</note></term>, but will aim for something in between. But let these remarks suffice regarding the cause of the diversity of sounds in a single string.

# DEVICE V.
## EXHIBITING ALL KINDS OF HARMONIC SYMPATHETIC EXPERIMENTS.

We call those "sympathetic experiments" where objects resonate untouched at the sound of others, by a certain wonderful proportion and similarity of the sounding bodies. By this proportion, it happens that at the sound of one, another is also moved without being touched. This proportion has a place not only in <term>homogeneous<note>bodies made of the same material or of the same type, such as two identical strings</note></term> bodies, but also in <term>heterogeneous<note>bodies of different materials or types, such as a string and a wooden pipe</note></term> ones. From this, many miracles of nature occur, the reason for which no one could easily explain unless they possessed a distinguished knowledge of the <term>Secrets of Music<note>original: *Musicorum Arcanorum*; referring to the deeper, often hidden laws of harmony and physics</note></term>. It is almost common knowledge that one string can move another untouched, tuned not only in <term>unison<note>original: *unisonum*; two notes of the identical pitch</note></term>, but also in the <term>octave<note>original: *diapason*</note></term> and the <term>fifth<note>original: *diapente*</note></term>; however, the untouched string always trembles more violently the closer it is to a unison. We shall not assign the reason for this effect in this place, since we shall explain it most extensively in the following book, but we shall only reveal the mode by which many admirable things can happen in <term>natural magic<note>original: *Magia naturali*; in Kircher’s era, this referred to using the laws of physics and nature to produce seemingly miraculous effects</note></term>.

...will be even more clearly perceived. Take an instrument distinguished by its strings and constructed from the most resonant wood, of the type we proposed in **Device III**. You shall tune the strings of this instrument most perfectly to a certain organ, so that each individual string is in perfect unison with the individual pipes of the organ. Once this is done, you should move back with the instrument from the sound of the organ until you see the strings move and find that they give off a perfect sound; this will be its "proportionate distance." But if some strings do not move, it will be a sign that the pipe does not respond perfectly to the string, or that it is an <term>octave<note>original: *Octavâ superiori*; the same note at a higher pitch</note></term> higher. Or, you might operate more deliberately in this way: apply the instrument here and there to the interior walls of neighboring rooms, or, ideally, upon a beam continuous with the organ, until it catches the movement and sound of the strings, and you will have what you seek. For a stringed instrument will perfectly exhibit all the harmony of the organ within a separate place. And lest you think I am merely passing on a theory, read what we have reported elsewhere regarding the **Mainz Organ**, where a <term>lute<note>original: *Chelyn*; specifically referring to a stringed instrument with a vaulted back</note></term> hanging in a nearby choir loft was set in motion by a chance occurrence; then no doubt will remain regarding the truth of the matter. In this, there is only one difficulty: that one finds the perfect similarity and proportion of the bodies along with the proportionate distance. Whoever finds this can undoubtedly boast that they have discovered something marvelous in nature. For there is such a mutual similarity between certain sounding bodies that as soon as one sounds, the other is found to sound as well, as we have shown with great curiosity in our <term>Universal Music<note>original: *Musurgia* (referring to Kircher’s 1650 work, *Musurgia Universalis*)</note></term>. Another experiment teaches us all these things: if someone lightly suspends harps, theorbos, lutes, harpsichords, and similar stringed instruments in a very resonant room (after first removing all tapestries that deaden sound), they will perceive, not without wonder, a certain exotic harmony caused by the voices of speakers or the motion of the air, just like an organist inciting the instruments.

# EXPERIMENT II.

->ON SYMPATHETIC HARMONY THROUGH<-
->conic sections.<-

If anyone knows how to form a <term>parabolic<note>A parabola is a specific U-shaped curve. Kircher is suggesting that shaping an instrument like this will focus sound waves.</note></term> or hyperbolic cylinder, he will know that he has penetrated the greatest secret in <term>Musurgia<note>original: *Musurgia*; the art and science of musical composition and acoustics, specifically referring to Kircher's own monumental work, *Musurgia Universalis*.</note></term>. Let us reveal the secret.

By means of a <term>parabola-shaper<note>original: *Paraboloplastae*; a specialized tool or template used to carve a perfect parabolic curve into material.</note></term>, let a concave parabolic semi-cylinder be formed. This is done if you draw the parabola-shaper with a constant and straight motion through material suitable for this purpose, such as plaster, along its length; for by that motion in the yielding and soft material, the said parabolic semi-cylinder will be formed, which is of wonderful power in exhibiting sympathetic harmony. Let the business be set up thus: Let the two <term>foci<note>The "focus" is the point where a parabola concentrates all incoming parallel waves (or sound).</note></term> in the terminating parabolas be observed with the greatest care, from one of which if a string is drawn, terminating and stretched in the other opposite focus of the parabola, the instrument will be ready for sympathetic sound. But let us explain the matter with letters and a diagram. Let the parabolic cylinder be **A B C D E F**. The two parabolas terminating the cylinder are **A B C** at the top and **D E F** at the bottom. Let **H G** be the foci of the equal parabolas. I say that the string or gut-chord **H G**, through...

<summary>Kircher explains how to use parabolic geometry to create an acoustic chamber where a string is stretched between two focal points. He claims this design creates a "perpetual sound" through sympathetic resonance, referencing his previous research on Egyptian "magical" statues.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Experiment II from page 153, detailing the acoustic properties of a parabolic cylinder and how it focuses sound waves and air currents onto a central focal line to create sound or motion.</meta>

[it follows that the string] stretched through both focal points will be set in motion by any movement, even the slightest. For since any section of the semi-cylinder made <term>normally<note>In geometry, "normally" means at a right angle (perpendicular) to an axis.</note></term> to the axis is a parabola, and within that semi-cylindrical surface infinite parabolas can be conceived; it happens that any voice or sound and any motion of the air falling upon this semi-cylindrical parabolic surface—provided it falls upon the cylinder at right angles—forms as many parabolic <term>actinobolismos<note>original: *actinobolismos*; a term Kircher uses to describe the "casting" or "projection" of rays of light or sound.</note></term> as there are equal parabolas conceivable in the said semi-cylinder. Furthermore, since the string is stretched through the focal points **H** and **G**, it consequently passes through the foci of all the other parabolas conceivable in the said semi-cylinder. Since, in truth, the union and confluence of rays occur in the centers of the foci, the intensification of sound will also occur in them. Therefore, the entire string, agitated by the manifold confluence of rays and the multiplication of impulses, will tremble and sound.

# COROLLARY I.

<margin>How Perpetual Harmony can be achieved.</margin>
*From this it is clear that if someone were to stretch three or four strings near each other around the foci, tuned according to the <term>consonances<note>Kircher is referring to the musical intervals of the third, fifth, and octave, which create a pleasing chord.</note></term> of the 3rd, 5th, and 8th, the result would be that the machine would perpetually exhibit a harmonious murmur when placed in a suitable location or exposed to the wind. For this resonant line **H G** has a certain physical width and some space within which the intensification of the rays and the acoustic or resonant effects occur.*

# COROLLARY II.

->*How a certain statue might be moved perpetually.*<-

<margin>A statue that is always moving.</margin>
From this it follows, secondly: that if someone, in place of a string, were to skillfully balance a flexible steel <term>blade<note>original: *pinnam*; literally a wing or fin, here referring to a thin, flexible strip of metal.</note></term> at the focal point **G** with an attached weight **V**, and were to fix a small figure with movable and rotating limbs upon the head of it, it would happen that this statue would move perpetually along with the blade. For since a perpetual confluence of the agitation of the air occurs in the single line **V O**, that blade, being continually incited, will also perpetually move the said small figure resting upon it—and indeed, it will do so more vehemently the more forceful the motion of the air becomes.

From these examples, it is clear how much the knowledge of <term>conic sections<note>The mathematical study of curves—such as parabolas, ellipses, and hyperbolas—formed by the intersection of a cone and a plane. ## EXPERIMENT III.

->TO EXCITE UNTOUCHED STRINGS<-
->with the sound of glass.<-

If someone rubs the rim of a glass cup with fingers previously moistened, he will soon perceive a certain tinkling sound; if, moreover, he stretches a string above the cup tuned in <term>unison<note>original: *æquifonum*; having the same pitch or frequency.</note></term> to that sound, he will perfectly sense not only the vibration of the string, but also its sound. If anyone, therefore, stretches corresponding strings over several glass cups, he will have a music composed of a certain wonderful tinkling and the resonance of strings; regarding this experiment, <margin>see us treating this more extensively in the first part of *Musurgia*.</margin> <note>original: *Musurgia Universalis* (1650), Kircher's encyclopedic work on music theory and acoustics.</note>

## EXPERIMENT IV.

->SO THAT ONE MIGHT THINK THEY HEAR<-
->the massive roaring of bells.<-

This is also a notable experiment that carries listeners into the greatest wonder. Take one of the strings of a larger viol (which is commonly called a <term>Violone<note>A large, bass-range string instrument, the ancestor of the modern double bass.</note></term>); to this, hang a bronze plate or any other tinkling and sonorous body; then, having wound both ends of the string around your fingers, insert them into your ears. When the hanging plate is struck against another nearby body, you will perceive a sound that you would never have first believed possible: namely, a sound far exceeding the ringing of all bells. If, however, in place of the plate you take a bronze ruler (the longer it is, the more suitable it is for the proposed task), you will perceive a truly formidable sound from the low and high tones; and indeed, the longer the string is, the more intense the sound it will give off. The cause of this thing is nothing other than the vibration of the tinkling metal, which, while it communicates its ringing to the string, conveys the vibration communicated to it by the string to the sense of hearing, where

<summary>Kircher describes two acoustic experiments: sympathetic resonance between a rubbed glass and a string, and a method of amplifying sound through a string held to the ears to mimic the sound of massive bells.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, sympathetic vibration, glass armonica, resonance, violone, bells, conic sections, Musurgia Universalis</keywords>

<header>PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.</header>

the closed and internal air is so violently shaken by its <term>acoustic muscles<note>Kircher uses this metaphorical term to describe the physical movement and elasticity of air as it transmits sound waves.</note></term>, that it also produces an extremely powerful sound. It is truly marvelous that no other bodies, but only those that possess a natural resonance—such as metal plates and all types of rods—produce this effect.

### COROLLARY.

If you lower an instrument of this kind into a strongly resonating <term>cistern<note>original: *cisternam*; here referring to a large, hollow chamber or well that acts as a natural echo chamber.</note></term> by a string, and insert both ends of the string into your ears, then strike a metal plate against the wall; you will hear, beyond what can be described, a violent roaring composed of various voices.

# EXPERIMENT V.
## **TO ESTABLISH MUSIC FROM EVERY KIND OF WOOD, METAL,**
## *or Glass.*

How the <term>Xylorganum<note>original: *Zylorganum*; a precursor to the xylophone or glass harmonica, composed of tuned pieces of wood or glass.</note></term>—which is constructed from a diverse and harmonic arrangement of various wooden or glass tiles of different sizes—is to be built, has been discussed at length in *Musical Instruments* <note>original: *Organica Musica*, likely referring to a section within Kircher’s earlier work, *Musurgia Universalis* (1650).</note>; therefore, you may consult the cited passage.

# EXPERIMENT VI.
## **CONCERNING THE VARIOUS ALTERATION OF SOUND IN**
## *strings.*

A string produces sound according to the condition of the bodies placed beneath it at the point of support, which is called the <term>bridge<note>original: *ponticulum*; the small piece of wood that holds the strings up from the body of a stringed instrument.</note></term>. For instance, if you place a bladder inflated with air, into which pebbles or peas have first been tossed, under a large stretched string and scrape the string with a horsehair bow, you will hear a certain ridiculous sound fit for theatrical <term>Interludes<note>original: *Intermediis*; musical or dramatic performances inserted between the acts of a play.</note></term>. If you place a glass body under the same string, it will mimic a foreign tinkling sound, and so on with other materials; all of these provide vast material for creating exotic instruments.

# EXPERIMENT VII.
## SO THAT THE DEAF MAY PERCEIVE MUSIC.

<image-desc>To the right of the text is a vertical illustration of a "Colachon," a musical instrument with an exceptionally long, thin neck and a small pear-shaped body. The head features several tuning pegs. To the right of the neck is a small rectangular grid diagram (likely representing a fretboard or fingering chart) with four vertical lines and four horizontal lines; below the grid are the characters "N 1 2 3" and within the grid are several small circles marking specific positions.</image-desc>

A <term>lute<note>original: *testudo*; literally "tortoise," a traditional Latin term for the lute due to its curved, shell-like back.</note></term> with an elongated neck will achieve the intended purpose. Let there be a lute—commonly called a *Colachone* <note>The Colascione is a long-necked lute from Southern Italy, often having only two or three strings.</note>—equipped with strings, and let the deaf person grasp the very end of the neck with their teeth. Afterward, let the lute be stirred into harmonic motions [played]. I say that the deaf person will perceive the harmony; indeed, nothing has taught us the truth of this matter except for an experiment actually performed on a deaf person. For the sound of the lute, propagated through the neck into the mouth and through the nerves in the <term>acoustic organ<note>original: *organo acustico*; Kircher refers to the physiological structure of the inner ear and its connection to the nervous system.</note></term>, wonderfully excites the power of hearing. I am also persuaded that a deaf person would perceive that violent sound of bells mentioned in *Experiment IV* in a similar way.

# COROLLARY.

*From these things it is clear* that if, in any stringed instrument, a certain neck or long arm continuous with the instrument is extended to any distance—such as through intervening walls, provided it does not touch those walls—the result will be that the end of the neck, when grasped by the teeth, will present the sound of the <term>cithara<note>original: *cytharæ*; used here as a general term for a stringed instrument.</note></term> no differently than if the instrument were present. For we have said in the preceding sections that the propagation of sound through the neck of a cithara provides the same effect as it does in long wooden beams. Since the neck is continuous with the entire body of the instrument, it is certain that the more distant parts, and indeed the whole body, will tremble along with the sound of the strings, just as the nearby parts do. Because this trembling is a harmonic propagation of the air, it will consequently present the sound to the acoustic organ through the mouth and teeth in a remote location just as distinctly as if the instrument were present.

### ARTIFICE I.

<margin>Just as visible images are introduced by an optical tube, so are sound images introduced by an acoustic tube.</margin>
Just as optical tubes are constructed today—which display within a room whatever is being done by people outside, represented to the right, the left, below, and above—the whole ingenuity of the device consists in the various reflections of external images into a mirror, adjusted according to the movement of the tube. Thus, if anyone desires to know what is happening in the forum or the square, he first turns a tube constructed with excellent skill, together with the mirror of a small box designed to block out excess light. Soon, the various forms, dress, and gestures of the men, reflected within the mirror, will appear to the one looking into the tube just as if they were present. Thus, the <term>tube-watcher<note>original: *tubispex*; a term Kircher coined to describe an observer using a telescope or periscopic device.</note></term> will see everything happening outside, while remaining invisible to everyone himself. This is truly a new and unusual thing in its application, with which we also once played.

In no different a way, we will prepare an acoustic tube for you, through which you may hear, within a secret room, all those things that people chatter about, whether in the forum or the courtyard of some palace. Let the Palace be **A B C D**. Let the Forum or square or courtyard of some palace be **C H**. Let a tube **E F G** be made, led through the interior of the palace into a secret room **G L**. I say that within the room **G L**, however many are there, they will hear distinctly and clearly all the words, quarrels, laughter, and such things of the people lingering in the courtyard or square **D H**. Voi- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "voces," meaning "voices."</note>

This is established to us by the experiments we have conducted.

## DEVICE II.

### THE CONSTRUCTION OF HEARING INSTRUMENTS.

Instruments made according to the model of animal ears—specifically those animals possessing great acoustic power—also have a great power to strengthen hearing. Animals endowed with extremely sharp hearing include the Deer, the Dog, the Wolf, and all other long-eared animals. Among them all, however, the structure of the rabbit's ear is most wonderful. Since it is a very timid and entirely defenseless animal, nature seems to have fortified it with both extremely sharp hearing—acting like a scout to sense dangers ahead—and feet, which serve like weapons for running. 

Therefore, if someone were to have a hearing tube <note>original: *tubum oticum*</note> fashioned after the model of a rabbit's ear, he would find a wondrous secret for strengthening hearing. An organ of this kind has, first, a very long external ear—a sort of elliptical cartilaginous envelope—which is versatile in every direction to better collect sound rays within it. Then, from a narrow opening, it expands into a long and hollow space, eventually ending in a <term>cochlea<note>Latin for "snail shell"; Kircher refers here to the spiral cavity of the inner ear.</note>. Behind this cochlea is stretched that membrane which we called the <term>tympanum<note>The eardrum.</note> in Anatomy, along with the <term>malleus<note>The hammer-shaped bone.</note>, <term>incus<note>The anvil-shaped bone.</note>, and <term>stapes<note>The stirrup-shaped bone.</note>. 

Thus, the voice is collected by that long and versatile ear within the hollow passage of the internal ear, where, increased by a single reflection, it glides with great force into the snail-like winding <note>original: *cochleato meandro*</note>. Striking the eardrum there, it moves the internal air, from which that extremely sharp sensation finally occurs, which we marvel at in rabbits. Therefore, if you compare a hearing tube to the industry of nature, you will find, as I said, a certain wonderful secret for easily strengthening hearing.

To proceed more securely in this imitation of nature, we arranged for the ears of certain animals (those excelling others in hearing) to be prepared by the most skilled Anatomists. We displayed these for inspection in Illustration 2 <note>original: *Iconismo 2*</note> of our *Musurgia* <note>Kircher refers to his monumental work *Musurgia Universalis* (1650), which explored the physics of sound and music.</note>, so that you might shape the work of your art according to the hidden structure of nature in the hearing organ—now brought into the open—and manufacture hearing tubes with practiced skill.

# PRACTICAL APPLICATION
## Construction of Hearing Tubes.

-> ALL KINDS OF ACOUSTIC INSTRUMENTS <-
-> *to be fashioned for the use and convenience of the hard of hearing.* <-

Anyone who has correctly understood the preceding principles will have no difficulty in constructing acoustic instruments of every kind. All tubes—whether circular, parabolic, hyperbolic, or elliptical—when constructed on a smaller scale and applied to the ears, strengthen the hearing wonderfully. Among the various types, however, the elliptical and spiral <note>original: *cochleatus*</note> tubes seem to surpass all the rest. Let the elliptical tube **O** be fashioned with such skill that one of the acoustic centers precisely corresponds to the ear of the hard-of-hearing person **S C**, and the other end to the speaker **B V**, as appears in the following figure.

ON THE CONSTRUCTION OF VARIOUS INSTRUMENTS.

# TECHNICAL DESIGN III.

## FROM THE STOREHOUSE OF NATURAL MAGIC

->*To construct a statue that utters all kinds of sounds, both articulate and inarticulate.*<-

Various authors have devised different theories regarding this wonderful mechanism; those who follow the doctrines of the more secret philosophers believe it is entirely possible to achieve. Indeed, they assert that **Albertus Magnus** <note>A 13th-century friar and philosopher rumored to have built a mechanical brass head that could speak.</note> constructed a human head with such admirable skill that it could perfectly pronounce articulate words. We have also shown in many ways in our work *Egyptian Oedipus* that the **Egyptians** likewise fashioned various statues that could articulately pronounce whatever was desired. Some, however, rejecting this as contrary to the laws of nature, cannot at all persuade themselves that such a machine can be made. They claim that the machines of **Albertus** and the **Egyptians** were either fraudulent forgeries or were constructed with the help of demons—much in the same way we read of demons in ancient times giving responses through oracles and statues with an articulate voice. 

Nevertheless, many believe a statue can be built with such ingenuity that it may pronounce certain words articulately; for they argue that the larynx, tongue, and other instruments of the voice can be so adapted after the model of nature that, when animated by wind, they produce the manifest effect of articulate speech. Whatever the truth may be regarding that famous head of **Albertus Magnus** and the other machines of the **Egyptians**, we do not wish to dispute them here, as the matter is essentially <term>impossible <note>original: *ἀδύνατον*</note></term> in that specific form. Therefore, we shall demonstrate in this place another method for constructing such a statue, showing how it may be put into practice. 

Lest we seem to promise this only with "bottled-up words" <note>An idiom meaning empty or inflated speech.</note>, we will teach here how to fabricate a statue that not only speaks articulately but also produces any sounds and songs, responds to anything asked, imitates any animal voices, and performs innumerable <term>certain things <note>original: *ἅτινα*</note></term> which are truly <term>marvelous <note>original: *παράδοξα*</note></term>. You shall begin as follows: In a room **A B C D**, let the spiral tube <note>original: *tubus cochleatus*</note> described in the preceding pages be led to point **E**. Or, in a vertical tube **S**, let a statue be made with movable eyes and mouth, breathing with the posture of a living body. We have taught exactly how such a thing may be made in our work *Thaumaturgic Statics* <note>Another of Kircher's works, *Magnes sive de Arte Magnetica*, often touched on such "statics" or mechanical wonders.</note>. This statue should be placed in a certain designated spot so that the end of the spiral tube is connected to the hollow of the mouth—

...precisely corresponds to the cavity, and you will have a statue that utters anything articulately, perfect and complete. For this statue will **chatter perpetually**, now producing human voices, now the voices of animals; at one moment laughing and chuckling, then singing, and immediately after weeping and wailing; sometimes it will even be heard blowing out the most violent winds to the amazement of all. 

For since the **opening of the spiral** <note>Original: *Orificium cochleæ*. Kircher refers to his "cochleate" or snail-shell shaped acoustic tubes, which he believed amplified and directed sound better than straight pipes.</note> corresponds to a public place, all the words of men spoken outside, being received within the spiral tube, will manifest themselves within the mouth of the statue. If dogs bark, the statue will bark; if someone sings, it will respond with singing, and so on. If the wind blows, it is received within the spiral and will force the statue to "vomit" out very strong winds; thus, if a flute is applied to its mouth, it will appear to play it. If you move a trumpet to its mouth, the trumpet will blare; it will exhibit innumerable such playful tricks through the arrangement of the hidden spiral channel.

The statue is **E**. The spiral tube is inserted into the very thick wall **D E**. The opening of the tube, which spreads wide at **I H K**, is positioned so that whatever is uttered in a public place or marketplace and collected within the mouth of the tube, is reproduced in the chamber by statue **E**. 

If, however, you arrange this spiral tube vertically, the device will achieve an even better effect, according to those principles...

## ARTIFICE IV.

### *How to arrange a twisted spiral cone or "cochleate" tube within a building so that it renders any articulate sounds from a public place, however remote, into an inner chamber as certainly and distinctly as if they were right at the ear, without anyone suspecting where they come from.*

->*See the Illustration*<-

We have deservedly reserved for this place the most perfect and most admirable Echo-building <term>Echotectonicum: From the Greek for "echo-construction," the science of building spaces for specific acoustic effects.</term> machine of all. I know not what could be achieved in natural magic <term>Magia naturalis: The study of nature's hidden powers to create effects that seem miraculous but are based on physics.</term> that is more wonderful. But, lest I keep the curious Reader in suspense any longer, I shall explain the matter in a few words. 

Let a single or spiral tube—a twisted cone—be carved into rock or a natural cliff, or if the location does not allow for that, let it be shaped from plaster, clay, or volcanic stone <note>Original: *topho*. Tufa, a porous rock common in Italy, easy to carve for acoustic experiments.</note>. It should be designed with this ingenuity: that it has a very wide, outstretched opening facing a distant public place frequented by crowds of people. The other end of the tube, however, should terminate in a very small hole within an inner chamber, remote from the crowds. 

The interior conical, cylindrical, or spiral surface should be polished as exactly as possible—whether in stone, plaster, or another material made of iron or bronze plates. If the stone is too rough, the inner surface should be encrusted with plaster to achieve a smoother polish. Once this machine is finished, I say that nothing can be said so secretly in the opposite forum, street, or public place facing the mouth of the spiral tube that is not carried through the tube or spiral into that hidden chamber. In this room, therefore, one may hear the various voices of animals, the secret murmurs of men, singing, and the weeping and wailing of men...

...men you will perceive as distinctly and clearly as if they were occurring in the room itself, without anyone even suspecting where such a prodigious sound effect originates. Contemplate the structure in the *preceding Illustration*, where the Palace is represented by the letters A B C D. A spiral tube <term>cochleatus tubus: From the Latin 'cochlea' (snail shell); a tube twisted in a spiral shape which Kircher believed amplified sound through multiple internal reflections.</term> is inserted into the wall or carved into the rock S I K; its opening is H, its endpoint is E, and the public square is L. In this square, nothing can be uttered so secretly that it is not perceived within the chamber, however distant it may be. This is the machine that far surpasses not only parabolic but also elliptical and all acoustic structures proposed thus far, as it is constructed according to the model of nature observed in the structure of the ears, and it abounds with infinite reflections of voices (as we showed in the preceding problem, from which the voice is wonderfully intensified). Furthermore, a tube of this kind can be set in a dual position: either horizontal or lateral, like H E, or vertical, like P O. Both produce remarkable effects, but the vertical is more powerful, because the voice is carried upward more easily than downward or sideways. We have represented both tube-paths in the preceding *Illustration*. We discovered this marvelous machine by way of the Ear of Dionysius <note>The 'Ear of Dionysius' is a famous limestone cave in Syracuse, Sicily, known for its ear-like shape and extraordinary acoustics. Legend claims the tyrant Dionysius used it to overhear the secrets of his prisoners.</note> the *Sicilian*, which still survives in Syracuse; see the description of it provided a bit more fully in the preceding pages.

### Digression on the Spiral Cone.

Why there is such power for multiplying sound in a spiral or twisted helical cone should rightly seem a wonder to anyone. Certainly, when I had thought long and hard with myself about this business, I finally discovered that a helical cone twisted in a certain ratio affects something of a parabolic nature; as infinite gatherings of sounds occur within it, it is no wonder that it obtains such power in multiplying sound.

Indeed, it is known that a helical line equal to a parabolic line can be produced, which the curious *Reader* may understand thus: let there be a helical line marked with the letters a b c d e f n within the circle N g n o. I say that if the axis of the parabola G S is assumed to be equal to the semi-perimeter n g N of the circle n o N g, and the semi-ordinate <term>semi-ordinata: A geometric term referring to a line segment perpendicular to the axis of a conic section (like a parabola), reaching from the axis to the curve.</term> of the parabola T S is assumed to be equal to the semi-diameter a g of the said circle; then, through the vertex G of the axis of the parabola, G T is described, which passes through the endpoint of the semi-ordinate S T.

He also begins a geometric proof linking the spiral (helix) to the properties of a parabola to explain its acoustic power.</summary>

...passes through. I say that the parabola **G T** will be equal to the helical line **a b c d e f n**. All of these things depend on the various motions by which both lines are similarly and equally described.

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing a spiral (helix) line labeled with the letters a, b, c, d, e, f, n. The circle's perimeter and its interior chords are marked with points A, B, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, K, N, and O.</image-desc>

For if you divide the tangent of the parabola **G m** into any number of equal parts of the radius of the circle **a g**, the motions from **A** to **g** will be equal to the motion from **G** to **Q**; and the motion from **N** through the semi-perimeter **g h i k n** is, by hypothesis, equal to the motion from **G** to **S** made through the axis of the parabola. From this it is clear that if you take a <term>semi-ordinate: In geometry, this refers to half of a chord that is perpendicular to the axis of a conic section like a parabola.</term> equal to **G N**, or double **S T**, and apply it in the continued parabola **X V** between **G V** and **G X**, the parabola **G T X** will be twice as large as the <term>helix: A spiral curve; Kircher uses this to model the path of sound within a coiled tube.</term> **a b c d e f n**, or equal to that same helix taken twice. If, however, you apply a semi-ordinate three times larger than the diameter **a g** in the continued parabola, you will have a parabola three times larger than the helix, and so on to infinity; all of which I would demonstrate more fully here if I had not reserved them for other places.

It suffices, therefore, to know that the power of the <term>cochleate tube: A spiral-shaped acoustic tube, named after the 'cochlea' or snail shell.</term> possesses something entirely in common with parabolic tubes; I have thought it necessary to indicate this here so that <term>young scholars: Original Latin 'Tyronibus,' referring to students or beginners in the arts and sciences.</term> might apply their minds to more deeply examining such inexhaustible treasures of nature.

<image-desc>A geometric diagram showing a parabolic curve plotted against a vertical axis (G-S-V) and a horizontal tangent line (G-Q-N-R-M). Points T and X are marked on the curve, with vertical lines connecting them to points N and R on the tangent line, illustrating the mathematical relationship described in the text.</image-desc>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut divider featuring symmetrical foliate scrolls and floral motifs.</image-desc>

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof comparing the properties of a spiral (helical) line to a parabola, arguing that the acoustic power of spiral tubes is mathematically related to parabolic curves. Kircher intends this demonstration to inspire students to study the mathematical "treasures" found in nature's designs.</summary>

<header>BOOK I, SECTION VII OF PHONURGIA.</header>

# A RECAPITULATION OF NATURAL MAGIC.

## I ASSERT THAT A STATUE CAN BE CONSTRUCTED *hanging in the middle of the air, which most perfectly pronounces any sounds, whether articulate or inarticulate.*

<margin>
The wondrous construction of a magical statue.
</margin>
We rightly call this proposition a <term>Recapitulation: A summary or bringing together of previous points (Latin: "Anacephaleosis").</term>, since whatever descriptions of wondrous things have been brought forward thus far are contained here as if in a certain summary. For what I say is certain and undoubted, and has been wonderfully proven by experiment as far as was possible: namely, I say that a statue can be constructed which, placed in the middle of a room or even hanging in the air, performs all the aforementioned things. Thus, those standing by will indeed see and hear the miracle of a speaking statue, yet they will by no means be able to penetrate the cause of the hidden mystery. They will observe the movement of the eyes; they will marvel at the agility of the lips and tongue; they will behold with astonishment the frame of the whole body breathing life. But by what artifice the statue was built, or what hidden mechanism of its movement it possesses, no one will be able to detect, since the statue hangs in the middle of the air supported by nothing, connected to no tube, and animated by no wheels; but is constituted by a purely natural artifice and a various combination of more recondite arts. 

Let no one persuade themselves that I am promising <margin>impossibilities <note>original Greek: "αδύνατα"</note></margin>; whatever I promise can be brought to an infallible effect. Nevertheless, I have judged it more prudent to suppress the method in silence, lest such sublime mysteries of secrets—which ought to be revealed only to Great Nobles—become common to everyone. If the stories spread everywhere regarding the Head of <term>Albertus: Referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval scholar rumored to have built a mechanical brass head that could speak.</term> are true, I would certainly say that no artifice remains in the world for architecting a similar head except that which I have described. 

The <term>Combinatorial Art: A method of arranging and combining symbols or concepts to discover new knowledge; Kircher viewed this as a universal key to all sciences.</term> is a matter of such great importance; by its help alone all the secrets of the world are opened, and whatever is abstruse and admirable in any science is brought forth. Whoever has mastered this will find, in the investigation and scrutiny of hidden things, nothing so closed or concealed that they cannot intimately penetrate and make their own. For all the secrets of nature lie hidden in a certain application of things, just as certain and hidden meanings lie under various names; he who knows how to successfully extract these—truly, regarding such a person, I think that saying of <term>Phocylides: An ancient Greek poet known for his moral maxims.</term> can be fittingly said: *You shall be near the gods.* <note>original Greek: "πλῆσιος ἔση θεοῖς"</note>

<meta>catchword: APPEND- (referring to the next section's Appendix)</meta>

<summary>Kircher describes a "magical" speaking statue that appears to float and speak without visible mechanisms, attributing its power to Natural Magic and the "Combinatorial Art." He claims this art is the key to unlocking all of nature's secrets and suggests it was the same method used by Albertus Magnus.</summary>
<keywords>speaking statue, Natural Magic, Combinatorial Art, Albertus Magnus, Phocylides, acoustics, automata, hidden mechanisms</keywords>

# APPENDIX.
## ON THE WONDROUS ART OF SOUND-MAKING
<note>original: "Phonurgia"</note>
->*That is,*<-
### Regarding the famous and almost miraculous <term>Musical Machine: A complex mechanical device involving pipes and strings (Latin: "Machina Organica").</term> of Michele Todini of Savoy, a most celebrated Roman Musician.

If I remember having observed anything rare, unusual, curious, and most worthy of admiration anywhere in the realm of harmony, I could safely assert it to be that machine composed of every kind of instrument. It produces effects that have perhaps been unheard of until now. This work, begun eighteen years ago and finally brought to the highest perfection, is kept by **Michele Todini** of Savoy, a *Roman* Musician. He is a man most skilled—if any other ever was—in both the theory and practice of music. He resides in his house near the alley commonly called *the Arch of the Ciambella* <note>original: "l' Arco della Sciambella." This refers to a site in Rome near the Pantheon, named after a large circular ruin.</note>. He is a man of exceptional integrity and has always been closely joined to me by a bond of friendship among other musicians. But lest I keep the *Reader* in suspense any longer, I will explain the mechanism of such an excellent work in a few words here at this most opportune place.

Within two rooms of his house, which are most magnificently decorated, he displays these admirable specimens of his art to the great astonishment and admiration of all. A great number of people flock there, not only the <term>Cardinals: High-ranking officials of the Catholic Church (Latin: "Purpuratorum Patrum," literally "Purple-clad Fathers").</term>, but also Princes and men of every notable condition. In the first room, an Organ meets the eye at first glance; it is arranged according to all the laws of <term>Optics: The study of light and sight; here used to mean the visual symmetry and perspective of the instrument.</term>, with a multiple row of pipes and splendid gold inlay. Around its sides, four harpsichords <note>original: "Clavicymbala"</note> are seen arranged. The first of these is larger in size and can deservedly be called an **Arch-harpsichord** <note>original: "Archicymbalum." A specialized harpsichord designed with extra keys to play microtonal intervals.</note>. It is "pregnant" with all kinds of hidden mechanical offspring; from it, as if from a source, the mechanical power that drives the entire movement is derived into the other three smaller harpsichords, as is evident in the *figure* <note>Kircher refers to a diagram not present on this specific page.</note>, in which the Arch-harpsichord is marked with the letter A. The other two

...the smaller ones B and C. Then there is a middle one, somewhat larger than the others, D, arranged in such an order and separated from one another in such a way that each is supported by its own small columns in a completely free space. They neither touch the wall, nor do they seem to be sustained by any other visible support that can be seen around them.

<margin>The order in which the Author of this marvel performs the demonstration for his audience.</margin>
Thus, the Author, acting as the <term>Music Master: A leader or conductor of a musical ensemble (Latin: "Symphoniarcha")</term>, preparing to give a demonstration of his art on the machine, readies himself at the <term>Archiclavicymbalum: A massive, complex harpsichord and organ hybrid (A)</term>. Adjusting the <insert>stops or</insert> registers in a manner unknown to everyone, he begins to play a concerto composed by himself with such great, sweet, and elegant harmony that it immediately captures and pricks up the ears of all the listeners with the sweetness of the sound. After this, the harpsichord B sounds most sweetly, followed by harpsichord D playing in concert with B, and finally instrument C; then all the harpsichords together produce a full and most perfect sound and harmony. In this, what leaves the audience as if thunderstruck is that on the three harpsichords B, C, and D, the little hammers—which they call <term>tasti: The keys or strikers that activate the strings (Latin: "marculi")</term>—are seen to move and leap up alternately, now on harpsichord B, now on C, then on all three at intervals as if in competition, without any trace of a human hand, without the help of hidden strings, and without any ingenious arrangement of wires through the nearby wall or floor. Indeed, the harmony which they present is a wonder...

<meta>catchword: inju-</meta>

arranged in this way, and composed with the highest skill by the Author, it not only wonderfully engages the ears of the listeners, but also makes their eyes appear as if astonished by the leaping of the <term>little hammers: The internal parts of the instrument that strike the strings (Latin: "marculi")</term>; one might rightly call it a magical incantation. While these things are happening, the Author, by changing a <term>stop: A handle or lever that controls the sound profile (Latin: "registro")</term> with a hidden motion, soon displays the sweetest harmony of a stringed instrument—which they call a *Violin*—together with cymbals. But where that violin is hidden, or by what mystery it is situated inside the *Archicymbalum* <note>original: "Archicymbalum"; a massive, multi-keyboard harpsichord/organ hybrid</note>, in what way, or by what art these strings of a different nature—being made from animal intestines—are struck by the action of a <term>plectrum: A small tool used to pluck or strike strings</term> alone, is considered by the estimation of even the experts to be a new and, until now, unknown invention. 

Nor is this the end: once the violin symphony is finished, behold, the Author suddenly displays the harmonic sound of the *Lyre* so skillfully consonant with the cymbals that he holds all the listeners suspended in mind and ear by the rarity of the prodigious sound, especially since the <term>Master Musician: The conductor or leader of the performance (Latin: "Symphoniarcha")</term> always touches the same keys on the *Archicymbalum*, and now displays the harmony of the violin, now of the lyre, most excellently interwoven with the cymbals. After this, behold, the Master Musician suddenly turns the Organ—equipped with a great variety of stops—into a most beautiful melody, where the organ is driven now with a mournful sound, now with a joyful and excited one, and then to stir up military furies. 

How this harmonic work was constructed, or with what hidden art it was arranged, I would not wish to describe here, lest I should in any way prejudice the incomparable Author's invention; thinking it a sin to defraud him of both his invention and the immense expenses which he spent with incredible labor and sweat over nearly eighteen years in perfecting this artifice. He often complained to me that he had frequently lost heart due to the insurmountable difficulties occurring, but indeed, just as persistent labor conquers all, so too he took heart again at my encouragement to continue the machine, until he finally brought it to that perfection which we have described.

Since it is so arranged for craftsmen of such great genius that they seem unable to rest until they add new inventions through the continuous agitation of the mind; he had with him a musical instrument which they commonly call a *Musetta* <note>original: "Musettam"; a type of small bagpipe</note>, which is constructed from three pipes and a skin bag, by which the <term>Pipe-player: A musician who plays a wind instrument (Latin: "Auloedus")</term> presses the bag with his left arm, and with his fingers moved to the holes of the pipes in the manner of pipe-players, displays a melody to the ears hardly...

# PHONURGIA BOOK I. SECTION VII.

...unpleasant. Therefore, considering the construction of this instrument, and how something similar could be achieved through the <term>Keyboard: The set of keys played by the fingers (Latin: "Tastaturam")</term>, as they call it, so that whatever others perform with great effort on the *Musetta* <note>A sophisticated, bellows-blown bagpipe popular in the Baroque era</note>, he might achieve with his fingers on the keyboard of the Harpsichord and with his feet. Thus, intent on continuous study, he finally perfected it; he displays this in the second room of his house, which is adorned with a great apparatus of mythical allusions. It is truly a marvelous spectacle for the eyes and ears to see someone touching the keys, and yet to hear the <term>wind-bag: The bellows or reservoir for a pipe instrument (Latin: "utriculum")</term> of the Harpsichord, or a *Musetta* sounding a melody composed exactly according to the laws of music.

But how he was able to adapt the connections of the <term>stop: The mechanism that controls groups of pipes or strings (Latin: "Registri")</term> to the openings of the multiple pipes so that they always sound the desired melody; by what art, while playing the harpsichord, he animates the machine into such an exotic sound—now opening the orifices of the pipes with wind and fingers, now closing them—no one until now has been able to grasp. Everything is constructed with such secret artifice that it eludes the attempts of even the greatest masters of <term>organ-building: The craft of designing and maintaining pipe organs (Latin: "Artis Organicæ")</term> who excel in similar works. The machine is truly admirable, and perhaps much more excellent than the preceding one because of the impenetrable power of his genius in arranging these things. For what is more prodigious than for a Harpsichord to sound, and in place of its expected tone, to hear a wind instrument—the *Musetta*—which is <note>original: "toto cælo differens"; literally "differing by the whole sky," meaning worlds apart</note> vastly different? Wherefore many simpler people, who do not grasp the cleverness of such an unfamiliar art, suspect it to be necessarily constructed by satanic art.

I have thought it necessary to explain these things to the *Reader* in few words, partly so that my *Phonurgia* <note>Kircher's term for the "science of sound-making"</note> would not lack anything rare, hidden, or unheard of concerning the multiple prodigies of <term>Polyphony: Music consisting of several simultaneous melodies (Latin: "Polyphoniæ")</term> that might be a delight to the *Reader*. I also do this out of love and veneration for a friend, so that I might commend his genius to posterity for his rare industry in crafting these harmonic machines. At the same time, I wish to exhort and urge him to explain the construction of the entire work in order, and to communicate the secret to the <term>Musical Republic: The international community of musicians, theorists, and scholars (Latin: "Reipublicæ Musicæ")</term> in a dedicated book. This is so that once the craftsman has passed away, these inventions are not handed over to eternal oblivion along with their author, as <term>Histories: Historical records or chronicles (Latin: "Historiæ")</term> teach us has happened with many new inventions.

# PHONURGIA
## BOOK II.
### THE NEW SCIENCE OF SOUND,
->In which the hidden and obscure principles of sounds<-
->are explained through numbers.<-

### PREFACE.

<margin>The power of sound in stirring the emotions of the soul.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'Q' containing a miniature woodcut illustration of a landscape with trees and rolling hills.</image-desc>
SINCE I foresee that many will desire to know the origin of sound more precisely, and to give them full satisfaction, we shall demonstrate here more fully those things which we previously investigated in *Musurgia* <note>Kircher refers to his 1650 masterpiece, *Musurgia Universalis*, a foundational text on music and acoustics</note>. Indeed, the historical records of all writers are filled with accounts of how much power harmonic sound possesses, not only in stirring the minds and passions of men, but even over animals themselves. Therefore, we shall reveal the cause of such a miraculous effect, beginning the matter "from the egg" <note>Latin: *ab ovo*; an idiom meaning to start from the very beginning</note>. From this, the curious *Reader* will clearly learn how a plucked string moves another untouched string <note>A reference to sympathetic resonance</note>; how, from that point, either voices or musical instruments stir the spirits; and what wonderful effects nature produces—both in artistic harmonic symphony and in the collision of sounding bodies themselves—concerning the emotions of Anger, Love, Compassion, and Fury. Finally, we shall show how men situated in even the most remote places can be stirred to any emotion through the varied harmony of the trumpets (already explained in the preceding pages) by different <term>phonaskoi: trainers of the voice or leaders of sound (from the Greek "phonaskos")</term>. This occurs with even greater wonder as the harmony, propagated across vast distances, presents itself more secretly to the ears of the listeners. But let us now return to the thread of the <term>Rhombus: A diamond-shaped geometric figure or logical path, likely referring to a diagram in his earlier work</term> we left behind.

<summary>Kircher introduces Book II, titled "Phonosophia," shifting from mechanical devices to the mathematical and physical principles of sound and its profound emotional impact on humans and animals.</summary>

PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION I.
<meta>catchword: tarant</meta>

# SECTION I.
## On the Prodigious Power and Efficacy of certain Sounds.

# CHAPTER I.
## ON THE WONDERFUL POWER OF MUSIC.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' showing a man playing a harp in a landscape with animals, trees, and buildings in the distance, representing Orpheus.</image-desc>
WE SAY that the Magic of Consonance and Dissonance is nothing other than that faculty which produces prodigious sounds, which surely holds no small place among the other species of natural magic <note>Natural Magic, in Kircher's time, was not sorcery but a branch of science exploring the "occult" or hidden forces of nature, like magnetism or resonance</note>. For there is in certain sounds such an admirable power of both altering and attracting <note>original: "alterandi & trahendi vis"</note> that the human intellect seems scarcely able to reach their true cause. No one is ignorant of how much magnetism resides in musical melodies, or how much efficacy there is in their attractive power. Thus, it was not without reason that the Ancients portrayed ORPHEUS, the musical magus, drawing animals, forests, and even the very rocks themselves by the magic sound of his lyre. The poet CLAUDIANUS <note>Claudius Claudianus (c. 370–404 AD), a Latin poet</note> wrote about this quite elegantly in the preface of the second book of *The Abduction of Proserpina* in these words:

> *So the poet, rejoicing in his fatherland's festive time,*
> *takes up again the long-unused melodic strings of the lyre,*
> *And sitting down, having tuned the strings with a gentle plectrum,*
> *he drew forth the noble ivory with a joyful thumb.*
> *Scarcely was he heard when the winds and waves were calmed,*
> *and the sluggish Hebrus <note>A river in Thrace, modern-day Bulgaria/Greece</note> grew numb with frozen waters.*
> *Mount Rhodope stretched out its parched mountains for the songs,*
> *and Mount Ossa, leaning forward, shook off its icy snows.*
> *The tall poplar descended from bare Mount Haemus,*
> *and the friendly Pine drew along the oak as its companion,*
> *And although the Laurel had looked down upon the arts of the god <note>Referring to Apollo, who pursued the nymph Daphne as she turned into a laurel tree</note>,*
> *it came, suited to the voices of ORPHEUS.*
> *The gentle Molossian hounds <note>An ancient breed of large dogs from Epirus</note> looked upon the hare without harm,*
> *and the lamb offered its side to the wolf as a neighbor.*
> *Peaceful deer play with the striped tiger,*
> *and the stags did not fear the Massylian mane <note>Referring to the lions of North Africa</note>.*

Indeed, this prodigious attraction of animals, forests, and stones is to be understood partly figuratively <note>original: "tropologicè"</note> and partly allegorically; No-

<summary>Kircher introduces the concept of "Natural Magic" in acoustics, using the mythological figure of Orpheus and the poetry of Claudian to illustrate the magnetic and transformative power of musical harmony over nature.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, natural magic, magnetism, Claudian, harmony, acoustics, Greek mythology, resonance</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 172, picking up mid-sentence. The word "târant" is the conclusion of the Latin "putârant" (they thought/held), following the quote from Claudianus on the previous page. This section elaborates on the philosophical and "magnetic" interpretations of the Orpheus myth.</meta>

# ON THE PRODIGIOUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS.

<margin>What the attraction of stones performed by Orpheus signifies.</margin>
...for the Ancients held that the greatest power of Music is over the soul, and that the soul is changed according to its various sounds and harmonies, following them just as wax follows wherever you may twist it. For this reason, they called Music the "principle of all things" <note>original: "ἀρχὴν πάντων"</note>, and as *Psellus* <note>Michael Psellus (c. 1017–1078), a Byzantine philosopher and polymath</note> says in his *Music*, "the ancients said that music holds the universe together" <note>original: "τὴν μουσικήν οἱ παλαιότεροι συνέχειν εἶπον τὸ πᾶν"</note>. Furthermore, they saw that this same Music was able to compose and vary a person's character; for nothing flows so easily into tender and soft minds as the various sounds of singing. It can hardly be said how great its power is in either direction: for it both incites those who are sluggish and makes those who are agitated grow weary; at one moment it relaxes the mind, and at another it contracts it, as will be explained more fully hereafter. Therefore, the "rocks, forests, and animals"—that is, men who were utterly senseless, beast-like, and monstrous in their cruelty—were drawn by the divine sound of Orpheus's lyre and led toward humanity and a civilized way of life. Others, however, understand this said "attraction" allegorically. Since Orpheus was a distinguished <term>Astrologer</term> and Musician, being most perfectly skilled in both arts, he would so opportunely temper and mix his sounds as to imitate the harmony of the celestial stars (which he understood better than anyone). He would call forth this harmony in such a way that he drew all their influence and power into himself, relying on which he could, by his playing, attract and soothe whatever he wished. Some also add that because he knew best by what proportion and what <term>consonance</term> <note>The sounding together of notes that produce a pleasing effect</note> each thing was established and composed by nature, and which star it obeyed or was subject to, he adapted musical ratios to those things and their stars. Thus, by the power of the stars, he enticed even inanimate objects into motion, drawing out the "seeds of harmony" latent within them through his own external harmony—no differently than iron strikes out the latent fire from a flint, or the blast of a bellows brings forth a hidden flame. For in all things there are certain hidden sparks, as it were, and seeds of harmony or feeling; so much so, that the ancients even called **GOD** Himself the "Harmony of all things" <note>original: "Ἁρμονίαν πάντων"</note>.

<margin>The power of Harmony lies hidden in all things.</margin>
Hence, on the authority of **PROCLUS** <note>Proclus Lycaeus (412–485 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher who believed the entire universe was linked by "chains" of sympathy</note>, all things sing hymns to the Leaders of their own order: some in an intellectual way, some rational, some natural, and others in a merely sensible manner. And truly, if anyone (he says) were to hear the "pulsation" which individual things produce in the air as they orbit—such as solar things toward the Sun, or lunar things toward the Moon—he would surely perceive a certain sound of that kind, aptly composed toward its King, whatever kind of sound individual things are able to produce. Therefore, if the magnetic power of Music moves all things, how much power must it have over men?

<meta>catchword: notum (known)</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains that the myth of Orpheus moving rocks and trees is an allegory for music's power to civilize "wild" men and its ability to tap into the "magnetic" harmony of the universe. # PHONURGIA BOOK II, SECTION I.

...is well known; for no heart could be so harsh or wild that it would not be relaxed by suitable melodies and songs that soothe the mind, or conversely, be tightened and restrained by those that are unseemly and discordant. **Musaeus** <note>A legendary polymath and philosopher of pre-Homeric Greece.</note> proclaims song to be the sweetest thing given to mortals from heaven, soothing all. Drums resound in war, 
<margin>The power of Music over human minds.</margin>
to add courage to those about to do battle; trumpets and pipes echo to rouse the spirits of soldiers in fighting. **Timotheus** the musician, as often as he pleased, would so inflame the mind of **Alexander the Great** with the Phrygian mode <note>A musical scale associated in antiquity with excitement, passion, and warlike energy.</note> that he would run wildly to his weapons; and when he wished otherwise, by changing the tone, he would suppress the ferocity of his mind and draw his softened spirit toward feasts and banquets. Something similar is read concerning a certain lutenist of the *King of Denmark*. Did not **Pythagoras**, according to **Cicero's** testimony, render a certain young man of Tauromenium—who was spurred by a mad love as if by a gadfly—peaceful and gentle by playing a slow spondee <note>A solemn, steady musical rhythm.</note>? Certainly, **Theophrastus** is said to have employed musical sounds to restrain the impulses and disturbances of the soul. **Clytemnestra** <note>The wife of Agamemnon; legend says her husband left a musician to guard her virtue with his songs.</note> was urged toward restraint and modesty by a lutenist, [until he was removed], at which point she seized available weapons and cast violent hands upon those sitting near her. Not only men, but even animals themselves are captured by musical melodies. Elephants, on the testimony of **Strabo**, are enticed by drums; swans by the lyre.
<margin>The power of Music as a healer of diseases.</margin>
>*The fowler sings sweetly on his pipe while he deceives the bird.*
The innate wildness and savage monstrosity of bears is not halted except by the sound of a pipe; **Pythagoras** is said to have repressed the attacks of wolves with the measures of a flute. Finally, music is a most excellent medicine, suitable for dispelling all diseases. Both sacred and profane histories abundantly relate that those suffering from <term>sciatica</term> <note>nerve pain in the hip and leg</note>, melancholy, madness, demonic possession, and poisoning have been cured by it, as will be discussed later. However, as for the claim alleged by ignorant authors here and there that **Asclepiades** <note>A famous Greek physician who practiced in Rome.</note> cured the deaf with a trumpet, I would not want it understood as if he achieved this by the sound and melody of the trumpet; but rather, 
<margin>How the curing of the deaf by a trumpet is to be understood.</margin>
because a device fashioned in the shape of a trumpet was applied to the ears of the deaf man (just as many hard-of-hearing people still use today, the construction of which we have provided in the preceding pages), so that the quality of sound and the unity of words, increased by various reflections, might present them more effectively to the auditory organ of the deaf man. Since the deaf were wonderfully helped by this, the matter was accepted into legend: that **Asclepiades** healed the deaf with the sound of a trumpet.
These things having been thus premised, let us now see what [is required] to draw minds...

—is this magnetism begun? What proportion do consonances <note>Harmonious intervals or sounds that agree with one another.</note> have with the ears, so that we are so greatly delighted by them? And what indeed are number, weight, and measure <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 11:20, suggesting that God ordered all things by these three principles.</note>, both in sounds and in the ears, or in the soul, as it is delighted by sounds, or, upon hearing them, is seized by I know not what passion? For there are certain sounds so troublesome and awkward that by their harshness the teeth themselves chatter; others are so fitting and elegant, flowing in so sweetly, that they seem to snatch the soul out of itself. <margin>Various opinions regarding the power of Music.</margin> When I examine these things, it can scarcely be said how much controversy and disagreement there is among Authors concerning the power and efficacy of harmony of this kind, or how many discordant opinions of almost all writers there are regarding the consonances of melodies. Some attribute this power to GOD, the source of all consonance, or to the soul composed of numbers; some to I know not what celestial influences or the cabalistic ten-stringed lute <note>original: "cabalisticum decachordum." A reference to mystical interpretations of the biblical ten-stringed instrument, often linked to the divine order of the universe.</note>; others to a hidden sympathy of the soul with musical numbers; and many to Geometric ratios. Leaving behind all these discordant views, which are as if far removed from the truth, let us finally reveal what we determine concerning this drawing faculty of Music and its attraction of the soul, and by what reason and process it produces such varied effects not only in humans, but even in brute animals themselves.

# CHAPTER II.
## ON THE NATURE AND PRODUCTION
### *Of Consonance and Dissonance.*

All wonder why the soul is so greatly affected by consonances, yet shrinks so much from dissonances; in order that their causes and reasons may be understood,

<margin>The intensification and relaxation of sound from speed or slowness.</margin>
*It must first be noted* that two things are to be considered in Consonances: the collision of bodies, which occurs through the motion of sounds, and proportion. These are like two principles—the former being Physical, the latter Mathematical—by which all consonances are caused. Physics considers the motion; Mathematics <note>original: "Mathesis." In this context, it refers to the mathematical science of proportions and ratios.</note> weighs the quantity, numbers, weight, measure, and every proportion of one sound to another. Furthermore, since every intensification or relaxation of voices and sounds flows from the speed or slowness of motion, a voice or sound will necessarily be caused to be more intense, higher-pitched, and denser, the faster the motion has been;

<summary>Kircher explores the "magnetic" pull of music on the soul, dismissing mystical theories in favor of a dual Physical and Mathematical explanation based on motion and proportion. He begins to define how the speed of motion determines the pitch and intensity of sound.</summary>
<keywords>harmonics, consonance, dissonance, musical magnetism, mathematical proportions, physics of sound, cabalistic decachord, musical theory</keywords>

--- Page 210 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the physics of sound, moving from the philosophical ratios mentioned previously to the physical mechanics of pitch production in strings, instruments, and the human body.</meta>

# BOOK II, SECTION I OF THE PHONURGIA

<margin>The cause of high and low pitch in humans.</margin>
—[The motion is] faster; indeed, it is more relaxed and infrequent as the motion becomes slower. All of these matters are learnedly weighed by Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), a Roman senator and philosopher whose works on music were the standard textbooks for centuries.</note> in *Music, Book 1, Chapter 3*, where he teaches that sounds are necessarily lower <note>original: "graviores," meaning heavy or deep in pitch.</note> when they are produced by slower and more infrequent motions—the very slowness and scarcity of the striking—while they are higher <note>original: "acutiores," meaning sharp or high in pitch.</note> if the motions are faster and more rapid. This is so much the case that the same string <note>original: "nervus," literally a nerve or sinew, but used here to mean a musical string.</note> sounds higher if it is tightened, and lower if it is loosened; for when it is more tense, it yields a faster pulse, returns more quickly, and strikes the air more frequently and densely. However, a string that is looser produces slackened pulses, and by the very weakness of its striking, it trembles for a shorter time and with infrequent effect. 

From this, it follows that pipes or flutes—as we demonstrated in our *Organ Music* and in the seventh book of our *Musurgia* <note>Referring to Kircher’s own massive work, *Musurgia Universalis* (1650).</note>—sound now higher, now more muffled and lower, according to the ratio of the closed and open finger-holes, due to the compressed or loosened air. This is evident both in organ pipes and in the <term>larynx</term> <note>The voice box.</note> or human <term>trachea</term> <note>original: "aspera arteria," the "rough artery," an old anatomical term for the windpipe.</note>. For in these, the longer the tongue, or <term>epiglottis</term> <note>The flap of cartilage at the root of the tongue.</note>, or that fissure where the striking of the air causes the sound, and the wider the pipe, the lower the sound it will produce; the shorter and narrower it is, the higher the sound. Consequently, those whose larynx is wide and long usually intone with a deep and bass voice, while those whose larynx is narrow and small speak with a high and sharp voice. But we have spoken of these matters more extensively elsewhere.

<margin>Sound is not one continuous thing, but composed of many parts of sounds.</margin>
*Secondly, it must be noted* that the sound of a vibrating string or an emitted voice is not a single continuous thing, but is forged from discrete and interrupted motions of this kind, as was stated in our *Chordosophia* <note>The "Wisdom of Strings," a section of Kircher's musical theory.</note>. This is true even if our ears are quite unable to perceive the intervals between them, just as our eyes cannot judge whether a burning firebrand, which someone whirls in a circle as fast as possible, exists in one spot or if the whole circle has become made of fire. Boethius also declares this by the example of a top spun by boys; if a red mark is painted along the length or height of the cone, the whole top will appear dyed in a red color when it spins, not because it actually is, but because the speed of the red mark overtakes the uncolored parts and does not allow the others to appear. In a similar way, a more intense string rebounds and trembles with more strikes, and a relaxed one with fewer; for as often as a string is struck, it is not just one sound that is emitted, nor one percussion made, but the air is hit as many times as the trembling string strikes it. Hence, by the addition of motions, a high pitch is stretched out from a low one, and by the removal of motions, a low pitch is relaxed from a high one, since a high pitch [is composed] of more motions...

<summary>Kircher explains the physical basis of pitch, attributing high and low sounds to the frequency of vibrations in strings, instruments, and the human larynx. He argues that sound is not continuous but consists of rapid, discrete pulses, comparing the auditory effect to the visual persistence of a whirled firebrand.</summary>
