...is established. However, how we might cut any given number or magnitude so that the divided parts are in a given harmonic proportion <note>original: "proportione harmonica." In the Renaissance and Baroque periods, this referred to a series of numbers where the differences between terms are in the same ratio as the terms themselves, used to define musical intervals.</note>, we have discussed extensively elsewhere.

<margin>In what the power of Music consists.</margin>
With these things established, I finally infer that the wondrous power of Music, which it possesses in moving the emotions of the soul, does not flow immediately from the soul <note>original: "anima." In Kircher's philosophy, the "anima" is the immortal, immaterial part of a human.</note>—which, since it is immortal and immaterial, maintains no proportion to the voice and sound, nor can it be changed by them—but from the spirit <note>original: "spiritu." Not the "Holy Spirit," but the "animal spirits": a subtle, physical vapor that 17th-century science believed acted as a bridge between the physical body and the immaterial soul.</note>, which is the instrument of the soul, by that principal conjunction of the soul by which it is annexed to the body. For a spirit of this kind, since it is most subtle—a certain bloody vapor, extremely mobile and thin—is easily incited by harmonically agitated air. The soul, sensing this agitation, induces various effects according to the varying stimulation of the spirit. Hence, when the spirit is incited by faster and more frequent harmonic motions and is, as it were, "rippled," a certain rarefaction <note>The process of becoming less dense; an expansion.</note> arises from that rippling; from the rarefaction of the spirit, in turn, arises a dilation <note>A widening or expansion of the vessels or the spirit itself, associated with positive emotions.</note>, which is followed by joy and gladness. All of these effects will be greater to the degree that the melodies <note>original: "moduli."</note> are more elegant, more suitable, and more proportionate to the natural temperament and constitution of the man. *From this it happens that*, while we perceive a most perfectly constituted harmony and a most sweet melody, we seem to feel a certain tickling in the heart and soul, as if attracted and absorbed by this fitting stimulation. This variety of emotions is promoted most of all by the different musical modes or tones <note>original: "modi seu toni musici." These refer to the different scales (like Dorian or Phrygian) which were historically believed to provoke specific emotional responses.</note>, which have been dealt with elsewhere; furthermore, by the diminutions of the notes <note>original: "diminutiones." A technique of breaking a long note into many fast, shorter notes; a form of musical ornamentation.</note>, the various reciprocity of ascent and descent, and the various combinations of dissonances artfully mixed with consonances. That the spirit is moved in this way, and no other, is manifest from the first of the following experiments.

...because they are consonant or dissonant, or because they are hurried or slow, or what is more significant, because they tend toward the high pitch for cheerfulness, or sink into a low and relaxed sound for commiseration and tears. But as for love and hatred—even though these are the most powerful of all emotions—Music does not excite them directly, because love and hatred of someone are specific to an individual object, as has been said elsewhere. 

Music, however, moves only general emotions <note>original: "affectus." In 17th-century theory, these are not just "feelings" but physiological states where the "animal spirits" are moved in specific patterns.</note> of the soul. Nevertheless, love is excited from joy as a general emotion, as if from a certain friction; truly, since Music cannot generate sadness, neither can it generate hatred. That Music cannot generate sadness is clear from this: that sadness leads toward death, but music leads toward life. But why Music—as **ECCLESIASTES** <note>Kircher is likely citing the Book of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 22:6: "A tale out of season is as music in mourning."</note> rightly says—is an "untimely story in mourning," and why we, when mourning or with a heart suppressed by a certain heavy sadness, reject all Music, *this is the reason*: because the spirit or humor, frozen and constricted by fear or sadness through a strong imagination of impending evils, rejects every commotion and renders itself as if incapable of it. If, therefore, there were a musician who could dissolve this condensed spirit with well-ordered Music, he would not move the listener to sadness, but would bring relief to the pain by causing the spirit to expand somewhat. 

We have said that we are moved by Music to eight primary affects, which nevertheless can be conveniently reduced to these three Affects: namely, *Joy*, *Remission* <note>original: "Remissionem." Here meaning a state of relaxation or release of tension.</note>, and *Mercy*, as was also said above. That other affects subsequently result from these—such as Love, Sadness, Audacity, Fury, Temperance, Indignation, Gravity, and Religion—is by accident; for all these follow from these three general affections of the soul, as we have taught with many reasons in our *Rhetorical Musurgia* <note>original: "Musurgia nostra Rhetorica." Various intensifications and relaxations of tones, various alternations of ascent and descent, and various and differing repercussions of voices are the cause of various magnetic attractions of souls. Technical terms for the "upbeat" and "downbeat" or the rise and fall of a musical line.</note>, by which consonants are connected to dissonants by such strict laws of friendship that nothing sweeter can be perceived; these are further increased by various diminutions <note>original: "diminutiones." A melodic decoration where long notes are broken into many short, fast notes.</note> of voices and the mutual, friendly pursuit of artificially woven fugues. *Second*, a determinate meter comprised in verse. *Third*, the words themselves, or

Consequently, it undergoes various alterations caused in turn by the harmony of sound, and it necessarily follows that the mind is inclined toward the state and disposition of the spirit. Hence, the wanton delight in charming, soft, and higher-pitched melodies, because the effect these produce in the excitement of the spirit is proportionate and similar to the emotion toward which they are already inclined.

From this, two people—one burning with heavenly love, the other with earthly love—are moved by different impulses or desires by one and the same tone, whether it be the Dorian or Lydian mode <note>Dorian and Lydian are ancient musical scales. The Dorian was often associated with solemnity or morality, while the Lydian was sometimes viewed as soft or mournful.</note>. The former joins a desire for the heavenly Homeland with a contempt for earthly things; the latter joins the heat of carnal fellowship with a desire for fleeting things. One must judge other cases similarly. Thus, everyone is more delighted by that harmony which is more similar and proportionate to their own nature and <term>complexion: the specific physical makeup or "humoral" balance of a person</term>, and which is more in agreement with their present disposition.

# CHAPTER IV.
### AN INVESTIGATION INTO THE CAUSES OF
->*Consonant and Dissonant Numbers.*<-

<margin>Why some sounds are sonorous and others are not.</margin>
Furthermore, how Music moves the emotions of the soul through its melodies is easily understood from what has been said before. Now there remains only to explain how some sounds are consonant and others are not, and why only consonant sounds move the emotions while dissonant ones do not. To understand this, it must be known that just as consonance is a proportion of voices or sounds that are different from each other but reduced into one—or a mixture of high and low sounds falling sweetly and uniformly upon the ears— <margin>What consonance and dissonance are.</margin> so dissonance is a harsh and unpleasant striking of mixed high and low sounds reaching the ear.

Since, therefore, pleasure is perceived from the hearing of two different tones, the cause is that the tremors from the vibration of the string...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion on how musical vibrations affect the human "spirit" (the physiological medium of emotion) to a scientific demonstration of the physics of harmony, beginning with the concept of the unison.</meta>

# BOOK II OF PHONURGIA. SECTION I.

...resulting [from vibrations], are often joined and agree in one, through which union the spirit, similarly excited, stimulates the imaginative power toward pleasure; but toward displeasure, if the said tremors either do not join at all, or join only rarely. So that anyone may know how this union occurs, it seemed fitting to demonstrate the whole matter here through several propositions.

## PROPOSITION I.

->***TWO STRINGS OF THE SAME TONE, WHEN STRUCK AT THE***<-
->***SAME TIME, NECESSARILY PRODUCE A UNISON, AND IN EVERY***<-
->***VIBRATION THEY JOIN TOGETHER PERFECTLY AND IN EQUAL TIME.***<-

Although we have demonstrated all these things at length and from first principles in the *Chordosophia* <note>meaning "string-wisdom"</note> of our *Musurgia* <note>Referring to Kircher's earlier encyclopedic work, *Musurgia Universalis* (1650), which explored the mathematics and physics of music.</note>; yet because this place demands the origin of harmonies as if by its own right, we thought the same things should be briefly repeated here. I suppose, therefore, from the demonstrations in the cited book, that all <term>vibrations <note>original: "diadromos." In early acoustics, this referred to the full path or "excursion" of a vibrating string.</note></term>, whether equal or unequal, of a single string are of equal duration. Let there be, then, two strings equal in both tension and thickness, A B and C D. I say that from their striking, a unison is necessarily born.

For since, from the demonstrations in the place cited, strings of the same tone relate to each other as 1 to 1, and the time is also equal and of equal duration, the end-point of the vibrations in each string will necessarily be <term>isochronous</term> <note>original Greek: ἰσόχρονος, meaning occurring in exactly the same amount of time.</note>; that is, it happens at the same time. For each string—A B pulled to E, and C D pulled to F—at one and the same time, hitting the air at the points of reflection (this one at M, that one at I), will cause exactly the same tones. Indeed, on the return, hitting the air at one and the same moment of time at S and T, they will cause the same tones as before; and thus they will always produce the same and identical sounds until the trembling strings, having reached their center of gravity <note>Kircher uses "centrum gravitatis" to refer to the string's state of rest or equilibrium.</note>, come to rest.

Since, therefore, the strikes against the air at the points of reflection join perfectly at the same moment of time—and this in every vibration or back-and-forth movement—it necessarily follows from there, as has been said, that the tone of the unison will result, which is the fount and origin of all harmonies. Therefore, two strings of the same tone necessarily produce a unison; *which was to be demonstrated*.

<meta>catchword: PROPO-</meta>

<summary>Kircher explains how the physical union of air vibrations from two identical strings creates a 'unison,' which he identifies as the fundamental basis for all musical harmony and its effect on the human soul.</summary>
<keywords>unison, acoustics, string vibration, isochronism, Musurgia Universalis, physics of music, consonance</keywords>

HEADER: ON THE WONDROUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS.

## PROPOSITION II.

### *TWO STRINGS EQUAL IN THICKNESS, OF WHICH ONE IS DOUBLE THE OTHER, WILL NECESSARILY PRODUCE THE DIAPASON, OR OCTAVE, WHEN STRUCK, AND WILL UNITE THEMSELVES IN EQUAL DURATION OF TIME.*

Consider now two strings, AC and BC, of which the latter stands in a double proportion <note>A 2:1 ratio, the mathematical basis for the octave.</note> to the former. I maintain that when these strings are struck, their sounds will be joined not in equal time, but in unequal time. For since the relationship of one string to another is the same as the duration of one motion to the duration of the other—specifically regarding the vibrations of the struck strings—it follows necessarily that string BC completes its <term>vibrations <note>original: "diadromos." In 17th-century acoustics, this term described the path or "running" of a string from one side to the other during a single oscillation.</note></term> twice as fast as string AC completes its own.

<image-desc>A scientific diagram depicting the vibration of two strings. The upper string, labeled A and C, shows a wide vibration arc with points G and S. Centered between the two diagrams are the letters T and V. The lower diagram shows a smaller vibrating string or segment with points labeled R, D, F, S, and C, with arrows indicating the direction of movement.</image-desc>

<margin>How the Octave is brought to its conclusion in double time.</margin>
For let the two strings be struck at the same time: string AC at point G and BC at point F. String AC will necessarily cross the entire space of the vibration GO once, while in that same interval, string BC will have traveled from F to D. Thus, with the first string being at point O (the end-point of its swing) and the second string being at point D (still in the middle of its motion), no union of sounds occurs. However, when string BC reaches V, string AC will at that same moment reach F. At this point, having reached the terminal points of both strings—the latter at V and the former at S—the sounds caused by the movement of the air first unite themselves perfectly. Since string BC runs twice as fast as string AC, the union of these sounds will necessarily produce the <term>Diapason <note>From the Greek "dia pason," meaning "through all [notes]." This is the technical term for an Octave, the most perfect harmony after the unison.</note></term> or Octave. Therefore, the Octave is caused by the union of sounds flowing in unequal time; which was the point to be demonstrated. But let us demonstrate these things in another way.

<meta>catchword: Another [way]</meta>

<summary>A geometric and physical demonstration of the Octave (1:2 ratio), explaining that harmony is produced when vibrations of different speeds periodically coincide at the same terminal point.</summary>

BOOK II OF PHONURGIA, SECTION I.

### Another Demonstration of the Same Principle.

Let the larger string be the <term>Hypate <note>original: "hypate." In ancient Greek music theory, the *hypate* was the "highest" string on the lyre's frame but the "lowest" in pitch.</note></term>, and the <term>Nete <note>original: "Nete." The "lowest" string on the frame but the "highest" in pitch—the Octave.</note></term> (or Octave) be the smaller string, which is half the size of the larger one. Furthermore, let the space through which the <term>vibrating <note>original: "vibriſſata."</note></term> string runs and returns be represented by the points B and C; let the space of the Nete (the smaller string) be F G, which is half of the former. Thus, as string relates to string in length, so shall the space it traverses relate to the other space. Let us therefore suppose 

<image-desc>A perspective drawing of a monochord-like instrument, a long rectangular wooden box with two strings of unequal length stretched across the top. How the Octave consists in a double proportion.

that these strings are struck simultaneously at points B and F. Each string will then run from B and F: the larger one toward S, and the smaller one toward G. But when the smaller string reaches G, having attained the limit of its motion, it will cause a sound through the striking of the air. Meanwhile, the string B C is at S, being only as far from the limit C as it has traveled from B. While the larger string is still silent, it eventually reaches C and sounds there from the striking of the air; but in that same interval of time and space, the smaller string has already returned from G to F. Thus, it will sound again at F—the other limit of its path—at the exact moment the string B C sounds at C.

Here, then, occurs the first union of the sounds of both strings. This union happens as often as the <term>quivering <note>original: "tremebunda."</note></term> string B C strikes the air. Since the smaller string traverses its space twice while the larger string traverses its own once, the smaller string must necessarily strike the air twice as fast as the larger one. Consequently, it will sound higher in pitch. Through this frequent union of vibrations—and because of the large <term>auditorium <note>In this context, Kircher refers to the physical space or the "hearing mechanism" of the ear.</note></term>—it affects the <term>vital spirits <note>original: "spiritus vitales." In 17th-century science, these were subtle fluids believed to circulate in the body and transmit sensory data to the soul.</note></term> so sweetly that it produces that delight in the soul which we call the first degree of harmony.

From this it follows that in strings of equal tension, when the striking of the air occurs in both strings at the same time and space, a <term>unison <note>original: "unifonus."</note></term> is produced. The unison relates to harmony as unity relates to number, or as a point relates to a line; therefore, it cannot strictly be called "harmonic" itself. However, the <term>Diapason <note>original: "Diapaſon," the Greek term for an Octave.</note></term> is the sweetest of all <term>consonances <note>original: "conſonantiis." Musical intervals that sound stable and pleasant together.</note></term> because the union of the low and high sounds, perfectly mixed, occurs more frequently than in any other consonance. In this interval, the smaller string has only one sound that does not unite with the larger string, whereas in other consonances...

From this it follows that if two strings B C and F V are equally tensioned, and string B C is set into vibration to produce sound, the other string F V—no matter how untouched it may be—will likewise sound. <note>This refers to the phenomenon of sympathetic resonance, where a resting string begins to vibrate if a nearby string tuned to the same frequency is plucked.</note>

From the Greek for "running through," referring here to the back-and-forth travel of a vibrating string.</note></term> of the struck strings. Therefore, let there be a certain larger string A D, and let the space it covers when struck be A B C D. Moreover, let the other smaller string E F G relate to the first

as two relates to three, which is the <term>three-to-two <note>original: "sesqui-altera"</note></term> proportion of the <term>Fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note></term>. Let both be moved at the same moment (for we are speaking not of a string moving from one place to another, but of the sound that propagates along the string from one end to the other, where, in our way of conceiving it, it causes sound by striking the air—I wish you to take good note of this). Both will run, therefore: the larger from A toward D, and the smaller from E toward G. Soon, therefore, while the smaller [vibration] reaches F, the larger will begin to resonate at D. ...must complete, just as much as the motion of the smaller string from E to C. At A and C, the movements of both strings will first be united at the endpoints of the sound. You see, therefore, that just as the vibration of the strings relates to one another, so do the sounds to the sounds; since, therefore, the vibration of the smaller string relates to the vibration—or the <term>course and recourse <note>Kircher uses "cursum recurfumque" to describe the back-and-forth oscillation of a vibrating string.</note></term>—of the larger string as 3 to 2, so also do the sounds of the smaller string relate to the larger. Thus, the smaller string, having always omitted two sounds which do not unite, will join the sound resulting from the third vibration to the larger string. Hence, the union of low and high sounds in a <term>Fifth <note>original: "diapente"</note></term> occurs more slowly than in an <term>Octave <note>original: "diapason"</note></term>, and consequently it gradually recedes from the sweetness of the former.

## PROPOSITION IV.

->***TWO STRINGS OF EQUAL THICKNESS***<-
->*sound a <term>Fourth <note>original: "Diatessaron"</note></term>, because the sounds unite in a*<-
->*<term>four-to-three <note>original: "sesqui-tertia"</note></term> proportion of time.*<-

First, let the space of the larger string be A, through which, once set in motion, it runs through A V X E C. Let the space of the smaller string be D F E. Let the string relate to the string, and the space to the space, as 3 to 4—namely, in a <term>sesquitertia <note>A mathematical ratio of 4:3, which defines the musical interval of a Perfect Fourth.</note></term> proportion, in which the ratio of the Fourth consists. Both strings having been moved from their endpoints A and D toward C and E, it is certain that the motion of the smaller string, when it arrives at E, will first strike and sound again; and when it has run back from E to F, then the motion of the larger string will first begin to sound at C. 

Therefore, when the smaller string, coming to D, sounds for the second time, the motion of the larger string returning from C will be imminent at point X; and when the smaller string comes to F, the larger will hold at A, where, having reached the limit, it will strike and sound for the second time. Furthermore, the smaller string, having moved forward from F to E, will sound there for the third time, while in the meantime the larger string has moved to V. Thus, no union of sounds has yet been made; they will first acquire this union when the motion of the larger string arrives from V to C, and the smaller from E to D. For they reach the limits of their motion in equal time and space—namely, the larger at C.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 184 regarding the mathematical ratios of musical intervals. <header>ON THE PRODIGIOUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS.</header>

...and the smaller [string] in D. At that point, the union first occurs, such that in this consonance, the smaller string—having always omitted three tones that do not unite with the sounds of the larger string—finally joins the sound resulting from the fourth vibration. The same judgment applies to the other consonances, which will always be more imperfect the further they recede from the simplest <term>Octave <note>original: "Diapason"</note></term>, and the less frequently they unite their sounds, as happens in the major third, the minor third, the sixth, and similar intervals. Thus, the <term>major third <note>original: "ditonus"</note></term>, having always omitted four sounds that do not mix, joins only every fifth; whereas the <term>minor third <note>original: "semiditonus"</note></term>, omitting five unsociable sounds, joins the sound resulting from the sixth vibration of the string to the larger string. See those things which we have treated most extensively regarding these matters in our *Chordosophia* <note>original: "Chordosophia"; literally "The Wisdom of Strings," likely referring to a section in Kircher's earlier work, *Musurgia Universalis*.</note>.

<margin>The Octave is the most perfect of all consonances.</margin>
If, therefore, the slow and fast motions are proportional to one another and mix easily, a <term>consonance</term> arises; but if they are disproportionate and allow no mixture or union, a <term>dissonance</term> is born. Furthermore, they will mix more easily the more similar they are and the closer they are to the source—that is, to unity or the unison. For this reason, the <term>Octave <note>original: "Diapason"</note></term> is the most perfect of all consonances, because it is completed by the first, most perfect, and simplest proportion, distant from the <term>monad <note>The number one, representing the source of all numbers.</note></term> only by unity; such is the double proportion, which stands as one to two. Next to this in perfection is the <term>super-particular <note>A ratio where the larger number is equal to the smaller plus one part of it (e.g., 3:2).</note></term> or <term>sesquialter <note>The 3:2 ratio.</note></term> proportion, which is the <term>Fifth <note>original: "Diapente"</note></term>, standing as 2 to 3. This is followed immediately by the <term>sesquitertia <note>The 4:3 ratio.</note></term> proportion, standing as 3 to 4, which corresponds to the consonance of the <term>Fourth <note>original: "Diatessaron"</note></term>. In the harmonic arrangement of tones, the Fourth is perfect, but in the arithmetic arrangement, it is judged imperfect and dissonant; hence, it is only perfect and consonant <term>relatively <note>original: "secundum quid"; a philosophical term meaning "in a certain respect" rather than absolutely.</note></term>. It follows that these proportions—the sesquialter and sesquitertia—are consonant because the Octave is constituted from them when joined together; they could not constitute the most consonant of all intervals if they themselves were not consonant. Indeed, the whole of Music consists of these three consonances, and all of Music is nothing other than harmony consisting of these three consonances; nor is any <term>Symphony <note>In this context, a "sounding together" or polyphonic composition.</note></term> possible without these three.

<margin>All Music consists of three consonances.</margin>
Therefore, the perfect ratio of consonances—whether simple or composite—consists of these three. However, from this point up to the number six, the consonances depart from their perfection. Such are the <term>Major Third <note>original: "Ditonus"</note></term>, the <term>Minor Third <note>original: "Semiditonus"</note></term>, and the <term>Major Sixth <note>original: "Hexachordon majus"</note></term>,

...the first of which, consisting in the <term>5:4 ratio <note>original: "sesqui-quarta"</note></term>, relates as 4 to 5; the second, in the <term>6:5 ratio <note>original: "sesqui-quinta"</note></term>, relates as 5 to 6. The third, in the <term>5:3 ratio <note>original: "super-bipartiente tertias"</note></term>, relates as 3 to 5. These are called imperfect consonances, which, together with the perfect ones, complete the entire circle of consonances. You see, therefore, how the consonances flowing from the unit [the number one] fulfill the circuit of all their perfection in the number six <note>The "Senarius," or the numbers 1 through 6, which Kircher believed contained the ratios for all harmonious musical intervals.</note>, so that no consonances other than those mentioned can be assigned. He who penetrates these things deeply will clearly see how God (the Best and Greatest) is the Source of all harmony, and how the world flowed from Him, and that the work of the <term>six days of creation <note>original: "hexameron"</note></term> was established by Him; but these things we have treated more extensively in Books 3 and 4, pages 100 and 187, of our *Musurgia* <note>Referring to Kircher’s earlier 1650 work, *Musurgia Universalis*.</note>.

These consonances, therefore, according to their different arrangements on the musical scale, also cause the various constitutions of tones, semitones, <term>dieses <note>Small musical intervals, often quarter-tones or the difference between a major and minor semitone.</note></term>, and chromatics. From their various combinations, according to the varying <term>complexion <note>The physical and psychological temperament of a person, believed in the 17th century to be governed by "humors."</note></term> of men, the spirit is also variously affected. From this result various affections and impulses of the soul, as was said a little earlier; since our spirit is moved according to the logic of the agitated air, no differently than a struck string excites another untouched string. Whence, if our spirits were strings, they, excited by the melodies, would perfectly express the harmony, which I make manifest in the following experiments.

-> # PROPOSITION V. <-
### *On the logic of the SYMPATHY AND ANTIPATHY of Sounds.*

Note first that in sound of this kind, three things can be considered: first, the body which moves by sounding, and the body which is moved to sound by another. Second, a certain proportion or similarity between the sounding bodies. Third, a suitable Medium, such as the air.

Note second, that sound is not only propagated through the medium of the air, but wherever it finds a body in the Medium suitable for its preservation, it exerts its powers within it. For the air, which is dissipated from the sound, seeks union from itself and its own nature; but it preserves the tenor of the motion or sound better and more faithfully in a body apt and ready for its propagation, such as stretched strings.

<meta>This page transitions from the mathematical ratios of musical intervals discussed on page 186 to the physical and physiological effects of sound. Kircher begins to explain the phenomenon of sympathetic resonance—why one string vibrates when another is struck—and how this physical movement translates into the human ear to affect the emotions.</meta>

### ->ON THE MARVELOUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS.<-

<margin>How one string moves another string without being touched.</margin>
From this I infer that a plucked string excites another string of similar tension that remains untouched, either due to the great similarity and proportion which the sounding body and the resonant body have toward one another; for similarity and proportion serve as a kind of mimic of the total motion, acting as its base and foundation. Secondly, this occurs because of the motion of the air; for when the air is moved to the motion of the sound, and since the proportion of sound to sound is the same as the proportion of stirred air to stirred air (as was shown in the preceding sections), it happens that the air stirred by the sound strikes upon a body capable of receiving sound. As it carries the sound along, it strikes that body in the same way the air was struck by the first sound; both the air and the sound in a highly resonant and "airy" body seek union and propagation by their very nature.

<margin>Why do children and adults, when singing together, remain an octave apart?</margin>
Hence, a string moves an equally stretched string, or any other harmonic body or instrument, just as it would a string similar and corresponding to it in sound, because the air, being equally excited, strikes a body similar in both quantity and quality to the one by which it was stirred. From this, the second body—already being disposed toward the sound and "yearning," as it were, for its own perfection or well-being—is likewise stirred by the stimulation <note>original: "vellicatione," literally a plucking or twitching</note> of the surrounding air. Furthermore, a string sounding an <term>Octave <note>original: "Octavam"</note></term>, even if untouched, is nevertheless excited by a lower string because of the great similarity and proportion it has toward it. For in the opinion of all, it does not differ from the lower tone except in its high pitch; hence they are considered the same. Indeed, the high tone is virtually contained within the low tone (as Aristotle testifies), and it is nothing other than a certain sharper and more intense repetition of the lower voice. This is why women singing with men, or children and <term>eunuchs <note>Kircher refers to the high-voiced male singers, often *castrati*, common in 17th-century church music.</note></term> singing with men of more advanced age, naturally sing at the interval of a <term>diapason <note>An octave, the 2:1 ratio.</note></term>. Bells and other sounding instruments also ring out an octave that seems mixed with the lower tone, which would not happen unless a very great similarity and proportion existed between the two.

Furthermore, since the external air relates to the air internal to the string just as the tension of one string relates to another, and a string sounding an octave exists in a <term>1:2 ratio <note>original: "proportione subdupla"</note></term>, it follows that the air, according to this same proportion, also moves and excites the air that is internal and innate to the <term>auditory organ <note>The ear.</note></term>, which is contained between the small membranes. From what has been said, it is clear by what means Music stirs the various passions of the soul; for it has the power of moving the sense of hearing, either with delight, or

# PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION I.

<margin>The spirits in the heart are moved by sounds.</margin>
...with compassion, or in some other similar way, according to the variety of the sound and the moved air. This air moves the sense of hearing with a particular proportion; for when the air is moved in this or that manner and is breathed in by a human being, it moves the spirits in that same way. According to the variety of the sounds, various affections <note>In this context, "affections" refers to emotions or states of mind, such as joy, sadness, or fear.</note> are stirred up in living creatures, just as the spirits are moved by the air being stirred in various ways.

Since the heart is the throne of the spirits <note>Kircher refers to the "vital spirits," which early modern science believed were subtle vapors that carried life and sensation through the body.</note>, these spirits receive the trembling and leaping air into the chest and resonate as one with its "kinsman" (the sound). The remaining spirits located in other parts of the body follow these, moving or restraining the muscles according as the <term>law of numbers</term> <note>The mathematical ratios governing rhythm and melody.</note> either quickens with incitements, stays quiet with a composed tenor, or imitates rest with slowness. This is no different than the strings mentioned a little earlier, where if one string is touched, another equally stretched string trembles in sympathy.

For the spirits that are in the heart are excited by the motion of an external sound much more easily than strings are, because the union between them is so much greater. Thus, a mind depressed and withered by much care "grows green" again when the external air enters it; conversely, a mind that is too elated is restrained or even brought down from its high stage by the opposite process.

*From this, the cause is also clear* why most people, even while doing something else, will sing along with those who are singing; why when one person shouts, a desire to shout seizes everyone; and why in a battle or a public assembly, when one person vociferates or laments, the entire line or crowd immediately shouts or groans. Indeed, we have decided to demonstrate this excitation of our spirits by an external sound through the following experiment.

## Musical Experiment, the First.

Take a glass cup of any size and fill it with pure, clear water. Once this is done, if you rub the outer rim of the glass in a circle for a little while with a moistened index finger, you will eventually perceive a certain wonderful sound like tinkling metal. By this sound, the water is stirred and ripples so vehemently that it seems to be shaken by a wind. If you fill this cup only to the halfway mark, you will indeed perceive a sound, but it will be twice as sharp as the other, so that this sound resounds to the first one as a perfect harmony of an <term>octave</term> <note>original: "διαπασῶν" (diapason), the Greek musical term for an octave.</note>, and consequently produces an even more vigorous rippling of the water.

# ON THE MARVELOUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS.

Take note of this: if you divide this same goblet into five parts and fill three with water, leaving the other two empty, the glass will resonate the harmony of a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"; a musical interval of a perfect fifth.</note> for you, which a weaker rippling of the water will also accompany. Likewise, if the vessel is divided into seven parts and you fill four, leaving three empty, you will perceive a harmony which a weaker rippling of the water will follow, just as before. From this it is clearly evident that our <term>humors</term> <note>In early medicine, these were the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—thought to determine health and temperament.</note> and principally the **spirit**—which holds its seat and dominion primarily in the heart, the workshop of all emotions—are moved by exactly the same principle.

<margin>Why they are terrified by a violent sound.</margin>
Hence, when we perceive some violent sound, such as thunder or the explosion of a cannon, we are seized by a certain shuddering, because the spirit suffers a too violent striking and scattering by a sound disproportionate to itself; from this arise horror and terror. For this reason also, we cannot bear the screeching sound if someone scrapes iron with a knife, since by its harshness it affects the body poorly, twitching certain muscles that lead to the stomach and the brain. <margin>How brute animals are delighted by Music.</margin> All these things apply to animals as well; for there are those that both rejoice in melodies and are soothed by them. This could not happen if that power of Music flowed solely and immediately from the <term>rational soul</term> <note>In Scholastic philosophy, the "rational soul" was unique to humans; Kircher argues music acts on the "sensitive soul" or physical body, which humans share with animals.</note>. Nevertheless, man is delighted by Music in infinitely more ways than animals, because he understands melodies better and more perfectly.

->EXPERIMENT II.<-

Let nine strings be stretched over a solid, smooth, and hollow board in the order that appears in the following diagram. Let the first two strings, A and F, be equal in both quantity and quality (or tension)—that is, let them resonate in unison in their pitch—and let the rest be arranged by tone according to the diagram placed below.

<margin>One string is accustomed to moving another at a distance.</margin>
Therefore, if you strike the first string, F, with a pick, it will cause all the strings that are <term>isotone</term> <note>original: "ισοτόνους"; meaning having the same tension or pitch.</note> with it—that is, equally tuned—to resonate, however untouched they may be. There is this difference, however: the string which is closer to the one in unison will resonate more and seem to move more perceptibly. Thus, first, the motion and sound of the untouched string F, which is in unison, will be felt most strongly in response to the sound of the struck string; it will also excite the octave A and make it sound, though a little more weakly than the former, as it recedes somewhat from the unison. For if you place a feather upon it, [it will move...]

->### PHONURGY BOOK II. SECTION I.<-

...you will feel some vibration of the string, though it be slight, which I have found noted by almost no one; for most have believed that only the <term>Hypate</term> <note>The lowest note or string in the classical Greek musical system.</note> at unison and the <term>Nete</term> <note>The highest note or string in the classical Greek system.</note> are moved by another untouched string. But long experience has taught me the contrary: for all strings consonant with the <term>Hypate</term> are moved, but, as I have said, more effectively the closer they are to their source. Dissonant strings, however, being unable to be moved by a friendly provocation, will persist in their own stubbornness.

---

A technical illustration of a rectangular musical instrument (a polychord or monochord variant). Eleven strings are stretched across a wooden soundbox. On the left side, the strings are labeled with musical letters: A, E, D, C, B, A, G, F, and F. Two dotted curved lines indicate harmonic intervals: "Octave" <note>original: "Diapason"</note> spanning the full set of strings, and "Fifth" <note>original: "diapente"</note> spanning a smaller section. ---

<margin>A marvelous occurrence regarding sympathetic sounds.</margin>
Furthermore, not only do strings incite other strings stretched in the same way, but pipes and trumpets also cause strings that rejoice in a similar tone to sound. So that the truth of this matter may be more fully demonstrated, I will record here what once happened to me in **MAINZ** <note>original: "MOGUNTIÆ"</note>. While I was occupied with certain business during a solemn Sacred Rite in a choir room <note>original: "odæo"</note> separated from the church, behold—I heard a **large viol** <note>original: "chelin majorem"; a stringed instrument of the viol family.</note> resonating now and then through various intervals. It had been left hanging on the wall by one of the singers, who had previously tuned it exactly to the church organ <note>original: "organum templi"</note> without loosening the strings before departing. It sounded without being touched and with no one present, moved by some hidden motion.

Astonished by the novelty of the thing, I approached to examine this prodigious sound more closely. As I did so, I discovered that as soon as the **Organist** <note>original: "Organoedus"</note> played pipes that exactly corresponded in every way to the strings of the said viol, the strings themselves were stirred by that tone, just as if they had been struck by a plectrum. I later demonstrated this **harmonic sympathy** <note>original: "harmoniacam sympathiam"</note> to many others, much to their great wonder.

I later attempted to employ this **Phonurgy** <note>Kircher's term for the "science or work of sound."</note> in other places, but found that a setting similar to the one mentioned occurred more by chance than by design...

<summary>Kircher describes how strings vibrate sympathetically not just in unison, but across harmonic intervals, and shares a personal anecdote from Mainz where an organ's pipes caused a hanging viol to play itself through "harmonic sympathy."</summary>

But regarding the construction of this, since it is not the proper place for it here, we will perhaps speak more fully elsewhere, God willing.

## EXPERIMENT III.

Let five glass goblets be taken, all of the same size and capacity, which you will place adjacent to each other in the order expressed here in the underlying diagram. Let one goblet be filled with brandy <note>original: "aqua-vitæ," literally "water of life," referring to distilled spirits</note>, another goblet with a choice wine, another with thin <note>purified or light</note> water, another with thick water—such as sea water or oil—and the middle one with common water. This done, with the tip of a moistened finger, rub the rim of the goblet until you perceive that ringing sound <note>original: "tinnitum"</note> which we taught how to excite in the first experiment.

<margin>
Sound moves various liquids <note>original: "humores"</note> in different ways.
</margin>

For this very sharp sound will, quite marvelously, stir all the remaining liquids, and indeed more vehemently in proportion to how much thinner one liquid is than another. Hence the brandy in the goblet, which represents a fiery or <term>choleric</term> <note>In Kircher's time, the "choleric" humor was associated with the element of fire, heat, and rapid movement.</note> nature, will jump and vibrate more than the rest; while the wine in the goblet, representing a <term>sanguine</term> <note>The "sanguine" humor was associated with the element of air, thought to be warm and moist.</note> or airy temperament, [will move] moderately...

-># BOOK II OF PHONURGIA, SECTION I.<-

...will undergo such agitation; while thin water <note>original: "Aqua... subtilis"</note>, exhibiting a phlegmatic constitution, will cause a slower and more obtuse commotion than the others. Thick water contained in a goblet, however, will be scarcely capable of motion on account of its earthy complexion. In no different a way must we judge the power of Music to stir the affections of our mind. 

If, therefore, our spirit is refined and hot, Music will stir up proud, insolent, and angry movements; if it is more refined and of a moderate quality, it will incite one to love, joy, gladness, and amorous affections. If the spirit is more compressed <note>or "condensed"</note>, it will move one toward tears, religion, restraint, and other pathetic <note>In the 17th-century sense: related to "pathos" or deep feeling</note> affections. Finally, if the spirit is thick—as in men weighed down by sorrow—it will be incapable of all commotion, and for that reason, it is rightly said that "Music in mourning is an untimely tale" <note>A reference to the biblical book of Sirach 22:6: "A tale out of season is as music in mourning."</note>.

From this experiment, it also becomes clear how one and the same sound may achieve different effects in different men of different complexions <note>The "four humors" theory: the balance of blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile that determined temperament</note>. For a Choleric person is moved in one way, a Sanguine in another, and a Phlegmatic in yet another. All of these things shine forth so clearly in this single experiment that they demonstrate the moving power of Music almost apodictically <note>term: apodictically — with absolute, undeniable certainty</note>. This is also seen in strings of equal length but made of different materials, such as animal intestines or metals; for just as the weight of one metal relates to another, so does string to string, and sound to sound, and so forth.

## VARIOUS EXPERIMENTS.

At times, the violence in the excitation of the rippling water is such that it may even break the glass vessel itself. Not long ago, a small work appeared in *the Netherlands* <note>original: "Batavia"</note> titled *On the Breaking of a Bellied Glass Flask Caused by the Sound of the Voice*, by a certain inventor from *Amsterdam* named *Hasslurg*, or *a glass-worker*. He demonstrates the experiment in this manner: having first explored the sound of the glass vessel or flask by a preliminary tapping, he then sounds his voice into the opening of the glass, so tuned that he raises his voice nearly a diapason <note>term: diapason — the Greek term διαπασῶν, meaning an octave</note> above the sound of the glass—that is, just a little above the octave or the harmonic consonance known to Musicians, consisting of a double ratio <note>The mathematical ratio of 2:1 that defines an octave</note>. From this, the shattering of the glass vessel immediately follows. The learned man **Daniel Georg Morhof** <note>A German scholar whose work "Epistola de scypho vitreo per certum vocis sonum fracto" (1672) famously discussed this phenomenon</note> has attempted to inquire into the cause of this matter with great care.

# ON THE PRODIGIOUS POWER AND EFFICACY OF SOUNDS

...in the *booklet cited above*; but the case is still before the judge <note>original: "sub judice lis est" — a Latin legal idiom meaning the matter is undecided</note>; although many have attempted it, they have not done so with the success they hoped for. Moved by the novelty of the matter, Johannes Janssonius <note>Johannes Janssonius (1588–1664) was a famous Dutch publisher who produced many of Kircher’s works</note> sent me the booklet and asked me for the cause of this exotic effect. However, since I did not yet have full knowledge of the shape of the glass vessel, its width, capacity, and other circumstances, I did not think my judgment should be rashly offered in such difficult matters that I had not yet personally investigated—at least until I am made more certain of the exact and secure method of operation. Indeed, I would not easily admit that the cause of this effect can be attributed solely to the sound of the Octave <note>original: "Octavæ, quem διαπασῶν [diapason] efficit"</note>. There must, therefore, be another cause for this unusual effect. I myself have also performed and seen various experiments of breaking glass through sound alone; for we see that old windows are broken by the violent sound of thunder, as well as by the explosion of some nearby piece of artillery <note>original: "tormenti bellici," literally an engine of war</note>. But because these things are too well known for us to dwell on them further, let us move on to other things.

In years past, an invention appeared for preparing glass with a certain water in such a way that if one small piece of its tip is broken, the entire mass is reduced to an imperceptible powder; I have assigned the cause of this in my work *The Subterranean World, on the Art of Glassmaking* <note>original: *Mundo subterraneo, de arte vitriaria*. This refers to "Prince Rupert's Drops," which Kircher studied extensively</note>. If, therefore, a flask were blown from this glass material, there is no doubt that it would be instantly dissolved if a deep voice were sounded into it. The same would happen if you were to cut even the smallest notch into the side of the flask with a diamond stylus <note>original: "adamantino graphio"</note>; and then, if you sounded into it with a moist voice <note>original: "voce humidiori" — likely referring to a breathy or resonant quality of singing</note> after first warming it, it is inevitable that the flask would break. But these things will be more fully explained at a more opportune time.

# SECTION II.
## HEALING PHONURGY
<note>original: "Phonurgia Latrica" — "Latrica" is derived from the Greek word for healing or medicine</note>
->Or<-
->On the Disturbances of the soul, and curing diseases through the power of Music.<-

### CHAPTER I.
->ON THE CAUSES OF PRODIGIOUS CURATIVE MUSIC.<-

<margin>Cabalists <note>Followers of Jewish mysticism</note> ascribe everything to the Sephirotic channels.</margin>
<margin>Platonists attribute exotic effects to the Soul of the World.</margin>
<margin>Astrologers and Alchemists refer them to the influences of the celestial bodies.</margin>
Various authors assign various reasons and causes for this miraculous cure. The *Cabalists*, in their fashion, attribute everything to the Sephirotic channels <note>In the Kabbalah, the Sephiroth are the ten attributes or emanations through which the Divine is revealed</note>, through which divine power flows into the individual parts of the world. Indeed, the manner in which they assert this happens, we have described in our *Egyptian Oedipus* <note>Kircher's own encyclopedic work on ancient mysteries and hieroglyphs</note>, in the treatise explaining the tree of the ten Sephiroth. The *Platonists*, in their own way, establish the causes of these wonderful effects in the harmonic bonds of the World Soul <note>original: "Animæ Mundi" — the concept that the entire universe is a living, breathing being</note> (which they also call the *Colchodea* <note>A term used by Renaissance occultists to describe the intelligence or spirit governing the world</note>), which unites all things to itself. *Astrologers* and *Alchemists* attribute everything to the influences of the higher celestial bodies; since we have treated the theories of all these in the *Astrology of the Consonant and Dissonant*, or in *Mundane Music* <note>The "Music of the Spheres," or the mathematical harmony of the planets</note>, we refer the *Reader* there.

And I truly wonder that such wise men, having abandoned natural causes, have fallen into such absurd beliefs so far removed from all human reason. They seem to have done the same as those who, leaving behind their riches at home, travel to the *Indies* to acquire the more abundant riches found there, exposing themselves to countless dangers. But before we assign the genuine causes of each effect, let us first, in our usual manner, propose certain suppositions to establish our demonstrations on a more solid foundation.

I suppose, therefore, *First*, that the Air—not only the external air, but also the internal air <note>The "pneuma" or spiritus thought to reside within the human body and sense organs</note> present within every thing—is moved according to the nature of sound, such that if the sounds are in a double proportion <note>An octave</note>, they imprint that same proportion in the air; if in a triple <note>A twelfth</note>, triple; if in quadruple <note>A double octave</note>, quadruple, and so on for the rest. And just as visible forms <note>original: "species visibiles" — the philosophical term for the images or "likenesses" that objects send through the air to the eye</note> emanating conically from objects do not become sensible in the medium, unless while...

difference: the former exist permanently, while the latter exist transitionally. Thus, if the motion of the air, stirred harmonically, were visible to us, we would perceive the same harmony in any part of the air that musicians produce. I suppose, *Secondly*, that this kind of prodigious cure of diseases can be considered in three ways: First, supernaturally; Second, by the art of a Demon; Third, naturally. To the first kind belong all those cures that have a manifest miracle attached to them. Thus **Christ** our Savior recalled **Lazarus** from the dead with a loud voice and restored hearing to a deaf man by whispering this word *Ephetah* <note>A Latinization of the Aramaic "Ephphatha," meaning "Be opened" (Mark 7:34)</note> into his ears, along with similar events we read of both in *Sacred Scripture* and in the *Lives of the Saints*. Secondly, it is certain that sometimes such
<margin>Prodigious cures through Music can sometimes occur by the help of the devil through a pact.</margin>
prodigious cures produced by Music occur by the help of a Demon through an implicit or explicit pact. For just as Magi <note>Sorcerers or practitioners of magic</note>, having made a pact with a Demon, can set any sign by which the devil performs the effect of health, so too can the devil use Music or any musical instrument to perform said effect. By this art, the **Brothers of the Rosy Cross** <note>The Rosicrucians, a secret brotherhood of mystics often associated with alchemy and miraculous healing in the 17th century</note> are said to heal any diseases, even incurable ones. Thirdly, there is the natural power of Music, which was partly discussed in the preceding parts and will be further discussed in the following. We have undertaken to treat only this kind of natural power in this place.

I suppose, *Thirdly*, that our entire body is transpirable <note>original: "transpirabile" — porous or permeable, allowing "spirits" or air to pass through the skin and tissues</note>, and that the nerves and muscles receive the same impression through an external, material sound as strings stretched over smooth and
<margin>Just as strings, so also muscles and nerves can be stirred by Music.</margin>
sonorous wood acquire. And just as these strings are stirred not only by a proportional external sound of the air but also by an internal one, so too are the nerves and muscles agitated by the air and the "implanted spirit" (the leader of the motor faculty) enclosed within them. When the perceiving soul then feels this proportional form, it must necessarily undergo the greatest alterations of either joy or sadness.

I suppose, *Fourthly*, that music can cure not just any random diseases, but specifically those that depend directly on black and yellow bile <note>These are two of the four "humors" in pre-modern medicine; black bile was associated with melancholy, and yellow bile with anger or mania</note>. For I believe it is impossible <note>original: "ἀδύνατον"</note> to cure wasting diseases, epilepsy, gout, or very slow-moving ailments, nor even those where some vital organ has become corrupted.

I suppose, *Fifthly*, that it is necessary for the musician to have a profound understanding of the nature and temperament <note>original: "complexionem" — the unique balance of humors in an individual</note> of the person he wishes to cure. Furthermore, the greatest consideration must be given to the time, the place, and similar circumstances, without which I believe the intended effect can hardly be achieved. Having established these suppositions, let us now examine the miraculous cure of certain diseases, so that the curious *Reader* may know what to think of them.

### CHAPTER II.
### HOW DAVID, BY PLAYING THE HARP, RESCUED SAUL FROM THE EVIL SPIRIT.

<margin>The Rabbis assert that David cured Saul by the power of a ten-stringed Psaltery.</margin>
To better unravel this question, we will first cite the words of Holy *Scripture*; it is written thus in *1 Kings, chapter 16* <note>In modern Bibles, this is 1 Samuel 16:23</note>:

> Whenever, therefore, the evil Spirit of GOD seized SAUL, David took his harp and struck it with his hand; and SAUL was refreshed and felt better, for the evil Spirit departed from him.

The words of the SACRED TEXT clearly teach that music drove away some kind of evil Spirit of GOD, but various authors explain how this happened in different ways. The RABBIS say that in this passage DAVID, while curing SAUL, played a ten-stringed harp constructed after the model of the Sephirotic tree <note>original: "Zephyroticæ" — referring to the *Sefirot*, the ten attributes or emanations in Jewish Kabbalah</note>. They claim it produced this effect by being fruitful with the outpourings of the ten divine virtues, like certain fruits; for they say DAVID knew the star with which the harmony had to be joined so that the frenzy might be easily blunted and Saul might feel better; thus says Rabbi ABENEZRA <note>Abraham ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167), a celebrated Spanish Renaissance Jewish scholar</note> in the *Great Rabbinic Bible* <note>original: "Micra heggadolah"</note>.

<margin>Rabbi Abenezra devises astrological trifles regarding Saul's cure.</margin>
*Pico della Mirandola*, in his *7th and 8th Mathematical Theses*, says that Music moves the spirits <note>In Kircher's time, "spirits" were subtle fluids in the body that carried signals between the soul and the muscles</note> so that they serve the soul, just as medicine agitates them to rule the body. He argues that music heals the body through the soul, just as medicine heals the soul by means of the body. From these points, one can easily gather how DAVID easily suppressed the raging SAUL; who then does not see that ABENEZRA’S commentary is the most vain of fictions?

<summary>Kircher examines the biblical account of David soothing King Saul with music, dismissing astrological and Kabbalistic explanations in favor of a more "natural" medical theory involving the human soul and vital spirits.</summary>

-> MEDICAL PHONURGY <-

<page-num>197</page-num>

[be]? For David did not inspect those aspects of the stars while he calmed Saul's madness; rather, he struck [the strings] with his hand as often as Saul commanded, whether this or that astrological aspect was in force.

<margin>What things might dissipate the melancholic humor.</margin>
But we, having rejected all that has been said, assert that David freed Saul from melancholy and from that species of madness—which David himself perfectly understood—and subsequently even from the demon, not by herbs, juices, pills, or other substances that dissipate melancholy, but by the force and efficacy of Music alone. To demonstrate the matter more quickly: Note that those things cure madness or mania which open the pores, ward off smoky vapors, clear obstructions, and refresh the heart. A harmonic consensus <note>A musical harmony or arrangement</note> is able to achieve this; for since Music consists of sounds produced by the commotion of the air, when you use those sounds which move the aerial spirits of the madman, these spirits—having been made warmer through motion according to *Supposition 3* and also swifter—will eventually thin out and dissipate the entire mixture of the melancholic humor <note>In ancient medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids like black bile (melancholy) that dictated health and temperament</note>.

Or, if you prefer that we relax those spirits and make them quieter so they do not so sharply pierce the membranes of the brain, we ought to use very slow sounds and small intervals. Thus, through a harmony of slow motions, those spirits and biting vapors that fly out from the stomach, spleen, and midriff into the brain, being made slower, may leave the man at peace. <margin>The ways in which David cured Saul with music.</margin> Therefore, Davidic Music was able to soothe Saul in two ways. First, by moving, heating, and thinning Saul's spirits and vapors in such a way that the dissipated melancholic juice was driven out from the cells of the brain or dissolved into thin air, passing away through insensible transpiration, sweat, and the pores. Second, once those spirits abandoned the melancholic juice, the madness could not rage until they returned, because the humor itself is earthy and, as it were, destitute of action unless the vital and animal spirits move it and carry it here and there. But the spirits abandoned the humor when they flew to the ears, as if with loose reins, for the sake of perceiving the harmony; while the music lasted, the madness ceased. When it ceased, the spirits did indeed return, but having been made lighter and more agile, they were able to expel or thin the melancholy for some time, and perhaps convert some part of it into a more benign vapor or state. From this it is clear that this matter proceeded not from a casual sound of the harp, but from the greatest art and David’s supreme skill in playing; for David, being of the highest and most sagacious talent, and moreover as an armor-bearer <note>original: "Armiger"</note>...

<summary>Kircher argues that David cured Saul’s madness through the physical effects of music on the body’s "spirits" and "humors" rather than through astrology or medicine. He explains that specific musical rhythms can either dissipate harmful vapors through heat or calm them through slow movements.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the discussion of the physical and spiritual mechanisms by which David used music to cure King Saul's madness, specifically focusing on the interaction between music, the bodily humors, and demonic influence.</meta>

<header>PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION II.</header>

[Ar]morbearer <note>original: "miger", likely "Armiger"</note> of SAUL, since he always enjoyed his presence and because of the great familiarity he shared with him, knew his nature, his inclinations, the impulses of his soul, and the other passions by which he was agitated extremely well. Therefore, urged not so much by his own instinct as by a divine one, he undoubtedly took up the lyre, or whatever other instrument was at hand. For as it is related in the *Shield of the Heroes* <note>original: "Schilte Gilbormi," referring to the Hebrew work *Shilte Ha-Gibborim* by Abraham Portaleone, which discusses ancient musical instruments</note>, DAVID knew the use of musical instruments and played each one most expertly, as was said in the section on the *Instrumental Music of the Hebrews*. Thus, he knew how to skillfully adapt an appropriate sound to the <term>humor</term> <note>In historical medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids believed to control health and temperament; an excess of black bile caused "melancholy" or madness</note> of the *King*, perhaps by reciting certain rhythms which he knew were most pleasing for SAUL to hear and suited to the task, or even by enticing him with a metrical dance, until at last he achieved the intended effect. For when the harmonic motion of the lyre was joined by the sight of his Armorbearer—who was a youth of beautiful and handsome appearance by which Saul was usually wonderfully moved—the spirits of the muscles were excited. The words, joined to the harmony and rhythmically tickling the hearing, lifted the soul as if from a dark prison into a high region of light. In this state, the dissipated smoky spirits that had been pressing upon the heart finally gave the heart room to expand; from this expansion, joy and a rest from troubles necessarily followed.

## COROLLARY I.

<margin>How Music heals the melancholic and the demon-possessed.</margin>
It is clear from what has been said how, once the dark vapors of the heart were dissipated, the "evil spirit from the Lord" was consequently driven out. For since the <term>melancholic humor</term> <note>Often referred to as "black bile"</note> is dark, obscure, and gloomy, it truly offers a most fitting seat for Demons. This is evident in the demon-possessed, the frenzied, those possessed by spirits <note>original: "energumenis"</note>, and those in their menses; since they abound in much <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bile"</note>, they make themselves susceptible to the various illusions of Demons, who fix their seat in that dark vapor and agitate the soul in various ways. The most obvious signs of this are the "exotic operations" they perform: sometimes speaking in tongues they do not know, sometimes divining the future, or performing various other feats which can by no means be ascribed to pure human nature. Therefore, since the viscous and dreg-like melancholic humor is like an instrument for a Demon to agitate the soul in strange ways, it is no wonder that once the humor is dissipated, the soul [is restored to its] former...

restored to their former peace as if by a right of return <note>original: "postliminio," a legal term for the restoration of rights or status upon returning from exile</note>, as we will later say concerning those suffering from *Tarantism* <note>A condition, common in southern Italy, where victims were said to be cured of a tarantula’s venom only by dancing to specific music</note>.

### COROLLARY II.

It is evident from what has been said in what manner Music removes the Plague, Lycanthropy <note>A form of madness where the patient believes they have become a wolf</note>, Madness of the soul, and other disturbances in all those cases we have mentioned previously. This occurs through the benefit of the spirits <note>In 17th-century physiology, "spirits" were subtle fluids that carried commands from the soul to the body</note>, which, having been rendered more refined, faster, and warmer, dissipate and thin out or mitigate those <term>humors</term> <note>Bodily fluids like phlegm or bile; an imbalance was thought to cause disease</note> by which the diseases were produced. Furthermore, the mind is distracted for a time from its own imagination—that is, from the perception of the disease which afflicts the soul more than is just. Music so cheers the mind that, once aroused, it directs all the spirits to drive out the infirmity. These spirits, due to the effective command of the mind, run and fly here and there until they have fulfilled their orders, in much the same way as certain North winds, once stirred up, perfectly purge air infected by contagion and ward off the rot of that same air when mixed with foul vapors. For since the vital spirit is aerial or ethereal, as soon as it perceives sharp sounds and air moving quickly, it imitates that motion and its Rhythm or cadence. For just as the operation of the intellect follows the actions of the <term>phantasia</term> <note>The faculty of the mind that forms mental images</note>, and the acts of the will and the sensitive appetite follow the movements of the mind and imagination—so much so that you can hardly distinguish the movement of one from the other—so those corporeal spirits, which are the primary instruments of the soul, easily follow the impressions and movements of the alteration of the air.

## *CONCERNING THE MARVELOUS HISTORY OF A CERTAIN*
## *King of Denmark driven mad by the power of Music.*

The following example will show that Music not only releases one from madness but also incites one toward it. *CRANZIUS* <note>Albert Krantz (c. 1450–1517), a German historian</note> relates the case in Book 5 of *Denmark*, Chapter 3, as does *OLAUS Magnus* <note>A Swedish writer and archbishop (1490–1557) known for his descriptions of Northern peoples</note>, in these words:
> When *ERIC* <note>King Eric I of Denmark, known as "the Good" (r. 1095–1103)</note>, having now returned to his kingdom, was holding a solemn Court and delighting in the industry of many soldiers and craftsmen alike, there was present among others a Musician. This man claimed to possess such skill in his art that he could call men into whatever passions he wished; from

<margin>
The King of Denmark driven into a frenzy by the power of Music.
</margin>

*...he boasted that he could make the sad joyful, the cheerful sorrowful, the indignant peaceful, and the calm indignant, even driving them into a state of insane fury. The more he claimed he could perform these feats, the more he made the King eager to test them. Soon the artist began to repent of his boasting, wishing he had not spoken so highly of himself; for to test such things on a King was not without danger. Furthermore, he feared that if he failed to prove his words with actions, he would be held as a liar or something similar, which carried its own risk. He begged those he could to turn the King from this desire, but he achieved nothing. For the more he refused to demonstrate his art, the more he inflamed the King’s curiosity. When he saw he could not escape even if he fulfilled his boasts, he begged that all weapons by which injury might be inflicted be removed. Then, he arranged for several people to stay outside the sound of the <term>cithara</term> <note>A stringed instrument of the lyre family, often associated in the Renaissance with the power of ancient Greek music</note> who could be called upon, and ordered that once they were called, they should strike the cithara from the musician's hands and break it over his head. Once everything was properly arranged, he approached the King, who was shut away in the Hall with a few others, and began to play the cithara. At first, with a grave tone, he instilled a certain sadness in the listeners; then, by singing more pleasingly, he turned it into joy, so that they were nearly leaping about in jest. Then, attempting sharper <term>modes</term> <note>Specific arrangements of notes or scales; in early music theory, different modes were believed to provoke specific emotional "affects" like anger or piety</note>, he stirred up a kind of indignation. When this grew strong, the King and those standing by could be seen to fall into a frenzy. Soon he gave the signal to those hiding to enter and restrain the now-raging King; first, as agreed, they smashed the cithara, and then they moved to restrain the King. But so great was the man's strength that he killed some with his bare fists. Then, after he had been overwhelmed by many cushions, that heat of passion subsided. But after he had recovered, he grieved intensely that he had raged against those whom he had previously held as his most faithful servants.*

Behold a truly marvelous history, which <term>Saxo Grammaticus</term> <note>A 12th-century Danish historian and author of the *Gesta Danorum* (Deeds of the Danes)</note> also records in Book 12. They say this King, named ERIC and surnamed THE GOOD, flared into such a fury that, having broken down the doors of the court and seized a sword, he ran through four men. When his mind was restored, he left his son as Governor of the kingdom and set out for <term>Jerusalem</term> <note>A common act of pilgrimage or crusade to seek divine forgiveness for grave sins</note> for the sake of atoning for the homicides, and he died in <term>Cyprus</term>. Here, indeed, no small doubt arises: how could the sound of a cithara have such ineffable power that it could snatch a King, who was by nature good, merciful, and mild, into such great furies? For while the fact that Alexander was driven into a rage by Timotheus <note>A famous ancient Greek musician often cited as the ultimate example of musical influence over the passions</note> and incited to take up arms by the cithara was not so surprising in a King of effervescing bile and a truly martial soul...

->PHONURGIA LATRICA.<-
<note>original: "Phonurgia Latrica" — Healing Sound-Work, or the application of sound to medicine.</note>

For since he <note>Alexander the Great</note> was filled with fiery spirits, these—once stirred up by a <term>Martial mode</term> <note>A style of music associated with the Greek god of war, intended to provoke aggression</note>—could easily induce a state of fury through a skilled <term>cithara</term>-player who understood Alexander’s temperament.

But how this musician incited the excellent King of Denmark into such an excessive heat of the mind, I do not fully see. Certainly, several circumstances persuade me that this effect was not entirely natural. For the musician requested that, while the King was in his frenzy, the cithara should be smashed against his own <note>the musician's</note> head, and that only after it was shattered should the King be restrained. This action implies some sort of hidden <term>pact</term> <note>An agreement with a demon or supernatural entity</note>. For if, as he boasted, he knew how to rouse men into any emotion or change of mind by the sound of the cithara, why did he not call the raging King back from his violent commotion to a gentler state using softer rhythms? Historians record that <term>Timotheus</term> and <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>Ancient Greek thinkers and musicians famous for using music to influence behavior</note> recalled youths burning with lust back to self-control in this way. Furthermore, the musician's hesitation—his regret over his promised performance—clearly shows that he foresaw an infallible effect on the King and, consequently, feared that great danger threatened his own head. Also, I see no reason why he wanted the guards to stand outside the reach of the cithara's sound; for as has been said, music is not suited to inciting everyone in the same way, but rather different people are moved differently according to their various natural temperaments. Therefore, there was undoubtedly some hidden pact involved here, by which he produced the same effect so powerfully in everyone. Or, if there was no pact, I would say a demon involved itself in this action, as in the case of **SAUL**. We read that as soon as the evil spirit invaded him, he became so insane that no *Harp of David* <note>original: "Davidica cythara"</note> was sufficient to drive it out. For it is certain that **SAUL** was not always cured by **DAVID**, since twice he attempted to transfix the player with his spear; the harmonious sound was insufficient to dissipate such a violent commotion of <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis" — one of the four humors thought to cause melancholy or rage</note> maintained by a demon. However, the hatred **SAUL** had conceived against **DAVID** could also have greatly detracted from the skill of David’s art. In short, in such prodigious cures performed through harmony, I would believe that something preternatural always coexisted with the natural action—especially if they are driven so far by the force of music that they are carried away even into open murder, as is clear in this present example and in King **SAUL**. For those affected by <term>Tarantism</term> <note>original: "Tarantifmo" — a dancing mania attributed to the bite of a tarantula</note>, although they may assume entirely strange behaviors by the power of music and become like those who are frenzied, they never...

### A nearly miraculous acoustic display, which a nine-year-old girl gave here in Rome.

<margin>
Bringing music into practice.
</margin>
**I** shall add to these cases one no less [worthy] <note>original: ἄξιον (axion)</note> and indeed [paradoxical] <note>original: παράδοξον (paradoxon)</note> than the previous acoustic miracle, which occurred as follows. In the past months of the *Year 1673*, an honorable man and celebrated *Sicilian* musician, a native of *Palermo*, arrived here together with his wife and little daughter named FELICE, aged 9 years and measuring 4 1/2 <term>palms</term> <note>A Roman unit of measure; at 4.5 palms, the girl would be roughly 3 feet 3 inches tall</note> in stature. At such a tender age, she had acquired such great skill both in music and in playing stringed instruments that even among the most expert *Roman* musicians, there were those who suspected something higher than human art lay hidden within her. I too was invited to see and hear this kind of acoustic performance. I confess I attended all the more willingly because a burning desire had settled in my mind to understand both the method and theory of her art, as well as her gestures and expressions. First, I was introduced into the <term>Odeum</term> <note>A room or small theater dedicated to musical performances</note> which was equipped with every kind of instrument. While I waited there with a few companions, expecting the performance to begin with bated breath, the little girl soon emerged from a nearby chamber. She was not adorned with any cosmetic dyes, nor were her curls styled with curling irons; rather, she was dressed in simple and common clothing. Although her physical stature was like that of a pygmy, she nonetheless displayed a certain heroic authority <note>original: "viraginem" — a woman of extraordinary spirit or masculine strength</note> through the gravity and seriousness of her manners. Ready to give a demonstration of her art, she first greeted the guests with singular modesty and then began her performance on the psaltery, which they call the *Harp*.

The *Harp* was nearly 10 palms high <note>About 7 feet 4 inches, more than double the girl's height</note>, equipped with a triple row of strings and various divisions. Because of her small stature, she could not reach the <term>tuning pins</term> <note>original: "pyrolas" — iron pegs used to tighten strings</note> (which they call keys) to adjust the strings. Her father therefore lifted her and placed her on a seat. In this position, she began to tune the strings with such ingenious industry that—whereas this process usually brings boredom to other players and lutists—in her case, the very act of tuning was so full of harmony that it became a delight to the listeners. Once the adjustment of the strings was finished, she immediately began to strike the strings with the movement of her delicate fingers, using such talent that she held everyone present motionless with astonishment.
