# PHONURGIA LATRICA.

First, with a book placed before her filled with the harmonic compositions of the most celebrated musicians, in which nothing was found so hidden in the art of music that she did not express it most exactly and skillfully; indeed, now from the highest string to the lowest, and from here to the highest, and then again to the middle ones, through various ascents and descents and through an ingenious arrangement of fugues following one after another, she tickled the ears of the invited guests with wonderful grace. Nor did she stop here: none of those musicians whom they call **Castrati** <note>Male singers who were castrated before puberty to preserve a high soprano or alto range; they were the superstars of 17th-century opera and church music</note> could produce any melodic subject that the industrious singer (*the Italians call it "to accompany"*) <note>original: *accompagnare*</note> did not follow most exactly and most skillfully according to all the rhythms of the measures; this seemed a miracle to those who well knew the difficulty of the task. But now to other matters. After giving a demonstration on the Harp, the *fortunate* <note>A play on her name, Felice, which means "happy" or "fortunate" in Latin (*felix*)</note> songstress moved to the instrument they call the **Harpsichord** <note>original: *Clavicymbalum*</note>, where with equal success, both from the book and by her own power and invention, she brought forth the great wonders of her talent.

Next, a lyre, which they call a **Violin**, was handed to her, upon which she likewise played with such grandeur of art and such dexterity that whether one considers the power and efficacy of her mind, or the speed of her tender hands and the excellence of her art, it seemed nothing more could be desired. For now, scraping two, three, or four strings at once with the bow, she exhibited an entire symphony of violins; then she competed, always victorious, with her parent (who is himself conspicuous for his skill in playing), matching him voice for voice and stroke for stroke. There remained a spectacle which surpassed all the other feats reviewed thus far: the Harp and Harpsichord were arranged in such a position that she could conveniently touch both on either side. Thus, she first played a prelude on the Harpsichord with her right hand alone; then on the Harp likewise with the left hand alone; then she drove such continuous rhythms of tones from the strings of both instruments that you might rightly doubt whether the greater delight of the symphony reached the eyes from the movement of her hands, so dexterously occupied on both instruments, or the ears. This was especially true when the voice of the singer was added to the symphony of the two instruments; by this, she herself formed a perfect **Triphonium** <note>A "triple-sound" or trio performed by a single person</note>. And although her tender fingers could not reach the <term>diapason</term> <note>The interval of an octave</note>, she nevertheless adapted them with such skill that she always achieved it in all things.

And these are the things which [I describe] to the curious **Reader** concerning the nine-year-old girl’s Pho-

# CHAPTER IV.
## CONCERNING THE PRODIGIOUS CURE OF THOSE POISONED
## BY THE BITE OF THE TARANTULA through Music

**I**n my judgment, nothing can be more marvelous than the emotions and effects produced by the <term>Tarantula</term> in those poisoned by its venom. Relying on infallible experience, we believe we can demonstrate all the effects produced by this prodigious cure of diseases performed through music by following this single example. However, since we have treated this type of medicine in detail in our *Magnetic Art* <note>original: *Arte nostra Magnetica*. Kircher's 1641 work *Magnes* explored "magnetism" in a broad sense, including the attraction between music and the human soul.</note>, it seemed appropriate to repeat the same here only summarily, lest we appear to have passed over such a notable argument for the **Art of Consonance and Dissonance** <note>Kircher's theory that the universe is held together by harmonic "consonance" and that disease is a "dissonance" that music can resolve.</note>.

# QUESTION I.
## WHY DO THOSE SUFFERING FROM TARANTISM find a cure in no other way except by harmonic means, or by Music alone?

How great the power of Music is in stirring the passions of the soul, and what the cause of this harmonic energy might be, has been shown extensively in the preceding sections. Now it remains for us to finally demonstrate how the force of the disease in those suffering from *Tarantism* <note>A psychological and physical affliction attributed to the bite of the Tarantula spider, characterized by melancholy and an irresistible urge to dance.</note> can be driven out by the power of music, and the sick person restored to complete health. Therefore, without any roundabout words, let us begin the reasoning we have proposed.

<margin>The power of harmonic sound.</margin>
<margin>How the sick person is freed through Music.</margin>
Since strings or instruments, as we taught in the preceding pages, have the greatest power to stir the air in the same manner in which they themselves are moved, and since they excite a harmony pleasing to the ears and the soul through this proportional mixture of sounds, it happens that by this instrumental harmony—arising from the various and proportioned movement of the strings—the air is harmonically stirred. This air, stirred in a similar way according to the harmonic movements impressed upon it, penetrates inward. Once the <term>fantastic faculty</term> <note>The "imagination" or "fancy," which in 17th-century science was the part of the brain that processed sensory images.</note> is occupied by this pleasant motion, the air similarly moves the <term>spirits</term> <note>Refers to "animal spirits," the subtle vapors thought to travel through the nerves to communicate between the soul and the muscles.</note> by thinning them out. The spirits, thinned and rarefied by this motion, favorably affect the muscles, arteries, and the innermost fibers—the receptacles of the spirits. Since the fibers and muscles contain the vehicle of the latent poison (a sharp, biting, and bilious humor <note>The "humors" were the four vital fluids of the body; an excess of "yellow bile" was thought to cause heat and agitation.</note>) hidden in the innermost marrow, it happens that this humor, once roused along with the poison, becomes rarefied and heated. It affects the entire system of muscles with a certain itching or twinge. The patient, being sweetly affected by this pleasant twitching, is compelled to break out into dancing. The commotion of the whole body and the humors follows the dance; heat follows the commotion; the loosening of the whole body and the opening of the pores follows the heat; and finally, the transpiration of the poisonous vapor necessarily follows the opening of the pores. Since, however, the poison is so deeply rooted that it cannot be exhaled in a single dancing session, a certain portion evaporates little by little through movement each year until the whole is consumed.

However, as to why different people are affected by different musical instruments, I believe this should be attributed to the diversity of complexions and temperaments, or of the <term>Tarantulas</term> <note>Kircher believed the spider's own "temperament" influenced the type of music the victim would require; a "melancholy" spider required different music than a "fiery" one.</note> themselves, or of the men. For those who are melancholic, or have been struck by the venom of a duller *Tarantula*, are moved by noisy drums and clamorous instruments rather than by strings and lutes...

He notes that the cure often requires annual "booster" dances because the venom is so deeply embedded.</summary>

# BOOK II OF THE PHONURGIA, SECTION II.

<margin>
Different people are affected by different instruments according to the nature of the venom, the <term>complexion</term> <note>In Galenic medicine, "complexion" refers to the specific balance of humors (blood, phlegm, bile) in an individual.</note>, and the constitution of the person.

<margin>
A girl afflicted by Tarantism who could not be cured except by drums.

...are affected by strings; for since the <term>humor</term> <note>The bodily fluid infected by the venom.</note> is thick and sluggish, and the spirits follow the disposition of the humor, a great force is required for their stirring and dissipation. Hence it is written from TARANTO that there was a girl there afflicted with *Tarantism* <note>original: "Tarantismo"</note> who could be compelled to dance by no other instruments except the din of drums, the blast of muskets, the blare of trumpets, and similar instruments that produce a vehement sound; for a sluggish venom, in a body of a sluggish and cold temperament, required nothing less than a great force for its own dissipation. But those of a <term>choleric</term> <note>Hot and dry temperament.</note> or <term>sanguine</term> <note>Hot and moist temperament.</note> nature are easily cured by the harmony of the cithara, the lute, the viol, and the harpsichords arranged together, on account of the mobility and thinness of their spirits.

<margin>
A wondrous and altogether paradoxical thing observed in the Tarantula.
</margin>

Furthermore, this is especially most worthy of admiration: that this venom performs the same thing in a human, through a certain similarity of nature, as it does in the Tarantula <note>The spider itself.</note> in its own body; for just as the venom, stirred by music, excites a human to dance by a continuous twitching of the muscles, so it does to the Tarantulas themselves; which I never would have believed, had I not found it confirmed by the testimony of the most trustworthy Fathers <note>Likely referring to Kircher's fellow Jesuit brothers who provided reports from the field.</note> cited above.

For they write of an experiment of this matter in the city of ANDRIA, in the *Ducal Palace* <note>original: "Palatio Ducali"</note>, in the presence of one of our Fathers, and moreover the whole...

<header>MEDICAL SOUND-WORK.</header>

<margin>
A marvelous experiment.
</margin>
...took place at the Court. For the Duchess of that place, so that this wondrous marvel of nature might be more clearly revealed, ordered a Tarantula to be specially sought out. She had it balanced upon a thin straw and placed in a vessel filled with water, and then commanded a <term>lyre-player</term> <note>original: "cytharœdum"</note> to be called. At first, the creature gave no sign of movement at the sound of the lyre; however, as soon as the musician began to play a prelude with a sound proportioned to the creature’s own <term>humor</term> <note>The internal bodily fluids believed to govern health and temperament.</note>, the little beast did not merely attempt a dance with frequent hopping of its feet and agitation of its whole body, but truly seemed to express the dance by leaping to the harmonic rhythms. When the lyre-player stopped, the little beast also ceased its hopping.

<margin>
The Tarantula also dances to a sound proportioned to itself.
</margin>
Indeed, this phenomenon, which those present in **ANDRIA** marveled at as something exotic, they later discovered was a common occurrence in **TARANTO**. There, the musicians—who are accustomed to treating this affliction as a remedy and comfort for the poor, even being paid by public salaries from the Magistrates—usually begin by asking the infected person where, in what field, and of what color the Tarantula was that inflicted the bite, in order to hasten the cure of the patients more surely and easily. Once this is done, these "musical doctors" go immediately to the indicated place, where numerous Tarantulas of every kind are busy with the labor of weaving their webs. They then try various types of harmonies, to which—wondrous to relate—now these spiders, now those, begin to dance. This is exactly like what happens when two <term>polychords</term> <note>Instruments with many strings, such as lutes or harps.</note> are tuned equally; those strings which are similar to each other in tone and equally tensioned will move when the other is played, while the rest remain still. Thus, according to the similarity and condition of the Tarantulas, they find that now these, now those, will dance. When they see a Tarantula of the same color as the one described by the patient break into a dance, they take it as a most certain sign that they possess the true and certain melody proportioned to the <note>original: "τῷ γαργαρίζοντι" — likely meaning 'the vibrating' or 'titillating' venom</note> poisonous humor, and that it is most fit for curing. If they use this melody, they assert that they achieve an infallible effect in the cure.

## COROLLARY I.

<margin>
A viscous animal, subject to proportioned sound...
</margin>
*From this it is clear* that the same effect of dancing is produced in the Tarantula itself as it usually produces in a person who has been bitten. For since the humor of this little animal is very thin and viscous, and—owing to its subtlety—is easily affected by the sound-carrying air, as if for the purpose of receiving sound...

...the most suitable subject, it can be moved: it happens that, excited by the motion of the harmonic air, it causes a <term>vibration</term> <note>original: "vibrissationem"</note> exactly like that produced by the musician. From this, the animal itself, as if affected by an itch, is stirred to jump; especially if the sound is proportioned to the <term>humor</term> <note>Internal bodily fluids believed to govern health and physical reactions.</note> that is to be moved. That the viscous humor of spiders is a capable subject for sound is testified by **Peter Martyr** in his *History of the West Indies*, where he says a certain type of spider is found in **India** whose extracted venom is so tenacious that it is used by the natives not only for threads, but even in place of musical strings, just as silk from the silkworm is used among us.

Therefore, I believe that the fact that the animal is moved to dance should be attributed to the disposition and easily movable quality of the humor that has been harmonically excited by the air. For if we could perceive with our senses this disposition latent in the viscous humor, we would certainly hear a harmony not unlike that which musicians produce on strings. This also seems to be testified by certain experiments, as was said in the preceding pages. For we see that some people are affected by a certain sound, or screech, with great discomfort of the teeth, while this unpleasant screeching is accustomed to twitch the muscular parts of the gums. This also happens if you grip the neck of some stringed instrument with your teeth; for the <term>spirits</term> <note>In 17th-century medicine, "spirits" were volatile substances in the blood that carried sensation and motion through the nerves.</note> of the whole body, through the continuation of such parts, are dissolved and rippled into agitations exactly similar to those which a trembling string makes in the air. These things are also beautifully shown by experiments demonstrated in our *Magnetism of Music* <note>Referring to Kircher’s earlier work, *Magnes sive de Arte Magnetica*.</note>.

In this same way, the Tarantula, by its bite, infuses into humans a certain refined humor, which is like a vehicle for the latent and penetrating poison. When this is excited by the periodic heat of the summer sun, it spreads itself through the whole body—especially into the arteries, muscles, and innermost fibers. In this way, the body is gradually disposed to receive harmonic motions; and once disposed, by twitching the muscles according to the mathematical ratio of the harmony, it forces the sick person, <term>willy-nilly</term> <note>original: "velit nolit"</note>, to break out into leaps. Thus, the intended effect of healing is achieved.

# PHONURGIA IATRICA
->209<-

For a person struck by this or that Tarantula, this or that melody is appropriate. For some spiders, as has been said, are not moved except by a vehement noise, as happens in those Tarantulas endowed with a thicker <term>humor</term> <note>The bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which early modern science believed determined health and temperament.</note>; the same is true in humans. Others are moved easily and by any sound proportioned to them, as seen in those endowed with a thin and bilious humor. For by such motion, the <term>poisonous spirits</term> <note>original: "spiritus venenosi"; "Spirits" were thought to be volatile substances that carried signals and energy through the nerves.</note> are easily thinned out; since these spirits are sharp and biting, they infest the muscular system with their twitching and vehemently entice the victim to leap. Therefore, if a melancholy-inducing spider <note>original: "melangoga"</note> strikes a melancholic man, it will render him sluggish and sleepy; if a choler-inducing spider <note>original: "cholerogoga"</note> strikes a choleric man, it will make him active, unstable, frantic, and breathing "Martial furies" <note>War-like or aggressive rages, associated with the planet Mars and the humor of yellow bile.</note>, and so on for the others, especially if the music corresponds to the "Tarantistic" nature of the bite.

However, one might rightly doubt what sound is "proportioned" to the venom. I say, therefore, that the quality of the venom—excited by the heat of summer—causes a certain boiling or commotion of the humor, not unlike all periodic diseases. This humor, thinned by the power of the venom, is blown out into certain very thin threads, as if between the <gloss>intervals or spaces</gloss> <note>original Greek: "διαστήματα"</note> of the muscles. If, by the condition of the humors, these threads are such that they are easily stirred in harmony by an external sound, then—the subject itself being proportioned to the sound and vibrated by its motion—they will provoke the muscles to which they adhere into leaps, through both the twitching and the biting nature of the material.

<margin>Which music is most suitable for those afflicted by the Tarantula.</margin>
Hence, the more a sound possesses "diminutions" <note>A musical technique of dividing long notes into many fast, short notes.</note> of voices and notes, and the greater the mixture of high and low voices in a scale <note>original: "Tono"</note> filled with frequent semitones <note>Half-steps in music, which Kircher associates with intense emotional or physical response.</note>, the more pleasing that music will be to those affected by this disease. For by the speed of the motion, it twitches the fibers more vehemently and, consequently, more strongly provokes them to dance.

For this reason, lutenists <note>original: "Cytharœdi"</note> are accustomed to adorn their melodies with various diminutions of the notes as much as possible, and mostly in the **Phrygian mode** <note>An ancient musical scale often associated in the Renaissance with excitement or agitation.</note>, because of the frequent semitones of which it consists. But lest anything be lacking for the wishes of the curious *Reader*, we will place here the song in which those bitten by the Tarantula <note>original: "Tarantati"</note> take the greatest delight, which is commonly called the **Tarantella**.

# CHAPTER V.

## *ON THE VARIED PROPERTIES OF DIFFERENT TARANTULAS.*

<margin>A remarkable story confirms that Tarantulas are imbued with opposing powers.</margin>
Furthermore, this might rightly seem to someone to be almost prodigious and completely paradoxical: namely, that one Tarantula, due to a certain dissimilarity of natures, is entirely contrary to another. For they do not respond to <note>original: "patiuntur"; literally "suffer" or "allow," here meaning they are not affected by</note> the same instrument, nor the same leaps, nor the same gestures and symptoms, but entirely different ones. When this was reported to a certain Spaniard then staying in Taranto, it is said that he first received the news with laughter; despite the testimony of many trustworthy people, he refused to believe it until he had tested the danger on himself. 

Therefore, having sought out two Tarantulas of different color and quality, he placed them on his hand and voluntarily provoked them, allowing punctures to be inflicted on different parts of his hand. Once the bites were received and the poison gradually spread throughout his whole body, most severe <term>paroxysms</term> <note>Sudden, violent attacks or fits of a disease.</note> and the agonies of death were soon felt. Immediately, <term>lyre-players</term> <note>original: "cytharædi"</note>, flute-players <note>original: "auletæ"</note>, and musicians of every kind were summoned; various types of harmonies were attempted. 

At last, the sick man felt himself being strongly urged to dance by one particular melody; but it was in vain. For as much as the venom of one Tarantula urged him to the dance, the other venom—being contrary in its entire substance—resisted and pulled him back from dancing. They tried other types of melodies again with different instruments; but though he felt himself again powerfully incited to dance by one of them, it was in vain. For this venom, which incited the dance, was so inhibited by the previous incompatible and entirely contrary venom that what the power of one allowed, the other irremediably denied. 

Thus caught in this struggle of contrary qualities, while the sick man found no remedy for his resisting nature and the ferocity of the hostile <term>humors</term> <note>The bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which 17th-century science believed governed health and personality.</note>, and since no <term>exhalation</term> <note>Early modern medicine believed that dancing "exhaled" or sweated out the poison; if the victim couldn't dance, the poison remained trapped.</note> of the poisonous quality was permitted, his life at last [ended] miserably not

# MEDICAL SOUND-WORK

...he passed away amidst the sorrow and pity of those present; teaching by his own example how reckless and dangerous it is to expose oneself to fatal experiments of this kind without caution and foresight.

<margin>Music must cause the poison to be transpired <note>transpirare: in 17th-century medicine, this refers to the "breathing out" or sweating of toxins through the skin.</note>.</margin>
Unless, therefore, the music causes the poison to be transpired, it is certain that the victim of the Tarantula cannot live, or at least must lead a miserable life full of the most heavy symptoms. Two other examples confirm this: A certain man from the Holy Order of *Capuchins* in TARANTO was bitten by a Tarantula; his natural appetite was driven toward clear waters. While he was carried away by this urge—due to a certain magnetic affinity and relationship between the victim and the spider—his superiors did not permit him to use the bath, much less the remedy common to all, namely the dance, to break the force of the disease. Finally, he was so incited by the power of the biting quality that one day, having slipped out of the monastery like one who had lost his mind, he entered the sea toward which he was being driven with great force. He hoped that in this cooling water he might find some salutary remedy for the atrocity of the stinging disease; but it happened otherwise. For where he sought health, he found death; to the great sorrow of everyone, he was submerged and perished in the sea.

ROBERT SANTORUS, a *Nobleman of Taranto*, was bitten by a Tarantula, though he did not know it. He was gradually brought to his end; since the doctors were unable to discern any signs of the disease, it finally occurred to one of those standing by that the truth was this: the Nobleman was suffering from *Tarantism*. This conjecture was accepted by the medical council. Soon a skilled musician <note>original: "Sonator," literally "one who sounds" or a player</note> was summoned and brought before him as he lay in his death throes. In place of mourning, the customary types of melodies were attempted. At one of these, which was proportioned to his specific ailment, the man—who had been lying motionless, deprived of the help of all his senses and confined to his bed—was finally roused by the pleasant repercussions of the harmonic air <note>vibrations of the music</note>. As if awakened from a heavy and lethal stupor, he finally began to move his languid limbs, then to toss his arms, and soon, as the sound continued and his strength was resumed, to sit up in bed and twist his neck, showing manifest signs of the internal delight perceived from the music. Then, as the lute-player played more briskly, he even rose to his feet. What more? Finally, he surrendered himself to dancing with such vehemence that it seemed he could hardly be restrained. He who previously seemed buried by the power of a narcotic quality and brought to the gates of death, was now, by the admirable resonance...

## QUESTION II.

### WHY THOSE AFFECTED BY TARANTISM ARE SO GREATLY DELIGHTED BY
->*certain specific colors.*<-

It has been said that various Tarantulas excite those affected by Tarantism toward various colors; the reason why this happens must now be explained. I therefore assume, as proven by experience, that those bitten are inclined toward the color displayed by the Tarantula itself, such that those wounded by red Tarantulas are inclined toward red and flame-like colors; those by green, toward green; and so on for the others. Likewise, those Tarantulas which frequent waters, rivers, or the rims of cisterns similarly incline the bitten toward water; while those from hot and dry places usually stir those affected by the Tarantula <note>Tarantiacos: those suffering from the bite</note> toward bilious actions <note>bilious: relating to "yellow bile," one of the four humors, associated with heat, anger, and a dry temperament</note>.

If the person's <term>complexion: the specific balance of bodily humors (blood, phlegm, and biles) that determined an individual's health and temperament</term> corresponds to that of the Tarantula, the effect will be that much more powerful. There are, therefore, certain venomous animals that transfer the exact same condition they themselves suffer into others through their bite. Thus the *Dipsas* serpent <note>a legendary snake whose bite was said to cause intense thirst</note>, since it is always tortured by a burning thirst, creates such a state in a man by biting him, according to the verse:

> Just as the thirst twisting this creature is extinguished by no waters,
> So does it incite a rabid thirst with its bite. <note>original Latin: "Ut sitis hanc torquens nullis extinguitur undis, / Sic rabidam morsu concitat illa sitim."</note>

In the same way, the bite of a rabid dog produces that same passion in a man which it produces in the dog, namely *hydrophobia* <note>original Greek: ὑδροφοβίαν</note>, or the fear of water. The *Cerastes* <note>the horned viper</note> also—as I have learned from the Egyptians—strikes *pyrophobia* <note>original Greek: πυροφοβίαν</note>, or a fear of fire and light, into both the serpent and the man infected by its venom. Thus the numbing *Torpedo* <note>the electric ray, known for its ability to stun</note> makes the limbs numb; and just as a parent suffering from gout, leprosy, or epilepsy communicates those same diseases through their seed to their child; in no different way, the venoms of various Tarantulas, by an innate property, seem to incline the mind of the bitten person toward the color that they themselves reflect, or by which they are refreshed, through a hidden Magnetism or a certain blind affinity of nature; Tarantu-

<summary>Kircher explains the sympathetic link between a tarantula's appearance and its victim's symptoms, arguing that venom transfers the creature's own nature—such as its color preference or fear of water—to the human through 'hidden magnetism.'</summary>

### HEALING SOUND-WORK

Now, it is evident that they <note>the tarantulas</note> are refreshed by a certain color from the fact that, when placed upon various colored surfaces, they seek out that plane which is similar in color to themselves. From this it follows that, just as the spider's own venomous humor inclines its <term>imagination: the internal sense that processes sensory data</term> toward this or that color as being agreeable or disagreeable to its nature; so too that same humor, when transferred into a human body through a bite, will produce the same effect through a certain magnetic relationship and an occult and wonderful correspondence. For as soon as a sympathetic color—*for example, a poisonous one*—meets the affected person, they immediately feel themselves pleasantly and comfortably moved by it; conversely, they feel poorly if they gaze upon a color contrary to them. For the "species" <term>species: the "likeness" or visual form of an object that travels through the air to the eye</term> of the color perceived by sight is presented to the imagination as something pleasing, which causes a dilation of the <term>spirits: subtle fluids believed to flow through the nerves to communicate between the soul and body</term>. These dilated spirits, joined to the humor carrying the pleasing image, resonate with that sympathetic form. From this mutual "marriage" of natures results a certain pleasing affection, or a localized itching and tickling diffused through all the fibers of the body, from which finally arises pleasure and a vehement desire to join and unite oneself with the desired object. 

Therefore, just as one suffering from *Tarantism* is so affected by the proportion of musical numbers—that is, the mixture of deep and high-pitched voices—proportioned and similar to the venomous humor, that they apply their ears even to the instruments themselves to "drink in" that harmony and incorporate it into themselves because of the tickling of the vibrating harmonic air; so too is the sight of the stricken person carried toward a sympathetic color with such ardor that they seem to want to drink it in with their eyes and incorporate it into themselves, owing to the pleasing affection and tickling of the spirits produced therein. For the individual powers, directed by the spirits, are carried toward their objects in exactly the same manner as was described regarding the "Magnetism of the Imagination" in *Book 3 of the Art of Magnetism.*

<margin>The cause of <term>chromatophilia: a love of or attraction to colors</term> <note>original: "χρωματοφιλίας"</note></margin>
**A**t this point, someone might not undeservedly ask: in what way does the color white dissipate, black collect, red inflame, and green, crimson, or gold delight? Why do green and blue so greatly delight those affected by *Tarantism*, while red and flashing colors drive them into certain exotic <term>affections</term> <note>original: "παθήματα" (pathemata) — sufferings, passions, or states of being acted upon</note>? This is especially puzzling since these colors can only perform these actions through "species" <note>visual images</note>, which in themselves produce no real physical action; this is because they possess only an imperfect and diminished essence, which some call "real" and others "intentional," as it stands midway between the nature of real things and mental concepts.

<summary>Kircher explains that the "magnetic" attraction tarantula victims feel for specific colors is a physiological resonance where the venom in the blood reacts to visual stimuli, much like it reacts to musical harmony.</summary>

### PHONURGY BOOK II. SECTION II.

*I respond:* The "species" <term>species: the "likeness" or visual form of an object that travels through the air to the eye</term> of colors are sensory qualities endowed with an active power, though one less potent than the qualities themselves from which they are derived. For who would deny that the light scattered by the sun through the air, or the heat radiating from a fire, possesses the power to warm? Since light is the image and "species" of the sun, just as heat is a proxy form of fire, there is nothing to prevent objects from altering the sense organs through these "species," which act as their own natural and inherent properties.

Furthermore, it is a specific property of the tarantula's venom to incline toward one color or another according to its own nature. For the humor <note>the bodily fluid or "venom"</note> in the human body is so modified that it is carried to the <term>imagination: the "phantasia," or internal mental faculty</term> by the guidance of the <term>spirits: the subtle vapors believed to flow through the nerves</term>, which have been shaped by the "species" of the colored object.

Thus, experience shows that the color red wonderfully excites those who have been bitten by tarantulas of a similar nature. Because redness, through its specific action, inflames the eyes due to its likeness to fire and twitches the visual muscles (as is seen in lions and bulls), a great concentration of spirits gathers in the eyes. When these spirits are dilated and ignited by the flaming color, they twitch both the visual muscles and other connected muscles throughout the rest of the limbs. From this twitching, a great itching sensation is felt, leading to the movements which Music, through its harmonic air, greatly encourages. Consequently, when a person feels the greatest pleasure from that most pleasing color combined with Music, they are driven, whether they wish it or not, into leaps and dances.

<margin>Harmony exists within colors.</margin> For there is a certain harmony in colors as well, which refreshes the spirit no less than Music does. This analogy of harmonies possesses the greatest power in stirring the emotions of the soul; for just as the most pleasing golden and purple colors arise from yellow, red, and blue, so too from these two <note>gold and purple</note> comes green, the most delightful of all colors. By the same logic, since green is composed of the most pleasing golden and purple hues and is most perfectly balanced, it is no different than the <term>octave: the "Diapason," or most perfect musical interval</term> being composed of the <term>fifth: the "Diapente"</term> and the <term>fourth: the "Diatessaron"</term>. Just as the octave moves the ears most perfectly of all, so green moves the eyes, such that nature seems to have provided it solely for pleasure and the refreshment of the sight.

<header>PHONURGY OF HEALING</header>

## QUESTION III.
# WHY THOSE AFFECTED BY TARANTISM FEIGN SO MANY
### *different behaviors?*

It has often been said that those affected by Tarantism <term>Tarantism: a dancing mania traditionally blamed on the bite of a tarantula spider</term> assume various behaviors during the time of their paroxysm <term>paroxysm: a sudden attack or peak of a disease's symptoms</term>; some indeed act like soldiers, others as Leaders and Governors, some as Boxers, and some even act as Preachers. The question is asked: from where do these behaviors arise? For it is certain that passions of this kind did not exist beforehand, yet the victims are notably transformed as soon as they perceive the sound of musical instruments. This requires explanation.

<margin>
The imagination, when intensely occupied with a thing, transforms itself almost into the very thing it desires.
</margin>
It must be noted that there are certain poisons which, by a peculiar logic, have power over the imagination <term>phantasia: the internal mental faculty believed to process sensory images</term>. By their power, the humors <note>the four vital bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, black bile, and yellow bile</note> throughout the entire body are stirred and rise toward the brain. Afterward, the "spirits" <term>spirits: subtle vapors believed to flow through the nerves to transmit movement and thought</term> are invaded, followed by the imagination itself. Finally, with the help of these spirits, the bodily humors are excited according to the "species" <term>species: the mental forms or images of things</term> conceived in the imagination, varying according to the specific temperament of the individual. 

Therefore, it is not surprising if the infected, aided by these spirits, believe themselves to be whatever the imagination presents to them. For example, the poison introduced by a Dipsas <note>original: "Dipfade" – a legendary viper whose bite was said to cause unquenchable thirst</note>, or Aconite <note>original: "Aconitum" – a highly toxic plant also known as monkshood</note> taken in large quantities, seems to transform men into fish, geese, ducks, or divers <note>original: *mergos* – referring to cormorants or diving birds</note>. Since such poison, by its very nature, inclines the victim toward water due to the intolerable thirst it excites, the imagination—with the help of the spirits—immediately adopts those forms toward which it is inclined. It busily and laboriously conjures up images of water and finds insatiable joy in the sight and drinking of it. Consequently, the victim will necessarily desire to be that which most enjoys the things they crave—such as aquatic creatures—and finally, because the force of the disease perpetually stimulates the imagination toward the desired thing, the damaged imagination will envision itself as one of them.

Thus, feverish patients occasionally wish they were fish, so that they might quench their thirst with a freer draft of water. Conversely, the poison introduced by the bite of a rabid dog naturally inclines one toward a fear of water and causes them to continually imagine the gaping mouth of a dog.

For the imagination, occupied by the form of the biting dog, is struck by a certain occult force contrary to water, so much so that it cannot even tolerate the sight of water; hence they are called hydrophobes <note>original Greek: *ὑδροφόβοι* – literally "those who fear water"</note>. You may reason in no different a way concerning Tarantulas, since according to the variety of different Tarantulas...

He compares this to rabies (hydrophobia) or the thirst-inducing bite of the Dipsas snake, where the mind adopts a persona that matches its physical cravings or fears.</summary>

[of the spi]-ders <note>original: *araneolarum*. This humor, however, being brought into the brain, incites the **imagination** <term>phantasia: the mental faculty that processes sensory images</term> toward that which it otherwise inclines. Here, struck by the **harmonious air** <note>Kircher's term for music or sound waves</note> and diffused through all the limbs, the venom spreads by sharply stimulating and twitching them.

From this, movements conforming to the excited humor soon follow—I mean acts of indignation, anger, fury, mental instability, and similar rational passions are excited. These are further and further promoted by the flash of unsheathed swords and the sight of red objects, as was also said in the preceding sections. But the **bile-driving** quality acts more mildly in a **phlegmatic** <note>a person dominated by phlegm, usually characterized by a calm or stolid temperament</note> person; you should reason no differently concerning **blood-driving** <term>hæmagogis</term>, **phlegm-driving** <term>phlegmagogis</term>, and **black-bile-driving** <term>melangogis</term> Tarantulas in **sanguine**, **phlegmatic**, and **melancholic** persons. Since these points are clear from what has come before, I will gladly omit them here.

# SECTION III.
## Concerning Various Prodigious Sounds.

### CHAPTER I.
#### ON THE DEFINITION AND DIVISION OF
->*Prodigious sounds.*<-

<image-desc>Large decorative initial 'S' entwined with leafy vines and floral motifs.</image-desc>A **prodigious sound** <note>original: *Sonus prodigiosus*. In Kircher’s context, this refers to sounds that are marvelous, portentous, or seemingly supernatural in their origin or effect.</note> is nothing other than a certain unusual sound established to signify something, which, while it strikes the ears vehemently and the listeners do not know its cause, sweeps them into the greatest admiration. It is of three types: natural, preternatural, and supernatural or miraculous.

To the **Miraculous sound** belongs that great sound which suddenly arose while CHRIST was preaching (*John 6*), filling all the listeners with great wonder; such was the Music of the Angels at the Birth of CHRIST; such it was on the day of PENTECOST according to that passage from the *Acts of the Apostles (suddenly there came a sound from heaven, as of a rushing mighty wind)*; such are the sounds mentioned here and there in Holy Scripture, like the clangor of trumpets on Mount *Sinai* while *Moses* received the Law from GOD.

### CHAPTER II.
#### ON THE FALL OF THE WALLS OF JERICHO
->*which followed the sound of the trumpets.*<-

<margin>
Hebrew: JERICHO.
Others claim it was called Hiericuntum from the moon. <note>The Hebrew word for moon, *yareach*, is etymologically related to the name Jericho.</note>
</margin>WHETHER that prodigious sound of the trumpets and the shout of the people caused the fall of the walls of *Jericho* physically or miraculously is a matter of controversy. From the *Rabbis*, RALBAG <note>Levi ben Gershom (1288–1344), a medieval philosopher and commentator also known as Gersonides.</note> first thinks they collapsed by the vehemence of the sound, in which opinion the Holy Fathers themselves also shared, namely St. AUGUSTINE in *Sermon 206, On Time*: *After this,* he says, *they came to the city of JERICHO, and its walls were cast down to the foundations at the shout of the people and the sound of trumpets;* and St. JEROME in his *Letter to ABIGAUS*: *The walls fell down,* he says.

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "prodigious sounds"—extraordinary or seemingly supernatural auditory phenomena—and begins an investigation into whether the collapse of Jericho's walls was a physical acoustic event or a divine miracle.</summary>
<keywords>prodigious sound, acoustics, Jericho, miracles, St. Augustine, St. Jerome, Ralbag, biblical history, trumpets</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the fall of the walls of Jericho, specifically debating whether the event was a result of natural acoustic forces or a divine miracle. The previous page ended mid-sentence while introducing the question.</meta>

the walls of **Jericho** *were overthrown by the clanging of the priestly trumpets*; **Saint Ambrose** asserts the same in *book 30, chapter 10*, as does **Origen** in *homily 6*. **Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath often called the "father of acoustics."</note> among more recent authors thinks they fell entirely by the intensity of the sound; indeed, he even prescribes a proportion between the moving object and the object moved. Since these ideas are more ingenious than true, we shall not dwell on them,

<margin>The walls of Jericho collapsed through a miracle.</margin>
but we say briefly that they fell miraculously at the sound, as a sign of the Divine will; however, we prove that they could not have been moved naturally (whatever **Mersenne** says) in this way. No sound of men or trumpets, no matter how great, intense, or multiplied, can naturally cast down or move a wall or any great mass on its own; which I show thus. Three things concur for a sound: *First*, the collision of two bodies, either solid or quasi-solid. *Second*, the breaking and crushing of the air, or a similar body, intercepted between those two. *Third*, the diffusion of this broken and crushed body, and its flight or retreat, as it were.

<margin>How sound moves things.</margin>
And this is that by which anything can be moved and impelled; for while it thus flees and retreats, if any bodies are encountered, it strikes them, and if it can in any way, it pushes them forward; and indeed it always moves and pushes the neighboring air encountered first, because otherwise a "penetration of bodies" <note>A reference to the Aristotelian principle that two physical bodies cannot occupy the same space at the same time.</note> would be allowed; and the air is quite ready to receive motion of its own accord, as it were. But it does not always move distant air, because the impetus of that fleeing air is gradually weakened, and so it ceases to proceed and push forward of its own; but in that progress of its own and the impulse of foreign bodies, it must be considered that for it to be able to proceed and push far and strongly, it is necessary first that it be broken, crushed, and extruded with great force; second, that it be not something meager and thin, but have a greater quantity. For if the former is lacking, it is stopped immediately from its flight, nor will it move the nearby air vehemently even at the very beginning; if the latter is lacking, the voice will be stifled by the surrounding air diffused far and wide, nor can it proceed or push anything; to these things is added moreover that there is little force even of the greatest sounds in an open Medium, such as open fields are, and that they are easily dissolved and dissipated into the vast air that surrounds us, so much so that most Philosophers assert that not the true and real sound, but only its "species" <note>original: *speciem, seu simulachrum*. In 17th-century physics, this refers to the sensory image or likeness of a thing transmitted through a medium, rather than the physical substance itself.</note>, or image, reaches ears slightly further away.

If indeed sound had this power, already in the cities being besieged <note>The Latin catchword "gnan-" at the bottom of the page suggests the word *expugnantur* or *expugnandis*, referring to the process of storming or capturing cities.</note>

<summary>Kircher refutes the theory that the walls of Jericho fell due to natural acoustic resonance or force, arguing that the physics of sound dissipation in open air makes such a feat impossible without a divine miracle.</summary>
<keywords>Jericho, acoustics, Marin Mersenne, miracles, sound propagation, air displacement, Saint Ambrose, Origen, physics of sound, sound intensity</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the fall of the walls of Jericho. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought that if sound alone could destroy fortifications, traditional siege engines would be obsolete.</meta>

->CONCERNING VARIOUS PRODIGIOUS SOUNDS.<-

to be conquered, no other battering rams and catapults would be needed than the chaotic sound of men, horses, drums, trumpets, and cannons <note>original: "tormentorumque sonitu"</note>. But regarding what we stated in the preceding part—that sound possesses great power in moving certain things, as when an organ's sound makes a stone, statue, or similar object tremble—I would not want this to be understood as anything other than a harmonic agitation of the air. For even a single smaller pipe can move a massive weight, which ten pipes larger than said pipe might not move. We are not speaking in that passage, therefore, precisely of a violent agitation of the air, but of that by which we say sounds move another body proportioned to themselves, no differently than we said one string moves another. For if twelve strings are stretched on a many-stringed instrument <term>polychordo|A musical instrument with many strings, such as a harp or psaltery.</term>, of which the first and last are of the same pitch <term>isotonae|From the Greek, meaning strings tuned to the same tension or frequency.</term>—that is, equally stretched—I say that one of these will move the other, while none of the other ten strings from those listed could move either one, even if all are struck. You see, therefore, that for one body to move another, it is not the violence of the air that is required, but a proportioned and harmonic motion of the same to effect that movement. As for the testimonies of the Holy Fathers cited a little earlier, I say that none of the Holy Fathers believed that the walls of **Jericho** <note>original: "Jerichuntinos"</note> fell torn away by the natural force of trumpets and shouts alone, but rather as a sign; once this sign was given, God Himself, or through the ministry of Angels, destroyed and laid waste to the city. It remains, then, that the ruin of the walls of **Jericho** caused by the blaring of trumpets was not natural, but entirely miraculous; therefore, leaving these matters, let us proceed to the description of other sounds.

# CHAPTER III.
## ON PORTENTOUS SOUNDS OF THE SECOND
### *Class, that is, those which occur by a natural Agent, but by a force greater than human.*

We speak here of certain portentous sounds which seem to have neither a purely supernatural nor a purely natural power, but are produced for good or evil either by Angels or Spirits <note>original: "Genijs"; refers to tutelary spirits or non-human entities often discussed in early modern natural magic.</note>, whether good or bad.

PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION III.

## A Strange Story of a Certain Sorcerer, Bewitching Children by Sound.

<margin>
Mice attracted by the sound of a pipe.
</margin>

In the year of **CHRIST** 460 <note>original: "Anno CHRISTI 460." While the historical legend of the Pied Piper is usually dated to 1284, Kircher cites a much earlier date here.</note>, more than can be told, a marvelous thing happened in **HAMELIN**, a town of Lower *Saxony* situated on the *Weser* <note>original: "Visurgim"</note> river; the history goes as follows: Since the inhabitants in that year were infested by huge armies of mice and shrews, and the evil grew to such an extent that almost nothing of the fruits or crops could be found that was not subject to the gnawing of the rodents, various plans were being discussed for removing so troublesome and ruinous an evil. Meanwhile, there suddenly appeared a certain man, never seen before and of prodigious stature; who soon promised that he would immediately remove whatever mice were in that district, provided they would bargain with him for a certain sum of money. No sooner said than done; the reward being promised, the said man drew out a pipe <note>original: "fistulâ"</note> from the pouch with which he was girded, and as soon as he sounded it, behold, huge armies of mice, breaking out from all the corners of the houses, the ledges of the roofs, and the holes in the floors, followed that unusual sound of the Piper <term>Aulædi|A Greek-derived term for a flute-player or piper.</term> all the way to the river. The Piper, entering the river with his garments tucked up, was followed by the shrews, and there they were all destroyed by voluntary drowning. The man, having performed the enchantment, demanded the agreed-upon reward, but when the citizens began to equivocate regarding the promised sum, he rebuked them with threatening words, saying that unless they gave the reward, it would happen that he would exact another price much heavier than the one promised. His threats were received with laughter. But on the following day around noon, the said man appeared again in the dress of a hunter, with a terrible face and a purple hat of unusual design, and as soon as he sounded another pipe quite different from the first, behold, all the children of the whole town, from four-year-olds up to twelve-year-olds, went out and followed the prodigious sound. Now, there is outside the town on the bank of the *Weser* a certain cave in the mountain, large enough for the stabling of beasts; into this cave the hunter led all the children along with himself. And from that time, none of the children ever appeared again, nor was it ever afterward discovered what became of the children, or where they went. Because of this so wonderful event, the whole town, widowed of its children, is accustomed to calculate its years even to this day from the *Departure of our Sons*.

I too have been in that same city, I have seen the mountain itself, and the history mi-

depicted in a painting exhibited in the Church, which I contemplated with the greatest admiration; *The question is, therefore, what was that sound of the pipe that possessed such great power?*

*I respond:* It was undoubtedly a Demon, who, by the secret judgment of God, carried away the enchanted children to another part of the world. For the *Chronicles of Transylvania* <note>original: "Chronica Transylvaniæ"</note> testify that, around that same time, children of an unknown language suddenly appeared in *Transylvania*; settling there, they promoted their language to such an extent that, to this day, the *Transylvanians* speak nothing but German-Saxon. # CHAPTER IV.
## ON THE PRODIGIOUS SOUNDS OF BELLS.

Among the other bells that have stunned the world with their prodigious sound, that bell of **VELILLA** <note>original: "VILILLÆ." The Bell of Velilla de Ebro is a famous Spanish legend.</note> surely holds the first place—a continuous miracle of *Spain*, about which **VARIUS** writes thus in *Book 2 of "On Fascination," chapter 14*: *In the Kingdom of Spain, in a town called* **VELILLA** *within the Diocese of Zaragoza* <note>original: "Cæsaraugustanæ Diœcesis"</note>*, there is a certain bell which they call "miraculous." This bell, for several months before any disaster occurs in the Christian Republic, rings of its own accord with no one striking it; the testimony of this fact, documented by public notaries with many witnesses present, I have read with my own eyes in the letters which the Viceroys of that Kingdom wrote.* Our countryman **MARIANA** <note>Juan de Mariana, a famous Spanish historian.</note> describes it in these words: *Thirty-six miles below the City of* **ZARAGOZA** <note>original: "CÆSARAUGUSTAM"</note> *the town of* **VELILLA** *is situated on the bank of the Ebro* <note>original: "Iberi"</note>*, established from the ancient ruins of the Roman colony of Ilergetes. In this age and in the memory of our ancestors, it is famous for nothing more than its Bell, which—with no one moving it—is believed by the people there to signify, by its unusual and extraordinary tolling, most notable events for better or worse. Whether this is foolish or true, I do not dispute; nevertheless, men of no small reputation are cited as eyewitnesses to this miracle. They say that this Bell rang of its own accord on the day before the Kings were captured.*

Again, it is said to have sounded a third time on the third day before the *Calends of November* <note>October 30th</note> and on the following *Nones of January* <note>January 5th</note>, at which time the treaty of **MILAN** <note>original: "MEDIOLANI"</note> was settled and the King of **ARAGON** was restored to liberty; furthermore, before the death...

Various examples of the prodigious sound of bells.

...at the time of **PHILIP II** <note>Philip II of Spain (r. 1556–1598)</note> and several times in these recent periods, witnesses beyond all reproach heard it ringing, according to **NIEREMBERG** <note>original: "Norimbergio." Refers to Juan Eusebio Nieremberg, a Spanish Jesuit polymath.</note>. There is a similar bell among the *Japanese* which rings of its own accord whenever great rebellions and riots threaten the Kingdom. **JOANNES** *Lupus, Bishop of Monopoli*, reports that a small bell in the *Monastery of Zamora* <note>original: "Monaſterio Camorenſi." Likely the Dominican house in Zamora, Spain.</note> *of the Dominican Order* <note>original: "Patrum Prædicatorum," the Order of Preachers.</note> used to ring spontaneously three days before the death of any monk, even if no one was bedridden at the time. The same author reveals that a similar small bell existed in the *Monastery of St. Dominic in Cordoba* <note>original: "S. Dominici Cordubenſi"</note> which, by sounding of its own volition, portended the fate of either a Brother of that monastery or some distinguished man of the same Order. **ANGELO ROCCA** <note>original: "Angelus Rocha," an Augustinian bishop and famous collector of bells.</note> reports similar things about the prodigious sound of another bell in the *Dominican Priory of Salerno* as a herald of those about to die. **GIBELLINUS**, in the *Life of St. Menulphus*, reports that in *Germany*, in the *Abbey of Böddeken* <note>original: "Monaſterio Bodkenſi"</note>, a bell in a similar way and by spontaneous ringing signified the death of some nun.

There are also other musical instruments that sound of their own accord when more serious dangers to a kingdom are imminent; thus, at the tomb of *St. James in Compostela*, military instruments and noise declare future rebellions. Some pre-signify the victories of Catholics against the Infidels, such as the clashing of the bones of **FERNÁN GONZÁLEZ** <note>original: "Ferdinandi Gonzali," a legendary 10th-century Count of Castile whose bones were said to rattle in their tomb before battle.</note>. Regarding this, see **NIEREMBERG**. I could bring forward countless examples of prodigious sounds in this place, but he who desires more of this kind should read the *Theater of Human Life on Prodigies* <note>original: "Theatrum vitæ humanæ de Prodigiis," a vast encyclopedia by Theodor Zwinger.</note>; **HEGESIPPUS** and **JOSEPHUS** *on the signs preceding the destruction of Jerusalem*; **CORNELIUS GEMMA** in his *Cosmocritica*, and others. It remains to be investigated how that sound is produced; certainly, it is not natural, therefore it must happen beyond nature.

I say, therefore, that these prodigious sounds are caused by the help of Guardian Angels <note>original: "Tutelarium Angelorum"</note> through the merits of some Saint, who obtained this from **GOD** as a singular privilege—both for certain ends and reasons known to **GOD** alone, and to foresee the imminent evils of a kingdom. However, sounds of this kind occasionally happen also by the illusion of Demons <note>original: "Dæmonum"</note>, as used to happen in the prodigious sound of Oracles, about which read our *Egyptian Oedipus* <note>original: "Oedipum noſtrum hieroglyphicum," Kircher's own massive work on Egyptology and ancient mysteries.</note> in the *Treatise on the Oracles of the Ancients*; or even as happens occasionally in haunted places, about which consult **DELRIO** <note>Martin Delrio, author of a famous manual on magic and witchcraft.</note> and **THYRAEUS** <note>Petrus Thyraeus, a Jesuit writer on ghosts and demonic possession.</note>. We [leave] these kinds of sound-prodigies, as they are placed far beyond the goal of our Institute...

# CHAPTER V.

## *ON THE HIDDEN CAUSES OF CERTAIN SOUNDS, such as the formidable noise of the Smellen cave in Finland.*

Let us now look at that terrible cave in *Finland*, which <term>OLAUS Magnus</term> <note>A Swedish ecclesiastic and author of the famous 1555 work *History of the Northern Peoples* (*Historia de Gentibus Septentrionalibus*).</note> describes so beautifully in *Book 11, Chapter 3 of his Northern History*. This is near the coastal city of <term>VYBORG</term> <note>original: "Viburgium." Historically a key fortress city in Karelia, then part of the Swedish realm, now in Russia.</note> close to those same <term>Muscovite</term> lands. It possesses such a secret power that if a living animal is thrown into it, a formidable sound is excited within it; its vehemence so overwhelms the ears of those standing near that they can neither hear, nor speak, nor stand. It strikes the ears with this power much more severely than the most violent <term>bombard</term> <note>An early type of large-caliber cannon or mortar used in sieges.</note>, or weakens them in a moment so that they are hardly masters of themselves. 

But this work of nature does not seem to be idle. For when hostility threatens, the Governor of the land orders the ears of all to be closed with wax, and for those who are to survive to be hidden in cellars and caves. Then, fortifying himself, he precipitates some animal, suspended by either a rope or a spear, into the mouth of the cave. From this, such a horrific sound is immediately excited that the besieging enemies in the vicinity fall down like cattle to be slaughtered; having fallen, they remain for a long time to be despoiled. Returning to themselves, they no longer arrange to fight, but plan for their lives by flight. And so it happens that those who cannot be restrained from warlike fury by arms or strength are conquered, wasting away from the horror of "bellowing nature" alone.

<margin>The admirable force of the sound of the cave in Finland.</margin>
He asserts, moreover, that this cave is surrounded by several walls to ward off the dangers of those who approach it rashly. Pitiful examples of this calamity, according to the same witness and those who experienced it, were left to posterity long ago by the <term>Russians</term> <note>original: "Rutheni." A Latin term used historically for East Slavic peoples.</note>, who lost many thousands of their men there. <term>PLINY</term> <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist.</note> tells of a similar cave in <term>Dalmatia</term> with a vast, precipitous opening, into which, if any light weight is thrown on a calm day, a storm similar to a whirlwind bursts out. <term>PETER Martyr</term> <note>Peter Martyr d'Anghiera (1457–1526), a historian of Spanish exploration.</note> tells of a similar cave on the island of <term>Hispaniola in America</term> <note>The island comprising modern Haiti and the Dominican Republic.</note>, of such a horrendous sound and raging perpetually with such an atrocious tempest, that no one dares to approach within 5 <term>Miles</term> <note>original: "Milliaria."</note> with impunity—that is, without danger to life or of deafness.

<meta>catchword: Cause</meta>

<summary>Kircher describes the legendary "Smellen" cave near Vyborg, which produces a sound so powerful it was used as a sonic weapon against invading Russian forces. He compares this phenomenon to other "storm-caves" mentioned by Pliny and Peter Martyr.</summary>
<keywords>Smellen cave, Vyborg, Olaus Magnus, sonic warfare, acoustics, Finland, Pliny the Elder, Peter Martyr, Hispaniola, deafness, natural wonders</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into "miraculous" or "prodigious" sounds found in nature, specifically those caused by the geological structure of mountains and caves. It transitions from the Smellen cave in Finland to similar phenomena in Switzerland, Germany, and the Mediterranean, before beginning a new chapter on the acoustic properties of the Bothnian coastline.</meta>

PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION III.

The cause of this so prodigious sound—if the things which <term>OLAUS</term> <note>Olaus Magnus (1490–1557), a Swedish historian and geographer.</note> reports are true—is nothing other than the natural internal constitution of the mountain. This structure multiplies the sound through an infinite reflection of the voice and stirs the air; and since the agitated air can find no other exit except through the hole and orifice of the cavity, it is no wonder that the sound, constricted and compressed in that same place, should excite so formidable a noise along with the violence of a storm. I hear that something similar is found in the *Swiss* mountains, especially in a mountain which they call the *Cucumber* <note>original: "Cucumerem." This likely refers to a peak with a hollow or funnel-like summit, possibly related to local legends of "weather-holes" in the Alps.</note> because of its shape. In its summit, a deep abyss is seen, through whose orifice even a single stone thrown into the interior of the mountain excites such violent sounds that it not only strikes fear into those standing by, but even forces them to flee by the violence of the wind coming out.

For there is a wonderful power in enclosed mountains for multiplying sound, which experience also teaches us in very deep wells. There is also in <term>FULDA</term> <note>Kircher's birthplace in Hesse, Germany.</note> in my Homeland, on the *Mount of the Blessed Virgin* <note>The Frauenberg, a hill in Fulda featuring a Franciscan monastery.</note>, a well having a depth of nearly 300 palms, into which a stone thrown excites such a sound that it can seem like the explosion of a piece of artillery. I remember observing the same thing in a certain mountain of the *Lipari Islands* <note>A volcanic archipelago in the Tyrrhenian Sea north of Sicily.</note>; as a result, I do not think that cave of OLAUS is at all mythical, since wonders of such sounds frequently occur to those traveling the world. Truly, since we have reported these miracles of mountains in my work *The Subterranean World* <note>original: "Subterraneo Mundo." Refers to Kircher’s massive 1665 treatise on geology and the Earth's interior.</note>, we refer the curious Reader to that place.

---

# CHAPTER VI.

->ON THE PRODIGIOUS SOUND OF THE SHORES of certain parts of the *Bothnian Sea* <note>The northernmost arm of the Baltic Sea.</note>.<-

<margin>The admirable power on the shores of the Bothnian Sea.</margin>
While <term>OLAUS MAGNUS</term> treats the very high mountains of the *Bothnian Sea*, he also mentions the prodigious sound of the coastal caves. He says that no one can approach the roots of these caves on account of the vehemence of the sound; for they perceive such horror from the high collision of the waves that, unless they escape by rapid rowing or a strong wind, they become almost lifeless from fear alone. Moreover, the bases of said mountains have certain tortuous fissures at the entrance and exit of the waves, and internal receptacles crafted by the stupendous workmanship of nature—

<summary>Kircher explains that "miraculous" mountain echoes and cave noises are caused by natural internal geometry and compressed air, citing examples from Switzerland, Germany, and the Lipari Islands. He then begins a discussion on the terrifying acoustic power of coastal caverns in the Bothnian Sea.</summary>

# OF VARIOUS PRODIGIOUS SOUNDS. 225

<margin>Horrendous sounds of the shores in the Bothnian Sea.</margin>
...are formed, in which through a long chasm that formidable sound generates a sort of subterranean noise. Many who once imprudently attempted to investigate its cause were immediately lost when their ships filled with water; and so they died in wonder. Indeed, that deadly sound, reaching the ears of sailors from many miles away, warns them to flee far from the snares of this most sorrowful "chattering" <note>The author uses "garrulitatis" (chattering/talkativeness) metaphorically to describe the sound of the water.</note>, which those positioned close by cannot endure. Of a similar mountain, **VINCENT OF BEAUVAIS** <note>Vincentius Bellovacensis (c. 1190–1264), a Dominican friar and author of one of the Middle Ages' greatest encyclopedias.</note> tells in these words: *Among the Tartars* <note>A historical term used by Europeans to describe the various nomadic peoples of Central Asia and the Mongol Empire.</note> *there is a certain small mountain, in which a hole is said to be; from this hole in winter such great storms of wind emerge that people can hardly pass by, and only at manifest peril.* The cause of this is nothing else but hidden subterranean waterfalls <term>catadupae</term> <note>original: "catadupae," a term derived from Greek for waterfalls, particularly the cataracts of the Nile, here used for underground cascades.</note>; as we have demonstrated at length in our *Subterranean World* <note>Kircher's own 1665 masterpiece, *Mundus Subterraneus*.</note>, and as is evident from the cave in Hispaniola mentioned above.

**PAUSANIAS** <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD.</note> reports that the shore of the Aegean Sea mimics the sound of a kithara <note>An ancient Greek musical instrument of the lyre family.</note>. This is surely for no other reason than the varied makeup of the cavernous shores; receiving the force of the rushing waves and multiplying the sounds, they present a certain harmony to those listening from a distance. For I think no one can doubt that the large or small size of caves performs the same function in producing sound as hollow resonant bodies, such as jars of differing sizes. Hence another difficulty is solved concerning the prodigious sound which **LAET** <note>Joannes de Laet (1581–1649), a Dutch geographer whose "History of the New World" was a standard reference.</note> reports in his *History of the New World* is heard near the shore of the Guatemalan sea in *New Spain* <note>The colonial Spanish territory including Mexico and parts of Central America.</note> when the East wind blows. He says the sound so closely mimics the sound of our own organs that the natives call it the *Dance of the Gods* <note>original: "Choream Deorum."</note>. The cause of this is none other than the channels of the mountain having different sizes; air is driven into these through caverns by the violence of the sea and compressed. When it strikes the openings of these channels, it produces the same effect as in trumpets, pipes, and other bodies exposed to the wind when it is agitated and struck against them in various ways.

<margin>Marvelous story of 3 mountains in Persia.</margin>
To these we shall add the wonderful story told of certain British and Persian mountains by **CLEMENT OF ALEXANDRIA** <note>A Christian theologian (c. 150–215 AD) who often blended Greek philosophy with Christian thought.</note> in the 6th book of his *Stromata* <note>The "Stromata" or "Miscellanies" is one of Clement's major works.</note> in these words: *Those who have written histories also say that on the island of Britain there is a certain cave situated beneath a mountain, with an opening at the top. When the wind falls into the cave and is struck against the hollow of the pit, the sound of cymbals is heard, as if they were being struck rhythmically,*

<summary>This page explores how natural geological formations—caves, chasms, and coastal rocks—act as musical instruments or amplifiers, creating sounds like organs, cymbals, or kitharas when wind or water passes through them.</summary>

# PHONURGIA BOOK II. SECTION III. 226

...are perceived. Often when leaves move in the forests due to a sudden and dense rush of wind, a sound similar to the song of birds is produced. Furthermore, those who have traveled through *Persia*, in the higher places within the region of the *Magi* <note>The Magi were a priestly caste in ancient Persia, associated with Zoroastrianism and later, in Western tradition, with "magic" and the Three Wise Men.</note>, report that three mountains are positioned one after another in a long plain; the first mountain emits a sound like the shouting of many thousands of men, just as if they were in the air. When they reach the middle mountain, they perceive a louder and clearer noise at the same time. Finally, they hear people singing a **Paean** <term>Pæana</term> <note>original: "Pæana," a Greek song of triumph, thanksgiving, or victory, often dedicated to Apollo.</note> just as if they had won a victory. The cause of any such sound is, I believe, both the smoothness and the hollowness of the locations; thus, the wind that has entered is thrown back, and proceeding back into the same place, it sounds more vehemently. If the things **Clement** <note>Likely referring to Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–215 AD), an Early Christian theologian who often cited Greek geographical lore.</note> relates about the *British* mountains are true, certainly the cause of that prodigious sound can be nothing else but the channels of different sizes in a cavernous and perforated mountain, from which such a disposition is born. For a cave situated beneath the mountain sends the captured wind into the channels where, more strictly narrowed and striking against the openings, it generates diverse sounds, just as we have said above regarding the *Guatemalan* mountains.

Indeed, the triple difference in the murmur which **Clement** reports the three *Persian* mountains produce must be attributed in the same way to the arrangement of the mountains. For the noise of an army or a *Caravan* passing by is reflected in that same place, and multiplied by various reflections within the hollows of the mountains; since it reflects the muffled murmur of the passing men, it is not surprising that the noise of a tumultuous army is perceived. Since the reflection of the second hollow mountain doubles the noise from the first mountain—charged with both the direct and reflected sounds of the passing *Caravan*—it is no wonder that the noises are heard more loudly. As for the **third mountain**, being further away due to the natural disposition of the soil and the alpine cavities, anyone who has more deeply contemplated the preceding wonders of sound will not be surprised that it returns something similar to the choruses of those singing a *Paean*.

### ON VARIOUS PRODIGIOUS SOUNDS

<margin>Miracles of nature in an acoustic or auditory mirror.</margin>
...discovered. In these mountains, there is a huge rock, hollowed out by the industry of nature such that it appears as a mirror to those looking from a distance. He says that another rock stands opposite to this one, on whose summit nothing can be said so softly, even by those far away, that it is not heard. Indeed, for those shouting in said place, the sound is so intensified that it seems like the voice of some army. The local priests of that place know the hidden power of nature’s resonance; to show themselves as divine, they warn men positioned on the mountain peak about future things with hidden voices of this kind. These men, thinking themselves inspired by the voice of a Deity, not rarely fall into the greatest calamities while they hasten too thoughtlessly to carry out the commands. If these things are true, I would believe it happens by no other means than through a <term>reflecting</term> <note>original: "ἀνακαμπτικόν" (anakamptikon). A Greek term for something that reflects or "bends back" waves, used here by Kircher to describe the physics of an echo.</note> object endowed by nature with a spherical or parabolic shape, by which the sonic images flow together into a single point on the rock positioned opposite. From this, many accounts related by historians—which were commonly regarded by most as fables and superstitions—are explained.

<margin>Herberstein in the History of Russia.</margin>
HERBERSTEIN <note>Sigismund von Herberstein (1486–1566) was a diplomat and historian known for his detailed descriptions of Russia and its geography.</note> relates, *in the Province of Candora <note>Kondia, a region in the far north of Russia.</note>, the furthest land of the North, that there is a river which no one has yet crossed because of the frequent ghosts appearing there and the voices of men and animals often heard on the opposite bank. He also says that hardly a day or week passes, especially in the summer time, in which such prodigious voices are not heard.* I certainly judge that these are no other portents than the real voices of men and animals arising in the nearer fields of the river and reflected from the hollow mirrors of the rocks across the water. These strike simple and inexperienced men positioned on the banks with this vain dread and a certain Panic terror, since nothing can more easily deceive men than this playful talkativeness of nature. CARDANO <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was a famous Italian polymath who wrote extensively on "subtlety" or the hidden workings of the natural world.</note> in *Book 18 of On Subtlety* relates a similar deception concerning one of his acquaintances.

<margin>An extraordinary case.</margin>
*A certain friend of ours,* he says, *when he was traveling near a river and did not know the ford, began to shout, "Oh!" to which a hidden Echo replied, "Oh!" He, thinking it was a person, asked in Italian: "Where should I cross?" <note>original: "Onde devò passar?"</note> "Cross," <note>original: "passa"</note> was the reply. Then he asked, "Here?" <note>original: "qui?"</note> "Here," was repeated. But there the waters were rushing with a deep whirlpool, wherefore he, terrified, asked again, "Should I cross here?" Echo replied, "Cross here." To him asking the same thing often, it replied the same. Therefore, since the friend was caught between fear and the necessity of swimming, and a dark and unseasonable night pressed upon him, he persuaded himself that some demon wanted to [lure] him into the torrent...*

<sig>Ff 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: him</meta>

<summary>Athanasius Kircher explains supposedly supernatural voices and "ghostly" sounds in Russia and elsewhere as natural acoustic reflections caused by the parabolic or spherical shape of mountain rocks. He uses a story from Cardano to illustrate how a simple echo can be mistaken for a deceptive demon or a person leading a traveler into danger.</summary>
