PHONURGY BOOK II. SECTION III.

...he thought the voice would cast him down headlong; therefore, having returned from there, he told the whole story to **Cardano** <note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576) was an Italian polymath. Kircher admires him for providing rational, physical explanations for phenomena that others attributed to demons.</note>, *who demonstrated by the very fact that it was not the snares of a demon, nor the illusion of a phantom, but a playful trick of sporting nature.* You may also experience a wonder of nature of this kind in **Syracuse** in the ruins of the **Palace of Dionysius** <note>Kircher refers to the "Ear of Dionysius," a limestone cave in Sicily famous for its acoustics. Legend says the tyrant Dionysius used it to overhear the whispered secrets of his prisoners.</note>, where in a certain chamber nature reflects sounds so aptly that I cannot recall having heard anything more marvelous of a similar type. Many things could be said in this place regarding the prodigious reflection of the voice and sounds, but since all these things have been said and explained elsewhere, putting an end to this *Teratology* <note>original: "Teratologia." In this context, it refers to the study of marvels or "prodigies" rather than the modern medical definition.</note> here, we proceed to some experiments.

---

# EXPERIMENT I.
->*On the Cure of Melancholy.*<-

You might easily rouse a man oppressed by deep sleep or some more violent disturbance of the mind if you sound a louder noise—either from a <term>phonic trumpet</term> <note>Kircher’s "tuba phonica" was an early form of megaphone or speaking trumpet designed to amplify and project sound over long distances.</note> or another instrument—for the "foster child of black bile" <note>A reference to the theory of the four humors; "black bile" was thought to cause melancholy. Kircher suggests that loud, rhythmic sound can physically disrupt this stagnant state.</note>. Nor do I doubt that with instruments of this kind you might easily stir into dancing those suffering from *Tarantism* <note>A supposed dancing mania in Southern Italy blamed on spider bites; the "cure" involved musicians playing specific melodies to make the victim dance until they sweated out the poison.</note>; for the more violently they are incited to dance, the sooner they will be drenched in a great abundance of sweat and liberated from the malignancy of the hidden poison, as was stated in the preceding pages.

---

# EXPERIMENT II.
->*On the Howling of Wolves.*<-

We conducted an experiment of this on *Mount Eustachiano* <note>A mountain near Rome (Mentorella) where Kircher restored a sanctuary; he spent much time there observing nature.</note> through a certain Surgeon, who knew how to imitate the howls of wolves (of which a great number dwell in the hidden places of said Mountain) so truly that to those hearing from a distance he seemed to be a wolf. Standing on a high rock, he emitted those howls through our phonic trumpet, which incited both the wolves to howl and the dogs to bark, to the great wonder of those standing by; and there was a great and violent—yet not unpleasant—contest between the screams of the howling and the barking. Nor do I doubt that by imitating the voices of other animals and birds through such sounds, a practitioner of <term>Phonurgy</term> <note>The "science of sound," a term coined by Kircher.</note> could achieve similar effects. But *let these things already said concerning the arguments of this Phonurgical Work be sufficient.*

<meta>This page marks the grand conclusion of Athanasius Kircher’s "Phonurgia Nova" (New Science of Sound). Following the previous page’s discussion of natural acoustic wonders like the Ear of Dionysius and medicinal uses of sound, Kircher pivots from scientific observation to a final theological meditation, framing his study of acoustics as a way to better hear the "divine inspiration" of the Creator.</meta>

ON VARIOUS PRODIGIOUS SOUNDS.

## CONCLUSION.

Having at last struggled out from the vast ocean of Nature, which plays with such wondrous power through <term>sound-phenomena</term> <note>original: "phonismis." Kircher uses this term throughout his work to describe the various physical manifestations and echoes of sound in the natural world.</note>, we have reached the harbor. Therefore, nothing else remains but that, with eyes raised toward heaven, we give due thanks in hymns, songs, and voices to the supreme <term>Conductor</term> <note>original: "Symphoniarchae." A Greek-derived term meaning a leader of a choir or orchestra; Kircher uses it to personify God as the director of universal harmony.</note> of the harmonic choir. For He has delighted me in His creation and in the works of His hands; having been introduced into the <term>music hall</term> <note>original: "odaeum." Historically an ancient Greek or Roman building for musical performances; here, Kircher uses it metaphorically for the divine theater of the universe.</note> of His divinity, He has so seized my heart and filled my ears with even the slightest whisper of His infinite sweetness, that I was nearly deafened to all the pleasures and joys of earthly corruption, as if to the tumultuous <term>cataracts</term> <note>original: "catadupas." Historically referring to the waterfalls of the Nile, which were famously said to deafen those living nearby; Kircher uses it to represent the chaotic, distracting noise of the material world.</note> of this falling world. Let all things, therefore, be to the glory of His Name, who gave me the ability, and who opened my ears so that I might hear through the <term>sounding</term> <note>original: "phonismo."</note> of divine inspiration.

Furthermore, it remains that the soul, with ears raised to heaven, may become worthy to reach—after the frailty of this mortality—the <term>polyphonies</term> <note>original: "polyphonismos." Kircher uses this to describe the complex, multi-voiced celestial harmonies he believes exist in the afterlife.</note> of the heavenly Choirs, beyond which there is nothing more that can be desired. This shall come to pass if we serve GOD with fear and love, always mindful of the terrifying sound of that *Apocalyptic* trumpet:

> **Arise, you dead, and come to Judgment.** <note>original: "Surgite Mortui, venite ad Judicium." This is a traditional liturgical and artistic depiction of the call to the Last Judgment.</note>

->To the Greater Glory of GOD, and of the<-
->Virgin Mother MARY.<-

## CONCLUSION

Truly, it is right that the same Nature be seen here in these <term>sound-phenomena</term> <note>original: "phonismatis." Kircher’s specific term for the physical manifestations of sound and echoes.</note>; indeed, it is demonstrated rather than merely attempted, so that what is unique in some things may be found in others above. And since we have looked into these same waters, as if through tubes, a hundred times and more, it is right to give due thanks to the <term>Symphonic Conductor</term> <note>original: "Symphoniacus." Referring to God as the master of the universal orchestra of creation.</note> of the tones of Harmony. He is the one who delights us in the stretches of the world and in the works of His own nature; He introduces us, through these divine flows, into this [realm] of His own sweetness. So much has my mind discovered in this little book that, although the opposite was previously true, it was not lacking in all the proper delights and joys—as if He were casting down the funeral rites by the lights of the world. May all things be forever directed toward the glory of Illumination. For this reason, He has hereafter given to the public this sound-manifestation of divine inspiration; He has opened our ears. For the rest, it remains that the soul be worthy of the celestial heavens, so that after the education of this mortality, it may one day join the <term>polyphonic</term> <note>original: "polyphonismos." A musical term for multiple independent melodies; Kircher uses it here to describe the complex, harmonious singing of the saints in heaven.</note> choir of the chosen Saints. This is the thing that can be most desired, a perennial state; and so, if we serve GOD with fear and love, the memory of this entire work may always be what we hoped for: **Arise, O Mortal,**

->**and come to the Judgment.**<-

->To the Greater Glory of GOD<-
->And of the<-
->Virgin Mother MARY.<-

# AN EPISTLE
## OF FATHER FRANCESCO ESCHINARDI
### OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS.
->To<-
## FATHER ATHANASIUS KIRCHER
->of the same Society.<-

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'F' embellished with scrolling foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

Rivers return to the place from whence they flow. Whatever I have meditated upon regarding sound returns to You, as if to its very Origin; indeed, through your most learned <term>Musurgia</term> <note>original: "Musurgiam." Referring to Kircher’s 1650 masterpiece *Musurgia Universalis*, which surveyed music, harmony, and the physics of sound.</note>, you have rightfully achieved this: that no one in the future shall treat this subject without deriving their own Work from your *Musurgia* as if from a fountainhead. I say no more, lest I cause a blush to your remarkable modesty amidst such universal applause; but I have spoken the above only so that everyone might know the reason why I address the following observations on sound to you. But just as it is not inconsistent with the name of a "Tribute" that rivers, while entering the sea, are carried into it with a contrary force and a different flavor: so I would wish that my meditations on sound—even if they may at times seem dissonant from your own opinion—might nonetheless be tributary to you, and that you might look upon them as your own, since they ultimately took their beginning from you.

I shall summarize here those things which I have ready to be sent to the press <note>original: "typis mandanda." A common 17th-century phrase meaning "to be printed."</note> not long from now; and in the meantime, I submit them to your prudent judgment. First, I judge that the easier propagation of the voice along the surface of a long beam is not to be attributed to the air inside the beam, but rather to its harder parts, which confine the voice so that it does not scatter in that direction, and, as it were, "solidify" it in a certain respect, thereby making it stronger,

<summary>This page features a dedicatory letter from Francesco Eschinardi to Athanasius Kircher, acknowledging Kircher's "Musurgia Universalis" as the definitive source for acoustic science. Eschinardi introduces his own upcoming theories, specifically arguing that sound travels along solid beams due to the material's hardness confining the sound waves.</summary>
<keywords>Francesco Eschinardi, Athanasius Kircher, Musurgia Universalis, acoustics, sound propagation, Jesuit science, dedication, physics of sound</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the letter from Francesco Eschinardi to Athanasius Kircher. The previous page ended with the partial word "efficienti-", which is completed here by the first syllable "bus." The discussion transitions from the general nature of sound to specific observations on how sound travels through solids versus air, and how architectural features like tapestries affect acoustic clarity.</meta>

# E P I S T L E

...efficient causes, for an impulse is propagated further and more strongly in a solid. This is confirmed by the fact that—as You yourself point out—we would obtain this same effect even from an oblong **metallic plate**; in such a case, we cannot easily appeal to the aforementioned "internal air" as the cause. On the contrary, however, I believe it should be attributed to internal air when the ear is applied directly to a wall and perceives a voice made on the other side notably more strongly than if it were distant by, for example <note>original: "v. g." for *verbi gratia*.</note>, a palm's breadth from the wall. *The reason is* <note>original: "Ratio est."</note> because the internal air of the wall, on account of its enclosure, becomes solid in a certain respect <note>original: "secundum quid." A Scholastic term meaning "in some way" or "under a certain qualification."</note>. But the external air (I speak of the air beyond the wall) immediately flows away; and so, by yielding, it loses much of the strength of the voice, all other things being equal. Note that I have been speaking of the voice, and not of sound made by striking the wall, for the latter is better propagated through the hard parts of the wall.

Regarding the common observation that in rooms and churches whose walls are draped with tapestries, sound is heard less well because the soft material dulls it: I think a careful distinction must be made between "confused sound" and "distinct sound." For indeed, a confused sound will fare less well, because it truly becomes less strong. But for distinct sound, these soft materials do not cause harm; on the contrary, they help with clarity. Because they do not reflect sound perceptibly, it ensures that the first syllable, for example, having been reflected, does not reach the ears at the same time as the second syllable arriving directly. Note that in **Music**, this matter must be observed even more carefully, for not only must the confusion of syllables be avoided, but also that of musical notes, as otherwise many dissonances might follow. Indeed, the eye can see many things distinctly at once, because individual things affect different parts of the **Retina** <note>The light-sensitive layer at the back of the eyeball.</note>. But the ear perceives through the impulse re-
<meta>catchword: ceptum</meta>

<summary>Eschinardi discusses the physics of sound propagation through solids and air, arguing that architectural dampening (like tapestries) can actually improve the clarity of speech and music by preventing overlapping echoes.</summary>

<keywords>acoustics, sound propagation, echoes, architectural acoustics, music theory, retina, Francesco Eschinardi, Athanasius Kircher</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Francesco Eschinardi’s letter to Athanasius Kircher regarding acoustics. The previous page discussed how sound is conducted through solid walls versus air; this page transitions from the physical mechanics of the eardrum to the practical application of acoustics in architectural spaces like churches and the design of musical instruments.</meta>

# E P I S T L E

...received in the eardrum <note>original: *Tympano*. Eschinardi is using the anatomical term for the middle ear to explain how sound waves are physically processed.</note>; moreover, for each individual sound, the whole eardrum trembles. (As for what should be said regarding the theory explaining vision through physical impulse—since the retina is no less solid than the auditory eardrum—let the authors of that theory consider it). However, what the eye does all at once, the ear does equivalently in succession; for sound is something successive, while color is something permanent. <note>Eschinardi notes a fundamental difference between the senses: vision perceives a whole scene at once, while hearing relies on the passage of time as vibrations follow one another.</note>

Furthermore, the crowding of people in churches harms distinct sound not so much because of weak reflection, but because of the "vaporous" air <note>Likely referring to the humidity and carbon dioxide produced by a large crowd, which changes air density.</note>. For this reason, when it is said that some singers are suitable only for small chambers <note>original: *cubiculis*</note>, this proposition must be understood only in a material sense—not that they are always aided by any reflection from the chamber, but because their voice comfortably extends only through the space of a chamber.

Hence also, vaults <note>original: *fornices*. Arched or vaulted ceilings were common in Baroque architecture but often created echoes that blurred musical clarity.</note> in churches tend to hinder rather than help the hearing of distinct music, songs, and so forth; and from this, the enjoyment is diminished, etc.

Nevertheless, in practice, some useful reflections can be found, about which we shall speak in their proper place. In large churches, the most suitable location for singers will be one that is not extremely low, but moderately elevated in proportion to the church, so that they may be heard from all parts of the building. However, it is necessary to assist them with appropriate reflections that do not discord noticeably from the direct voice.

I believe that the flared lip on the openings <note>original: *orificiis*</note> of trumpets generally helps, first of all, toward a sweeter sound. For through that widening of the opening, the encounter with the external air becomes less rapid, and thus an abrasive and unpleasant noise is not produced—just as the clash with the sea is diminished at the diverging mouths of rivers.

G g

<meta>This page continues the correspondence between Francesco Eschinardi and Athanasius Kircher. Having previously discussed how the ear processes sound waves and how environmental factors like crowds and architecture affect acoustics, Eschinardi now turns to the mechanical "tremor" (vibration) of musical instruments and how their physical construction amplifies sound.</meta>

# E P I S T L E.

Secondly, in **military trumpets**, the physical construction especially aids in producing a stronger sound because of a stronger vibration, both in terms of the material <note>original: *vestis*, literally "garment," referring here to the metal casing or "skin" of the instrument</note> and in terms of its arrangement and shape. This is not because this vibration of the trumpet or tube is universally required for sound in its simplest sense, as many people believe—for the essence of sound is satisfied if compressed air, struck by a solid or striking a solid (which is the same thing), vibrates, as I have explained in another small work <note>original: *Opusculo*</note>—but rather, it serves to increase the sound through the interaction of two sounding bodies.

I also observe that the aforementioned vibration in the military trumpet is of a different kind from the vibration essentially required for sound; for this reason, the **trembling bridge** <note>original: *pontem tremulum*. A unique feature of the marine trumpet where one foot of the bridge is left loose to tap against the soundboard.</note> contributes most to the perfect imitation of a military trumpet when played on a **marine trumpet** <note>original: *tuba marina*. Despite its name, this is a single-stringed bowed instrument that produces a brassy sound due to its vibrating bridge.</note>.

I further observe that this perceptibly trembling sound is equivalent to several sounds producing various consonances, and thus it possesses a special delight.

I notice a special vibration in sounding instruments, for example, those commonly called **harpsichords** <note>original: *clavicimbali*</note>. Although the instrument will produce a deeper or higher sound if struck depending on whether its mass is larger or smaller, when it resonates <note>original: *consonat*</note> with a plucked string, it follows the vibration of the string entirely. It produces a specific sound of the same pitch as the string, regardless of whether the mass of the wooden body is larger or smaller.

I believe that to achieve the multiplication of sound in a **spiral trumpet** <note>original: *tuba cochleata*, a trumpet with multiple coils, named after the *cochlea* or snail shell</note>, a somewhat stronger vocal force is required than in simple instruments, due to the multiple reflections and windings within; yet that greater vocal force multiplies the sound in a greater proportion than the ratio of one force to another, as I shall explain at length. Furthermore, the production of the aforementioned force is linked...

<summary>Eschinardi explains how the physical vibration (tremor) of an instrument's body amplifies sound and how specific components, like the trembling bridge of the marine trumpet, can imitate other instruments. He also discusses how the resonance of a harpsichord's wooden body syncs with its strings regardless of its size.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Francesco Eschinardi’s letter to Athanasius Kircher regarding acoustics. # E P I S T L E.

-> G g 2 <-

is related more to space than to time (as is also the case with other taut bodies), in such a way, however, that although a greater force <note>original: *impetus*</note> is produced at the beginning than at the end, nevertheless, if the motion occurs freely and without interruption, the force will be greater at the end than at the beginning.

From what has been said above, the ear is not much aided by a trumpet of this kind applied directly to it for hearing a voice or sound, unless the force of the air received within it through the wider opening <note>original: *orificium*</note> is significant.

The **reason** why a slow pronunciation is required in conophonic trumpets <note>original: *tubis Conophonicis*; these were conical speaking trumpets or megaphones designed to project the voice over long distances</note> is, among other factors, due to disordered reflection. Because of this, some lines of sound converge at the focus later and others sooner; this does not happen in elliptical devices, as I have noted elsewhere, although I do not deny that many reflections occur in conical ones.

A difficult objection can be raised against a stronger sound coming precisely from a stronger trumpet, all other things being equal. Namely, that the same force impels a larger mass of air more weakly than it does a smaller one. But in a small work <note>original: *Opusculo*</note> recently printed by me, I judged that the answer must be that, although the force might be weaker in individual parts of the enclosed air, the entire collection of air contains more force than in a shorter trumpet. This is because, due to the length of the trumpet, the force of the tense breath <note>original: *spiritûs tensi*</note> emitted from the human mouth acquires greater power as it travels, as I indicated above. And for this reason, even if the trumpet is shortened, it loses its strength in a greater proportion than the proportion of the shortening itself.

On this occasion (so that I may finally submit all things to your judgment), I add that I have shared with many people that a magnetic needle <note>original: *Acum magneticam*</note> can safely and usefully be spun upon an iron tip. The **reason is** that, on one hand, the material of the pivot cap <note>original: *pileoli*; the small cap at the center of the needle that rests on the pin</note>, since it is not made of iron, does not stick to the tip, and

# E P I S T L E.

-> X 3 <-

...on the other hand, the remaining parts of the magnetic needle <term>Acus magnetica|The magnetized needle of a compass</term> are indifferent <note>original: *indifferentes*; in 17th-century physics, this refers to a state of equilibrium where there is no preferred direction of movement</note>, so that they are drawn equally at every point of the circumference; no less than a heavy object on a horizontal plane is in a state of indifference. Therefore, the needle is not hindered by the attraction of the iron tip from turning according to magnetic requirements. I advise, however, that the pivot cap <note>original: *pileolum*; the small cap that sits on the central pin of the compass</note> can be usefully made of glass.

I have also discovered a new method for investigating the declination <note>the angle by which a wall deviates from a true cardinal direction (North, South, East, or West)</note> of a wall for sundials <term>horologium murale|A vertical sundial mounted on a building's wall</term>, through a single and brief operation toward the sun at any instant; without any subsequent calculation or the drawing of lines. Moreover, a magnetic needle is far less required—though some people sometimes secretly imply its use in their own devices, even while they otherwise promise a way of constructing a wall sundial without knowing the declination. It is explained very briefly in the present figure:

<image-desc>A geometric diagram used for finding the declination of a wall for sundials. It consists of a vertical line M A, an angled line A B representing the axis mundi, and a triangular sector labeled N Q V. A horizontal line connects M to V. Various internal lines are labeled with points O and T. The diagram is positioned to the left of the text describing it.</image-desc>

...in which **A B** represents the axis of the world <term>Axis mundi|The imaginary line around which the heavens appear to rotate, aligned with the Earth's poles</term> according to the proper altitude of the Pole. **N Q V** is a sector for solar declinations <note>the sun's angular distance north or south of the celestial equator</note>. A sighting vane <term>dioptra|An instrument with sights used for measuring angles or aligning with celestial bodies</term> is to be placed at the proper solar declination. **M** is the center, around which **A M** is turned until the shadow covers the sighting vane and its sights <note>original: *pinnacilia*</note> equally, meanwhile turning the sector to catch the sun as needed.

Finally, to the machines recently published by me, especially for the lunar eclipse, I have added a perpetual screw <note>a worm gear, allowing for precise, continuous rotation</note> for the greater ease of a slow, uniform daily motion. There now exists in Rome a machine skillfully fashioned from metal by Mr. Girolamo Caccia <note>original: *D. Hieronymo Caccia*; a noted Roman instrument maker</note> under my direction, though not without his own particular industry and genius; for he is indeed most skillful in first constructing machines of this kind.

<meta>This page transitions from the specific scientific correspondence of the previous pages to a formal glossary. While the previous page concluded Francesco Eschinardi’s letter regarding magnetic and solar instruments, this section provides the fundamental definitions for the science of acoustics as established by Athanasius Kircher. The catchword "INDEX" suggests that this glossary serves as an alphabetical or systematic guide to the specialized vocabulary used throughout the treatise.</meta>

# EXPLANATION OF TERMS,
## and of the exotic names that occur throughout this work.

<term>Phonurgia|Phonurgy</term> is the **faculty that produces wonders through sounds.**
**The Anacamptic Art**, or **Echonics**, is the faculty that explains the nature and properties of the reflected ray.
<gloss>phonokampsis</gloss> **Phonokampsis** <note>original Greek: φωνοκάμψις</note> is nothing other than the reflection of a sound-line.
A **phonic center** is the point from which the spreading of a sound or voice begins.
A **phonocamptic center** is the furthest point where a reflected line ends.
A **phonic, sonorous, or vocal line** is that along which the voice travels and returns.
**Orthophonic** <note>original: *Ortophona*; from the Greek for "straight sound"</note> is a line through which the sound is reverberated back toward the very place from which it flowed.
**Loxophonic** <note>original: *Loxophona*; from the Greek for "slanting sound"</note> is a line which, falling obliquely onto a wall, is reflected obliquely.
**Anaphonic** <note>original: *Anophona*; from the Greek for "upward sound"</note> is a line which, falling obliquely upwards, is reflected.
**Cataphonic** <note>original: *Catophona*; from the Greek for "downward sound"</note> is a line which is reflected downwards.
A **phonocamptic object** is a wall, a mountain, or any other obstacle by which a voice is reflected.
A **phonic triangle** is that which is formed by the direct and reflected lines along with a base, which is the distance between the phonic center and the phonocamptic center (or the center of the direct and reflected voice).
**Phonism** is the collection or radiation of phonic species <note>original: *specierum phonicarum*; in 17th-century science, "species" were the sensible forms or "images" of sound that traveled through a medium to the ear</note> enclosed within tubes.
**Polyphonism** is the multiplication of a voice reflected in various ways within a concave body.
**Acoustic organs** are instruments that propagate sounds to remote places.
A **phonoclastic body** <note>from the Greek *klasis*, meaning breaking or refraction</note> is one in which sound is refracted, as occurs in water.
A **conical tube** is an instrument, such as a trumpet, extended into a cone shape.
**Echometry** is the faculty of investigating the measurement of direct and reflected lines.
**Elliptical, parabolic, and hyperbolic surfaces** are those arising from various sections of a cone; see the diagrams of these in their proper places.
A tube is called **cochleated** <note>original: *cochleatus*; spiral-shaped like a snail shell</note> when it is twisted into a winding passage within a conical form.
An **otic tube** <note>original: *Oticus tubus*; an ear trumpet</note> is what we call the device we use to speak to the hard-of-hearing by placing it to the ear.
**Diapason** is a consonance in music which they call the **Octave**.
**Diapente** is the consonance which they call the **Fifth**.
**Diatessaron** is the consonance which they call the **Fourth**.
**Nete** <note>from the Greek *nētē*, the "lowest" string in position but highest in pitch</note> is the string which represents the highest voice in the Octave.
**Hypate** <note>from the Greek *hypatē*, the "highest" string in position but lowest in pitch</note> is that which signifies the lowest string of the Bass.
**Isotonic strings** are the same as strings that are equally tensioned.

<vocab>Phonurgy, Anacamptic Art, Phonokampsis, Phonic center, Orthophonic, Loxophonic, Anaphonic, Cataphonic, Phonism, Polyphonism, Acoustic organs, Phonoclastic, Echometry, Diapason, Diapente, Diatessaron, Nete, Hypate</vocab>

<summary>This page provides a comprehensive glossary of acoustic and musical terminology used in the 17th-century study of sound. It defines the geometric behavior of sound waves (reflection and refraction) and introduces specialized instruments like ear trumpets and conical speakers.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, phonurgy, echo, reflection, refraction, musical intervals, scientific instruments, geometry of sound</keywords>

AND WORDS.

Cucumber Mountain <note>original: "Montis Cucumeris"; likely referring to a peak in the Swiss Alps known for unusual echoes.</note> in Switzerland: a similar effect. 224.
Another mountain in Britain producing the sound of many cymbals. 229.
Another similar one in Hispaniola <note>The island in the Caribbean containing modern-day Haiti and the Dominican Republic.</note> in America. 225.
Smellen in Finland near the city of Vyborg: a terrifying sound, by which men fall down as if dead. 223.
Inhabitants of that place who take precautions for themselves, and turn every evil against the besieging enemies. ibid. <note>original: "ibidem," meaning "in the same place."</note>
Concave sphere: the most suitable of all for propagating sound. 40.
Spiral line: see Line.
<term>Corporeal spirits</term>: how they follow alterations, impressions, or movements of the air. 190. <note>In early modern medicine, "spirits" were subtle fluids or gases in the body thought to transmit sensory information and movement.</note>
Statue moving itself to a certain sound. 152.
Another [statue] pronouncing all kinds of sounds, articulate and inarticulate: how it is to be made. 161. & 162.
Another hanging in mid-air similarly pronouncing everything by a craft still more admirable than all others, which the Author decided not yet to reveal. 166.
Stratagem by which the Army of the Turks could be terrified. 138.
Parabolic surface is twofold: obtuse and acute. 92.
The difference of each in establishing the <term>acoustic point</term>. ibid. <note>The focal point where sound waves converge in a curved surface.</note>
<margin>+</margin> A partially deaf person: by what craft it is to be managed so they can perceive Music. 147.
Likewise, so that a conversation can be conveniently held with him. 160.
<margin>+</margin> How to understand the deaf cured with a trumpet by <term>Asclepiades</term>. 174. <note>Asclepiades of Bithynia was an ancient Greek physician who advocated for music as a medical treatment.</note>
<margin>+</margin> The rationale of <term>sympathy and antipathy</term> in sounds. 187. <note>The idea that certain sounds naturally attract or repel each other, or affect the human body through "resonance."</note>
<margin>+</margin> Various experiments of harmonic sympathy. 151.

-> T. <-

Tarantula: what kind of animal it is. 204.
It consists of a viscous and thin humor, easily moved by and proportioned to sound. 207.
The Musical Antidote for the Tarantula. 209.
That Tarantulas consist of contrary qualities, whence it is demonstrated by a wonderful story that those suffering from <term>tarantism</term> <note>A supposed dancing mania caused by the bite of a tarantula spider, cured only by specific music.</note> are to be cured by contrary harmonies. 210.
<margin>+</margin> Reason why the Tarantula dances to sound. 207. & 208.
Those suffering from tarantism: why they can be cured by no other means than a harmonic one, and a different one according to the diversity of their <term>complexion</term>. 205. <note>In the humoral theory of medicine, "complexion" refers to a person's specific balance of bodily fluids.</note>
A certain girl bitten by a tarantula who could only be

<column-break/>

cured by drums. 206.
Those bitten by tarantulas are affected by different colors, and which ones, and why. 212.
They similarly adopt different behaviors and movements, and why. 215.
In <term>Taranto</term>, musicians are supported by public funds from the magistrate; their diligence and observation in seeking out the convenient harmony for curing the tarantula-bitten is described. 207.
The sound of an earthquake: what it is like. 121.
Corinthian Theater. See Corinthian.
<term>Timotheus the Musician</term>: how often he pleased, he would draw the mind of Alexander the Great either to fury, or to gentleness or joy, by means of a Phrygian sound. 174. <note>The Phrygian mode was anciently considered an exciting, warlike musical scale.</note>
The sound of thunder: most powerful. 121.
How far it was heard by the Author. Ibid.
The sound of military engines <note>original: "tormentorum bellicorum"; referring to cannons or large artillery.</note> made at Rome is heard at Frascati or Tivoli. Ibid.
From the island of Capri, it is heard as far as Naples, 30 Italian miles away. ibid.
Whether the sound made at Gravelines in Belgium is heard in England, the Author doubts. ibid.
Oblong beams: how and why they propagate sound so miraculously. 65.
Why they provide nothing when tied together or enclosed in a wall, unlike a conical tube. 66.
The trumpet <note>original: "Tuba."</note> is an ancient instrument already used in the Old Testament. 59.
How it follows a natural numerical progression. 60.
The higher it ascends, the further it always recedes from the perfections of the consonant. 61.
Why it moves through sounds in the highest voices. ibid.
In the generation of sounds, it performs the same by addition as a string does by division. ibid.
Various classes of trumpets. 59.
Why the <term>ductile trumpet</term> <note>original: "Ductilis"; referring to the slide trumpet or trombone.</note> can express all tones in order, but not the ordinary or military trumpet. 61.
The <term>acoustic tube</term> <note>original: "Tubus acusticus"; Kircher's term for the speaking trumpet or megaphone.</note> is not an invention of the English, but of the Author. 112.
The wonderful effects of this tube in the Author's own museum. ibid.
His Imperial Majesty recently made an experiment of the same tube from Ebersdorf even to Neugebäude. 114.
How it should be constructed to be suitable not only for speaking, but also for hearing or receiving a response without the tube of the speaker. 125.
The size of the acoustic tube. 118.

<meta>This page concludes the alphabetical "Index of Things and Words." # INDEX OF THINGS AND WORDS.

*Circular [tube]: why it propagates the voice better.* 66.
*Spiral-shaped: why it intensifies sound more than all others.* 71.
*It requires, however, a stronger breath from the blower.* col. 4. <note>original: "insufflantis"; refers to the person blowing or speaking into the instrument.</note>
*And it is twofold: conical and cylindrical.* 130.
*Conical: how it is to be constructed.* 124.
*Divided into parts, it is not as suitable, and completely unfit for receiving a response.* 124. & 125.
*It surpasses all other tubes.* 131.
*It aspires to something of a parabolic shape.* 164. <note>In acoustics, a parabolic shape helps focus sound waves to a specific point.</note>
*What unique properties it has above others for the augmentation of sounds.* 117.
*The cause of its <term>Polyphonism</term>.* ibid. <note>Polyphonism: the production of multiple echoes or layers of sound from a single source.</note>
*Why it requires a slower pronunciation than others.* col. 5.
*Cylindrical: why it is less fit for sound than the conical.* 67.
*Elliptical: it provides a huge multiplication of sounds, and why.* 129.
*Helical: requires a skilled craftsman, and why.* 116.
*Spherical: no less so.* 128.
*Serpentine: emits a sound very much suited to a bass voice.* 135.
*Drum-like [tube]: extends an immense sound into vast spaces.* ibid. <note>original: "Tympanites"; likely referring to a wide-flared or resonant acoustic chamber.</note>
*Tubes which only emit a violent sound are military trumpets, war-horns, etc.* 131.
*Various tubes for emitting a prodigious sound.* 128.
*Its opening with an extended lip helps produce a sweeter sound, and why; indeed, in military ones, a stronger sound.* col. 3. & 4.

### V.

*Vesuvius: provides a very new echo in the valley after the last eruption.* 88. <note>original: "post ultimam ruinam"; refers to the famous volcanic collapse or eruption of Mount Vesuvius.</note>

<column-break/>

*Villilla in Spain: its miraculous bell sounding of its own accord whenever anything adverse happens in the Christian Republic.* 221. <note>The "Bell of Velilla" was a famous Spanish legend of a bell that supposedly rang by itself to predict deaths of kings or national disasters.</note>
*Nieremberg <note>original: "NORIMBERGIUS"; refers to Juan Eusebius Nieremberg (1595–1658), a Jesuit polymath.</note> testifies that it has sounded miraculously even in modern times.* ibid.
VITRUVIUS *described the Corinthian Theater.* 73. <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman architect whose works were foundational for Renaissance acoustics.</note>
*His errors in Music.* 77.
*Voice: whether it can be enclosed in a channel so that it persists.* 62.
*The deception regarding this by* CORNELIUS AGRIPPA, *vigorously defended by* ALEXIUS WEHER.* ibid. <note>original: "Impostura"; refers to the occultist Agrippa's claim that voices could be trapped in a pipe and heard later—a precursor to the idea of recorded sound.</note>
*The Author's decision and the refutation of the opposing views.* ibid.
*Sent out from the center against a three-sided object, it returns to itself increased by a triple <term>phonism</term>.* 32. <note>Phonism: an instance of sound reflection or an echo.</note>
*Against a four-sided object, by a quadruple one.* 33.
*Against an equilateral Pentagon, it returns increased by 5 phonisms.* 35.
*Against a hexagon, it returns with 6 phonisms.* ibid.
*Against a dodecagon, 12 phonisms.*
*In a cylindrical [space], it is reflected from all points of the circumference.* 38.
*In a Pyramidal [space], how it does not return to itself.* 39.
*It is propagated on the surface of a long beam, not through the internal air.* col. 1.
*When it is reflected back on itself.* 19.
*Or in the opposite direction.* 20.
*The more remote the space it travels, the weaker it becomes.* 46.
*It presents itself more slowly to the ears than light does to the eyes.* 122.
*Why it is reflected from the bottom of a valley to someone standing on a mountain.* 42.
